Oral Abstracts Oral Abstract Session 01: National Surveys and Large-Scale Data of IM OA 01.01 Effects of Acupuncture for Pain Management among Inpatients with Cancer: A Secondary Analysis of University vs Public Hospital Settings George S Yeung MD , Sanjay Reddy MD, Wendy Hartogensis PhD, MPH, Donald I Abrams MD, Anand Dhruva MD, Frederick M Hecht MD, Maria T Chao DrPH, MPA UCSF, San Francisco, CA, United States Purpose : Acupuncture is increasingly recognized as a safe and effective integrative therapy for cancer pain management. Most evidence comes from university hospitals, with limited data from public hospital settings. Understanding potential differences in acupuncture use, effectiveness, and implementation across hospital types is essential to promote equitable access and guide acupuncture’s integration into inpatient pain management. Method(s) : We performed a secondary analysis of the Pragmatic Research of Acupuncture and pain Counseling eXtended to Inpatient Services (PRACXIS) randomized controlled trial evaluating acupuncture and pain counseling for hospitalized adults with cancer pain. The study was conducted at a university hospital (UH) and a public safety-net hospital (PH). The analysis consisted of two parts: First, we compared demographic and clinical characteristics of all cancer inpatients (UH n = 14,336; PH n = 1,146, 2019–2020). Second, we used intent-to-treat linear mixed models to examine heterogeneity of treatment effects among 447 randomized participants (UH n = 338; PH n = 109). Results : Among all cancer inpatients, PH patients were more racially and linguistically diverse compared with UH (24.5% vs 12.6% Latino, 31.5% vs 14.5% Asian/Pacific Islander, 17.5% vs 5.0% Black/African American, 49.7% vs 76.8% White); and had higher metastatic disease rates (21.9% vs 12.9% metastatic). Among the randomized PRACXIS participants, differences in treatment outcomes varied by hospital. In the no-acupuncture group, UH patients showed significantly greater improvement in pain relief (–0.44 [95% CI –0.85, –0.03]), symptom severity (0.26 [95% CI 0.06, 0.46]), and symptom burden (0.43 [95% CI 0.17, 0.69]) than their PH counterparts. For patients who received acupuncture, between-hospital differences were reduced for pain relief (0.03 [95% CI -0.36, 0.43]), symptom severity (0.00 [95% CI -0.20, 0.20]), and symptom burden (0.15 [95% -0.11, 0.41]). Conclusions : Acupuncture reduced between-hospital disparities in outcomes, suggesting that incorporating nonpharmacologic approaches may mitigate potential disparities in hospital-based pain management. OA 01.02 Is there an Integrative Safety Net? A National Survey of Traditional, Complementary, and Integrative Health Services in US Safety-Net Primary Care Clinics Dr Ariana Thompson-Lastad PhD 1 , Dr Isabel Roth DrPh 2 , Dr Stephanie Christian PhD 3 , Dr Nadine Ijaz PhD 4 1 University of California San Francisco, San Francisco, CA, United States 2 University of North Caroline, Chapel Hill, NC, United States 3 Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center, New York, NY, United States 4 Carleton University, Ottawa, Ontario, Canada Purpose : Safety-net clinics such as Federally Qualified Health Centers (FQHCs) provide primary care to one in nine people in the United States. Although treatment guidelines now recommend traditional, complementary and integrative medicine (TCIM) for chronic pain and other common conditions, little is known about the availability of TCIM within United States safety-net clinics. Group Medical Visits are a common model used in primary care to provide TCIM services. This is the first national survey of US safety-net clinics that aims to assess the prevalence and availability of TCIM and group medical visit programs. Method(s) : Recruitment for this brief online survey was conducted through professional organizations and networks. All employees of safety-net health clinics (FQHCs, community health centers, Indian Health Service sites) were eligible to participate. Descriptive analyses were conducted. Results : We received 123 responses from 93 safety-net clinics in 30 states, the District of Columbia, and Puerto Rico, with the most represented states being California (N=26), Oregon (N=7) and New York (N=7). TCIM practitioners most commonly employed by safety-net clinics included acupuncturists (n=36); midwives (n=30), yoga therapists or teachers (n=18), and chiropractors (n=17). The most widely available TCIM services included acupuncture, mindfulness, other mind-body practices, osteopathic manual treatment and herbal medicine. 56% of organizations offered group medical visits, most commonly for diabetes, chronic pain, or well-child care. Over half of respondents were interested in participating in future research on integrative healthcare and/or group medical visits. Conclusions : This is the first national survey to investigate how widely TCIM and group medical visits are provided in US safety-net clinics. We identified many examples of these programs across the country, offering TCIM to patient populations often excluded from integrative health care and related research. Findings can inform future efforts to provide equitable access to guideline-concordant care, including group medical visits and integrative care. OA 01.03 Research Interest in and Use of Complementary and Integrative Health Therapies among Older Adults on Glucagon-Like Peptide-1 Receptor Agonists Dr. Sarah J Park ND, MPH 1 , Dr. Rachel L Rosen PhD 2 , Dr. Dennis Muñoz-Vergara DVM, MPH 1 , Dr. JoAnn E Manson MD, DrPH 1 , Dr. Gloria Y Yeh MD, MPH 3 , Dr. Peter M Wayne PhD 1 , Dr. Howard D Sesso ScD, MPH 1 1 Brigham and Women's Hospital and Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 2 Massachusetts General Hospital and Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 3 Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center and Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States Research suggests complementary and integrative health (CIH) therapies may enhance diabetes self-management. However, their role alongside glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonist (GLP-1RA) therapy, where side effects can hinder adherence to medication and recommended lifestyle changes, remains largely unexplored. Purpose : We conducted a cross-sectional analysis of the COcoa Supplement and Multivitamin Outcomes Study (COSMOS) 2024 questionnaire to examine whether self-reported GLP-1RA status was associated with CIH use or research interest among older US adults. A total of 15,732 participants were classified as Method(s) : GLP-1RA users for diabetes (DM-users) or weight management-only (WM-users), or non-users with diabetes or obesity. We estimated multivariable odds ratios (ORs) and 95% confidence intervals (CI) of lifetime and recent use of, as well as research interest in, any CIH category and each of six categories, including manual therapies, mind-body therapies, herbal products, acupuncture, spiritual practices, and cannabis/psychedelics. Results : Among 592 GLP-1RA users (503 DM-users, 89 WM-users), over 75% reported having used at least one CIH therapy in their lifetime or in the past 12 months (most commonly, manual therapies, spiritual practices, and herbal products), while 60.5% reported interest in their research. Compared to non-users with diabetes, DM-users had 1.36 times the odds of lifetime cannabis use (95% CI: 1.03, 1.81), as well as greater research interest in all CIH categories except spiritual practices (all P≤0.05). WM-users had 2.13 times the odds of any lifetime CIH use (excluding spiritual practices) than non-users with obesity (95% CI: 1.10, 4.14), largely driven by significant associations for manual therapies and acupuncture (all P<0.05). Conclusions : In this trial cohort of older adults, participants taking GLP-1RAs for diabetes were more likely to report interest in participating in research across most CIH categories, while those taking them for weight management-only were more likely to have ever tried manual therapies or acupuncture. OA 01.04 Prevalence and Emerging Trends in Dietary Supplement Use Among U.S. Adults, 1999–2023: Two Decades of NHANES Data Dr. Chun Sing Lam 1 , Dr. Kelli O’Connell 2 , Dr. Maria J. Monroy-Iglesias 2 , Dr Peng Wang 2 , Dr. Mengmeng Du 2 , Prof. Edward L. Giovannucci 3 , Dr. Jun J. Mao 1 , Dr. Elizabeth D. Kantor 2 1 Integrative Medicine and Wellness Service, Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center, New York, NY, United States 2 Department of Epidemiology and Biostatistics, Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center, New York, NY, United States 3 Departments of Nutrition and Epidemiology, Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : Dietary supplement use is widespread among U.S. adults, yet recent population-level data are limited. Understanding long-term and emerging trends, particularly following the COVID-19 pandemic, is essential for guiding research. This study aimed to evaluate national trends in dietary supplement use among U.S. adults from 1999 to 2023 and to assess changes in overall prevalence, supplement categories, and individual product use, with further evaluation of changes in use across pre- and post-pandemic periods. Method(s) : We conducted a cross-sectional analysis of 11 cycles of the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES, 1999–August 2023), including 63,442 adults aged ≥20 years. Supplement use during the past 30 days was assessed overall, by supplement category (vitamins, minerals, multivitamin/multimineral [MVMM], botanicals, and non-vitamin non-mineral [NVNM] products), and by individual supplement type. Trends over time and differences between pre-pandemic and post-pandemic periods were examined using survey weighted regression and generalized linear models. Results : Supplement use increased from 51.0% in 1999–2000 to 59.9% in 2021–2023 (P-trend <0.001). Use of vitamin D (5.1% to 29%) and vitamin K (0.3% to 2.1%), both excluding MVMM, notably increased. Supplements marketed for anti-inflammatory or immune-related functions—curcumin, ashwagandha, prebiotics/probiotics, and zinc—also grew, while several trace minerals declined. Supplement use with purported immune-related functions increased markedly in the latest cycle (Aug 2021–Aug 2023) compared with the pre-pandemic period. Conclusions : Dietary supplement use among U.S. adults has continued to rise, with notable increases in specific vitamins and products marketed for immune-related, stress adaptation and anti-inflammatory functions. These patterns indicate a broadening of supplement use, with increased uptake of products marketed for specific health functions. The COVID-19 pandemic may have further heightened interest in immune-supporting products. Ongoing monitoring of supplement use trends is needed to understand their evolving public health implications in the U.S. adult population. Oral Abstract Session 02: Mind-Body Practices for Oncology OA 02.01 Teaching Cancer Survivors Coping Skills from a Mind-Body Intervention for Fear of Recurrence: Exploratory Outcomes from a Pilot Randomized Controlled Trial Aimee J Christie PhD 1 , Caleb Bolden BA 2 , Elyse R Park PhD, MPH 2,3 , Gloria Y Yeh MD, MPH 3,4 , Conall O'Cleirigh PhD 2,3 , Hang Lee PhD 2,3 , Jeffrey Peppercorn MD 2,3 , Lynne I Wagner PhD 5 , Elisabeth C Henley BA 6 , Lara Traeger PhD 6 , Ade Adamson MD, MPP 7 , Anthony Sung MD 8 , Daniel L Hall PhD 2,3 1 The University of Texas at MD Anderson Cancer Center, Houston, TX, United States 2 Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA, United States 3 Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 4 Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States 5 Gillings School of Public Health, University of North Carolina, Chapel Hill, NC, United States 6 University of Miami, Coral Gables, FL, United States 7 The University of Texas at Austin, Austin, TX, United States 8 The University of Kansas Medical Center, Kansas City, KA, United States Purpose : Fear of cancer recurrence (FCR) is highly common and, if poorly managed, can be distressing and impairing. We developed a virtual, mind-body resiliency intervention for FCR in survivorship (IN FOCUS, NCT04876599 ), which was shown to be feasible and improved FCR post-intervention. The purpose of this study was to explore IN FOCUS effects on proposed intervention mechanisms: coping styles and perceived coping skills. Method(s) : A single-blinded, 2-arm, randomized controlled trial was conducted from July 2021 to March 2022 comparing IN FOCUS (8 weekly, 90-minute, synchronous virtual group classes teaching cognitive behavioral techniques, relaxation training, meditation, adaptive health behaviors, and positive psychology skills) to usual care (synchronous virtual community group support referral) among cancer survivors with non-metastatic disease and clinically elevated FCR (FCR Inventory severity ≥ 16). Measures included coping styles (Brief COPE) and perceived coping skills (Measure of Current Status-Part A). Intent-to-treat analyses with separate general linear mixed models were used to identify group-by-time effects (Cohen’s d; 0.5 a medium effect, 0.8 a large effect) from baseline through 2 months and 5 months. Results : Sixty-four survivors enrolled (age M=52 years, time since completing primary cancer treatment M=5 years). By 5 months, survivors randomized to IN FOCUS (vs. usual care) demonstrated multiple effects on coping in the medium to large range. Compared to usual care, IN FOCUS increased use of problem-focused coping using instrumental support (d=0.6), planning (d=0.6), active coping (d=0.5), and positive reframing (d=0.5). Similarly, IN FOCUS improved emotion-focused coping using venting (d=0.7), acceptance (d=0.6), humor (d=0.5), and religion (d=0.5). IN FOCUS also enhanced survivors’ coping confidence (d=0.8), relaxation skills (d=0.6), and assertiveness (d=0.5). Avoidance-focused coping and awareness of physical tension exhibited less robust changes by 5 months. Conclusions : For cancer survivors with FCR, IN FOCUS shows promise of strengthening problem-focused and emotion-focused coping, reducing avoidance-focused coping, and enhancing perceived coping skills. Cancer survivors can enhance multiple aspects of coping with FCR through interventions such as IN FOCUS that teach mind-body resiliency techniques. OA 02.02 Safe delivery of psilocybin-assisted therapy in oncology: models and stakeholder perspectives Dr Suzanne Grant PhD Western Sydney University, Sydney, NSW, Australia Purpose : Existential distress in cancer is common and only partially addressed by standard therapies. Psilocybin-assisted therapy (PAT) shows promise, but models of delivery for oncology remain undefined. We conducted three qualitative studies—healthcare professionals (HCPs), people with cancer, and psychedelic researchers—to identify requirements for safe, acceptable, and legitimate PAT delivery in cancer care, focusing on governance, workforce, set/setting, and integration within oncology settings. Method(s) : Semi-structured interviews (n=40) were conducted with Australian HCPs (n=11) from oncology, psycho-oncology, nursing, psychiatry, and pharmacy; adults with advanced cancer (n=15); and researchers conducting PAT studies (n=14). All received ethics approval (St Vincent’s HREC 2023/ ETH00860 ). Reflexive thematic analysis was applied. Interview guides focused on probed models of delivery and care (e.g., basic-support vs EMBARK), screening, dosing-day supports and preferred settings, types of practitioners to involve, and integration; HCPs and patients also completed brief knowledge/attitudes measures to contextualise interviews. Results : Synthesis across groups highlighted: (1) multidisciplinary governance with oncology–palliative oversight; (2) the need for rigorous screening, preparation, skilled facilitation, and structured post-session integration; (3) trauma-informed, culturally responsive environments; (4) workforce training/ supervision and role clarity (e.g., co-facilitator models); and (5) clear referral/funding pathways. HCPs reported interest despite having knowledge gaps and implementation barriers. With a preference for team-based delivery. Patients prioritised safety, consent clarity, compassionate containment, and acceptable settings. Researchers emphasised therapist training, cost, supervision structures, and group therapy models. Conclusions : Stakeholders endorse a research-led translation of PAT into oncology using integrative supportive-care models under defined governance. Core specifications include robust screening, preparation, skilled co-facilitation, safe environments, and structured integration, supported by workforce training and culturally safe practice. These study provides actionable input for protocols, model delivery design, and regulatory planning while providing an understanding of current limitations (evidence gaps, workforce readiness, resources). OA 02.03 Impact of Mindfulness and Tai Chi on Sleep, Fatigue and Pain in Cancer Survivors: Results of a Hybrid Comparative Effectiveness and Preference Trial Yan Ma 1 , Linda E. Carlson 2 , Jennifer M. Jones 3 , Oluwaseyi A. Lawal 2 , Devesh Oberoi 2 , Katherine-Ann Piedalue 2 , Daniel Santa Mina 3 , Michael Speca 2 , Peter M. Wayne 1 1 Osher Center for Integrative Health, Harvard Medical School and Brigham and Women’s Hospital, Boston, MA, United States 2 Department of Oncology, University of Calgary, Calgary, AB, Canada 3 Department of Supportive Care, Princess Margaret Cancer Centre, Toronto, ON, Canada Purpose : The MATCH (Mindfulness and Tai Chi for Cancer Health) study is a partially-randomized, patient-preference, comparative effectiveness trial to understand the effects of Mindfulness-Based Cancer Recovery (MBCR) and Tai Chi/Qigong (TCQ) in distressed people after cancer treatment. Method(s) : Participants with a preference for either MBCR or TCQ received their preferred intervention, while those without a preference underwent block randomization into either the MBCR or TCQ program. Further, within the preference and non-preference groups, all participants were randomized into immediate intervention or waitlist control in a 2:1 ratio. We evaluated the efficacy of MBCR and TCQ, whether participants’ choices impacted intervention effects, and correlations among changes in sleep, fatigue, and pain as planned secondary outcomes, assessed by Pittsburgh Sleep Quality Index, Functional Assessment of Chronic Illness Therapy - Fatigue, and Brief Pain Inventory, respectively. Results : Participants (n=587, 75% female) had a mean age of 60.7 years, with all types and most stages of cancer. At post-intervention, compared with waitlist controls, the TCQ group had significantly improved sleep quality and effects remained significant after adjustment for age, sex, cancer stage, and preference (β =-1.90, 95% CI: -3.18, -0.62; p=0.004). Compared with waitlist controls, both MBCR and TCQ groups had significantly improved fatigue (MBCR: β=3.66, 95% CI: 0.25, 7.08; p=0.036. TCQ: β=3.42, 95% CI: 0.10, 6.73; p=0.043) and pain interference (MBCR: β=-1.10, 95%CI: -2.09, -0.11; p=0.029. TCQ: β=-1.52, 95%CI: -2.49, -0.55; p=0.002). Significant correlations were observed among score changes for sleep, fatigue, and pain interference, suggesting mild to moderate associations. Conclusions : Compared to waitlist controls, MBCR and TCQ both improved fatigue and pain interference at post-intervention, and TCQ also improved sleep quality. Correlations across outcomes highlight the importance of addressing clusters of cancer symptoms within the framework of whole-person-health. Acknowledgements: NCCIH K23AT012475 and K24AT009282. Lotte & John Hecht Memorial Foundation (grant #4000). OA 02.04 Scaling an Effective Intervention of Silence or Mindfulness Meditation to Reduce Anxiety, Emotional and Physiologic Distress for Cancer Patients Prior to Surgery Dr. Maryanna D Klatt PhD 1 , Angela Emerson MPH 1 , Jacqueline Caputo MPH 1 , Yulia Mulugeta B.S. 1 , Nina Knight BS 1 , Dr. Beth Steinberg PhD 1 , Ramalakshmy Shankarganesh 1 , Dr. Anne-Marie Duchemin MD 2 , Dr. Elizabeth Palmer Kelly PhD 3 , Dr. Tim Pawlik , PhD, MPH, MTS, MBA, FACS, FSSO, FRACS 3 1 Center for Integrative Health, College of Medicine, The Ohio State University, Columbus, Ohio, United States 2 Department of Psychiatry, College of Medicine, The Ohio State University, Columbus, Ohio, United States 3 Department of Surgery, The Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, Columbus, Ohio, United States Purpose : This study was conducted to scale and evaluate the effectiveness of a 10-minute audio-guided meditation/silence on the anxiety/emotional distress levels and vital signs of patients undergoing cancer surgery, which was previously shown to be effective with patients undergoing orthopedic surgery. Method(s) : Consented patients awaiting cancer surgery were randomly assigned to 10-minutes of guided meditation or silence, administered through noise cancelling headphones in the pre-operative area. Emotional distress/anxiety and vital signs (i.e. pulse, respiration rate, and blood pressure) were assessed pre/post interventions. Results : Patients who received either guided meditation or silence (n=61) achieved significant decreases on the Emotional Distress Scale (p=0.00001) pre to post intervention, with no significant differences between groups receiving meditation (n=28) or silence (n=33) (p=0.5298). Patients rated their anxiety levels on a scale of 1(no anxiety) to 10(high anxiety). Combining the groups, anxiety levels were significantly reduced pre/post intervention (p=0.00001) with no significant difference between groups (p=0.8178). Consistent with the emotional distress/anxiety findings when randomized conditions were combined, systolic blood pressure was significantly lower pre/post (p=0.0006), and diastolic blood pressure was lower but not significant (p=0.0537). There were no significant changes pre/post of pulse rate (p=0.9464) nor respiration rate (p=0.5248) following either intervention, and no significant differences pre/post of any vital signs between groups. Conclusions : Providing either a 10-minute guided meditation or silence prior to surgery reduced anxiety, emotional distress, and some signs of physiologic distress in cancer patients. These mind/body interventions were successfully piloted in orthopedic surgery, then scaled to cancer surgery to address greater associated pre-surgical patient stress. Implementing either guided meditation or providing noise-canceling headphones for silent relaxation could be adopted as a low-cost standard practice in pre-operative care as a means to reduce and improve emotional distress/anxiety and correlated vital signs before surgery. Oral Abstract Session 03: Integrative Health for Low Back Pain OA 03.01 Which lumbar spinal stenosis patients will improve with nonsurgical treatment? A secondary analysis of a randomized controlled trial Eric J Roseen DC, PhD 1 , Clair N Smith MS 2 , Asifa Rahim MPH 1 , Conor Deal 1 , Ryan Fischer DC, PharmD 1 , Natalia E Morone MD, MS 1 , Andrew Flack DC 3 , Charles Penza DC, PhD 3 , Pradeep Suri MD, MSc 4 , Paul E Dougherty DC 5 , Debra K Weiner MD 2 , Michael J Schneider DC, PhD 2 1 Boston University, Boston, MA, United States 2 University of Pittsburgh, Pittsburgh, PA, United States 3 Orlando VA Medical Center, Orlando, FL, United States 4 University of Washington, Seattle, WA, United States 5 Syracuse VA Medical Center, Syracuse, NY, United States Purpose : Lumbar spinal stenosis (LSS) can be disabling and is a leading reason for spine surgery in older adults. While nonsurgical treatments are recommended for LSS, it remains unclear which patients will benefit most. Method(s) : This is a secondary analysis of a clinical trial which randomized 216 adults with LSS to: manual therapy with individualized exercise (MTE); group exercise (GE); or medical care (MC). We evaluated the association of baseline characteristics with 2-month changes in primary outcomes: symptoms/function on the Swiss Spinal Stenosis questionnaire (SSSQ) and walking capacity on the self-paced walking test (SPWT). To explore heterogeneity of treatment effects, we evaluated unadjusted stratified estimates when comparing MTE vs. GE/MC. Additionally, we included an interaction term in models to test for statistical interaction. Results : At baseline, participants (mean age=72, 54% female) had moderate LSS-related symptoms/impairment (mean SSSQ score=31.3) and limited walking capacity (mean=451 meters). The overall improvement on SSSQ was 2.5 points; larger improvements were observed among younger, non-white, non-smoking participants, and those with worse baseline symptoms/impairment. Overall improvement on the SPWT was 205 meters; larger improvements were observed among younger participants, those with higher baseline physical activity levels and participants without knee osteoarthritis. For SSSQ, the treatment effect was larger among adults aged <70 vs older adults (MTE vs. GE/MC; mean difference [MD]=-4.06, 95%CI=-6.29 to -1.83 vs. MD=-0.47. 95%CI=-2.63 to 1.69, respectively; p-for-interaction=0.02). For walking capacity, the treatment effect was larger among adults with hip osteoarthritis compared to those without (MTE vs. GE/MC; MD=500 meters, 95%CI=71 to 929, vs MD=13 meters, 95%CI=-120 to 147, respectively; p-for-interaction=0.007). Conclusions : In a sample receiving nonsurgical treatments for LSS, we identified patient-level characteristics associated with larger improvements and/or treatment effects. If confirmed in larger randomized controlled trials, these findings may guide clinical decision-making to enhance clinical outcomes. OA 03.02 Examining Interoceptive Sensibility as a Mechanism of Change in Movement-Based Interventions for Chronic Low Back Pain: A Secondary Analysis of a Randomized Controlled Trial Katherine E Gnall MS 1 , Crystal L Park PhD 2 , Tania Huedo-Medina PhD 3,4,5 , Wolf Mehling MD 6 , Erik Groessl PhD 7,8 , Angela R Starkweather PhD, ACNP-BC, FAANP, FAAN 9 1 University of Connecticut, Storrs, CT, United States 2 University of Connecticut, Storss, CT, United States 3 Ikerbasque Research Foundation, Bilbao, Basque Country, Spain 4 University of the Basque Country, Leioa, Basque Country, Spain 5 University of Connecticut, Storrs, MA, United States 6 University of California San Francisco, San Francisco, CA, United States 7 University of California San Diego, San Diego, CA, United States 8 Veteran's Affairs San Diego Health System, San Diego, CA, United States 9 Rutgers University, New Brunswick, NJ, United States Purpose : While yoga is effective for chronic low back pain (CLBP), less is known about how yoga reduces pain nor whether it operates distinctly from other movement interventions. One proposed mechanism is improvement in interoceptive sensibility (IS; one’s awareness/interpretation of and response to internal sensations). The present secondary analysis from a randomized controlled trial (RCT) tested whether yoga results in changes to IS compared to stretching/strengthening and whether such changes account for yoga’s effect on pain. Method(s) : 168 adults with CLBP (Mage=43.56, 66% female, 73.2% White) participated in a 12-week RCT (yoga versus stretching/strengthening) and completed assessments at baseline, mid-treatment, and post-treatment. Three subscales of the MAIA-2 (Not-Worrying, Self-Regulation, and Trusting) assessed IS. Pain was assessed via self-report pain severity/interference (BPI-SF) and laboratory-assessed pain sensitization (Quantitative Sensory Testing (QST); pressure, heat, and cold pain). Unconditional growth models assessed changes over time, and multilevel mediation analyses with Monte Carlo 95% confidence intervals tested IS as a mechanism of change. Results : Not-worrying (p=.012), but not Self-regulation nor Trusting (ps>.05), changed over time. Path a was not significant for any IS domains (ps>.05). For the pain severity model, path b (IS domains predicting pain) was significant at the between-person level for Trusting (p=.02). For the pain interference model, path b was significant at the between-person level for Self-Regulation and Trusting (ps<.05). Across all QST measures, path b was significant at the between-person level for Not-Worrying (ps<.01). Path c (total effect) was not significant (ps>.05). None of the indirect effects were significant (95%CIs included zero). Conclusions : Results suggest that aspects of interoceptive sensibility are associated with better pain outcomes among adults with CLBP. Additionally, movement interventions may help improve aspects of interoceptive sensibility, though yoga may not differ from more traditional stretching/strengthening programs in improving interoceptive sensibility and pain among adults with CLBP. OA 03.03 Acupuncture vs. Pharmacotherapy for Chronic Low Back Pain: Comparative Risk of Adverse Events among Medicare Beneficiaries Dr. James M Whedon DC, MS , Dr. Leah Grout PhD Southern California University of Health Sciences, Whittier, CA, United States Purpose : In 2020 Medicare initiated coverage of acupuncture for treatment of chronic low back pain (cLBP). The comparative safety of acupuncture vs. medication for management of cLBP has not been evaluated among Medicare beneficiaries. The only serious complication reported to be associated with acupuncture is pneumothorax, but its incidence is very low. By contrast, prescription analgesics carry a significant risk of adverse effects. The purpose of this study was to compare acupuncture vs. analgesic pharmacotherapy for the risk of adverse events among Medicare beneficiaries with cLBP. Method(s) : Using a retrospective cohort design, we analyzed Medicare claims and administrative data spanning a four-year period. We included Medicare Part B beneficiaries with an episode of cLBP. The acupuncture cohort received acupuncture for cLBP; the pharmacotherapy cohort received an encounter for cLBP with a primary care physician, a prescription for analgesic medication from the same physician within seven days, and no acupuncture. We measured cumulative outcomes at six months, comparing risk of adverse outcomes between the two cohorts using multivariable modeling and weighted propensity scoring. Results : The adjusted odds of any adverse event were 51.48% lower in the acupuncture group compared to the pharmacotherapy group (adjusted odds ratio=0.495, 95% CI: 0.481-0.489, p<0.0001). After adjusting for covariates, the cumulative risk of adverse events in the acupuncture group was 66.04% lower than in the pharmacotherapy group. The risk of adverse drug events in the acupuncture group was 47.22% lower than in the pharmacotherapy group. The risk of pneumothorax in the acupuncture group was 73.44% lower than in the pharmacotherapy group. Conclusions : Among Medicare Part B beneficiaries with chronic low back pain, the risk of adverse outcomes, including adverse drug events and pnueumothorax was lower for those treated with acupuncture as compared to those who saw a primary care physician with a prescription for analgesics. OA 03.04 Association Between Spinal Manipulative Therapy for Low Back Pain With or Without Sciatica and Opioid Use Disorder: A Retrospective Cohort Study Dr. Robert J Trager DC 1,2,3 , Zachary A Cupler 4 , Jordan A Gliedt 5 , Ryan A Fischer 6 , Roshini Srinivasan 7 , Hannah Thorfinnson 8 1 Connor Whole Health, University Hospitals Cleveland Medical Center, Cleveland, OH, United States 2 Department of Family Medicine and Community Health, School of Medicine, Case Western Reserve University, Cleveland, OH, United States 3 Department of Biostatistics and Bioinformatics Clinical Research Training Program, Duke University School of Medicine, Durham, NC, United States 4 Physical Medicine & Rehabilitative Services, Butler VA Health Care System, Butler, PA, United States 5 Department of Neurosurgery, Medical College of Wisconsin, Milwaukee, Wisconsin, United States 6 Section of General Internal Medicine, Department of Medicine, Boston University Chobanian & Avedisian School of Medicine and Boston Medical Center, Boston, Massachusetts, United States 7 Duke University Department of Medicine, Duke University Hospital, Durham, North Carolina, United States 8 Butler VA Medical Center, Butler VA Health Care System, Butler, Pennsylvania, United States Purpose : Prior research has found that patients who receive spinal manipulative therapy (SMT) for low back pain (LBP) are less likely to be prescribed opioids. However, the clinical implications of this finding are uncertain. We tested the hypothesis that opioid-naïve adults receiving SMT for LBP would be less likely to be diagnosed with opioid use disorder (OUD) compared to matched controls prescribed ibuprofen through two years of follow-up. Method(s) : We queried a United States data resource (TriNetX, LLC) for patients age ≥18 years who had a new episode of LBP with or without sciatica from 2015 to 2023, allowing for ≥2 years of follow-up to 2025. We excluded individuals with serious pathology, pre-existing OUD, and concurrent or prior opioid prescription within the preceding year. We divided patients into two cohorts: (1) SMT and (2) ambulatory ibuprofen prescription, using propensity matching for OUD risk factors. Our primary outcome was the risk ratio (RR) of OUD. We secondarily explored RRs for long-term opioid use and opioid prescription, and opioid mean prescription count. Analyses were conducted in TriNetX and R, using logistic regression for matching, standardized mean difference to assess cohort balance (threshold of ≤0.1), and contingency tables for RRs, using a significance threshold of P<.05. Results : Following matching, 24,993 patients remained per cohort. Comparing the SMT cohort to ibuprofen cohort, there was a significantly lower incidence and risk of OUD [95% CI] (0.24% vs. 1.51%; RR=0.20 [0.15, 0.28]; P<.001), long-term opioid use (0.42% vs. 1.85%; RR=0.23 [0.18, 0.28]; P<.001), and opioid prescription (30.96% vs. 45.00%; RR=0.69 [0.67, 0.71; P<.001]). SMT recipients also received fewer opioid prescriptions [standard deviation] (1.0 [3.3] vs. 2.1 [5.7]; P<.001). Conclusions : Adults receiving SMT for new LBP had a lower risk of OUD compared to matched controls prescribed ibuprofen. These findings corroborate existing guidelines which recommend first-line SMT for LBP. The role of SMT in mitigating opioid-related harms requires further investigation. Oral Abstract Session 04: Integrative Research Training OA 04.01 Summative Metrics from the Building Research across Inter-Disciplinary Gaps (BRIDG) Postdoctoral Research Training Program Dr. Heather Zwickey PhD 1 , Dr. Yvonne Lin PhD 2 , Dr. Ryan Bradley ND, MPH 3 , Dr. Cathryn Booth-LaForce PhD 2 1 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 2 University of Washington, Seattle, WA, United States 3 University of California San Diego, San Diego, CA, United States Purpose : The National Center for Complementary and Integrative Health (NCCIH) addressed the need for rigorously trained clinical scientists in complementary and integrative health (CIH) by creating and supporting a T90/R90 postdoctoral clinical research training program. T90/R90 trainees participated in a shared translational science curriculum delivered online every week and quarterly in-person retreats. Summative evaluation metrics included publications, grants, retention in research and career progression. Results : Key outcomes of the BRIDG program include: the development of a cadre (n=20) of clinical researchers prepared to design and conduct rigorous multidisciplinary CIH research; a collaborative, distance-learning model for CIH research education; and a digital archive of didactic materials accessible to the broader research community. From 2015-2025, ten CIH practitioners and ten PhDs participated in BRIDG. Four of the trainees have received NIH K-awards, and seven have submitted grant applications. Overall, lessons learned include that CIH practitioners need additional time and mentorship to become competitive (e.g., publications and additional training); PhD scientists are largely attracted back to major academic research institutions; and advancement is dependent on the availability of faculty positions. Conclusions : Based on summative evaluation metrics, the T90/R90 BRIDG program was effective in training multi-disciplinary CIH clinical researchers. The NIH has since sunsetted this program. OA 04.02 Building Research Culture and Infrastructure at Complementary and Integrative Health (CIH) Institutions: Measuring What Matters Dr. Heather Zwickey PhD 1 , Dr. Paul S Amieux PhD 2 , Dr. Michele Maiers DC, PhD 3 , Dr. Patricia M Herman ND, PhD 4 , Dr. Ian D Coulter PhD 4,5 1 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 2 Bastyr University, Kenmore, WA, United States 3 Northwestern Health Sciences University, Bloomington, MN, United States 4 RAND, Santa Monica, CA, United States 5 University of California, Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CAN, United States Purpose : Traditional CIH institutions have trained clinicians. Historically they did not include research programs or grants. As these institutions begin to build research departments, they are finding it increasingly difficult to compete against the much larger academic health science centers. Moreover, CIH institutions often lack research infrastructure and culture and the associated well-trained faculty and staff to conduct rigorous, high-quality studies. To assist institutions in developing infrastructure, the RAND Research Across Complementary and Integrative Health Institutions (REACH) Center program created a checklist and roadmap that institutions may use to build their research programs. Method(s) : Using comparative analysis we identified the essential components of infrastructure and culture of successful and rigorous research programs and derived specific metrics for a checklist. Results : The resulting checklist included: Do they have the documented full support of leadership at the institution to develop a research culture and infrastructure? Do they have a strategy for mentoring existing faculty and staff in research, or a formal collaboration with a research-intensive organization (such as RAND) to provide said mentoring? Do they have an IRB or access to an IRB at another institution? Do they have a Grants and Contracts Specialist or an Office of Sponsored Programs/Office of Research Administration? How many full time FTE are devoted just to research at the institution? Is there protected time for faculty to engage in research? Is there a research seed grant program at the institution? How many PubMed articles do they publish per year? Do they teach an Evidence-Informed Practice class, a Critical Evaluation of the Literature class, or a Research Methods and a Critical Thinking class. Conclusions : CIH institutions need infrastructure and a culture to participate fully in research. This checklist will help them determine how to move from where they are to achieving that goal. UCSD REACH partners with complementary and integrative health (CIH) institutions aiming to build capacity for research through acculturation, training, ideation and collaboration on WPH research. Curriculum development aimed to broadly improve literacy, research skills and facilitate acculturation, 3. Focused investment in training and mentorship via a Scholar’s program, 4. Pilot grants to foster bi-directional idea generation and collaboration, and 5. Development of clear pathways to access additional UCSD research resources and opportunities. Results : Initially, UCSD REACH has partnered with six CIH institutions (3 EAM, 2 DO, 1 DC, 1 ND, 1 PT). OA 04.04 RAND REACH Scholars Program: Revolutionizing Research Education for Complementary and Integrative Health Professionals Dr. Heather Zwickey PhD 1 , Dr. Michele Maiers DC, MPH, PhD 2 , Dr. Ian D Coulter PhD 3 , Dr. Patricia M Herman ND, PhD 3 1 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 2 Northwestern Health Science University, Bloomington, MN, United States 3 RAND, Santa Monica, CA, United States Purpose : Developing a new research education model for early career clinician scientists within CIH clinical academic institutions. Method(s) : We used key informants to assess the needs of and resources available to CIH institutions and their faculty and staff for research, while acknowledging the challenges faced by these institutions where research activity must be accommodated around academic demands and funded through student tuition. Results : The RAND REACH Scholars Program consists of training and graduate coursework, an expanded research network, structured assistance on grants, projects and publications, and mentorship from experienced researchers and clinician scientists. A faculty scholar is given 20% release time to participate in the program. Scholars self-assess their research skills using an individual development plan and meet with the REACH Center leadership team monthly to develop a research project or continue an existing project. Didactic training includes weekly online coursework (e.g., Research Design, Scientific Writing, Grant Writing, Secondary Data Analysis), and an annual methods bootcamp. Mentorship can take several forms: a series of weekly mentoring sessions with specific mentor faculty on a predefined and agreed upon topic; working with the Collaboration Coordinator to be connected to external mentors; and/or identifying mentors they meet through the Idea Incubator gatherings, Help Desk interactions, coursework, and other Center activities. Mentors are chosen based on their substantial research track record, publications, relevance, and experience in negotiating the world of research. Of the 20 scholars enrolled now over two cohorts, 12 are DCs, 4 are NDs, 2 are DAOMs, 1 is an ND-DC, and 1 is an MPH. Three scholars have developed and received competitive pilot grants for projects. Conclusions : The RAND REACH Scholars Program is building critical capacity in CIH research by equipping clinician-scientists in their home institutions with the skills, mentorship, and collaborative networks necessary for long-term success. Oral Abstract Session 05: Provider Stress and Wellness OA 05.01 Expanding a Pre-surgery Brief Mindfulness Exercise for Surgical Teams: Improving Focus, Stress, and Patient Safety Dr. Maryanna D Klatt PhD 1 , Angela Emerson MPH 1 , Jacqueline Caputo MPH 1 , Odysseas Chatzipanagiotu MD 2 , Yulia Mulugeta BS 1 , Dr. Anne-Marie Duchemin MD 3 , Slate Bretz BS 1 , Dr. Elizabeth Palmer-Kelly PhD 2 , Dr. Beth Steinberg PhD 1 , Dr. Tim Pawlik MD, PhD, MPH, MTS, MBA, FACS, FSSO, FRACS 2 1 Center for Integrative Health, College of Medicine, The Ohio State University, Columbus, Ohio, United States 2 Department of Surgery, The Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, Columbus, Ohio, United States 3 Department of Psychiatry, College of Medicine, The Ohio State University, Columbus, Ohio, United States Purpose : The study assessed the feasibility and impact of a 1-minute audio-guided meditation on self-reported stress and patient safety, initially implemented with an orthopedic surgical team then scaled to a cancer surgical team. Method(s) : Before each surgery, attending surgeons granted permission via email. A research associate then briefed the surgical team in the operating room, after which the staff participated in a 1-minute audio-guided meditation. Team members were invited to complete a post-surgery survey via a QR code flyer. Results : A sample size of 73 health care providers participated in the study. This included surgeons (n=20), an anesthesiologist (n=1), CRNAs (n=3), nurses (n=14), surgical techs (n=6), medical residents (n=11), PA/RNFAs (n=2), medical device reps (n=2), medical students (n=8), radiologic techs (n=2), and (n=4) surgical team members did not select a role. Among the 73 participating healthcare providers, responses to the brief meditation intervention were positive. On a 5-point Likert scale, 60% of participants agreed or strongly agreed that they felt calmer during surgery (mean = 3.69, SD = 0.76) and more clear-minded (mean = 3.64, SD = 0.82). Additionally, 56% reported that the exercise helped reduce their overall stress during the workday (mean = 3.58, SD = 0.93) and contributed to patient safety during surgery (mean = 3.65, SD = 0.95). Finally, 60% indicated they were likely to recommend the meditation exercise to other surgical teams (mean = 3.68, SD = 0.97). Conclusions : Members of surgical teams responded favorably to the brief meditation intervention. Introducing a surgical team meditation exercise prior to the commencement of surgery may enhance team members' sense of calm and concentration, potentially contributing to improved teamwork, patient safety, and clinical outcomes. Continued research is warranted to determine optimal process and timing for successful implementation with the intent for a pre-surgical team meditation to become standard practice prior to surgery. OA 05.02 Initial Outcomes from a Course in Human Flourishing to help Medical Students Thrive Prof Aviad Haramati PhD , Ms. Katherine Bohigian BS Georgetown University Medical Center, Washington, DC, United States Purpose : To improve the well-being of students and faculty at Georgetown University SOM, a new course was developed for medical students, based on three frameworks of Human Flourishing. The premise is for students and faculty to thrive in medical school, various domains of Human Flourishing, such as meaning/purpose, character/values, and positive relationships, need to be aligned along with physical and mental well-being. This presentation will describe the development of the course and the initial outcomes. Method(s) : Eight domains of Human Flourishing and operationalized into a 9-week course with each session addressing one of the domains and including an activity for discussion and reflection. To build capacity for course facilitators, a 3-day immersion training retreat was developed. This experiential 9-session course is taught to groups of 10 students with 2 faculty facilitators. Pre- and post-course surveys assessed a variety of parameters using validated instruments. Qualitative analysis was conducted on the responses to several open-ended questions. Results : The course was piloted with 10 medical students in Fall 2023 and implemented for 20 first-year students in Spring 2024. In Spring 2025, 11 medical students participated. Quantitatively, an increase was observed in the Flourishing Index (68.6±13.2 to 75.6±8.1, P<0.04) and a decrease in Perceived Stress (20.2±5.2 to 16.9±4.0, P<0.07) after the course, both changes with significant effect sizes. Qualitative analysis suggested the experience was impactful to students in terms of new skills and insights they gained. Faculty training retreats were held for 10 participants in each of the past 3 years. Outcomes data from both the course and retreats provide strong impetus for continued investment in this program. Conclusions : Operationalizing specific domains of Human Flourishing into a course that focuses on specific elements and tools that can be taught to medical students can lead to fostering self-awareness, self-reflection and improved well-being. OA 05.03 Well-being as everyone’s responsibility: promoting occupational well-being through actionable data Dr. Amy B Locke MD 1 , Dr. Megan Call PhD 2 , Dr. Angela Fagerlin PhD 2 , Dani Nives LCSW 2 , Whitney Werner MBA/MHA 2 1 University of Utah, SLC, UT, United States 2 University of Utah, SLC, Utah, United States Purpose : With many possible drivers, improving the well-being of healthcare workers is a problem with multiple non-linear impacts. Understanding local drivers of well-being can help leaders and teams act to improve the work environment; however, most institutions survey their faculty and staff with a variety of instruments in a fragmented way, making it difficult to synergize action planning. Since first measuring burnout in 2016, we have continued to optimize data collection and utilization. In 2023, we successfully combined all engagement and well-being surveys administered at our institution into a single instrument as a part of a larger Well-being Strategy. Method(s) : We brought together leaders from HR, Office of Faculty, Patient Experience, Communications, Wellness and other leaders for design and implementation. Beginning in 2023, the survey was administered to 24,000 individuals via Qualtrics. Leaders had access to interactive dashboards. We met with and encouraged leaders to use a quality improvement model to address issues. We shared institution-wide strengths and weaknesses and highlighted common themes. Results : Our survey has an annual response rate of 60% . Unified communications provided guidance to leaders across the organization. Each leader has access to their team’s data, associated drivers, and resources to act. Results influenced content of the institution’s Strategic Plan and relevant committee work. Feedback on the process was collected at each step. Support for teams is available from HR, the Offices for Faculty and Wellness and includes a Joy in Work program based on the Institute for Healthcare Improvement’s model. Actions taken are shared widely to encourage adoption of best practices, including an annual hospital-wide poster fair. Conclusions : This complicated project required time commitment and coordination. Ongoing opportunities include understanding matrixed teams, psychometric evaluation of the survey, coaching of teams, and accountability. Actionable data helps make our health system a better place to work. OA 05.04 The Compassion Champion Model: A Peer-Led Framework for Cultivating and Sustaining Compassion in Organizations Dr. Sivaranjani Penna MD 1,2 , Melanie Bateman RN, BSN 1,2 1 Welugu, Narberth, PA, United States 2 Bryn Mawr Hospital, Bryn Mawr, PA, United States Purpose : Despite strong evidence linking compassionate healthcare to improved outcomes, clinician well-being, and reduced costs, compassion remains underprioritized in healthcare systems. There is a need for models that embed compassion into daily practice, transforming it from an aspirational ideal into an organizational norm. The Compassion Champion Intervention (CCI) is an integrative model that harnesses clinicians’ innate humanitarian capacity through structured training, peer reflection, and brief compassion practices within routine workflows to cultivate sustainable compassion as a foundation for clinician well-being, patient connection, and culture of care. Method(s) : A nine-month mixed-methods design will assess feasibility and impact. Primary outcomes are compassion and burnout; secondary outcomes include patient experience and organizational engagement, assessed through validated measures. The intervention includes three phases: (1) identifying clinicians exemplifying compassionate care (top quartile on a compassion scale); (2) training them through six weekly 60-minute sessions on compassion, reflection, and communication; and (3) empowering them to lead weekly huddles and monthly Compassion Circles for four months, later transitioning to unit-led circles. Quantitative data will be analyzed using mixed-effects modeling; qualitative reflections through thematic analysis. A preliminary feasibility and acceptability survey was conducted. Results : Among 32 respondents, feasibility was strongly endorsed (15.6% strongly agree; 53.1% agree) and acceptability was moderate (12.5% strongly agree; 37.5% agree). High neutral responses (22% for feasibility; 44% for acceptability) suggest opportunities to strengthen engagement and understanding. Positive effects are anticipated across primary and secondary outcomes. Conclusions : The preliminary results support the feasibility of the model and lay the groundwork for larger-scale evaluation across varied healthcare environments. By transforming compassion from an individual virtue into a shared practice, it aligns clinician well-being with patient-centered care and organizational culture, laying the groundwork for larger-scale implementation and evaluation. Oral Abstract Session 06: Implementation and Efficacy of Integrative Clinical Nutrition OA 06.01 Nutrition-Based Group Medical Visits for Chronic Disease Risk Reduction: A Mixed Methods Evaluation Martha Shepherd DO, MPH 1 , Divya Kaushal MS 2 , Sara Johnson Ph.D. 3,4 , Morgan Stine MPAP, PA-C 1 , Kimberly Gill RN, MPH, BSN 1 1 Vanderbilt University Medical Center, Nashville, TN, United States 2 Geisel School of Medicine, The Dartmouth Institute for Health Policy & Clinical Practice, Hanover, NH, United States 3 ProChange Behavior Solutions, Narragansett, RI, United States 4 Health Enhancement Research Organization (HERO), Raleigh, NC, United States Purpose : Fewer than 10% of U.S. adults meet fruit/vegetable guidelines, and only 7% reach fiber recommendations. The Ardmore Institute of Health’s Full-Plate Living (FPL) program is a fiber-focused, whole-food curriculum that can be offered as a group medical visit (GMV)). This quality improvement initiative at Metro Nashville Public Schools' (MNPS) Employee & Family Health Care Centers evaluated a 4-session FPL GMV in an employer-sponsored health center. Method(s) : Participants over the age of 16 with any diet-related diagnosis. Biometrics and vital signs were recorded at each session. PROMIS-10 Global Health Scale, Rate Your Plate (RYP) tool, and a four-item fiber-specific subscale were captured in Epic at the initial and final sessions. A REDCap survey was administered to assess acceptability. Descriptives were examined, and paired t-tests were conducted. Open-ended responses were evaluated inductively. Results : Twenty-six participants enrolled; 23 attended Session 1 (91% female; mean age = 54.1 years; 54.5% White, 36.4% Black). Retention at Session 4 was 87%. Complete paired pre- and post-data (n=19) showed an increase in RYP scores (Session 1 mean= 51.2 (8.78) to Session 4 (mean= 55.9 (7.92)) (p < 0.001, d = 0.51). Fiber scores increased from 6.7 to 8.4 (p < 0.001, d = 0.70). Thirty-two percent of participants reported an improved physical health score, and 26% experienced an improved mental health score. Two-thirds experienced a decrease in diastolic pressure (mean= –4.7 mmHg). The mean satisfaction score for FPL GMV was 9.42/10; 90% of participants agreed that “FPL enhanced my overall health and well-being”, and 100% were more confident making healthy choices. Qualitative findings highlighted the value of culinary demonstrations, peer accountability, and group support. Conclusions : The results demonstrate the feasibility, acceptability, and effectiveness of integrating FPL GMV's into primary care settings and underscore that GMVs are a promising and scalable form of whole-person care for other lifestyle medicine pillars. OA 06.02 Artificial Intelligence (AI) text-based Nutrition Assistant (InaⓇ) for Nutrition Support in Breast Cancer Survivors: A Preliminary Analysis Gabriel Lopez MD 1 , Marissa Buchan RD, CSO 2 , Melanie Guirette PhD, RD 2 , Susan Bratton 2 1 University of Texas, MD Anderson Cancer Center, Houston, TX, United States 2 Savor Health LLC, New York, NY, United States Purpose : Digital health technology using AI and machine learning (ML) offers a unique opportunity to enhance nutritional support for oncology patients by providing evidence-based recommendations in real-time. Method(s) : Users across the US, including self-selected people with cancer, cancer-caregivers, or oncology health care providers, accessed a text-based AI-nutrition expert-platform (InaⓇ) for nutrition support and guidance. InaⓇ is powered by AI, ML, and a proprietary database of over 120,000 interventions based on clinical guidelines and peer-reviewed literature. Self-reported outcomes included baseline demographics, comorbidities, nutrition barriers, symptoms; as well as survey scores (1-5 Likert scale, 5 best) on user experience / satisfaction and engagement / adherence. We assessed (Spearman correlation) associations between user engagement (e.g., time on platform, messages sent, and surveys completed) and average survey scores. We examined (Kruskal-Wallis test) differences in comorbidities and nutrition barriers across tertiles of increasing message count. Results : The subset analysis included 730 users (breast cancer, 726 women), average age 53 (range 26-82), majority overweight (31%) or obese (35%), 25% with metastatic disease. The top three self-reported nutrition barriers were related to time (not enough, 37%), cost (of healthy food, 35%), and nutrition knowledge (not sure what to choose, 33%). Engagement was high, with users remaining on the platform a median of 9 months. Overall user satisfaction was high (4.2 average). Higher message counts associated with significantly higher scores for overall experience, accuracy, help managing side effects, and likelihood of recommending Ina (p < 0.001). Users reporting nutrition barriers related to time and nutrition knowledge were also more likely to engage with Ina. Conclusions : AI-nutrition support was well received by users with breast cancer, with engagement positively correlating with survey scores. Additional research is needed to understand which persons are more likely to benefit from AI-nutrition support and when to “prescribe” the intervention. OA 06.03 Tailoring A Culinary Medicine Program to Support Positive Dietary Changes in Oncology Patients Julia Schlossman MS, MD 1 , Sue Czap MS, RD 2 , Bailey Garlipp RD 2 , Sydney John MPH 2 , Alissa Huston MD 2,3 1 Boston Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States 2 Pluta Integrative Oncology & Wellness Center, Rochester, NY, United States 3 University of Rochester Medical Center, Rochester, NY, United States Purpose : Nutrition serves as a critical component of supportive care for oncological patients. To address this need, we implemented a pilot study to assess the impact of a Culinary Medicine Program (CMP) on cancer patients’ eating habits, cooking confidence, and nutritional knowledge. Although many participants reported baseline nutritional knowledge, post-program assessments and qualitative feedback demonstrated an overall improvement, underscoring the impact of a formal CMP for oncology patients. OA 06.04 Primary Safety Results of Xanthohumol in Crohn's Disease: A Phase 2 Clinical Trial Ryan Bradley ND, MPH 1 , Carina A. Staab DC, MEd 2 , Paige E. Jamieson PhD 3 , Blake O. Langley ND, MS, LAc 4 , Tom O. Metz PhD 5 , Jan F. Stevens PhD, PharmD 3 1 University of California, San Diego, La Jolla, CA, United States 2 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 3 Oregon State University, Corvallis, OR, United States 4 Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, Seattle, WA, United States 5 Pacific Northwest National Laboratory, Richland, WA, United States Purpose : Basic science evidence suggests xanthohumol (XN), a polyphenolic compound from hops, may ameliorate the increased gut permeability, intestinal inflammation, and altered bile acid metabolism associated with Crohn’s disease. Therefore, the purpose of this research was to determine the safety and tolerability of supplemental xanthohumol in participants with Crohn’s disease via a Phase 2 clinical trial. Method(s) : We conducted a randomized, triple-masked, placebo-controlled Phase 2 clinical trial of xanthohumol in human participants with Crohn’s disease under an FDA-approved IND. Candidates were screened using the Crohn’s Disease Activity Index (CDAI) to confirm they were not in remission. Eligible participants were randomized to either xanthohumol (24 mg/day) or a rice protein placebo for 8 weeks. Clinical laboratory toxicology measures (i.e., eGFR, BUN, Cr, RBC, WBC, PLT, ALT, AST, GGT, etc.), urine and stool collections, and adverse events were collected biweekly. Mean changes in lab parameters and changes in the proportion of participants with abnormal labs were used to determine clinical safety, while self-reported adverse events, CDAI and PROMIS-29 scores were used to assess tolerability. Results : Twenty adults were randomized and 19 completed the trial. XN was well tolerated with adherence exceeding 95%. Neither halting nor stopping criteria were met, and all laboratory elevations were minor and transient in both groups. There were no attributable serious adverse events in either group, and all moderate AEs were short-term and self-resolving. Between group comparisons demonstrated differences for changes in BMI and GGT favouring XN: BMI: -0.67 (0.0, 1.3), p=0.04; GGT: -4.8 U/L, 95% CI 0.8–8.8, p=0.02. There were no significant changes in either CDAI or PROMIS-29 compared to placebo. Conclusions : XN at 24 mg/day was safe and well tolerated in adults with active CD, supporting further research in inflammatory bowel disease. Changes in BMI and GGT may reflect beneficial effects on hepatic and metabolic health. Oral Abstract Session 07: Integrative Health Policy OA 07.01 Traditional, Complementary, and Integrative Healthcare, Global Advocacy Roadmap - Positive and Negative Determinants of the Implementation of the global Traditional Medicine Strategy Dr. John S Finnell ND, PhD, MPH, MS 1,2,3 , Dr. Tabatha Parker ND 4 1 Bastyr University Research Institute, Bastyr University, Kenmore, WA, United States 2 Dept. of Epidemiology, UTHealth Houston School of Public Health at Austin, Austin, TX, United States 3 Central Texas Veterans Health Care System, Austin, TX, United States 4 Helfgott Research Institute, National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States Purpose : Traditional, Complementary, and Integrative Healthcare (TCIH) perspectives remain underrepresented in global health policy discussions. Therefore, a rapid-cycle analysis was conducted to characterize civil society perspectives on the implementation of the global Traditional Medicine Strategy 2025–2034 (TM Strategy). The study was determined to be exempt from IRB review (Bastyr University IRB: #25-0017). Statistical analyses were conducted using STATA Stata 18.0. Results : Descriptive statistics revealed broad response (N=334) across Stakeholder Role (%): Patient (10.9%), Health Care Practitioner (46.3%), Medical Doctor (25.5%), Educator (25.2%), Researcher (26.1%), Government (5.9%), Policy Maker (7.8%), Other (11.2%). The majority (58%) of respondents agreed or strongly agreed that the TM Strategy addresses the needs and challenges of TCIH in their region. Conclusions : The results show broad response across stakeholder roles, stakeholder type, role in healthcare, and geographic scope. A limitation was the electronic questionnaire required access to technology. A strength was questionnaire translation from English to Chinese, French, Portuguese, Russian, and Spanish. A clear majority of respondents reported the following strategies as being very or extremely important to the implemntation of TCIH: 1) strengthening regulation and governance; 2) ensuring safety, quality, and efficacy; 3) integration of TCIH into national health policies, programs, and service delivery; 4) education, accreditation, and licensing of TCIH practitioners; 5) digital innovation and interoperability; and 6) promoting public health literacy and awareness of TCIH. OA 07.02 Is It Time to Include Meditation, Yoga, and Tai Chi in Health Promotion Policies? Proposing Evidence-Based Strategies and Action Steps Dr. S. Prasad Vinjamury MD (Ayurveda), DACM, MPH 1 , Ms Marita La Monica MPH, RYT 2 1 Southern California University of Health Sciences, Whittier, CA, United States 2 City University of New York, New York, NY, United States Purpose : Non-communicable diseases (NCDs) account for 81% of global deaths and affect 60% of Americans living with chronic conditions. Despite widespread use and growing evidence, traditional, complementary, and integrative health care (TCIH) practices such as meditation, yoga, and tai chi lack systematic incorporation into health promotion and primary disease prevention policies. This abstract examines policy gaps, reviews high-quality evidence, and proposes action steps for integrating TCIH practices into national health promotion frameworks. Method(s) : Analysis of current U.S. health promotion policies, including Healthy People initiatives, revealed that TCIH practices are not explicitly integrated despite their relevance to primary prevention. Systematic reviews and meta-analyses were examined to assess the evidence base for meditation, yoga, and tai chi in addressing modifiable NCD risk factors. Results : Policy Gaps: Current Healthy People frameworks fail to achieve 66% of trackable objectives, with disproportionate impacts on low-income and racially diverse populations. TCIH practices remain unarticulated in Leading Health Indicators despite addressing physical, mental, and social health dimensions. Evidence: High-quality studies demonstrate that yoga controls cardiovascular risk factors including hypertension, metabolic syndrome, and diabetes. Meditation shows sustained effects on stress reduction, blood pressure, insulin resistance, smoking cessation, and cognitive function. Tai chi demonstrates significant improvements in balance, cardiorespiratory fitness, mobility, and reduction in falls and stroke risk among older adults. Provider-based systems including Traditional Chinese Medicine, Ayurveda, chiropractic, and naturopathy also emphasize lifestyle-based, person-centered approaches to wellness that are evidence informed. Conclusions : Evidence-Based Strategies: (1) Create national frameworks integrating WHO's TCIH strategy and PAHO's culturally appropriate health promotion approaches; (2) Explicitly utilize meditation, yoga, and tai chi in health promotion policies with programs targeting underserved populations; (3) Increase NCCIH funding for prevention research and TCIH workforce development. Action Steps: Urge HHS to integrate TCIH into Healthy People initiatives; establish reimbursement for TCIH primary prevention services; implement community-based and school-based programs; fund randomized controlled trials; support credentialing and training standards for TCIH professionals. These low-risk, accessible practices offer culturally responsive tools to address health inequities and reduce the NCD burden. OA 07.03 Acupuncture and Herbal Medicine in Transition: Trends, Challenges, and Opportunities for Integration DR Belinda J Anderson PhD, MA(Ed), LAc Pace University, New York, NY, United States. Albert Einstein College of Medicine, New York, NY, United States Purpose : The acupuncture and herbal medicine (AHM) profession in the United States faces significant challenges, including declining numbers of practitioners, school closures, and shifting healthcare dynamics. At the same time, opportunities are emerging through expanded insurance coverage, policy initiatives, and research advancements. This study aimed to analyze the forces shaping the profession and identify future directions for integration into mainstream healthcare. Method(s) : A comprehensive review of national data sources, including licensure statistics, accreditation reports, federal policy developments, insurance coverage trends, and peer-reviewed publications, was conducted. These findings were synthesized with emerging initiatives from professional organizations and governmental agencies to generate a strategic overview of the profession’s current status and trajectory. Results : Between 2018 and 2023, the number of licensed acupuncturists declined by 8.9%, with parallel decreases in school enrollment and accredited programs. Contributing factors included the COVID-19 pandemic, demographic shifts, and financial pressures. Despite these challenges, use of acupuncture in the U.S. continues to rise, supported by expanded insurance and Medicare coverage. Legislative efforts (HR 3133) seek to grant provider status to licensed acupuncturists, which could transform employment opportunities and access to care. Research output has grown rapidly, supported by the NIH National Center for Complementary and Integrative Health. Emerging areas of focus include implementation science, practice-based research, big data and AI applications, and the Whole Person Health framework. The 2023 formation of the AHM Coalition and the inclusion of Traditional Medicine diagnoses in ICD-11 further strengthen professional legitimacy and integration. Conclusions : The AHM profession stands at a critical juncture. While structural vulnerabilities threaten sustainability, growing policy recognition, insurance expansion, and robust research create opportunities for deeper integration into U.S. healthcare. Strategic alignment around advocacy, research, and whole person health frameworks will be essential to ensure the profession’s relevance and long-term contribution to addressing chronic pain, the opioid crisis, and patient-centered care. OA 07.04 Integrating Thai Traditional Medicine into Health Promotion in Thailand: Towards Strategic Development Pathways Ritichai Pimpa , ZhiMin Xiao, Nestor Asiamah University of Essex, Colchester, Essex, United Kingdom Purpose : This study investigates the challenges and opportunities that shape strategic development pathways for enhancing health promotion at the primary care level in Thailand through the integration of Thai Traditional Medicine (TTM). Although TTM has deep cultural roots and significant preventive health potential, its integration into the national healthcare system remains limited. Method(s) : Fifteen in-depth, semi-structured interviews were conducted with key stakeholder experts. These participants included practitioners, policymakers, professional council members, academics, and interdisciplinary professionals working across various regions of Thailand. The data was analysed using the framework analysis method, guided by five domains based on the Ottawa Charter for Health Promotion. Each domain was examined in terms of both its challenges and potential opportunities. Results : Several key challenges emerged, including unsupportive policies and systemic limitations, unclear career pathways and workforce issues, as well as widespread misconceptions and negative public perceptions of TTM. However, the study also identified promising avenues for development. These include a growing societal shift toward holistic and preventive health, the potential of community-driven and elderly-focused health services, and the richness of Thai cultural heritage, which offers a strong foundation for building a health-oriented economy. Conclusions : The findings suggest that the effective integration of TTM into primary care requires comprehensive systemic reform, well-defined governance structures, and the creation of official civil service pathways for TTM practitioners. Promoting collaborative governance and building public trust will be crucial for positioning TTM as a central pillar in Thailand’s culturally-rooted, holistic health promotion strategy. Participants were all women, average age 43 years, 46% White, 45% Hispanic, 48% married, and 36% employed full time. Of the 28 participants enrolled, 25 (89.2%) attended at least one group session. Of participants who attended sessions, 64% attended 6 or more sessions (16/25). Among SMART-LAD participants, 78% (11/14) attended 6 or more sessions, and 69.2% were satisfied with the SMART-LAD content. 73.9% (17/23) were satisfied with teleconferencing, and 79.1% (19/24) felt satisfied with the number of sessions. 91.7% (22/24) of participants expressed comfort/trust in the groups. Conclusions : Both interventions for caregivers of children with LAD were feasible and acceptable. The SMART-LAD program may have fostered deeper engagement and content satisfaction. Overall, the program structure and processes functioned well in both arms and both languages. OA 08.02 Feasibility and Acceptability of a Dyadic Mind-Body Resiliency Intervention for Cancer Survivors and Caregivers: A Randomized Pilot Trial (Project ForTe) Dr. Elyse Park Ph.D., MPH 1,2 , Brett M. Goshe PhD 1,2 , Anna Barata PhD 1,2 , Lucy Finkelstein-Fox PhD 1,2 , Joanna Cloutier BA 1 , Emelia Farnam BA 1 , Katie Holmbeck BA 1 , Elizabeth Waldron PhD 1,2 , Giselle K Perez PhD 1,2 , Laura Malloy LICSW 1 , Isabelle Miranda BS, BA 1 , Wren Hager BA 3 , Nora Horick MS 1,2 , Areej El-Jawahri MD 1,2 1 Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA, United States 2 Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 3 Wellesley College, Wellesley, MA, United States Purpose : Caregivers of cancer survivors struggle with chronic and ongoing stress spanning the survivorship continuum. The primary goal of this randomized pilot trial was to determine the feasibility and acceptability a mind-body resiliency group intervention, simultaneously delivered to cancer caregivers and survivors, and determine its preliminary efficacy for improving resiliency and stress coping among caregivers and survivors. Method(s) : From 7/2023-3/2024, 47 dyads were randomized to a 9-session virtual mind-body resiliency or 3-session online support group (CanCare).
