Conclusions : We conducted a pilot randomized trial in which we established the feasibility (study retention, intervention engagement) and acceptability (overall satisfaction) of a simultaneously delivered group mind-body resiliency intervention for survivors and caregivers. However, dyadic enrollment was challenging, and we did not meet our enrollment goal. Results show promising effects on improving stress coping and resiliency. OA 08.03 Examining perceptions of movement-focused vs. meditation/breathwork-focused yoga with the Essential Properties of Yoga Questionnaire Camille L Garnsey , Katherine E. Gnall, Erika Osherow, Sarah Ahmadi, Dr. Crystal Park PhD University of Connecticut, Storrs, CT, United States Purpose : Yoga has shown efficacy in improving physical and mental health and supporting well-being. As practiced in the West today, yoga typically integrates physical postures, breathwork, and meditation. However, yoga is a broad discipline, and classes vary considerably in the elements emphasized. Little is known about how participants perceive these differences. This secondary analysis of an 8-week pilot RCT compared participants’ perceptions of yoga elements across two distinct yoga conditions. Method(s) : Stressed adults (N = 40, Mage = 19.8, 93% female, 70% White) participated in a pilot trial testing two types of asynchronous online yoga. Participants were randomized to either a.) a condition emphasizing physical postures and active movement (i.e., movement-focused; n = 20) or b.) a condition emphasizing breathwork and meditation (i.e., meditation-focused; n = 20). Participants engaged in eight hour-long weekly sessions. At post-intervention, they completed the Essential Properties of Yoga Questionnaire (EPYQ) assessing perceived emphasis across six elements of yoga. Three elements were intentionally manipulated in the design of the two conditions (i.e., meditation/mindfulness, breathwork, physicality), and three were not (i.e., acceptance/compassion, mental/emotional awareness/release, body awareness). Independent-samples t-tests with 1,000 bootstrap samples (bias-corrected 95% CIs) compared conditions. Results : The meditation-focused condition was rated higher on meditation/mindfulness (Ms = 3.60 vs. 2.63; p = .005) and mental/emotional awareness/release (Ms = 4.39 vs. 3.60; p = .02). Movement-focused classes were rated higher on physicality (Ms = 2.40 vs. 3.36; p < .005) and body awareness (Ms = 3.52 vs. 4.06; p = .02). No significant differences were observed for acceptance/compassion or breathwork (ps > .05). Conclusions : Participants perceived the intended differences in physicality and meditation but not in breathwork, suggesting breathing may feel central to yoga regardless of explicit breathwork practices. Without intentional manipulation, differences also emerged in emotional awareness and body awareness, suggesting classes with distinct emphases may naturally direct attention toward different elements. These findings provide insight into perceptions of yoga’s core elements. Future research is warranted to examine how perceived emphasis relates to intervention mechanisms and effectiveness. OA 08.04 Measuring Holistic Ways of Coping for Black Women in the Context of Clinical Practice and Research Dr. Vanessa N. Oliphant PhD in Clinical Psychology , De'ja Broyles M.A., Coutney Harold M.S. University of Mississippi, Oxford, MS, United States Purpose : The purpose of this study is to emphasize the importance of measuring holistic ways of coping for Black women in the context of clinical practice and research. There is a need for greater emphasis to be placed on the lived experiences of Black women and to assess and affirm the ways in which they have found survival strategies in their protective coping. Method(s) : The current study used an African-centered psychological measurement to assess the degree to which Black women endorse culturally specific coping strategies. Specifically, the Africultrual Coping Systems Inventory (ACSI) was used to measure Black women’s use of collective coping, spiritual-centered coping, ritual-centered coping, and cognitive emotional debriefing. Results : 282 cisgender women who self-identified as African American/Black (M age = 20) completed the ACSI measure. By conducting an exploratory analysis, results indicated that the Black women in the sample were less likely to use ritual-centered coping (M = 1.54, SD = 2.07), more likely to use cognitive emotional debriefing (M = 12.04, SD = 8.46), and equally used spiritual-centered coping (M = 7.40, SD = 6.07) and collective coping (M = 7.39, SD = 5.83). Conclusions : Overall, this study can provide important implications for Black women's use of culturally relevant coping strategies and emphasize the need to assess and affirm their survival strategies in the context of clinical practice and psychological research. By doing this, we can better identify areas to improve clinical interventions and future research that centers Black women. Oral Abstract Session 09: Clinical Efficacy and Implementation of Traditional Chinese Medicine OA 09.01 Enhancing Safe Use of Chinese Herbal Medicine: A National Herb–Drug Interaction Database for TCM Practitioners Dr Phoebe Zhou Western Sydney University, Westmead, NSW, Australia Purpose : The concurrent use of Chinese herbal medicine with conventional drugs is increasing globally, yet evidence-based guidelines for safe co-administration remain lacking. To address this, the NICM Health Research Institute and UnityHealth Pty Ltd have developed a pioneering Chinese Herb–Drug Interaction Database for healthcare professionals. This project aims to enhance safety and communication in integrative medicine, supporting practitioners with evidence-based tools to guide safe prescribing, foster collaboration, and improve patient outcomes worldwide. Method(s) : Since 2017, this initiative has progressed through rigorous phases of literature review, data synthesis, and interaction and evidence evaluation. The information supporting each interaction is derived from a comprehensive literature review of both English and Chinese databases (i.e. PubMed and CNKI) and compiled into detailed monographs with key extracted data. Based on the available literature and levels of evidence, each monograph provides practitioner guidance and commentary on the strength of evidence. The monographs and conclusions are reviewed by toxicologists and pharmacologists before uploading to the system. Results : The database now has included detailed monographs for 200 commonly used Chinese herbs, with 544 specific herb–drug pairings and 607 comprehensive interaction reports. Notably, the evidence-based classification system highlights few potential safety concerns but primarily beneficial interactions that may enhance clinical outcomes. Among the monographs, 4 describe likely significant interactions with strong evidence, 104 identify potential interactions with limited evidence, and 294 suggest possible therapeutic synergies. This nuanced approach empowers TCM practitioners with practical, clinically relevant information to optimize patient safety, improve communication, and support integrative treatment strategies. Conclusions : Supported by the Australian Government’s National Industry PhD Program (2024–2027), this project anticipates a significant advancement in supporting TCM practitioners with an evidence-based tool to guide safe and effective herb–drug co-administration. Integrating this resource into clinical practice will enhance safe prescribing, encourage multidisciplinary collaboration, and ultimately improve patient health outcomes within integrative healthcare settings worldwide. The model demonstrates Taiwan’s leadership in developing evidence-based integrative pediatric care and serves as a replicable framework for global application. OA 09.06LB Inequitable Discrepancies in Payment for Acupuncture under Medicare Part B Dr. Louis A. Kazal, Jr. MD Geisel School of Medicine at Dartmouth, Hanover, NH, United States Purpose : In 2020 the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS) initiated reimbursement for acupuncture for treatment of chronic low back pain. Following the CMS decision, the number of encounters for acupuncture accelerated rapidly, despite CMS rules that restrict payment for acupuncture services to medical physicians and certain specialties working under their supervision. Independent licensed acupuncturists are ineligible for payment. While encounters did increase, the restrictive terms of coverage may compromise adequate and equitable patient access to acupuncture. The purpose of this study was to describe trends and patterns in payment and nonpayment for acupuncture under Medicare Part B. Method(s) : We employed a cross-sectional design to examine 2020-2023 Part B Medicare claims. Acupuncture services were identified by procedure code and summarized mean payments by place of service category. Acupuncture services were defined as paid if the associated payment amount was greater than zero; unpaid services as those with payment amount of zero. We categorized paid and unpaid services by claim type, setting, year, clinician specialty, and line diagnosis. Results : Acupuncture services provided under Medicare Part B for the period 2020-2023 totaled 1,575,147. Mean payment for acupuncture provided in a physician office visit setting was $36.61. Frequency of acupuncture services increased each year during the four-year period. Among all services billed, 48% were paid. The highest number of total acupuncture services was provided in California (235,103); Montana had the lowest number of total services (65). Physical Medicine and Rehabilitation had the highest number of total services, and the most paid services. ICD-10 code M5459 (other low back pain) was the most frequent diagnosis for which acupuncture was provided. Conclusions : The frequency of acupuncture services increased each year during the four-year period. We found unwarranted variation in rates of successful claims for reimbursement of acupuncture services by state, clinical specialty, and diagnosis. Results : Feasibility milestones for participant recruitment (2.8/month), retention (87.5%), data completeness (>85%), and safety monitoring were all achieved. Adherence to chiropractic care was also acceptable (>93%), but attendance in online Tai Chi classes was slightly below expectations. Administrative issues regarding recruitment, training, and monitoring the fidelity of community-based chiropractic care and Tai Chi, as well as ensuring secure data exchange between community practices and the research team, were successfully addressed. The chiropractic care and combined chiropractic care plus Tai Chi groups showed declines in NDI and BOP scores from baseline to week 16, which persisted through week 24. Declines in NDI and BOP over time were slightly less in the EUC group compared to the other two groups. All groups showed similar declines over time in POM and PI. Conclusions : Feasibility results, along with patient-reported clinical outcomes and qualitative insights, support and guide a follow-up, multi-site, fully powered 2 x 2 factorial trial to assess the independent and combined effects of chiropractic care and Tai Chi for CNP. OA 10.02 Association between spinal manipulative therapy and cervical artery dissection in patients with neck pain or headache: a retrospective cohort study Dr. Robert J Trager DC 1,2,3 , Dr. Anthony N Baumann MD, DPT 4 , Dr. Collin M Labak MD 5 , Dr. Kelsey L Lewis DC 1 , Jaime A Perez PhD 6 , Dr. Christine M Goertz DC, PhD 7 , Dr. Andres Schuster MD 8 1 Connor Whole Health, University Hospitals Cleveland Medical Center, Cleveland, OH, United States 2 Department of Family Medicine and Community Health, School of Medicine, Case Western Reserve University, Cleveland, OH, United States 3 Department of Biostatistics and Bioinformatics Clinical Research Training Program, Duke University School of Medicine, Durham, NC, United States 4 Department of Orthopaedic Surgery, Cleveland Clinic Akron General, Akron, OH, United States 5 Department of Neurosurgery, University Hospitals Cleveland Medical Center, Cleveland, OH, United States 6 Clinical Research Center, University Hospitals Cleveland Medical Center, Cleveland, OH, United States 7 Duke Clinical Research Institute, Duke University School of Medicine, Durham, NC, United States 8 Harrington Heart and Vascular Institute, University Hospitals Cleveland Medical Center, Cleveland, OH, United States Purpose : Cervical artery dissection (CeAD) has been proposed as a sequela of spinal manipulative therapy (SMT), however, care-seeking for CeAD symptoms (e.g., neck pain/headache) may overestimate observed associations. Among adults experiencing new neck pain/headache, we tested the null hypothesis of no significant CeAD risk within 30 days following SMT compared to matched ibuprofen-prescribed controls. Method(s) : We searched United States TriNetX Linked health records/claims data (2010-2025) for adults experiencing new episodes of neck pain/headache receiving (1) chiropractor-administered cervical SMT or (2) outpatient visit with ibuprofen prescription, excluding those with previous cerebrovascular disease and/or CeAD, using propensity matching to minimize confounding. Results : After matching, there were 431,492 patients per cohort. Carotid artery dissection risk was lower; vertebral artery dissection risk was not significantly different. Data quality supported balanced confounders and minimal follow-up loss (<3%). Negative control outcomes were partially balanced. Conclusions : This study found no increased CeAD risk after chiropractor-administered cervical SMT compared to ibuprofen prescription in adults with neck pain/headache. Despite the rarity of these events, all clinicians, including chiropractors, should maintain vigilance for CeAD symptoms. OA 10.03 The association of neighborhood socioeconomic status with access to chiropractic providers: a geospatial analysis of two US geographic regions Ryan A Fischer DC 1 , Shikhar Shrestha MS, PhD 2 , Robert J Trager DC 3,4 , Robert B Saper MD, MPH 5 , Eric J Roseen DC, PhD 1 1 Boston Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States 2 Tufts University, Boston, MA, United States 3 University Hospitals, Cleveland, OH, United States 4 Case Western Reserve University, Cleveland, OH, United States 5 Cleveland Clinic, Cleveland, OH, United States Purpose : Chiropractic care (i.e., manual therapy, individualized exercise, and patient education) is a guideline-concordant approach for first-line management of common and disabling musculoskeletal conditions such as low back pain. However, variability in geospatial access to chiropractic care, and whether variability relates to neighborhood socioeconomic status, is understudied. Method(s) : In this geospatial analysis, we evaluated the association between neighborhood socioeconomic status and the number of chiropractic providers in two US geographic regions. Neighborhood socioeconomic status was defined using the area deprivation index (ADI) for each neighborhood (defined as a census block group) in Suffolk County, Massachusetts and Cuyahoga County, Ohio. Higher ADI scores (range: 1-10) indicate worse socioeconomic status. Chiropractic providers in each county were identified by triangulating publicly available data (e.g., state board/licensing websites, payor directories, provider websites). Geographic information systems software was used to illustrate ADI-ranked neighborhoods and plot provider locations. We identified providers within a catchment area from the neighborhood centroid based on assumed travel distances, i.e., 0.5- and 5-miles for Suffolk and Cuyahoga counties, respectively. To evaluate the association of ADI rank with the number of chiropractic providers, we estimated incident rate ratios (IRRs) using a Poisson model adjusted for neighborhood population size. Results : We identified 92 and 214 chiropractic providers within 628 and 1135 census block groups in Suffolk and Cuyahoga counties, respectively. A one unit increase in ADI rank was associated with a decrease in the expected count of providers in the catchment area, by 27% (IRR=0.73, 95%CI: 0.72-0.75) in Suffolk County and 4% (IRR=0.96, 95%CI: 0.96-0.97) in Cuyahoga County. Conclusions : Geographic areas with worse neighborhood-level socioeconomic status had fewer chiropractic providers after controlling for population size. Further work is needed to evaluate and encourage equitable geographic distributions of chiropractic providers. OA 10.04 Mediators of the Effect of Chiropractic Care on 52-week Outcomes for Active-duty US Military Members with Low Back Pain in a Pragmatic Clinical Trial: A Secondary Data Analysis Zacariah K Shannon DC, MS, PhD 1 , Cynthia R Long PhD 1 , Robert D Vining DC, DHSc 1 , Joan Walter JD 2 , Ian Coulter PhD 3 , Christine M Goertz DC, PhD 4 1 Palmer College of Chiropractic, Davenport, Iowa, United States 2 N/A, N/A, N/A, United States 3 RAND Corporation, Santa Monica, California, United States 4 Duke University, Durham, North Carolina, United States Purpose : The purpose of this work is to explore pain and non-pain factors as mediators of the effect of chiropractic care on 52-week outcomes in a clinical trial subsample comparing usual medical care plus chiropractic care to usual medical care alone for US active-duty military personnel with low back pain. Method(s) : We used linear mixed effects regression models weighted by the inverse probability of missingness to predict and replace missing outcome values and generate a dataset with complete outcome data. We analyzed the complete dataset using natural effect models to evaluate change in individual mediators measured at the end of the 6-week chiropractic care intervention period as mediators of 52-week outcomes. We explored mediation of pain measures (Roland-Morris Disability Questionnaire (RMDQ) and numerical pain rating scale (NPRS), by non-pain measures (PROMIS-29: fatigue, sleep disturbance, and social role) and vice versa. Models were adjusted for confounding by trial allocation variables and baseline mediator and outcome values. Results : One hundred forty-four participants provided outcome data after 12 weeks and were included. Participants were mostly male (79%) between 18-50 years old (mean=33). Total natural effect (TNE) difference estimates favored chiropractic care for 52-week RMDQ and non-pain outcomes, with a negligible difference in NPRS. Indirect effects through RMDQ mediated 17% and 33% of the effect on 52-week sleep disturbance (TNE= 4.89, 2.65 to 6.96) and social role (TNE= 2.29, 0.16 to 4.45). Indirect effects through non-pain factors were smaller with the largest effect occurring through social role which mediated 19% of the effect on 52-week RMDQ (TNE= 1.97, 0.31 to 3.68). Conclusions : Change in pain-related disability and intensity were important drivers of the effect of chiropractic care on 52-week sleep disturbance and social role outcomes. OA 10.05 Massage as a non-invasive therapy for children with irritable bowel syndrome (IBS) Maria Mascarenhas MBBS , Alain Benitez MD, MTSR University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia, PA, United States Purpose : Irritable bowel syndrome (IBS) is a disorder characterized by abdominal pain and altered defecation patterns impacting quality of life (QoL). Treatments differ depending on the subtype; therapies often do not completely relieve symptoms. Myofascial release (MFR) is a massage technique targeting the fascia. MFR applied during osteopathic manipulations have resulted in improvement of abdominal pain, distention, constipation and QoL in adults. The purpose is to share the project’s feasibility, acceptability, and effect on symptoms and QoL of abdominal wall MFR intervention in children with IBS. Method(s) : Children meeting Rome IV criteria for IBS were enrolled and received six one-hour abdominal MFR sessions during six weeks by a certified MFR massage therapist and were educated on administering self-MFR at home using a rubber ball. Validated pediatric questionnaires, the PedsQLTM Gastrointestinal Symptoms Module and the Pediatric Quality of Life InventoryTM were administered at baseline, end of intervention, and one month post intervention. Outcomes were analyzed using Mixed Model for Repeated Measures method. Results : Of the 16 children enrolled, 14 (median age 16 years, 50% female) completed 6 MFR sessions and questionnaires. Self-MFR practice averaged three times per week and 7 minutes per day. Total GI symptom score significantly improved at end of intervention (p=0.005) and one month post intervention (p=0.0005). QoL total scores significantly improved at end of intervention (p=0.02). Similarly, caregivers reported total GI symptom and QoL scores significantly improved at both timepoints (p<0.05). No adverse events were reported. Conclusions : Abdominal MFR therapy is feasible and is well-tolerated in children with IBS. Preliminary results show that six weeks of MFR improves overall IBS-associated symptoms and QoL up to one month following the intervention. Randomized controlled trials will further evaluate the efficacy of abdominal MFR in IBS and variability between IBS subtypes that would support implementation as a non-invasive therapy for children with IBS. OA 10.06LB Mindfulness in the Orthopedic Waiting Room: Three Randomized Controlled Trials Testing Pragmatic Optimizations for Acute Pain and Anxiety Relief Adam W. Hanley PhD , Kennedi Childs Florida State University, Tallahassee, Florida, United States Purpose : Orthopedic clinic waiting rooms represent a predictable yet underutilized opportunity to deliver brief, nonpharmacologic pain management. Prior work suggests that ultra-brief, audio-guided mindfulness can reduce acute pain, but uncertainty remains regarding robustness and whether pragmatic design optimizations enhance outcomes. This study evaluated the effectiveness of ultra-brief mindfulness interventions delivered during routine orthopedic waiting time and tested whether patient choice, auditory enhancements, or content reconfiguration improved reductions in pain and anxiety. Method(s) : Three single-site randomized controlled trials were conducted in an orthopedic radiography waiting area (total N = 1,225). Adults presenting with pain completed pre- and post-intervention assessments of pain unpleasantness (primary outcome), pain intensity, and anxiety using 0–10 numeric rating scales. Study 1 (N = 283) tested patient choice versus random assignment to mindfulness or injury-management education. Study 2 (N = 296) compared mindfulness alone with mindfulness plus monaural tones, theta-range binaural beats, or binaural beats with ambient music. Study 3 (N = 646) used a dismantling design to compare four mindfulness variants differing in psychoeducation dose and attentional focus versus injury-management education. Interventions lasted 3–6 minutes and were delivered via pre-recorded audio. Outcomes were analyzed using generalized linear mixed models under an intention-to-treat framework. Results : Across all trials, mindfulness interventions produced significantly greater reductions in pain unpleasantness, pain intensity, and anxiety than injury-management education (all ps < .01). In Study 1, mindfulness reduced pain unpleasantness by 1.12 points (18.9%), whereas education showed minimal change. Patient choice did not moderate outcomes. In Study 2, all mindfulness conditions yielded comparable symptom reductions, with no benefit from auditory enhancements. In Study 3, all mindfulness variants outperformed education, including a practice-only condition without psychoeducation. Conclusions : Ultra-brief, audio-guided mindfulness delivered during orthopedic waiting time reliably reduces acute pain and anxiety. Core mindfulness practice appears sufficient, supporting scalable implementation in routine orthopedic care. Oral Abstract Session 11: Integrative Health Chronic Pain Research OA 11.01 Young Black Adults’ Chronic Pain, Integrative Medicine Use, and Intervention Preferences Dr. De'Sha Wolf PhD, MCR Portland State University, Portland, OR, United States Purpose : Nearly 20% of U.S. black adults experience chronic pain, yet prevention and treatment efforts neglect 18-44 year olds who make up 39% of the population. Foundational data on young Black adults' biopsychosocial pain characteristics, management, and use of integrative medicine is severely lacking. This presentation shares preliminary results from an NCCIH K01 award examining the sensory, behavioral, and social aspects of chronic pain among young Black adults, using national and local data. Qualitative data will come from pilot interviews with 14 Black adults living with complex chronic pain in the Pacific Northwest. Analyses examine environmental influences on pain, management strategies, access to integrative medicine, and intervention preferences. Results : The sample was majority female, low-income, with at least some college. Preliminary analyses indicate pain ratings over the past 7 days averaged 4-5 out of 10 (worst). Headache, low-back, abdominal, and chest pain were most common. 29% reported poor/fair social satisfaction, 32% poor/fair mental health, and 41% poor/fair physical health. 41% saw a mental health professional, 6% a traditional healer, and 12% a chiropractor. Interviews reveal minimal access to pain management programs for young Black adults. Participants preferred natural over pharmacologic treatments, which they viewed as overprescribed. They also emphasized addressing mental health in pain interventions led by culturally-congruent teams. Conclusions : These preliminary analyses provide the first national snapshot of emerging and young-to-middle age Black adults’ chronic pain experiences and integrative medicine utilization. They highlight age as a key factor in designing interventions to reduce pain and improve wellness across the lifespan for Black adults. OA 11.02 Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction for Rheumatoid Arthritis: Feasibility and Preliminary Results from the DREAMER-1 Pilot Trial Sarah L Patterson MD , Wendy Hartogensis PhD, Sarah Fisher MS, Krish Rai MS, Patricia Katz PhD, Patricia Moran PhD, Frederick Hecht MD University of California, San Francisco, San Francisco, CA, United States Purpose : Most individuals living with rheumatoid arthritis (RA), the most common autoimmune rheumatic disease, experience persistent symptoms despite pharmacologic treatment. Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR) is an 8-week program that teaches mindfulness and stress resilience skills and has demonstrated psychological benefits, but its use in RA has not been rigorously evaluated. The Defining Stress REsilience And Mindfulness Effects in Rheumatoid Arthritis (DREAMER-1) pilot study aimed to adapt MBSR for individuals with RA and evaluate feasibility, acceptability, and preliminary efficacy. Method(s) : We enrolled adults with physician-confirmed RA not in remission and without a regular mindfulness practice. During phase 1, we developed teacher training components to address RA-related challenges in MBSR courses, yielding Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction for Rheumatic Diseases (MBSR-RD). In Phases 2–3, 38 participants were randomized to MBSR-RD or treatment-as-usual (TAU). Acceptability, adherence, and safety were measured. Select patient-reported outcomes (PROs) were assessed using PROMIS instruments at baseline and post-intervention. Exploratory efficacy analyses used linear mixed models to estimate mean net between-group differences in change in PROMIS T-scores from baseline to post-intervention. Results : All 38 participants (100%) completed the study. Among those assigned to MBSR-RD, 83% attended ≥6 of 8 classes, 78% attended the retreat, and mean home practice averaged 46 minutes/day. All participants reported they would recommend the program; 94% rated quality as “good” or “excellent.” MBSR-RD produced significantly greater improvements in depressive symptoms versus TAU (mean –4.83 PROMIS T-score; 95% CI –8.96 to –0.70) and trends toward improvements in pain (–3.15; 95% CI [-6.92, 0.61]), fatigue (–3.61; 95% CI [-7.49, 0.27]), and sleep (–1.96; 95% CI [-4.29, 0.37]). No intervention-related adverse events occurred. Conclusions : DREAMER-1 demonstrates that MBSR with the inclusion of components to accommodate participants with RA is feasible, acceptable, safe, and may improve important PROs. These findings support a fully powered randomized trial to evaluate efficacy and mechanisms. OA 11.04 Are we underestimating the impact of context effects on healing? Insights from 4 trials evaluating integrative health interventions for chronic low back pain Dan Cherkin PhD U.W. Osher Center for Integrative Health, Seattle, WA, United States Purpose : To optimize the healing potency of health care interventions, we should consider how we might promote both their specific and contextual effects. This would require a major reconceptualization of how we design and provide health care. This presentation explores findings of four effectiveness trials of interventions for chronic back pain to stimulate thought about the importance of both specific and contextual effects on outcomes. Method(s) : All 4 trials included the treatment under study, a credible active comparison group and a “usual care” group. The primary outcome was percentage of participants improving by a clinically meaningful amount on the Roland-Morris Disability Questionnaire. Primary endpoints ranged from 8 to 26 weeks after initiating treatments. Results : In all four trials, outcomes did not differ significantly between the specific treatments and active comparison groups (e.g., in the acupuncture trial, about 60% of participants improved in the needling tailored to TCM diagnosis, standardized point needling, and toothpick stimulation in standardized points groups). The percentages of participants improving in the usual care groups was similar across trials (38% to 45%). In all trials, percentages of participants improving with active interventions was about 50% greater than that for usual care. Conclusions : These findings raise questions relevant for finding ways to improve patient care, including: • Is just providing patients with a credible intervention in an engaged and caring manner, regardless of specific treatment, an effective treatment? • Why not re-conceptualize specific non-specific effects as specific effects (e.g., listening to patient, caring)? In the emerging Whole Person Health context, we should recognize that many contextual factors can promote healing. A rational system designed to optimize patient outcomes and reduce suffering should embrace strategies for optimizing the totality of effects. Oral Abstract Session 12: Integrative Digital Health Interventions OA 12.01 Mindfulness Without Borders: Feasibility Insights from Two Pilot Studies of a Spanish-Language Digital Mindfulness Program for Cancer Survivors in Bogotá and Chicago David Victorson 1,2 , Raúl Murillo 3 , Nicolás Martínez-Ramos 4 , Bruriah Horowitz 5,2 , Christina Sauer 5,2 , Judy Guitelman 6 , Elena Navas 7 , Gabrielle Osher 5 , Madeline Cetilin 5 , Dershung Yang 8 , Claudia Tellez 5 1 Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Evanston, IL, United States 2 Osher Center for Integrative Health at Northwestern University, Chicago, IL, United States 3 Pontificia Universidad Javeriana and Hospital Universitario San Ignacio, Bogotá, Distrito Capital, Colombia 4 Pontificia Universidad Javeriana, Bogotá, Distrito Capital, Colombia 5 Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, United States 6 Alas-Wings Latina Breast Cancer Alliance, Chicago, IL, United States 7 Global Health Beat Foundation, Evanston, IL, United States 8 Bright Outcome, Buffalo Grove, IL, United States Purpose : Spanish-speaking cancer survivors face barriers to culturally tailored support. Bogotá participants were young adult survivors who received the 9-week program, while Chicago participants were adult survivors who received the same program plus optional weekly Zoom meetups. We examined feasibility, acceptability, and engagement across sites to identify implementation facilitators and barriers. Method(s) : Participants were Spanish-speaking cancer survivors recruited through community and clinical partners. Baseline surveys assessed sociodemographic, clinical, and psychological characteristics. Engagement metrics were automatically captured through the digital platform. Acceptability was assessed post-intervention via usability and satisfaction questionnaires. Bogotá participants spent more total minutes on structured class content but engaged less in independent practice and social features. Higher baseline distress did not predict greater engagement, underscoring the possible importance of synchronous social reinforcement to sustain use. Optional meetups were associated with substantially higher adherence, suggesting that hybrid asynchronous–synchronous formats may optimize engagement. Findings highlight key contextual, cultural, and structural factors to consider when scaling digital mindfulness programs in global oncology survivorship settings. OA 12.02 Patient Experiences of Mind–Body Processes in Chronic Pain: Our Whole Lives Virtual Group Visits Nathaniel Glassy Ph.D., ALM 1,2 , Noah Babbott-Bryan 1 , Joshua Phillips 1 , Laura Orozco Segura 1 , Dr. Paula Gardiner MD, MPH 1,2 , Niina Haas MA 3 1 Cambridge Health Alliance Center for Mindfulness and Compassion, Malden, MA, United States 2 UMass Chan Medical School, Worcester, MA, United States 3 BrightOutcomes, Buffalo Grove, Il, United States Purpose : Chronic musculoskeletal pain reduces quality of life and functioning. Our Whole Lives (OWL), a 24-week randomized trial of an online integrative group visit program, combines mindfulness and self-management strategies. Method(s) : We report on the qualitative analysis of participants in the intervention arm. Virtual focus groups used a semi-structured guide, and rapid matrix analysis applied an a priori framework aligned with the interview structure. We summarized matrix cells and identified recurring experiential domains including interoception, emotion regulation, self-management, compassion, and group dynamics consistent with Yalom’s therapeutic factors of group process (i.e., universality, shared hope). Results : Thirty-five adults with chronic musculoskeletal pain completed interviews (mean age 50 years). Most were female (69%). Self-identified race: White 74%, Black 14%, Asian 6%, multiracial 6%, Hispanic/Latine ethnicity 11%. Participants reported pain intensity remained generally stable, yet their relationship to pain and their bodies shifted. Many described increased awareness of bodily sensations, and clearer differentiation between physical pain and emotional responses, using practices including body scans, mindful eating, breathing or mindful pauses to pace activity and respond to tension. Self-management became more deliberate and compassionate; participants treated pain as a signal for care through kinder self-talk, breaks, movement, despite self-compassion being initially difficult. Group processes, including feeling less alone, mutual validation, and a safe, nonjudgmental space with the facilitator, fostered universality, hope, and accountability for mindfulness practice. Conclusions : Digital tools like community forums, live group sessions, and tracking prompts can enhance awareness of bodily sensations and emotional cues related to pain. Clinicians should measure success beyond pain intensity, focusing on function, acceptance, and self-management. Combining mindfulness with supportive group education empowers patients to manage chronic pain and prioritize quality of life. Conclusions : WHT significantly improved pain interference and secondary outcomes compared with mCBT-CP and UC. Findings support consideration for broader implementation of coordinated, whole-person, nonpharmacologic pain care in integrated health systems. OA 12.04 Mixed-Methods Studies to Inform Feasibility and Acceptability of Bedtime App-guided Mindfulness Meditation for Patients with Insomnia Yan Ma 1 , Peter Wayne 1 , Janet Mullington 2 , Suzanne Bertisch 3 , Gloria Yeh 4 1 Osher Center for Integrative Health, Division of Preventive Medicine, Brigham and Women’s Hospital and Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 2 Department of Neurology, Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center and Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 3 Division of Sleep and Circadian Disorders, Department of Medicine, Brigham and Women's Hospital, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 4 Division of General Medicine, Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : Mindfulness meditation (MM) apps for sleep have become popular, while little is known about the use of MM apps specifically at bedtime, or the efficacy and mechanisms of such practice on insomnia symptom severity and pre-sleep arousal. Method(s) : In Phase 1, we conducted a mixed-methods single-arm pilot study to explore the feasibility of 4 weeks of app-guided bedtime MM practice in 10 participants with insomnia disorder with sleep initiation difficulties. In Phase 2, we conducted focus groups (16 patients with chronic insomnia and 8 experienced instructors) to identify facilitators and barriers to app use and inform intervention refinement for a future randomized controlled trial. Results : Phase 1 completion rates were over 90% with 95% completion of in-lab studies, 100% completion of questionnaires, 91% adhered with the intervention. Self-reported measures suggested improvement in sleep quality (Pittsburgh Sleep Quality Index change of -3.7, 95% CI: -6.7, -0.7), insomnia severity (Insomnia Severity Index change of -4.5, 95% CI: -7.7, -1.4), and pre-sleep arousal (Pre-sleep Arousal Scale change of -7.7, 95% CI: -13.1, -2.3). Qualitative data revealed benefits and skills imparted by mindfulness including: decreased catastrophizing; acceptance/non-reactivity; body awareness/relaxation; self-kindness; awareness of sleep hygiene/bedtime routine; earlier defusing of stress; increased focus and presence; and calm throughout the day. In phase 2, suggestions from experienced instructors included setting up appropriate expectations, instructions on novel content and repetitive practice, integrating sleep hygiene tips in bedtime app use and MM practice. Conclusions : The study of bedtime app-guided MM for insomnia disorder with both in-lab and remote home sleep assessments is feasible and acceptable. In preparation for future efficacy or mechanistic studies, it is important to refine the MM app intervention and study protocol according to clinical sleep recommendations to minimize heterogeneity in implementing the practice, and to decrease risks of app use at night. Acknowledgement: Harvard Osher pilot grant, NCCIH K23AT012475. Oral Abstract Session 13: Clinical Mind-Body Research OA 13.01 Integrative Interventions for Chronic Multisymptom Illness in Veterans Barbara L. OA 13.02 Economic Evaluations of Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction for Mental and Physical Wellbeing: A Systematic Review Dr. Rebecca L Acabchuk PhD 1 , Dr. Eric Loucks PhD 1 , Dr. Patricia Herman 2 , Dr. Simon Goldberg PhD 3 , Dr. Blair Johnson 4 , Laura Haygood 1 , Regan Gregory 1 , Nana Amma Larbi MD 1 , Dr. Shufang Sun 1 , Chen Li PhD 4 1 Brown University, Providence, RI, United States 2 RAND, Santa Monica, CA, United States 3 University of Wisconsin, Madison, Wisconsin, United States 4 University of Connecticut, Storrs, CT, United States Purpose : Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR) is a group-based program with evidence for improving anxiety, stress, chronic pain, depression, and quality of life. Despite its demonstrated benefits, MBSR remains underused in healthcare. Policymakers and insurers typically request economic data to guide coverage decisions. In response, this systematic review summarizes findings on the cost-effectiveness of MBSR, examining how results vary by clinical population, type of analysis and length of follow-up. By synthesizing the most recent economic evidence and highlighting gaps, this review aims to guide coverage/reimbursement decisions and identify priority areas for expanding access to MBSR. Method(s) : Eligible studies evaluated standard or slightly modified MBSR programs reporting at least one economic outcome. A scientific librarian searched nine databases through March 2025. Screening and data extraction were performed in duplicate using CHEERS 2022 criteria, including risk of bias and grading tools for economic quality. Data were synthesized across populations, cost categories and perspectives (payer, societal). Clinical areas were mapped to identify cost-effectiveness evidence and gaps. Results : Eleven studies from six countries met inclusion criteria; data indicate favorable cost-related results for MBSR in nine studies. Five were randomized controlled trial-based economic evaluations, all showing cost-effectiveness; four reported cost savings from a payer/healthcare perspective (back pain, k=1; fibromyalgia, k=1; and mental health, k=2). Two Markov models predicted MBSR to be cost-effective for back pain across populations. Three studies using partial economic analyses (no health outcomes) found MBSR reduced healthcare costs for pain-related conditions (k=2) and mixed mental health (k=1). Conclusions : This systematic review of MBSR economic evaluations finds consistent evidence of cost-savings/effectiveness and improved outcomes for chronic pain and mental health. Knowledge gaps remain for MBSR digital delivery, preventive care and other health outcomes. While quality appraisal is ongoing, preliminary findings suggest MBSR may offer a cost-effective, scalable public health strategy. The purpose of this study was to evaluate the feasibility, acceptability, and appropriateness of augmenting BRZA deprescribing protocols with a combination of acupuncture and yoga in older adults. Method(s) : This single-arm clinical feasibility study enrolled adults aged ≥65 years who used BRZA at least four times in the last month. Results : Of 487 patients screened, 23 participants enrolled, with 14 completing the study (60% female, median age 70 years). Participants attended a median of 11.5 yoga sessions, 12 acupuncture sessions, and 11 psychiatric nurse practitioner sessions, with a median of 5.6 days/week of home yoga practice. Conclusions : Augmenting BRZA deprescribing protocols with acupuncture and yoga is feasible, acceptable, and appropriate for older adults. OA 13.04 ASMR Media as a Biopsychosocial Health and Wellbeing Tool Marcus Washington M.A. Industrial/Organizational Psychology , Dr. Catherine Daus Doctorates in Industrial/Organizational Psychology Southern Illinois University Edwardsville, Edwardsville, Illinois, United States Purpose : ASMR media contains specific sounds and visuals that produce Autonomous Sensory Meridian Response (ASMR), a sensory phenomenon characterized by tingling sensations and feelings of relaxation, with multiple studies reporting biopsychosocial outcomes. The current study aimed to explore 1) ASMR’s biopsychosocial responses, 2) differences between ASMR “experiencers” (AEs) and “non-experiencers” (non-AEs), 3) the impact of education on ASMR outcomes, 4) the utility of ASMR media as a health and wellbeing tool, and 5) the need for a more rigorous understanding of the ASMR experience. Method(s) : Sixty-one participants were recruited from a Midwestern university and assigned to one of four 2x2 mixed ANOVA conditions. First, participants were randomly assigned to an education group (education vs. no education) and then to an experience group based on their physical sensations (AE vs. non-AE). Results : Wilcoxon signed-rank tests were used for within-subject comparisons, Mann-Whitney U tests for main effects, and Kruskal-Wallis H tests for interaction effects. Analyses revealed that ASMR stimulation produced tingling sensations, non-tingling sensations (waves, vibration, spreading sensations), pain reduction (rrb = 1.00 for all groups), relaxation (rrb ranged from .97 to 1.00 across groups), and stress reduction (rrb ranged from .97 to 1.00 across groups). Furthermore, ASMR-induced wellbeing was the same for all groups, regardless of experience (rrb ranged from .008 to .226 across outcomes) or education (rrb ranged from .035 to .185 across outcomes). Conclusions : The results of this study support the use of ASMR media as an accessible, cheap, and effective health and wellbeing tool. The ASMR-Misophonia Synesthesia Spectrum (AMSS) was developed to provide an empirical and theoretical definition of the ASMR experience, while the revised ASMR Experiencer Questionnaire (R-AEQ) was developed to allow researchers to categorize participants within the AMSS. Farewell PhD , Dr. Jenn Leiferman PhD University of Colorado - Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, CO, United States Purpose : Perinatal women (e.g., pregnant through 12-months postpartum) experiencing adverse social determinants of health face exacerbated stress-related health outcomes, yet few interventions address their biopsychosocial recovery in accessible, non-pharmacological ways. Results : Phase 2 baseline findings revealed associations between nature exposure, enhanced psychological needs fulfillment (autonomy, competence, relatedness) and informed the importance of prevention strategies that promote biological (e.g., sleep), psychological (e.g., mindfulness) and social (e.g., peer support) resources to foster perinatal well-being. This work is especially relevant given the growing recognition of the role that environmental exposures play in shaping stress physiology, mental health, and recovery across the perinatal period. OA 14.02 Advancing Equity in Nature RX through Expert Insights on Inclusive and Accessible Nature Prescriptions:A Qualitative Interview Study Alexandra Haney 1 , Varsha Ganesh 1 , Timothy Chen 1 , Bruriah Horowitz 1 , Christina Sauer 1 , David Victorson 2 1 Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, United States 2 Feinberg School of Medicine, Evanston, IL, United States Purpose : Growing public health research supports the positive relationship between nature contact and improvements in human health. This study aimed to identify key strategies and considerations for making nature prescription consultations more inclusive and accessible for traditionally marginalized or underrepresented populations by learning from experienced nature prescribers. Method(s) : Experienced nature RX providers participated in a 45-minute recorded Zoom interview about strategies to enhance inclusivity and accessibility in nature prescription consultations. Results : Interviews with 15 experts generated 39 unique codes. Axial and selective coding resulted in 15 categories and five overarching themes: “Accessibility and Infrastructure Barriers” focused on environmental and transportation constraints; “Safety and Risks” addressed nature settings and privacy; “Historical Stigmas and Anxieties” related to exclusion and representation; “Personal Preferences and Identities” centered on personalized goals and community support; and “Combatting Location-Based Constraints” focused on ways to feasibly access nature in an urban environment. Themes underscored the need to address safety concerns tied to racial and ethnic identity, acknowledge historical exclusion from nature, ensure culturally relevant prescription approaches, and foster community-based trust. Experts also emphasized equitable technological access and clear communication as key to effective engagement. Conclusions : Findings underscore that inclusivity in nature prescription consultations requires more than access - it calls for cultural humility, contextual awareness, and recognition of systemic inequities shaping people’s relationships with nature. Incorporating diverse representation, personalized goals, and community-centered delivery models can strengthen trust and relevance. Future Nature RX initiatives should integrate equitable design, practitioner training, and policy support to ensure that all individuals can safely and meaningfully connect with nature. Limitations included subjective survey data and challenges in data collection due to brain fog and fatigue among participants. Despite these limitations, this study is the first to combine objective and subjective data on Long COVID in a GMV setting, making it a replicable and resource-efficient model for outpatient clinical care. Findings emphasize the importance of addressing physical, mental, and social aspects of Long COVID through innovative care models. Participants described high acceptability, citing the relaxing environment and provider empathy. Conclusions : This feasibility study confirmed acupuncture is feasible and acceptable treatment for Long COVID pain. A multisite trial to evaluate scalability is planned. OA 16.02 Effectiveness of Short-Duration Acupuncture Sessions on Quality of Life During Infusion Therapy at an Academic Cancer Center Dr. Jacob D. Hill ND, MS, FABNO , Dr. Jasmin Hundal MD, Dr. Caitlin Rader DO, Ning Guo MS, Dr. Laura Shoemaker DO, Dr. Thuy Nguyen DAOM, LAc, Dr. Robert Saper MD, MPH Cleveland Clinic, Cleveland, Ohio, United States Purpose : Acupuncture is an evidence-based integrative oncology intervention increasingly adopted by cancer patients and oncology providers for managing cancer-related side effects. Existing research predominantly examines 30–60-minute sessions administered weekly or biweekly over 6–12 weeks. Evidence regarding short-duration sessions and the cumulative effects of repeated brief treatments remains limited. Our team investigated the preliminary effectiveness of brief acupuncture sessions delivered concurrently with infusion therapy. Method(s) : We conducted a quality assessment/quality improvement project examining acupuncture for adults receiving infusion therapy at an academic cancer center. Patients received brief (20-minute) acupuncture sessions during infusion to address symptoms and improve quality of life. The Edmonton Symptom Assessment Survey (ESAS) was administered pre- and post-treatment. We analyzed pre/post changes in ESAS scores, compared outcomes across treatment frequencies (1, 2, or 3+ sessions), determined the probability of achieving a minimal clinically important difference (MCID, ≥1 point change), identified response predictors, and explored symptom score changes across subgroups (e.g., gender, cancer type). Results : Among 139 patients receiving acupuncture, 64.8% were female (n=90) and 84.2% White (n=117). Significant pre/post improvements (p<0.05) were observed in anxiety, depression, drowsiness, appetite loss, nausea, pain, dyspnea, fatigue, and wellbeing. Patients receiving only 1 session of acupuncture presented with significantly worse baseline symptoms across most ESAS domains compared to those receiving 2 or 3+ sessions. The probability of achieving MCID decreased significantly with multiple treatments. Increasing age positively predicted treatment response for appetite loss, and response patterns for tiredness and drowsiness differed significantly among patients with hematological malignancies, solid tumors, and benign hematological conditions. Conclusions : Brief acupuncture sessions administered during infusion therapy yielded significant improvements across multiple cancer-related symptoms. Observed patterns of reduced baseline symptom severity and decreased probability of achieving MCID among patients receiving multiple treatments may indicate cumulative therapeutic benefit. OA 16.03 Integrating Acupuncture into Acute Pain Care in the Emergency Department: An Implementation Science Approach Using the AIHI Framework Professor Zhen Zheng PhD 1 , Dr Heng Cheok MBBS, FACEM 2 , Ms Letty Bastian MNSc 2 , Mr Tom Collins MPhysio 2 , Prof Tony Zhang PhD 1 1 RMIT University, Bundoora, Victoria, Australia 2 Northern Health, Epping, Victoria, Australia Purpose : Pain accounts for one in two presentations to the emergency department (ED), and opioids are used to treat more than 60% of these cases despite their side effects and risk of dependence. Acupuncture is a safe, effective, non-pharmacological analgesic option, but has not been systematically integrated into ED workflows. The Personalised Acupuncture in Northern Hospital Emergency Department for Acute Pain (PANDA) project applies an implementation science approach to bridge this gap, aiming to develop an integrative, interprofessional model of acute pain care. Method(s) : The Acupuncture in Hospital Implementation (AIHI) framework, adapted from the EPIS (Exploration, Preparation, Implementation, Sustainment) and CFIR (Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research), guides a four-stage, iterative process. The AIHI framework enables systematic identification of contextual determinants, co-design of strategies with clinicians and experts, and evaluation of real-world implementation outcomes to inform scale-up. Results : Stage 1 (Exploration) includes surveys and audits that identified key barriers, including limited patient and staff knowledge, unclear referral pathways, and restricted ED space. Stage 2 (Preparation) involves co-designing an acupuncture-in-ED protocol with international experts and frontline staff, developing tailored education and workflow strategies and identifying champions and opinion leaders. Stage 3 (Implementation) has two part-time registered acupuncturists embedded in the ED five days per week. Implementation strategies include feedback loops, audit dashboards, and regular evaluation of implementation, clinical and system outcomes (% of patients referred for and receiving acupuncture, pain intensity, function, anxiety, analgesic use, length of stay). Stage 4 (Sustainment) focuses on cost-effectiveness and value-based healthcare analyses to inform future integration and policy translation. Conclusions : Guided by the AIHI framework, PANDA is expected to demonstrate the real-world integration of acupuncture into acute pain care in the Emergency Department through a systematic implementation science approach. This approach will provide a replicable pathway for integrative and interprofessional models that advance translational research and transform pain management in emergency settings. Feasibility was defined as successful initiation and uninterrupted use of VR throughout the procedure, including comfort and proper headset fit. Results : Thirty patients were analyzed (17 VR, 13 control). Feasibility was achieved in 94% of VR participants; one discontinued due to sinusitis-related discomfort. No adverse effects or workflow disruptions occurred. Conclusions : VR was safe, feasible, and well tolerated, with favorable trends toward reduced pain, anxiety, and physiologic stress. These findings support further evaluation in larger studies and suggest screening for sinus-related symptoms may enhance feasibility. OA 17.04 Physiological and Psychological Interaction Patterns in Mind-Body Interaction Therapy (MBIT): Reaction Dynamics, Narrative Analysis, and AI-Enhanced Case Generation Using Large Language Models (LLMs) Prof. Yu Du PhD University of Colorado Denver, Denver, CO, United States Purpose : Hypertension is increasingly understood through a biopsychosocial framework. Method(s) : During a 28-day intervention, daily patient-reported outcomes captured somatic and emotional responses such as sweating, yawning, temperature shifts, and emotional release. Reaction clusters were tight in Days 1–7 but became more dispersed by Day 28, reflecting individualized physiological integration. Narrative analysis revealed four thematic domains: Emotional Unburdening and Healing, Physical Sensation and Regulation, Reflections on Healthcare Experience, and Sleep Quality and Recovery, with sentiment trends generally positive. BN modeling identified Positive Emotional State, Living Conditions, and Health Literacy as key upstream factors influencing physical symptoms and psychological well-being. The LLM generated coherent narratives aligning with these themes, demonstrating utility for augmenting qualitative insight. Conclusions : MBIT supports an interconnected process of physiological and emotional regulation in hypertension care. Integrating clustering, narrative analytics, and causal modeling offers a robust framework for understanding mind-body dynamics. LLM-based narrative generation shows promise for enhancing qualitative data and accelerating pattern discovery in future research. Conclusions : This study demonstrated the feasibility and acceptability of PARTS as a group-based treatment for trauma-related symptoms. OA 18.04 The Impact of Yoga on Symptom Clusters of Posttraumatic Stress Disorder: A Systematic Review and Meta-analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials Dr. Sinead M Sinnott PhD 1,2 , Dr Laura E Laumann PhD 3,4 , Dr Katherine E Gnall PhD 5,1,2 , Dr Crystal L Park PhD 5 , Dr Yin Wu PhD 6 , Menaja Raja BS 5 , Annmarie Khawand BS 1 , Dr Linda Pescatello PhD 7 1 Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA, United States 2 Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 3 Department of Psychiatry and Human Behavior, Warren Alpert Medical School of Brown University, Providence, RI, United States 4 Center for Behavioral and Preventive Medicine, The Miriam Hospital, Providence, MA, United States 5 Department of Psychological Sciences, University of Connecticut, Storrs, CT, United States 6 Department of Research, Hartford Hospital/Healthcare, Hartford, MA, United States 7 Department of Kinesiology, University of Connecticut, Storrs, CT, United States Purpose : Posttraumatic Stress Disorder (PTSD) is a disruptive, multifaceted disorder, which impacts an estimated 6.8% of people in the United States (U.S.) at some point in their lives. Yoga has been shown to reduce PTSD symptoms overall, though little is known about the extent to which it reduces specific PTSD symptom clusters. In this systematic review and meta-analysis, we examine the relationship between yoga and change in each of the PTSD symptom clusters. We hypothesized that yoga participation would be associated with significant reductions in each PTSD symptom cluster, particularly hyperarousal. Method(s) : We searched Pubmed, PsycInfo, CINAHL, and SPORTDiscus for eligible studies through July 2025. Studies were included if they were yoga intervention randomized controlled trials, enrolled trauma survivors, and reported PTSD subscale scores. Standardized mean difference scores were computed and compared. A National Institutes of Health quality assessment tool was used to assess risk of bias. Results : In our direct models (k = 9 studies), yoga led to reductions in intrusion (d = -0.31, p =.027) and total PTSD (d = -0.29, p = .030) symptoms. When accounting for age, yoga also improved avoidance (d = -2.28, p = .003) and hyperarousal (d = -1.92, p =.007) symptoms, as well as intrusion and total PTSD symptoms, such that yoga led to greater symptom improvements among younger age groups. Substantial risk of bias was found across studies. Conclusions : Yoga showed the most robust effects for reducing intrusion and total PTSD symptoms. After accounting for age, yoga demonstrated efficacy in reducing avoidance and hyperarousal symptoms, in addition to improving intrusions and total PTSD symptoms. Yoga’s emphasis on mindfulness in the present moment may allow for attentional awareness that is protective against intrusions. Oral Abstract Session 19: Research of Traditional Health OA 19.01 Quantitative Study on the Effect of Yin/Yang Forces of Tai Chi Movement on the Flow of Blood and Electrical Energy in the Body Michael J. Pirrello, Ethan R. Tran , Jeremiah A. Susanto, Alan Guo, William Ma, Shin Lin Ph.D University of California, Irvine, Irvine, CA, United States Purpose : Chen Style Tai Chi emphasizes movements based on the principle of “within the Yin, there is Yang”, as illustrated in the Tai Chi symbol. The purpose of this pilot study is to determine how a slow (Yin) movement with increasingly more force (Yang) can affect the flow of blood and electrical energy in the body. Method(s) : Subjects were 10 healthy male/female college students. Blood flow/perfusion was measured as “flux” at the PC8 acupoint (right palm) with laser Doppler flowmetry (Moor DRT-4 monitor) and energy flow was measured as “Before Polarization conductance (BP)” from LU1 acupoint (right thumb) to a ground electrode (lower arm) with Motoyama's Single Square Voltage Pulse method using a Continuous AMI instrument (from California Institute of Human Science). Subjects pressed their right palm downwards against 0.0/2.5/5.0/7.5/10.0 lbs. of weight on a pulley to simulate Chen Style “White Crane Opening Wings” (5 cycles/minute). Results : Consistent with our previous studies, laser Doppler flowmetry showed that blood flow/perfusion increased to a peak as the hand pressed down on increasing amounts of weight. Similarly, the flow of electricity measured as BP also increased with weight. In a typical experiment when the subject did 6 repetitions of the downward movement against various amounts of weight, the mean peak sizes were 2434,SD27/2444,SD25/2485,SD37/2556,SD56/2685,SD124 (arbitrary units) for 0.0/2.5/5.0/7.5/10.0 lbs. of weight; when the three most prominent peaks at each weight level were selected for analysis, the means were 2485/2551/2629/2923, respectively. Conclusions : This pilot study indicates that when the hand presses effortlessly (Yin) in a slow-downward (Yin) movement, there is only a small increase in electrical conductance within the lower arm. However, when the hand presses down with increasing effort (Yang), the flow of energy increases with the level of force, in keeping with the Tai Chi principle of “within the Yin, there is Yang”. OA 19.02 Ancestral Knowledge and Gastrointestinal Health: Ethnographic Insights from Traditional Healers in Morelos, Mexico Zephelline A Huerta BA Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, New York, NY, United States Purpose : Gastrointestinal (GI) disorders in Latine communities often go underdiagnosed due to systemic disparities and culturally discordant care. Method(s) : An ethnographic study was conducted in Morelos, Mexico, with ten traditional healers (4 curanderas, 4 terapeutas, 2 integrative practitioners) recruited through community networks. Across all interviews, healers stressed honesty, compassion, and referral to biomedical care when appropriate. Conclusions : Traditional healers in Morelos conceptualize gastrointestinal illness as the embodied convergence of physical, emotional, and spiritual imbalance, with complementarity to biomedicine. These findings encourage allopathic clinicians to explore the emotional and cultural dimensions of digestive illnesses, ask patients about traditional remedies, and understand healing as both relational and contextual. Doing so will give a better understanding of patient experiences, foster culturally responsive dialogue, and provide care that is more comprehensive, compassionate, and effective. OA 19.03 Targeted Treatment in Major Depression Using Ayurvedic Dosha Subtypes: Development of the ADSM Clare Andriola B.S. 1,2 , Kiran Monteverdi B.S. 1,2 , Divjot Uppal 1 , Grace Reed B.S. 1 , Sarah Koh 1 , Natalie Sekowski Sekowski 1 , Jennifer Rand MS, AYT 1 , Yumi Munir B.S. 1 , Eliot Steer PhD, AP 3 , Shraddha Ravani PhD 2 , Aparna Upadhyaya NP 2 , Dr. Sudha Prathikanti MD, AP 1,2 , Juha Lee 1 1 UCSF Department of Psychiatry and Behavioral Sciences, San Francisco, CA, United States 2 UCSF Osher Center for Integrative Health, San Francisco, CA, United States 3 Palo Alto University, Palo Alto, CA, United States Purpose : Major depressive disorder remains a leading cause of disability and is often undertreated in primary care. This project developed and validated the Ayurvedic Depression Subtyping Measure (ADSM), a standardized tool to classify depression by dosha subtype within conventional psychiatric frameworks. Method(s) : The ADSM was created through a three-phase process. 1.) A Delphi panel of experts in Ayurveda, psychiatry, and psychology developed a 24-item instrument capturing dosha-based depressive features. 2.) A clinical validation with 30 adults diagnosed via the MINI compared ADSM subtype classifications with practitioner diagnoses using Cohen’s kappa to evaluate convergent validity. Classical patterns emerged, with Kapha-type depression—marked by lethargy, heaviness, excessive sleep, and social withdrawal—most prevalent, corresponding to melancholic presentations described in Western psychiatry. The ADSM was feasible to administer in research and primary care contexts, supporting its scalability and clinical utility. Conclusions : The ADSM provides an empirically grounded framework for integrating Ayurvedic subtyping into modern depression care. By translating dosha principles into a standardized format, the tool enables personalized, constitution-based interventions aligned with evidence-based psychiatry. Within a broader UCSF clinical trial exploring dosha-specific yoga for depression, the ADSM represents an early step toward establishing validated, culturally informed measures that operationalize traditional constructs for use in integrative mental health practice. OA 19.04 Evaluating the Psychosocial Effects of a Contemplative Photography Exhibition: Results from a Single Arm Study Joanna Ho 1 , Jingli Wang 1 , Darshan Mehta MD, MPH 2,3 , David Victorson PhD 4,5 1 Great Wisdom Buddhist Institute, Brudenell, PE, Canada 2 Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 3 Osher Center for Integrative Medicine at Harvard University, Boston, MA, United States 4 Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Evanston, IL, United States 5 Osher Center for Integrative Health at Northwestern University, Chicago, IL, United States Purpose : Mental health challenges motivated Zhen-Ru, the spiritual teacher of Great Wisdom Buddhist Institute and Great Enlightenment Buddhist Institute Society, to explore photography as a means of alleviating mental stress. A WH approach offers healthcare systems the opportunity to reinforce their commitment to patient priorities and preferences. This includes prioritizing provider retention to support development and sustainment of robust patient-provider relationships. OA 20.02 Heals on Wheels: Development and Implementation of Community-Based Workshops for Integrative, Whole Person Pain Care in Underserved Communities Dr Catherine Justice DPT 1 , Jadyn Knox BS 2 , Dr Stefanie Stevenson MD 1 , Dr Kate Shafto MD 1,3 , Dr Tegan Reeves PhD 1 , Jacinta Rebang MPH, PMP 1 , Dr Sue Haddow MD 1 , Dr Arti Prasad MD 1 1 Hennepin Healthcare Systems, Minneapolis, MN, United States 2 Hennepin Healthcare Research Institute, Minneapolis, MN, United States 3 University of Minnesota, Minneapolis, MN, United States Purpose : Chronic pain is a pervasive health issue that disproportionately affects low-income and other marginalized populations, who often lack resources and experience healthcare access barriers. The Heals on Wheels (HoW) program addressed this through the development and implementation of integrative pain workshops for underserved communities throughout Hennepin County, Minnesota. Method(s) : A multidisciplinary team developed a 4-week curriculum for 1-2 hour-long weekly workshops, hosted by community organizations serving populations at increased risk of disproportionate chronic pain impact. A worksheet was distributed to participants at the start and end of each workshop to explore perceptions of: pain, overall state, favorite moments, and future suggestions. Qualitative exploration of the worksheets was employed through transcription and thematic analysis. Results : Throughout 2022-2025, 13 workshop series occurred with 9 different community organizations, with total attendance of 369 (average 7.10/session). 265 questionnaires were completed by 163 unique individuals (71.82%). Average reported pain at the start of sessions was 5.40, reducing to 3.26 at the end (-2.14). Themes of before group state were: 1)Stable; 2) Health Concerns; 3) Overwhelm; and 4) Hope. Themes after group were: 1) Relaxed, calmer, tired; 2) Improvement, relief; 3) Gratitude, hope; and 4) Neutral. Dominant themes were relaxation, calmness, gratitude, and hope. Less common was the neutral or no change from a small subset. There were twelve themes of favorite part of workshops. including specific skills/techniques. Additionally, roughly 1/10 participants highlighted the importance of sharing and hearing others’ stories. Themes for future suggestions were dominantly expressions of positive feedback; other themes included wanting more time, session structure, and specific activities (e.g. hypnosis, aroma therapy). Conclusions : The HoW workshops were acceptable and feasible to implement. HoW is an innovative and accessible integrative healthcare delivery model. OA 20.04 The Ripple Effect: Coaches’ Perspectives on How Health and Wellness Coaching Improves the Lives of Veterans Dr. Kelly Dvorin PsyD 1 , Dr. Ekaterina Anderson PhD 1 , Dr. Sophia Bartels PhD 1 , Dr. Kimberlee Flike PhD, MSN, RN 1,2 , Matthew Martel 1 , Zenith Rai MPH 1 , Dr. Justeen Hyde PhD 1 1 Center for Health Optimization & Implementation Research (CHOIR) VA Bedford Healthcare System, Bedford, MA, United States 2 Solomont School of Nursing, University of Massachusetts Lowell, Lowell, MA, United States Purpose : Health and Wellness Coaching (HWC) is a vital element of the Department of Veteran’s Affairs (VA) Whole Health System, which focuses on delivering person-centered, holistic care centered on what matters most to patients. Coaches support and empower patients to identify and work towards health and wellness goals. HWC is a highly patient-, rather than diagnosis-driven, approach guided by patients’ values and priorities. While outcomes research on HWC in VA is developing, there is a need to continue building the evidence. Therefore, we sought to understand what coaches have observed as key impacts of the HWC process. Method(s) : We conducted qualitative interviews with 21 coaches and 7 coach supervisors as part of a larger evaluation of HWC in VA. Interview transcripts were coded in ATLAS.ti. A detailed thematic analysis was conducted on content related to impacts on Veterans. Results : Coaches identified a variety of impacts across multiple domains: shifts in internal processes or mindsets (e.g., increased confidence, motivation, and self-awareness), behavioral changes (e.g., quitting smoking, increased exercise, greater engagement in healthcare), and physical health outcomes (e.g., weight loss, lowered A1C). A major finding was that Coaches observed cascades of change in which one change spilled over into another and to another, or a “ripple effect.” For example, improvements in sleep hygiene led to changes in diet, and to having more energy, and ultimately to greater family engagement. Conclusions : Coaches painted a complex picture of how improvements manifest in Veterans’ day-to-day lives, describing interconnected and “rippling” patient impacts spanning multiple aspects of functioning (i.e., mental, behavioral, physical). This has implications for how we understand and measure HWC outcomes. As studies on HWC effectiveness are developed, it will be critical to utilize methodology that is well-suited to this complexity. It will also be important to examine the pathways that lead to the changes we elucidated. Oral Abstract Session 21: Clinical Studies of Yoga OA 21.01 Feasibility and Impact of Inpatient Yoga Therapy for HSCT Patients Across Two Global Settings Smitha Mallaiah MSc, C-IAYT University of Texas M D Anderson Cancer Center, Houston, Texas, United States Purpose : Hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) is a lifesaving treatment, but is also physically and emotionally challenging. By addressing cultural context and resource variability, this research examined scalability, whole-person supportive care models, and the application of YT for inpatient settings in high- and middle-income settings.
