Outcomes included validated symptom and quality of life (QOL) measures (GAD-7, PHQ-9, FACT-BMT), satisfaction surveys, and qualitative interviews. Results : Thirty patients enrolled (15 per site). MDA patients attended more sessions (mean 10 vs. 8), with high engagement at both sites. Clinically meaningful reductions in anxiety, depression, fatigue, and pain were observed. QOL scores (FACT-BMT) were consistently higher in the U.S. cohort, and Indian participants reported higher psychological burden, with both groups deteriorating in QOL post-transplant. Both groups valued the intervention’s emotional and physical support. Delivery was feasible in both high- and middle-income settings, with patients preferring in-person sessions during hospitalization and virtual formats post-discharge Conclusions : Delivering YT during inpatient hospitalization for HSCT patients is a novel and challenging setting, yet we found it is a feasible and culturally adaptable across diverse environments. Findings support scalable, low-cost YT interventions to improve outcomes, reduce distress, and expand whole-person care. Culturally tailored approaches can help close equity gaps in cancer care and align with global goals to measure meaningful outcomes and scale effective integrative therapies. OA 21.02 Cost effectiveness of yoga versus transdiagnostic cognitive behavioral therapy for emotional disorders Dr Danielle C. Mathersul PhD 1,2 , Prof Erik J. Groessl PhD 3,4 1 Murdoch University, Perth, WA, Australia 2 Veterans Affairs Palo Alto, Palo Alto, CA, United States 3 University of California San Diego, San Diego, CA, United States 4 Veterans Affairs San Diego, San Diego, CA, United States Purpose : Mental health conditions are the leading cause of years lived with disability. While evidence-based cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) is efficacious, response rates typically average only ∼50%. Yoga may offer another option. We examined preliminary cost-effectiveness of yoga versus transdiagnostic CBT (t-CBT) for improving adult mental health from a recently completed non-inferiority randomized controlled trial. Method(s) : Across 2022 to 2024, 123 community-dwelling Australian adults (85% female, mean age 38.75) diagnosed with an anxiety, depressive, or posttraumatic stress disorder were randomized to either 10-weeks group t-CBT or 10-weeks group yoga (weekly, 90-min telehealth sessions). The study was prospectively registered (ACTRN12622000544774) and had ethics approval. The primary outcome was the Kessler Psychological Distress Scale (K10; clinical improvement: scores decreasing by 5+ points). A functional impact measure was converted to Assessment of Quality of Life (AQoL)-4D utility scores (post-minus-pre, by intervention group). Intervention costs were estimated using data tracked during the study. Interventions were delivered by trainees and thus, personnel costs were reduced by 50%. Incremental cost and incremental effectiveness were calculated before being combined into an incremental cost-effectiveness ration (ICER). Costs were viewed from an organizational perspective and 3-month time horizon was used. Results : The mean cost to deliver 10 sessions of group t-CBT was $289/participant versus $159/participant for group yoga. Incremental cost was $130 and incremental 3-month-adjusted effectiveness was .0178. Change in utility scores over time did not significantly differ by intervention, however, 3% more participants had clinically significant improvement (K10) with yoga versus t-CBT. Therefore, the ICER for t-CBT versus yoga was ∼$7,300/QALY. Conclusions : Yoga costs less to deliver than t-CBT. Yoga had slightly better clinical efficacy (K10) and t-CBT had slightly better functional outcome (AQoL4D), yet differences were not statistically significant. Data are preliminary yet suggest yoga may be a cost-effective option for improving adult mental health. OA 21.03 Is a structured Yoga Intervention Comparable to Conventional Pharmacological Care in Managing Knee Osteoarthritis? Kavita Bakshi MSc University of Patanjali, Haridwar, Uttarakhand, India. Patanjali Research Foundation, Haridwar, Uttarakhand, India Purpose : Knee osteoarthritis (KOA) affects over 374 million people globally, demanding safer and more effective treatments for the increasing aging population. Conventional Care (CC) of Non-Steroidal Anti-Inflammatory Drugs (NSAIDs) primarily address symptoms but often causes adverse effects. Yoga offers a holistic approach enhancing joint function and well-being. In alignment with World Health Organisation’s healthy aging and traditional medicine goals, this study compares the effectiveness of Conventional Care of NSAIDs and a structured Yoga Module (YM) in improving clinical outcomes in KOA. Method(s) : A randomized controlled trial was conducted on 89 patients with KOA with age 45 to 70 years, symptom duration ≥6 months, Numeric Pain Rating Scale (NPRS) ≥4, and Kellgren-Lawrence grade between 1 to 3. Patients were assigned to CC, or a 45-minute YM for 60 days. Outcomes of pain (NPRS), inflammation (C-Reactive Protein), anxiety (Hamilton Anxiety rating scale, HAM-A), and quality of sleep (Pittsburgh Sleep Quality Index, PSQI) were assessed at baseline, day 30 and day 60. Results : Participants with mean age and standard deviation of 56.4 ± 6.98 years with 67.4% female participants completed the study. YM significantly reduced pain (p ≤ 0.001), inflammation (CRP, p = 0.007), anxiety (HAM-A, p ≤ 0.001), and improved sleep quality (PSQI, p ≤ 0.001) compared to CC, which also showed changes in pain (p ≤ 0.001), inflammation (CRP, p = 0.45), anxiety (HAMA, p ≤ 0.001) and sleep quality (PSQI, p = 0.38). Overall, YM demonstrated superior efficacy in modulating, inflammation and improving sleep outcomes. Conclusions : Both interventions improved pain and anxiety; however, yoga showed greater, sustained benefits, including reduced inflammation and better sleep. These findings suggest an immunomodulatory effect of yoga, supporting its role as a safe, integrative, and non-pharmacological pain management therapy that complements conventional care and promotes healthy aging in patients with KOA. Oral Abstract Session 22: Measuring Whole Health OA 22.01 Measuring What Matters: Development of the Whole Person Health Index for Integrated Health Self-Assessment Stephen J Blumberg PhD , Paul J Scanlon PhD, Kristen Cibelli Hibben PhD National Center for Health Statistics, Hyattsville, MD, United States Purpose : Whole person health is central to an emerging National Institutes of Health (NIH) strategy for addressing chronic disease. In collaboration with NIH, the National Center for Health Statistics (NCHS) developed the Whole Person Health Index (WPHI), a brief, person-centered tool for integrated health self-assessment. The WPHI consists of nine items measuring general health, quality of life, social and family connections, diet, physical activity, stress, sleep, existential wellbeing, and health management ability. Each item is rated on a five-point scale — excellent (5), very good (4), good (3), fair (2), or poor (1) — with the sum of responses indicating overall health status. Method(s) : The WPHI was tested in rounds 10 and 11 of NCHS’s Research and Development Survey (RANDS), using adult samples from commercial survey panels. Items underwent cognitive interviewing and pilot testing, and responses were compared with established measures in each domain. Descriptive and psychometric analyses assessed item performance and the index’s structure across population subgroups. Results : Forty cognitive interviews conducted in summer 2024 confirmed that respondents interpreted items within their intended scope. In RANDS 10 (April-May 2024, n = 5,017), individual WPHI items showed moderate correlations (0.3 < ρ < 0.7) with related validated scales, supporting construct validity. Data from RANDS 11 (January-March 2025, n = 4,778) demonstrated that WPHI has strong internal consistency. Factor analyses supported a single-factor model, and measurement invariance was confirmed across sex, race/ethnicity, and education, though differential item functioning was noted for self-rated health by age. Conclusions : The WPHI is a concise yet comprehensive measure of whole person health. Its items effectively capture intended constructs, and the index score is reflective of a single underlying concept. Now included in the National Health Interview Survey and deployed in NIH’s All of Us cohort, the WPHI offers a broadly applicable tool for monitoring whole person health. The pain education-based program follows the VA’s Whole Health model of care, empowering Veterans to manage their pain using conventional and complementary health approaches. Method(s) : Through a series of four, 60-minute sessions, Veterans share their personal experiences with pain through exploration of four pain neuroscience concepts. In conjunction with clinical services, battle plans can include referrals for CIH treatments, movement modalities, and well-being services. Health and wellness coaches support Veterans as they execute their battle plan and work towards self-defined goals. Results : Pain ratings and pain interference (PROMIS 6B) are assessed upon program entry and re-evaluated at 3 months, 6 months, and 12 months to gauge personal and program effectiveness. Statistically significant improvements in mean pain interference T-scores were noted at 3 months (n=16; mean change -5.86; p=0.0005) and 6 months (n=10; mean change -8.27; p=0.0056). While no significant change was noted in mean best pain ratings, a significant improvement was noted in mean worst pain ratings at 3 months (n=24; mean change -0.96; p=0.0256) and 12 months (n=13; mean change -1.54; p=0.0060). Conclusions : The initial findings from Pain University underscore the value of using a holistic, Veteran-centric approach to improve the well-being of Veterans living with persistent pain through education and conservative management. This model has vital implications for advancing pain care amidst national changes brought about by the Comprehensive Addiction and Recovery Act (CARA) and can play an essential role in evolving healthcare practices within the VA system and the broader medical community. OA 22.03 Health Risk Navigation Tool: Scaling Personalized Support for Whole-Person Health in the Workplace Justin Jackson , Amy Locke MD, Paul Estabrooks PhD, Kayla Norton, Selim Sheikh MD, Ryan Burns PhD, Megan Miller, Rachel Weir MD University of Utah, SLC, Utah, United States Purpose : A Health Risk Navigation Tool, launched in 2024 as part of an employee wellness program, aims to connect benefit-eligible employees to personalized, evidence-based health support. Through a custom Health Risk Assessment (HRA), the tool integrates screening, coaching, and referrals to promote whole-person health and support value-based care transitions. Method(s) : Employees were invited to complete an annual online HRA that incorporates validated measures of physical activity, nutrition, sleep, stress, mental health, and substance use. This resulted in over 141,000 health resource recommendations, with 84% of participants receiving at least one resource that they were ready to take action on. Tailored Care Pathways enrolled 1,461 employees, with 81% engaging in and completing the majority of the pathway. Primary care activation increased, with 1,037 employees requesting assistance, of which 70% initiated scheduling with a new provider. Behavioral health outreach addressed 143 urgent cases, including 43 individuals with very high-risk scores not previously in care. Conclusions : The Health Risk Navigation Tool demonstrated strong engagement and a scalable model for personalized health support. Future efforts will focus on expanding navigator capacity, targeting under-reached job groups, integrating HRA data into clinical workflows, and piloting bundled service models through redesigned Tailored Care Pathways. OA 22.04 Scaling the Integration of Well-Being Measurement in Healthcare Services: Lessons Learned from Early Adopters within the Veterans Health Administration Bella Etingen PhD 1,2 , Omonyele L. Adjognon ScM 3 , Matthew B. Martel BS 4 , Dawne Vogt PhD 3,5,6 , Timothy P. Hogan PhD 4,2 , Tamara Schult PhD, MPH 7 , Barbara G. Bokhour PhD 4,8 1 Research and Development Service, Dallas VA Medical Center, Dallas, TX, United States 2 Department of Health Economics, Systems, and Policy, Peter O’Donnell Jr. School of Public Health, UT Southwestern Medical Center, Dallas, TX, United States 3 Center for Health Optimization and Implementation Research (CHOIR), VA Boston Healthcare System, Boston, MA, United States 4 Center for Health Optimization and Implementation Research (CHOIR), VA Bedford Healthcare System, Bedford, MA, United States 5 Women’s Health Sciences Division, National Center for PTSD, VA Boston Healthcare System, Boston, MA, United States 6 Department of Psychiatry, Boston University Chobanian & Avedisian School of Medicine, Boston, MA, United States 7 Office of Patient-Centered Care and Cultural Transformation, United States Department of Veterans Affairs, Washington, DC, United States 8 Department of Population and Quantitative Health Sciences, University of Massachusetts Chan Medical School, Worcester, MA, United States Purpose : The Veterans Health Administration (VHA) is working to incorporate the Well-Being Signs (WBS) measure, a three-item, psychometrically valid measure of psychosocial well-being, into care. However, best practices for incorporating well-being measurement into healthcare settings remain largely unknown. This evaluation assessed best practices and experiences with WBS implementation among early adopters within VHA Medical Centers. Method(s) : We completed semi-structured interviews (n=25) with Whole Health Program (n=8) and clinical (n=3) leadership and healthcare team members (n=14). Participants were selected from five medical centers with the highest volume of WBS administration across VHA. Interviews provided data on strategies for promoting, and barriers and facilitators to, WBS use. We used rapid qualitative analytic techniques to generate insights into these three areas. Results : Five strategies promoted WBS use: training and mentorship to healthcare teams on WBS integration into practice; embedding WBS into existing workflows; permitting a flexible approach to WBS integration; providing incentives to healthcare teams to reinforce WBS use; and tracking use and sharing that information with healthcare teams. Four barriers hindered use: competing priorities and staffing challenges; perceptions that the questions were difficult for Veterans to answer; interpretation and follow-up on WBS scores; and IT/Systems issues. Four facilitators bolstered use: use of informal WBS ‘Champions’ and open discussions regarding its use; fostering healthcare team member buy-in; visible leadership support; and the brevity/familiarity of the scale. Conclusions : Our findings suggest strategies for healthcare systems looking to integrate well-being measurement into healthcare services, including: (1) make well-being measures easy for healthcare team members to find and use; (2) allow flexibility in how well-being measurement is incorporated into care and encourage this through leadership support, champions, accountability, and incentives, and; (3) provide training on how to measure well-being, integrate the information into care, and when to follow-up with patients who report poor well-being. Poster Abstracts Topic Area 01: Acupuncture P 01.01 Self-administered acupressure to improve xerostomia in head and neck cancer patients: preliminary findings for a randomized controlled trial Ms Xueyan Cheng the University of Hong Kong, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, China Purpose : Xerostomia is prevalent in patients with head and neck cancer (HNC). While acupuncture, grounded in traditional meridian theory, has demonstrated efficacy, the potential of self-administered acupressure remains unexamined. This study aimed to estimate the effect of self-administered acupressure for improving xerostomia in patients with HNC. Method(s): A two-arm randomized controlled trial was conducted. The intervention group received a 90-minute acupressure training session from a registered Traditional Chinese Medicine practitioner, a follow-up session (60 min), instructional materials (booklet, videos), and a practice diary. Participants performed self-administered acupressure twice daily for 12 weeks. The control group received oral care education. Both groups received twice-weekly reminders. Self-reported xerostomia was measured using the Xerostomia Questionnaire (XQ) at baseline, 4 weeks, 12 weeks, and 24 weeks. Unstimulated and stimulated salivary flow were collected at baseline, 12 weeks and 24 weeks. Results : Fifty-three participants were allocated to each group. Adjusted mixed-effects models showed the intervention group had significantly greater improvement in XQ scores at 4 weeks between-group difference in mean change: -6.86; 95% CI: -12.66 to -1.07, p=0.021) and 12 weeks (between-group difference in mean change: -8.59; 95% CI: -14.80 to -2.37, p=0.008) compared to controls. The effect was not sustained at 24 weeks (between-group difference in mean change: -0.77; 95% CI: -9.48 to 7.93, p=0.857). The model showed a significant group×time interaction (p=0.035). While between-group differences in salivary flow were non-significant, the intervention group showed significant within-group increases in unstimulated (mean change from baseline: 11.08, 95%CI 2.75 to 19.41, p=0.006) and stimulated saliva (mean change from baseline:8.48, 95%CI 1.76 to 15.19, p=0.010) at 24 weeks compared to baseline, which were not seen in the control group. Conclusions : This study provides promising evidence that self-administered acupressure can significantly improve xerostomia in patients with HNC. Future studies should explore the practical implications of self-administered acupressure with larger samples and assess its long-term benefits. P 01.02 Intraoperative Acupuncture for Total Knee Arthroplasty Dr Thoa Ho Doctorate of Acupuncture and Oriental Medicine 1 , Mr Steven W. Li Student 1,2 1 Department of Veteran Affairs, Orlando, Fl, United States 2 Florida College of Integrative Medicine, Orlando, FL, United States Purpose : The opioid epidemic necessitates effective, opioid-sparing strategies for perioperative pain management. Total knee arthroplasty (TKA), a common U.S. surgery, poses a high risk for postoperative opioid dependence. Consequently, non-pharmacological interventions like acupuncture have gained attention as adjuncts to multimodal analgesia protocols. Method(s): To evaluate the effect of intraoperative auricular acupuncture points during TKA on postoperative pain, opioid use, hospital stay length, and emergency department visits within two weeks of surgery. Whole Health providers screened and referred interested patients for auricular acupuncture. Consent was documented in EMR. Acupuncture was performed after anesthesia induction, following a time-out. Outcomes included pain, ED visits, and hospital stay. Follow-ups occurred on postoperative day 1 and week 1. Results : Patients who received intraoperative acupuncture during TKA had significantly lower postoperative pain levels. In traditional surgery, scores were 4.013 without acupuncture and 2.917 with acupuncture. In robotic surgery, scores were 3.615 without acupuncture and 3.167 with acupuncture. Acupuncture reduced oxycodone, morphine, and ketorolac use by 57%, 64%, and 95% in traditional surgery, and by 65%, 100%, and 18% in robotic surgery. ED visits were 16% without acupuncture vs. 12.5% with acupuncture. Hospital stays averaged 1.40 vs. 1.03 days in traditional surgery, and 1.22 vs. 1.00 day in robotic surgery. No needle site complications were reported. 76% would recommend acupuncture, and 55% reported benefits. Conclusions : In response to the opioid crisis, this study evaluated intraoperative auricular acupuncture in total knee arthroplasty (TKA) at the Orlando VA Medical Center. Using six classical auricular points, we assessed pain, opioid use, hospital stay length, and ED visits. Findings support acupuncture as safe and effective, with reduced pain scores, decreased medication use, shorter hospital stays, and fewer ED visits. No complications were reported, and 76% of patients recommended it, with 55% reporting benefits. P 01.03 Expanding Pediatric Integrative Care: A Protocol-Driven Auricular Acupressure Training for Front-line Providers Ms Lisa Squires BSN 1 , Dr. Maria Mascarenhas MBBS 1 , Dr. Lynn Gershan MD, CM, DABMA 2 , Celia Hildebrand DAOM, LAc 3 , Dr. Winona Chua MD 4 1 University of Pennsylvania, Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, Philadelphia, PA, United States 2 University of Minnesota, Minneapolis, MN, United States 3 Andrew Weil Center for Integrative Medicine, College of Medicine, University of Arizona, Tucson, AZ, United States 4 University of Pennsylvania, Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, Philadelphia, pa, United States Purpose : Auricular acupressure (ear seeding) is an accessible, low-risk intervention with emerging evidence supporting its use in managing pain, anxiety, substance use, and nausea. To enhance pediatric integrative care offerings, we designed and implemented a structured training program for nurses, physician assistants, and nurse practitioners. Method(s): We reviewed acupressure curricula at major acupuncture institutions and collaborated with a physician-acupuncturist and pediatric acupuncturist to develop a protocolized course. Online modules introduced historical context, Chinese Medicine theory, and ear anatomy. In-person sessions spanned two days—totaling 2 to 8 hours—featuring hands-on training using an electro-stimulation pen for point localization and MRI-safe Vaccaria seed placement on latex-free adhesive. Institutional approval and detailed policy development supported safe practice. The second day incorporated additional techniques, such as shonishin, tuina, and advanced protocols. Results : Clinically, auricular seeds have been well tolerated in pediatric settings, including use in infants (for reflux, feeding, opioid withdrawal) and older children for anxiety, nausea, sickle cell pain, musculoskeletal pain, and headaches. Conclusions : Our protocol-driven auricular acupressure training equips frontline providers with practical, low-risk tools to address pediatric symptoms. Initial clinical experiences suggest beneficial effects across a range of conditions. Further formal outcome studies are needed to quantify efficacy, establish standardized metrics, and support broader implementation. P 01.04 Analysis of the Effect of Muscular Activity on Blood Flow during Tai Chi Movement using Gaussian Process Machine Learning Modeling William Ma, Anna Kwan, Alan Guo, Ethan R. Tran, Jeremiah A. Susanto, Michael Pirrello, Shin Lin Ph.D University of California, Irvine, Irvine, CA, United States Purpose : The purpose of this pilot study is to use Gaussian Process Machine Learning Modeling to analyze a set of highly complex data from measurements of the effect of varying muscle activity on blood flow at different acupuncture points in subjects performing a slow (Yin) but forceful (Yang) downward hand movement that simulate Chen Style Tai Chi “White Crane Opening Wings” which follows the Tai Chi principle of “within the Yin, there is Yang”. Method(s): Subjects were 10 healthy male/female college students. Blood flow/perfusion was measured as “flux” with laser Doppler flowmetry (Moor DRT-4 monitor) at acupuncture points TH-5. LI-10, and LI-13 when the subjects were pressing downwards against various amounts of weight on a pulley (6 repetitions in 1 minute). Results : While there appears generally to be an upward trend of blood flow when the hand was pressing against increasing amounts of weight, there was a high level of variability (mean values accompanied by high levels of SD) among the 10 subjects and among measurements at the 3 acupoints. However, with Gaussian Process Machine Learning Modeling, we were able to reduce the complexity of the data to the following set of predicted total blood flux during the downward movements. TH-5: baseline=975 (arbitrary units), 0 lb=2106, 5 lbs.=3247, 10 lbs.=4171; LI-10: baseline=1603, 0 lb.=3256, 5 lbs.=3558, 10 lbs.=4354; LI-13: baseline=2132, 0 lb.=3439, 5 lbs.=4357, 10 lbs.=5800. Conclusions : Our analysis indicates that (1) the higher the slow (Yin) downward force (Yang) of the hand produces a higher level of blood flow, in keeping with the principle of “within the Yin, there is Yang” as illustrated in the Tai Chi symbol, (2) blood flow at LI-13>LI-10>TH-5, and (3) despite relatively high variance, there is strong overall correlation in blood flow pattern across different acupoints and levels of muscular activity during this Tai Chi-like movement. P 01.05 Scaling Auricular Acupuncture (NADA Protocol) Training to Improve Acupuncture Access Doctor Juli Olson DC, DACM, FAIHM 1 , Doctor Stephen Gregory DACM, LAc 2 , Doctor Claudia Voyles DAOM, LAc 3 1 VA Central Iowa, Des Moines, IA, United States 2 VA Portland, Portland, OR, United States 3 Central Texas VA Medical Center, Austin, TX, United States Purpose : The Veterans Health Administration (VHA) developed an internal VHA National Acupuncture Detoxification Association (NADA) Protocol training for those who have scope to deliver auricular (ear) acupuncture. Veterans and field providers were eager for an acupuncture protocol to address a broader range of conditions beyond pain. Method(s): This session shares the development of the VHA NADA Protocol training system and curriculum for healthcare providers, and discusses barriers and facilitators for launch. Infrastructure needed for the training included development of a training platform, training materials, Veteran-facing education, and national tracking information. This represents year over year growth of 63% from fiscal year 2023 to 2024 and a 75% growth from fiscal year 2024 to 2025. Conclusions : VHA’s prior success training a variety of professionals in the delivery of protocolized auricular acupuncture through the BFA/BAA training model paved the way for this new training. BFA/BAA limited use to pain diagnoses necessitated the use of another protocol. The NADA Protocol was a logical next step with a wider set of indications. The incorporation of the NADA Protocol into the largest healthcare system in the US provides a model for other institutions to train, adopt, and promote auricular acupuncture. P 01.06 Battlefield Acupuncture: A Gateway to Whole Health Jenny Rouchka MD Robley Rex VAMC, Louisville, KY, United States Purpose : Battlefield acupuncture (BFA) is a simple, fast, and effective auricular protocol using Chinese acupuncture points. The VA’s Whole Health (WH) model emphasizes self-management and is associated with decreased opioid use in Veterans with chronic pain. BFA can serve as a gateway to promoting Whole Health concepts. A shared medical appointment (SMA) is an efficient way to combine BFA with Whole Health education. Method(s): Each SMA treats up to ten Veterans per provider. While Veterans are treated in a group setting, they learn Whole Health concepts, emphasizing combining passive and active treatments to reduce chronic pain. The vision is to establish weekly group BFA/WH clinics in every community-based outpatient clinic (CBOC). To start, clinics will rotate weekly across four CBOCs to accommodate limited staff and minimize clinic schedule disruption. Newly trained providers will co-lead with experienced staff and then transition to an independently led clinic after several months. Whole Health education can be provided by a Whole Health coach, BFA provider, or video resources. Results : The first group BFA/WH clinic was established in August 2023 at the Robley Rex VAMC. Veterans reported decreased pain and increased self-efficacy. 47 providers were trained across multiple specialties, with one primary care provider (PCP) in each CBOC. 1,972 procedures were completed in 2025 with an average pain scale reduction of 1.23, with 809 done by PCPs with an average decrease of 2.16. Monthly community of practice calls and preceptorships supported clinical proficiency. Pre- and post-treatment pain scores, well-being indicators, Veterans’ experiences, and provider feedback will inform further program development. Conclusions : Battlefield acupuncture combined with Whole Health education in a group setting is an efficient, peer supported, and Veteran-centered approach to chronic pain management. P 01.07 Pinpointing Relief: Determining Optimal Acupuncture Sessions for Pediatric Pain and Distress Elena P Gareau BS , Saloni Dangoria, Dr. Natalia Jaramillo PhD, Sarah Khan, Margaret J Klein, Monika Kobylecka LAc, Mikki Eason-Marchi DACM, BSN, LAc, RN, CPHON, Dr. Jeffrey I Gold PhD Children's Hospital Los Angeles, Los Angeles, California, United States Purpose : Acupuncture is an integrative therapy used with patients experiencing chronic and acute pain. Patients were recommended a minimum of 5 acupuncture sessions as part of a pediatric hospital’s clinical research program aimed at reducing pain and pain unpleasantness. The present study compares acupuncture treatment outcomes, through reductions in pain intensity and pain unpleasantness, to determine the optimal number of acupuncture sessions. Method(s): Pain intensity and pain unpleasantness ratings were collected through self-report surveys pre- and post- each acupuncture session. Pain intensity was measured using the Faces Pain Scale – Revised (FPS-R) and pain unpleasantness was measured using a visual analog scale (VAS). Linear mixed models clustered by patient were used to examine associations between number of total acupuncture sessions and outcomes. Results : 113 patients (ages 9-21) were enrolled (568 sessions total, 409 had complete pre-post treatment assessments). Patients received an average number of 5.03 (SD=2.8) sessions, with ten being the maximum number of sessions. Preliminary findings demonstrate significant mean reductions in both pain intensity and pain unpleasantness within each session. While the reduction in pain intensity was significantly greater with 5 compared to 2 sessions (estimate: -0.90 95% CI [0.12, 1.67], p<0.05), a more pronounced reduction was observed when comparing 7 to 5 sessions (estimate: -1.11 95% CI [-1.88, -0.348], p<0.01). A significant reduction was also observed in the change in unpleasantness rating comparing 7 to 5 sessions (estimate: -14.33 95% CI [-18.18, -10.48], p<0.0001). Conclusions : These findings suggest patients with 7 sessions experienced the greatest reduction in pain intensity and pain unpleasantness compared to the recommended 5 sessions. Further research should expand upon these findings to investigate the impact of 7 acupuncture sessions on other health outcomes for pediatric patients. P 01.08 The Importance of Measuring Context in the Study of Whole Person Health: Acupuncture Healing and Persistence of Clinical Effect Dr Lisa A Conboy MA MS ScD 1 , Dr Xiaoming Sheng MSc, PhD 2 1 Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 2 College of Nursing at the University of Utah, Salt Lake City, Utah, United States Purpose : The last 50 years we have seen an increase in published biomedical science on acupuncture, how it works and for which conditions . Acupuncture has been incorporated into many health delivery systems and the results of such are arguably positive, such as Medicare coverage for acupuncture treatments for chronic Low Back Pain. Yet there remains a lack of consensus on how to model acupuncture as a treatment. Should acupuncture be conceptualized as a drug, with dose-response effects? Or is acupuncture treatment more of a behavioral intervention as it requires action on the part of the patient? Method(s): All subjects from our Army funded RCT of acupuncture for Gulf War Illness trial (n=104) were invited to participate a resurvey 5 years post study completion. We considered if use of acupuncture after the trial ended was related to level of pain relief. We used general linear models to compare difference in McGill pain scores between different treatment groups and number of treatments after the trial ended. Results : Fifty- one subjects from the original sample responded to the resurvey. Baseline to long term follow-up pain measures suggests that 71% of the clinical effect was maintained. We found that the number of treatments post-trial is related to treatment group during the trial, with the twice per week group (higher dose) having a higher number of treatments post study. Conclusions : Data from high quality systematic reviews indicate that acupuncture treatment effects persist in the case of pain. But this effect is determined using studies that did not consider post-study use of acupuncture. Research including longer term outcomes will allow us to see persistence and variance in real world context. This is important information for persistence has ramifications for patient care and ultimate cost effectiveness. P 01.09 The Impact of Treatment Frequency on Therapeutic Alliance and Clinical Outcomes in Acupuncture for Gulf War Illness Dr Linnette M Johnson DCN 1 , Dr Angela Incollingo-Rodriguez PhD 2 , Dr Benjamin Nephew PhD 2 , Dr Lisa Taylor-Swanson PhD 1 , Dr Lisa Conboy ScD 3 1 The University of Utah, Salt Lake, Utah, United States 2 Worcester Polytechnic Institute, Worcester, MA, United States 3 Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : This study examined how the therapeutic alliance (TA) develops during acupuncture for veterans with Gulf War Illness (GWI) and whether treatment frequency influences TA and clinical outcomes. Guided by Bordin’s framework, it examined when TA forms and their effect on patient outcomes, especially pain levels. The study hypothesized that stronger TA would predict better outcomes and that biweekly sessions would promote faster alliance formation. Findings aim to inform clinicians about TA’s role in integrative care. Method(s): This secondary analysis used data from a randomized clinical trial including 104 veterans treated by experienced acupuncturists. Participants received either biweekly acupuncture for six months or a two-month waitlist followed by weekly sessions. TA was measured using the WAI-SR at multiple time points, and outcomes included pain, functioning, mood, and social support. Paired t-tests and hierarchical regression were used to assess changes and mediation effects. All procedures met ethical standards and controlled baseline clinical differences. Results : Therapeutic alliance increased across all subscales in both groups. The weekly group started lower after the waitlist but improved rapidly once treatment began. Biweekly sessions led to significantly greater pain reduction, with the Task component of TA fully mediating this effect. Goal alignment was associated with better physical functioning, and Bond was associated with reduced anxiety. Although both groups reached similar TA levels by six months, early delays hindered initial alliance formation. Conclusions : TA significantly influenced pain and functional improvements in veterans receiving acupuncture for GWI. More frequent treatment promoted faster, stronger alliance building and greater clinical benefit, particularly through task agreement and shared goals. Waitlisting slowed early alliance development, but consistent engagement helped patients recover relationship strength over time. These findings highlight TA as a key therapeutic mechanism in integrative care and emphasize the importance of active listening, collaboration, and clear goal setting in clinical practice. P 01.10 Geographic Variation in Utilization of Acupuncture among Medicare Part B Beneficiaries Dr. James M Whedon DC, MS 1 , Dr. Louis A Kazal MD 2 , Dr. Leah Grout PhD 1 1 Southern California University of Health Sciences, Whittier, California, United States 2 Geisel School of Medicine at Dartmouth, Hanover, New Hampshire, United States Purpose : In 2020, The Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS) initiated coverage of acupuncture for treatment of chronic low back pain. However, CMS restricted the provision of acupuncture services to medical physicians and certain specialties working under physician supervision. Independent licensed acupuncturists are prohibited from billing Medicare for acupuncture services. Thus, the limited availability of acupuncture clinicians under Medicare may compromise adequate and equitable patient access to acupuncture. The purpose of this study was to quantify the utilization and treatment intensity of acupuncture under Medicare by state. Method(s): We employed a cross-sectional design to examine 2020-2023 Medicare claims and quantify patient use of acupuncture by state. We measured utilization as acupuncture recipients per 100,000 Part B beneficiaries and treatment intensity as acupuncture visits per 100,000 Part B beneficiaries. To model utilization and treatment intensity across US states we estimated adjusted rates for each state using Poisson regression models with state-level covariates. Quintiles of rates for utilization and treatment intensity were mapped by state to illustrate geographic variation. Results : The mean rate of utilization among all states was 235.4 recipients per 100,000 Part B beneficiaries. Utilization rates ranged from 1,337.3 in New Jersey to 13.0 in Montana. The mean rate of treatment intensity among all states was 1,721.9 services per 100,000 Part B beneficiaries. Treatment intensity rates ranged from 11,488.0 in New Jersey to 30.7 in Arkansas. Utilization and treatment intensity appeared to be relatively higher in west coast and northeastern states. Conclusions : Utilization and treatment patterns related to acupuncture for Medicare Part B beneficiaries vary widely by state. The reasons for the variation are unknown, but the variation is likely related to both the availability of acupuncture services under Medicare and patient preference. P 01.11 Use of Integrated Acupuncture and Respiratory Therapy to Facilitate Tracheostomy Weaning in Patients undergoing Neuro-rehabilitation Dr. Roohi Banu BNYS, MD 1 , Pradeep Kumar BSc (RT) 1 , Dr. Archana Purushotham MD, PhD 1,2,3 1 Svatantra Integrative Neuro Rehab, Bengaluru, Karnataka, India 2 Baylor College of Medicine, Houston, Texas, United States 3 Michael E. DeBakey VA Medical Center, Houston, Texas, United States Purpose : Patients often require tracheostomy following major neurological events such as strokes and brain trauma. Decannulation i.e. removal of the tracheostomy tube is an important step to achieve effective neurorehabilitation, but may be delayed by a variety of factors. We integrated conventional respiratory therapy with acupuncture in patients admitted to a neuro-rehabilitation facility to expedite weaning. Method(s): The integrative treatment protocol consisted of: 1. Respiratory therapy – Two sessions daily (20–25 minutes) comprising chest percussion, vibration, postural drainage, tracheostomy tube downsizing, and corking trials. 2. Acupuncture – Administered five times per week targeting neuro-rehabilitation points GV20, LI4, ST36, Yintang, PC6, and auricular Shenmen. Retrospective chart review was used to record the length and course of the weaning period. Results : 23 tracheostomized patients (aged 16–78 years; 15 males, 8 females; 7 ischemic strokes, 4 intracranial bleeds, 7 TBI, 3 intra-axial tumors) admitted to Svatantra Integrative Neurorehab between November 2024 and October 2025 were treated with this integrative protocol. Patients had undergone tracheostomies 132.7 ± 382.7 days prior to admission. 7 had a history of pneumonia prior to admission, and 1 had required recannulation after initial decannulation. All patients were successfully decannulated using the integrative protocol with a mean weaning period of 28.2 ± 17.7 days. None of the patients experienced any complications barring one who developed a local infection at the stoma site that was successfully treated. Conclusions : Integrating acupuncture with structured respiratory therapy appears to be a safe and effective approach to facilitate tracheostomy weaning in patients undergoing neuro-rehabilitation. Hypothesized modes of action include enhancing lung capacity, improving clearance of secretions and neuromuscular coordination. Larger randomized controlled studies are required to confirm the efficacy of this integrative protocol. P 01.12 Novel Adjunctive Approaches for Refractory Cryovasculitis-Associated Venous Ulcers: Evaluating the Role of Acupuncture Derived Plasma Induction in Healing Outcomes Dr. Lorien S Ahn MD , Dr. Qiuxue T Tan MD, Dr. Lawrence Taw MD, Dr. Ranko Tutulugdzija DACM, L.Ac., A.P., Dipl AOM, MSAOM UCLA, los angeles, ca, United States Purpose : Cryoglobulinemic vasculitis-associated venous ulcers pose a significant therapeutic challenge due to chronic inflammation, ischemia, and poor tissue regeneration. This case report describes an elderly patient with refractory ulceration successfully treated with an integrative acupuncture protocol emphasizing plasma induction, angiogenesis, and fascia engagement. The intervention led to complete wound closure, suggesting acupuncture as a viable adjunctive therapy in complex vasculitic wound care. Method(s): An 80-year-old female presented with bilateral lower extremity ulcerations secondary and mixed cryoglobulinemic vasculitis. The most severe lesion—a 2-inch wide ulcer over the right lateral malleolus—remained refractory to healing despite treatment with Rituximab, corticosteroids, and advanced wound dressings. Following Rituximab administration, the patient reported worsening symptoms, including cold extremities, progressive purpling of distal tissues, and systemic fatigue. Dermatology initiated topical therapy with timolol drops, silver sulfadiazine (Silvadene), and mupirocin, while pentoxifylline was prescribed to enhance peripheral circulation. Weekly acupuncture sessions were initiated at UCLA East-West Medicine. Results : After four acupuncture sessions, eight of nine smaller ulcers closed with visible skin regeneration. Following eight sessions, there was a marked reduction in pain, edema, and discoloration, along with partial closure of the primary malleolar ulcer. At three months, complete healing of the primary ulcer was achieved, accompanied by restored skin tone, resolution of pain, and improvement in systemic symptoms, including increased warmth in the extremities and reduced fatigue. These outcomes align with clinical evidence supporting acupuncture’s efficacy in managing venous ulcers. Conclusions : When combined with conventional medical treatment, acupuncture therapy may enhance local perfusion, modulate inflammation, and stimulate tissue regeneration—offering meaningful improvements in wound healing and symptom control, particularly in cases refractory to standard interventions. The systemic benefits observed in this case further support the potential of acupuncture as a complementary modality in the management of autoimmune and vasculitic disorders, warranting further investigation through rigorous clinical studies. P 01.13 Effects of Xuanzhong (GB39) Acupoint on Learning, Memory and NMDAR Signalling Pathway in Vascular Dementia Rats Dr Man Sze Lam MD (PhD) in Acupuncture and Tuina Changi General Hospital, Singapore, Singapore, Singapore. Guangzhou University of Chinese Medicine, Guangzhou, Guangzhou, China Purpose : The study aims to elucidate the mechanism of acupuncture at Xuanzhong (GB39) acupuncture point in treating vascular dementia, providing experimental evidence for clinical acupuncture practice. Method(s): Sixty SPF-grade adult male SD rats were randomly allocated to four groups: sham-operated (SVD), vascular dementia model (VD), memantine (VD+M), and GB39 acupuncture (VD+GB39). Vascular dementia was induced by bilateral common carotid artery ligation. Post-modelling, the VD+M group received intraperitoneal memantine and the VD+GB39 group received acupuncture at GB39 for 5 days per week over 4 weeks while the SVD and VD groups received no treatment. After interventions, 10 rats per group underwent novel object recognition, active avoidance and T-maze tests to assess recognition memory, associative learning, and working memory. Hippocampal morphology (CA1, CA3) was examined by HE staining. ELISA and Western blot were used to quantify bile acid metabolism-related proteins (CYP7A1, cholic acid, TGR5, FXR) and NMDAR signalling pathway-related proteins (NMDAR, CaMKII, CREB, BDNF) in liver and hippocampal tissues. Results : The VD+GB39 group demonstrated significant cognitive improvements compared with the VD group, including increased exploration duration and recognition index in the novel object recognition test, reduced conditioned-stimulus latency in the active avoidance test, and fewer working-memory errors with higher working-memory indices in the T-maze test (P<0.05). Histological assessment showed that the VD+GB39 group exhibited well-preserved CA1 and CA3 hippocampal neurons, with orderly arrangement, minimal disruption, and only mild density reduction, indicating substantially less pathological damage than observed in the VD group. Protein analyses showed significant upregulation of bile acid metabolism–related proteins in the liver and hippocampus, as well as NMDAR pathway–related proteins in the hippocampus, in the VD+GB39 group compared with the VD group (P<0.05). Conclusions : Acupuncture at the GB39 point may effectively alleviate learning and memory impairment in VD models by regulating bile acid metabolism and activating the NMDAR signalling pathway. P 01.14 How Do Differing Definitions of “Acupuncturist” Result in Variation by State in Licensing and Counts of Acupuncturists? Ms. Barbara A Gibson MS, C-IAYT Southern California University of Health Sciences, Whittier, CA, United States Purpose : While most states in the United States define licensed acupuncturists (LAc’s) as licensed providers who practice acupuncture in their state, 13 states list multiple practitioner types. This incongruity affects which types are considered fully licensed and what techniques they provide, creating confusion for patients, undermining where acupuncturists fit as healthcare providers, and necessitating more standardized definitions and licensing in the US. We present differences in state acupuncturist types and effects on license definitions and state counts. Method(s): A database was created collecting LAc’s counts from each state via internet searches of state board agencies and verified by calling or emailing state board offices from January 2025 through June 2025. Data captured state registry dates from late 2024 – May 2025. Online, clerk verified, roster, and multiple practitioner type counts were recorded for each state. For the 13 states with multiple types listed as acupuncturist practitioners, types recognized as LAc’s were recorded. Results : Counts from all 50 states and the District of Columbia were recorded. Of the 13 states with multiple practitioner types, six listed more than two types with three states recognizing more than one type as an LAc with calls and/or emails required to verify which types are recognized. Two states recognize physician acupuncturists as LAcs and one state recognizes acupuncture detoxification specialists as LAcs. State law and regulations verifications were required for some recognized types. Conclusions : Incongruities in acupuncturist types on state board online registries create confusion when recording state counts of LAc’s. Recognizing the variability within states will help state and national healthcare registries determine who is actively practicing, what training and techniques these acupuncture practitioners can offer, and help determine acupuncture workforce needs. This may lead to more standardized definitions and licensing of acupuncturists in the US. P 01.15 A Complex Adaptive System (CAS) perspective of the placebo effect Dr Lisa A Conboy MA MS ScD 1 , Dr Claire Cassidy PhD, LAc 2 , Tanuja Prasad 3 1 Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 2 Georgetown University, Washington, DC, United States 3 ApplyComplexity LLC, New York, New York, United States Purpose : Is the placebo effect universally documented across medical systems? Some medical systems, such as the Asian Medicines (AMs) Ayurveda and Chinese Medicine do not have the concept of placebo response or effect as a part of their medical model. Why might this be? A possible answer is that these systems use medical models that are complex and are better modeled using qualities of Complex Adaptive Systems (CAS). AM's interventions include placebogenic factors. Here the patient can be modeled as an intelligent system, capable of interacting with medicines but always interacting within their lived context. We know from high quality placebo research that contextual elements of the therapeutic encounter can be related to placebo rate (eg https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2364862/ ). Good science also tells us that biological and social qualities of patients are related to variation in placebo rate ( https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3479140/,https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2814899/pdf/nihms154054.pdf ). Method(s): We will review selected functions of CAS appropriate to the modeling of AMs and offer exemplar models using data from 2 RCTs of Acupuncture treatment for Irritable Bowel Syndrome, and Gulf War Illness. Results : Use of CAS modeling can show new ways of understanding illness and healing that support Whole Person Health. Our model shows how placebo effects can be understood using complex modeling and how we can additionally understand placebo responses using CAS modeling. Conclusions : Asian Medicine models are descriptive and analytical, embracing and including the many types of emergent changes that can happen when one intelligent system (the patient) interacts with a practitioner-guided perturbation (treatment). Placebo effect is often termed a Complementary Medicine by scientists as it appears to be affected by a set of mechanisms as of yet to be fully understood. Using a complexity lens may be the solution to a better understanding of the mechanisms and actions of placebo. P 01.16 Examination of the Actual Status and Usefulness of Acupuncture Intervention as Palliative Care at Hiroshima University Hospital Yoshihiro Tamura Hiroshima University Hospital, Hiroshima, Hirohshima, Japan Purpose : Acupuncture and moxibustion in palliative care have attracted attention as supportive therapies that alleviate distress and anxiety, reduce stress, and improve patients’ quality of life (QOL). However, few studies have examined their actual clinical use in university hospitals. This study aimed to analyze the current practice and clinical impact of acupuncture treatment at Hiroshima University Hospital. Method(s): A retrospective analysis was conducted on patients referred to Kampo clinical center and Integrative Medicine for acupuncture treatment by the Palliative Care Team or related departments between June 2023 and March 2025. Results : A total of 49 patients were included in the study. Acupuncture points were selected according to Kampo (traditional Japanese) diagnostic patterns and meridian theory. Contact needle therapy was primarily used, with moxibustion or massage added depending on the patient’s condition. Among the 49 cases, 47 were cancer patients and 2 were non-cancer patients. The main treatment purposes were fatigue (26.2%), pain (20.4%), loss of appetite (12.6%), nausea/abdominal distension (6.8%), numbness (5.8%), and edema (4.9%). Among the 18 cases with documented Performance Status (PS), moderate impairment (PS 2–3) accounted for 61.8%. Positive changes after treatment were recorded in 40 patients, including relaxation, improvement of gastrointestinal symptoms, and pain relief. Pain improved in 9 of 17 cases, and fatigue improved in 5 of 10 cases. Furthermore, during the acupuncturist’s intervention, patients often expressed unrecognized symptoms or emotions, which were subsequently shared among the medical team and contributed to improved quality of care. Each patient received 1–16 sessions, totaling 206 treatments, and no adverse events were observed. Conclusions : Acupuncture and moxibustion therapy in palliative care contributed to relaxation, alleviation of gastrointestinal symptoms, and pain relief, suggesting improved QOL. Furthermore, communication during acupuncture sessions facilitated emotional expression and information sharing, leading to better integrated care. The findings support the usefulness and safety of acupuncture in palliative care settings. P 01.17 Acupuncture Practice Patterns Within the University of California Health System Dr. Michelle L Dossett MD, PhD, MPH , Dr. Tae Youn Kim PhD, RN University of California, Davis, Sacramento, CA, United States Purpose : Electronic health record data can potentially provide useful information regarding clinical care and practice patterns. We examined data from the University of California (UC) Health Data Warehouse on acupuncture care, involving five medical centers and affiliated clinics. Method(s): Data was extracted for all encounters with at least one of four acupuncture procedure codes (CPT 97810, 97811, 97813, 97814) occurring between January 2013 and December 2022. Number of patients receiving acupuncture, patient demographics, insurance coverage, and medical conditions associated with acupuncture visits were examined to understand practice patterns across sites. Results : A total of 35,627 unique patients received acupuncture across five UC-affiliated health systems, representing a total of 277,697 acupuncture procedures over the last 10 years. The average age of acupuncture recipients was 53 years (SD 17) and 69% of recipients were female. UC acupuncture clinics serve a diverse group of patients with 60% identifying as White, 5% Black, 11% Asian, 14% other, and 13% Hispanic. Insurance status at the time of clinic visits varied across the five UCs with the proportion of private insurance ranging from 49% to 80% and Medicare coverage from 10% to 41%. Total acupuncture volume steadily increased over the 10-year period except for a decrease in 2020. The average number of acupuncture procedures per patient ranged from 4 to 11 across different sites. The most prevalent medical problems were associated with musculoskeletal conditions (back pain, 63%; soft tissue disorders, 45%; pain not elsewhere classified, 44%; joint disorders, 39%; neck pain, 22%; and spondylosis, 6%). Other common diagnoses associated with acupuncture care were anxiety or stress related disorders (15%). Conclusions : There is variability in acupuncture patient populations and practice patterns across the five UC health systems that offer acupuncture. The UC Health Data Warehouse is a useful tool to begin understanding the scope of acupuncture care across the sites. P 01.18 A Decentralized, Confirmatory Trial of Acupuncture for Gulf War Illness: Protocol and Study Launch Dr. Lisa J. Taylor-Swanson PhD, MAcOM 1 , Dr. Catherine Elmore PhD 1 , Ms. Kirsten Rauk BS 1 , Dr. Linnette Johnson DCN, CNS, LDN 1,2 , Dr. Jamie Villanueva DAOM 1,2 , Mr. Anthony Beal MAOM 1,2 , Dr. Lisa A. Conboy MA, MS, ScD 2 1 University of Utah, SLC, UT, United States 2 Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : Gulf War Illness (GWI) is a Chronic Multi-symptom Illness (CMI) characterized by fatigue, sleep and mood disturbances, cognitive dysfunction, and musculoskeletal pain, affecting up to 43% of individuals deployed to the Gulf theater of operations. The CARE study is a Phase II confirmatory trial of Acupuncture Therapy (AT) for the treatment of GWI. The purpose of this paper is to outline the study design and methodological framework of the trial, with the goal of providing a model for future naturalistic AT trials targeting veteran populations. Method(s): The CARE study is a pragmatic, randomized, decentralized trial (DCT) overseen by three institutions: the University of Utah, Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, Harvard, and the University of California, Berkeley. Approximately 220 participants and 30 Licensed Acupuncturists (L.Ac) will be enrolled across six sites. Veterans will receive acupuncture tailored to their individual presentation. Primary outcomes are the 36-item Short Form Health Survey (SF-36) and the McGill Pain Scale. Secondary outcomes include fatigue, mood, sleep, anxiety, depression, social support and networks, stress, autonomic symptoms (HRV), interoception, cognition, patient-reported expectations, therapeutic alliance, and blood-based biomarkers. This whole-person measurement toolkit investigates multiple, potentially synergistic, markers of change. Linear mixed-effects regression models will compare primary outcomes between groups at six months. Results : We have recruited LAcs in states with a high density of Veterans with GWI: FL, TX, WA, VA, CO and CA. Fifteen L.Acs have been recruited. This study is ongoing. Conclusions : The primary objective of this research is to confirm AT for managing symptoms of GWI. Data will be collected using a whole-person health framework, which may contribute to a better understanding of the physiological and psychological impacts of GWI and the development of novel biomarkers to improve diagnostics and therapeutic treatments. Results from this study might also bolster the presence and availability of AT in veteran health settings. P 01.19 Characteristics of Gulf War Illness Participants in an Acupuncture Study Kai-Yin Hsu NP, MAOM 1 , Joe Chang PA, MAOM 2 , Beth Ann Schmidt M.Ac 3 , Anthony Beal MAOM 4 , Dr. Lisa Taylor-Swanson PhD, MAcOM 5 , Dr. Lisa Conboy Sc.D, MA, MS 4 1 Tufts Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States 2 Inspire Santa Fe Medical Group, Santa Fe, NM, United States 3 New England School of Acupuncture, Worceseter, MA, United States 4 Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States 5 Universtiy of Utah, Salt Lake City, UT, United States Purpose : Gulf War (GW) veterans report significantly more nonspecific multiple complex medical symptoms, including fatigue, sleep and mood disturbances, cognitive dysfunction, and musculoskeletal pain. Results : One hundred and four subjects underwent randomization. Mean age was 48.2 years old, 13% were female.
