P 01.20 Treating Autoimmune and Multi Autoimmune Syndrome through acupuncture and herbal medicine addressing dysregulated emotions, PTSD, and sustained stress, using Western biomedical research to inform treatment Dr. Eugene L Mahoney DAHM 1 , Dr. Lisa Conboy 1 , Dr. Heather Zwickey 2 1 SIEAM, Seattle, WA, United States 2 Helfgott Institute, Portland, OR, United States Purpose : This study examines how unresolved psycho-emotional trauma and immune dysregulation co-contribute to autoimmune disease and explores acupuncture as an integrative intervention targeting both immunological and emotional healing. While Western research implicates elevated cytokines (e.g. IL-6, TNF-α) and Treg/Th17 imbalance in chronic autoimmune progression, Chinese medicine traditions link emotional stagnation (anger, grief, fear) to qi disruption and disease. The objective is to present a trauma-informed model by which acupuncture may restore both immune homeostasis and psycho-emotional resilience. Method(s): We conducted a comprehensive integrative review synthesizing (1) biomedical studies on trauma, cytokine pathways, Treg/Th17 dynamics, and gut microbiota; (2) classical Chinese medical texts (e.g. Nei Jing, Ling Shu); and (3) clinical acupuncture literature, including protocols such as the Eight Extraordinary Vessels, NADA auricular approach, electroacupuncture, and adjunct herbal and dietary strategies. A trauma-informed lens was applied to map emotional states (e.g. ACEs, threat-response mapping) onto acupuncture intervention design. In addition, clinical practice experience and a detailed case study were reviewed to illustrate real-world applications of these integrative approaches in managing autoimmune and psycho-emotional conditions. Results : Converging evidence supports that acupuncture modulates immune function (reducing pro-inflammatory cytokines, rebalancing Treg/Th17, improving gut barrier integrity) while alleviating anxiety, depression, and trauma symptoms. Auricular and 8 extraordinary vessel-based protocols appear to reduce hyperarousal and support emotional regulation. Herbal and nutritional co-interventions further support mucosal health and regulatory immune signaling. Together these suggest a dual path of effect: emotional resolution enabling immune restoration, and immunological balance fostering psycho-emotional stability. Conclusions : Autoimmune conditions are not purely biochemical; unprocessed trauma and emotional dysregulation are core to their persistence. Acupuncture represents a unique therapy bridging body and psyche, modulating immune pathways while facilitating emotional healing. An integrative protocol combining acupuncture, herbal support, nutrition, and trauma-informed assessment offers a promising pathway toward sustained remission. Future clinical trials should concurrently measure immunologic biomarkers and psychosocial outcomes to validate this whole-person model in integrative medicine. P 01.21 Exploring pain and other clinical outcomes associated with acupuncture: analysis of clinical registry and case report data Dr. Kathleen Lumiere DAOM, MS 1,2 , Dr. Lisa J Taylor-Swanson PhD 3 1 SIEAM, Seattle, WA, United States 2 Bastyr University, Kenmore, WA, United States 3 University of Utah, Salt Lake City, UT, United States Purpose : Traditional East Asian Medicine (TEAM), particularly acupuncture, is widely practiced but often studied using methods that underrepresent its complexity. Pragmatic observational designs may better reflect real-world outcomes. This study, part of the international Pragmatic Data Analysis Project, evaluated patient outcomes for pain and global health using registry and case report data, reflecting the experience of patients and practitioners. Method(s): Data were drawn from the ACU-Track Clinical Registry (n=221) and Convergent Points case reports (n=12), covering January 2021–November 2024. Inclusion criteria required baseline pain scores and TEAM treatment. Patient-reported outcome measures included combined pain scores (NPRS, VAS, PROMIS Pain), PROMIS-10 global health, and PGIC. Analyses employed descriptive statistics, paired t-tests, and correlation analyses (Pearson’s and Spearman’s), with significance set at p<0.05. Minimally clinically important difference (MCID) thresholds were applied to assess clinical significance. Results : A total of 233 patients (62% female; mean age 53.5±16.7) were analyzed. Significant reductions in pain were observed: 29.3% at week 2 (p<0.0001), 44.1% at week 6 (p<0.0001), and 38.8% at 1 year (p=0.02). PROMIS global health scores improved 27.2% at week 6 (p<0.001) and 35.2% at month 9 (p<0.001). PGIC indicated ≥MCID improvement in 87.9% of patients. Moderate but significant correlations between PROMIS, PGIC, and pain outcomes underscore the reliability of patient-reported measures. Conclusions : Patients receiving acupuncture experienced meaningful and lasting reductions in pain and improvements in global health, with benefits persisting up to one year. The convergence of PROMIS, PGIC, and pain outcomes supports the reliability of observed changes. While limited by attrition and lack of controls, these findings demonstrate the value of pragmatic data for evaluating TEAM therapies and contribute to evidence-based, patient-centered approaches for chronic pain management. Future work will refine analytic methods. P 01.22LB Acupuncture Embedded in Primary Care: Real-World Outcomes from One of the Largest Reported FQHC-Based Acupuncture Cohorts Lili Qiao DAOM, MHA , Alan Li MPH North East Medical Services, San Francisco, CA, United States Purpose : Federally Qualified Health Centers (FQHCs) serve aging and medically underserved populations, yet real-world evidence on acupuncture embedded in primary care remains limited. This study evaluated pain and quality-of-life outcomes and identified predictors of response among patients receiving acupuncture through the Integrative Medicine (IM) department at Northeast Medical Services (NEMS), an FQHC serving predominantly low-income, publicly insured Asian and immigrant communities in Northern California, United States. Method(s): We conducted a retrospective observational analysis of de-identified longitudinal data internally abstracted from the Epic electronic health record system. The NEMS IM acupuncture program operates across 10 clinical sites (6 in San Francisco County, 2 in San Mateo County, and 2 in Santa Clara County). Patients receiving acupuncture for back pain (n=2,304), shoulder pain (n=1,418), or quality-of-life (QOL) concerns (n=2,568) between January 2024 and December 2025 were included. Analyses were restricted to patients with ≥6 visits for pain conditions or ≥2 QOL assessments. QOL was measured using a multilingual 10-item subset of the WHOQOL-BREF focused on physical and emotional domains; non-clinical domains were excluded. Mixed-effects models adjusted for baseline severity, demographics, comorbidities, visit frequency, and insurance payer. Final analyses were completed by December 31, 2025. Results : Significant improvements were observed across all conditions (p<.0001). Mean back pain scores decreased from 5.09 to 4.30, shoulder pain scores from 4.99 to 4.03, and QOL scores increased from 40.73 to 43.90. Higher baseline severity and greater visit frequency predicted stronger improvement. Insurance payer type was not associated with outcomes. Conclusions : In a large, multi-site FQHC acupuncture program embedded in primary care, acupuncture was associated with meaningful improvements in pain and quality of life. These findings support acupuncture as a scalable, preventive, and cost-conscious approach to chronic pain management in aging populations and justify broader implementation across safety-net health systems. P 01.23LB Acupuncture to Improve Outcomes in Sepsis Patient: The ACTIONS trial Sarah El Iskandarani MD 1 , Azziza Ahdoot MA 2 , Susan Q Li MS 3 , Raymond Baser MS 3 , Jun J Mao MD, MSCE 3 , Shaista Malik MD, PhD, MPH 4 , Alpesh Amin MD, MBA 4 , Gary Deng MD, PhD 4 1 Harvard Medical School, Cambridge, MA, United States 2 Lake Erie College of Osteopathic Medicine, Erie, PA, United States 3 Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center, New York, NY, United States 4 University of California Irvine, Irvine, CA, United States Purpose : Sepsis is a leading cause of death among hospitalized patients, costing around 270,000 lives and more than $24 billion per year in the US. Acupuncture has been shown to reduce mortality in sepsis animal models via neuroimmune modulatory mechanisms. However, human trials with sham control are lacking. We conducted a first-of-a-kind randomized sham-controlled acupuncture trial evaluating acupuncture as an early intervention to prevent clinical deterioration of sepsis patients. Method(s): Patients hospitalized in general wards showing signs of emerging sepsis were randomized to true or sham electroacupuncture at bilateral ST-36 acupoints once daily for up to 10 days, or until ICU admission, death, or discharge, whichever occurred first. Feasibility (recruitment, attrition, delivery of interventions, and data completion) and clinical outcomes were evaluated on day 30. Results : Forty patients were enrolled over 10 months, meeting the target recruitment rate. Two withdrew from the study. All patients provided clinical outcome endpoints. ICU admission rates within 30 days were 1/20 in the true acupuncture group and 5/20 in the sham acupuncture group (5% vs 25%; absolute risk reduction of 20%; (80%CI: -39%, -1%)), meeting a priori criteria deeming a larger trial being warranted. Mortality within 30 days was 2/20 vs 3/20. By day 30, hospital stays of all 20 patients totaled 198 days in the true acupuncture group (235 days in the sham group). Total number of ICU days were 6 in the acupuncture group and 33 in the sham group. No clinically significant adverse events were reported. Conclusions : Conducting a randomized sham-controlled acupuncture trial in a fast-paced inpatient sepsis clinical setting is feasible. A signal toward reduction of clinical deterioration to ICU care was observed. Number of ICU days was also reduced. Acupuncture holds promise as a safe, inexpensive, non-pharmacological, and scalable adjunctive therapy in the management of sepsis. Topic Area 02: Group Medical Visits P 02.01 Integrative Medicine Group Medical Visits for perimenopause and menopause. One solution for unmet needs Dr. Debra G Bell MD University of Washington, Osher Center for Integrative health, Washington, United States Purpose : Driven by rising awareness and unmet needs in conventional care models, there is increasing public demand for effective care during perimenopause and menopause, Scientific literature highlights this gap and calls for improved approaches to managing menopausal symptoms. Offering group medical visits (GMVs) incorporating integrative medicine principles, can be a valuable approach to care for navigating these hormonal transitions. The GMVs, combine medical guidance, peer support, and integrative therapies to improve health outcomes during this life stage. Method(s): A four-session GMV series using an integrative framework. Sessions were interactive, combining didactic teaching, experiential practices and peer support. Topics addressed included hormonal and non-hormonal treatments, nutrition, sleep, sexual health, and stress reduction. The Menopause Rating Scale was administered pre- and post-program. Qualitative data was collected to evaluate the experience. Results : Preliminary results are based on the first of 4 cohorts of participants. The initial analysis demonstrates high satisfaction with the group format, valuing both medical insights and peer support. Qualitative feedback highlighted appreciation for the integrative approach, the opportunity to share experiences, and practical strategies for navigating this time of transition. Attendance and engagement remained strong throughout the four sessions, reinforcing the relevance of this model. Quantitative data showed improvement in MRS score in the majority of the participants. Complete results of the four cohorts will be available for presentation. Conclusions : Group medical visits using an integrative medicine approach, offer a powerful, scalable model to support women through perimenopause and menopause. The approach addresses both the foundational components of health, and the complex symptoms experienced during this life stage. Participants gain not only symptom relief but also empowerment through education, shared experiences, and the cultivation of self-efficacy. As demand for menopause care grows this model offers a timely, effective response to a long-overlooked aspect of women’s health. P 02.02 “On a Pain Island”: Social Withdrawal and Quality of Life in People with Chronic Pain in Safety Net Primary Care Nina Djukic MS 1,2,3 , Ariana Thompson-Lastad PhD 1 , Jhia Jackson 1 , Wendy Hartogensis PhD, MPH 1 , Denise Ruvalcaba 1 , Evelyn Garcia 1 , Julia Wu 1 , Samuel Solis 1 , Pamela Swedlow 4 , Jesse Wennik 4 , Maria T Chao DrPH, MPA 1 1 Osher Center for Integrative Health, University of California, San Francisco (UCSF), San Francisco, CA, United States 2 School of Medicine, University of California – San Francisco (UCSF), San Francisco, CA, United States 3 oint Medical Program, University of California – Berkeley, University of California – San Francisco (UCSF), Berkeley, CA, United States 4 San Francisco Department of Public Health, San Francisco, CA, United States Purpose : Individuals living with chronic pain commonly experience stigma, including internalized stigma, an individual-level process of identity devaluation endorsed by >one-third of people with chronic pain. This includes social withdrawal, the avoidance of social contact and support. We explored pain-related stigma, social withdrawal, and quality of life among low-income, racially diverse individuals. Method(s): We piloted a randomized trial of group-based integrative medicine among primary care safety- net patients with ≥3 months of chronic pain. Here we report mixed methods data from baseline surveys and semi-structured interviews at 3-month follow-up. Surveys included demographics, Internalized Stigma in Chronic Pain scale, Social Support and Pain Questionnaire, and quality of life (NIH PROMIS measures of global physical and mental health). We calculated descriptive statistics and Pearson’s correlation coefficient (r), and used abductive flexible coding for interview data. Results : Participants (n=47) were all low-income, majority female (68%), mean age=57 (SD=15); 19% African American/Black, 55% Latine, 19% non-Latine White, and 4% ≥1 race. Participants had long-term, severe pain (mean pain duration=12.9y [SD=9.6], pain-intensity=7.7 [SD=1.5]). Average PROMIS Global Physical and Mental Health T-scores were >1 SD below U.S. population mean. Internalized stigma was negatively correlated with global physical health (r=-0.31, p=0.049), global mental health (r= -0.32, p=0.041), social functioning (r=-0.40, p=0.010), and pain-relevant social support (r=-0.43, p=0.0060). Social withdrawal was significantly negatively correlated with global physical health (r=-0.32, p=0.029) and social functioning (r=-0.33, p=0.026). Qualitatively, people described complex and bidirectional relationships between social withdrawal, social support, and quality of life. Conclusions : Social withdrawal as a form of internalized stigma profoundly impacts the quality of life of low-income people with chronic pain. Interventions, such as Integrative Group Medical Visits (IGMVs), may improve chronic pain management in the safety net setting by addressing social withdrawal/internalized stigma along with pain-related outcomes. P 02.03 A Novel Whole-Person Pain Care Group Medical Visit and Community Workshop Training Program: Assessment of Individual and Organizational Growth Dr Kate G Shafto MD 1,2 , Catherine Justice DPT 1 , Jadyn Knox BS 3 , Dr Susan Haddow MD 1 , Jacinta Rebang MPH 1 , Carrie Howard MA 4 , Lindsay Roberts MA 4 , Dr Arti Prasad MD 1 1 Hennepin Healthcare, Minneapolis, MN, United States 2 Bakken Center for Spirituality and Healing, Minneaoplis, MN, United States 3 Hennepin Healthcare Research Institute, Minneapolis, MN, United States 4 Stratis Health, Bloomington, MN, United States Purpose : Chronic pain is highly prevalent, and Integrative Group Medical Visits (IGMVs) are an evidence-based model to improve access to non-pharmacological, whole-person pain care. Community workshop models further expand access. Literature on the impact of training in these models is limited. The Center for Teaching, Learning, and Coordination–Heals on Wheels (TLC-HoW) training program for IGMVs and community workshop implementation assessed participants’ individual and organizational-level growth. Method(s): TLC-HoW partnered with clinical teams from two Minnesota healthcare organizations who participated in a 10-week/20-hour virtual training on whole-person, non-pharmacological pain care IGMV and community workshop implementation. Each clinic conducted two community-based workshops and an 8-week IGMV series. Individual growth was assessed using a 7-item Knowledge (range 0-6), Skills (range 0-9), and Attitudes (range 0-6) (KSA) questionnaire, administered pre-, mid-, and post-training, and 6-month follow-up. Organizational-level change was assessed through structured qualitative interviews with a 4-category maturity matrix (range 1-5) before and after program implementation. Results : Average KSA scores (n=7) improved in Knowledge (+3.29), Skills (+3.86) and Attitudes (+0.57) from baseline to post-training with sustained improvement at 6-months. Average maturity matrix baseline scores for Foundational Capacity (3.38), Internal Processes (2.75), External Partnerships (2.75), and Sustainability (2.50) improved to 4.38 (+1.00), 4.08 (+1.33), 5.00 (+2.25), and 3.33 (+0.83), respectively. One clinic, in a larger health system, demonstrated substantial growth in External Partnerships, moving from 1.50 to 5.00 (+3.50), while Sustainability improved from 1.67 to 2.33 (+0.66). In the other, a smaller private practice, Sustainability moved from 3.33 to 4.33 (+1.0), with a 4.46 average across all categories. Conclusions : The TLC-HoW program successfully trained clinical teams and advanced organizational capacity for integrative pain care, community engagement, and IGMV implementation and sustainability. Private clinics may have more flexibility in implementation and sustainability than larger organizations. The TLC-HoW program serves as a successful, scalable model for advancing holistic pain care. P 02.04 Integrative Group Medical Visit with Chair Yoga for Patients with Chronic Health Conditions Paula Paula Gardiner 1 , Dr Richa Gawande Phd 1 , Julia Naganuma-Carreras 2 1 Cambridge Health Alliance, Cambridge, MA, United States 2 Harvard School of Public Health, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : Little is known about the feasibility of implementing mind-body practices into an insurance-reimbursable Group Medical Visit (MY-GMV) to support at a community health center. We report on preliminary implementation feasibility, acceptability, and patient ideas for shaping the intervention. Method(s): The Mandela Yoga Project (MYP) developed a peer-led yoga program. We conducted two pilots of a 6-week Mandela Yoga (MY) intervention integrated into a primary care group medical visit (GMV). Adult participants with chronic conditions were recruited via flyer, physician visits and messages. Feasibility, acceptability and feedback were assessed through attendance, surveys, and focus groups. Results : Groups took place between Fall 2024 and Fall 2025. Adults (n=29) average 53 years old (SD=13), spoke English (n=21), Portuguese (n=6), and Spanish (n=3); half (55%) were POC. Among the 29, 21 (72%) attended >=3 sessions; insurance was billed for all visits; Of 29, 15/15 (100%) respondents would recommend a friend/family. Focus group themes included reduced pain/stress, value of breathwork, suggestions for accessibility, and benefits of the group including reducing isolation and hearing other people’s perspective and chronic pain. Participants also found benefit with having a clinician in the group who could provide medical care as well. Conclusions : Yoga combined with a group medical visit was acceptable and feasible for primary care patients with chronic conditions. Future improvements include language access and recruitment, and cost effectiveness. Culturally resonant, community-rooted programs like MY-GMV could be a low-cost intervention in health centers. P 02.05LB Initiation of Community Workshops and Group Medical Visits for Chronic Pain at Three Clinical Sites Across Minnesota Jadyn Knox MA 1 , Catherine Justice DPT, C-IAYT, FAIMH 2 , Katherine Shafto MD, ABOIM 2,3 , Susan Haddow MD, IFMCP, ABOIM 2 , Jacinta Rebang MPH, PMP 2 , Arti Prasad MD, FACP 2 1 Hennepin Healthcare Research Institute, Minneapolis, MN, United States 2 Hennepin Healthcare Systems, Inc., Minneapolis, MN, United States 3 University of Minnesota - Bakken Center for Spirituality and Healing, Minneapolis, MN, United States Purpose : Chronic pain is prevalent, while access to non-pharmacological, whole-person pain care is limited. Integrative approaches to pain management, delivered in a Group Medical Visit (GMV) model and community outreach improves access to whole-person care. However, utilization of these models is limited. The Center for Teaching, Learning, and Coordination–Heals on Wheels (TLC-HoW) program assessed implementation and delivery of GMVs and community workshops within three Minnesota regions. Method(s): After undergoing training in the TLC-HoW model, clinical teams from three Minnesota healthcare organizations designed/developed, implemented, and facilitated pain-focused 8-visit GMV series and community workshops. Outcomes include: workshop and GMV attendance and GMV participant demographics, PROMIS-29 (pre- and post- GMV series), and a 1-item Global Impression of Change survey (post-GMV series), asking participants to reflect on their changes in well-being, with responses ranging from “very much worse” (-3) to “very much improved” (3). Results : A total of 8 Community workshops reached 202 individuals with information and experiences of various integrative health practices, and 5 GMV series enrolled 58 participants. Across sites, 72.4% of participants attended ≥ 5 visits and 29.3% of participants attended all 8 visits. Participants identified as Black (17.2%), White (69.0%), Multiple Races (3.4%), Unknown (10.3%), and 3.4% identified as Hispanic. Participants identified as women (69.0%) and men (31.0%). Participant ages ranged from 26 to 82 across two clinical sites, with one serving 18- to 24-year-olds. Average baseline PROMIS-29 Physical and Mental Health Summary Scores were 37.4 and 39.6, which improved to 37.8 (+0.4) and 41.0 (+1.4), respectively. Participants' average rating of Global Impression of Change was +1.3, suggesting improved well-being after GMV participation. Conclusions : The TLC-HoW program successfully initiated pain-focused GMV series and community workshops. Improvements in well-being and high attendance across varied regions and ages suggest the feasibility and utility of this model. Topic Area 03: Health Coaching P 03.01 Expanding the Integrative Health Coaching Model: Coach Reflections on Delivering Care to Adults with Intellectual and Developmental Disabilities Dr. Alyssa M Smith PhD 1 , Dr. Patricia Prelock PhD 1 , Sarah Pettineo BS 2 , Sydney Daniels BS 2 , Stasha Medeiros MS 2 , Dr. Karen Westervelt PhD 2 1 University of Arizona, Tucson, AZ, United States 2 University of Vermont, Burlington, VT, United States Purpose : Adults with intellectual and developmental disabilities (IDD) experience significant health disparities and limited access to tailored wellness interventions. Integrative Health and Wellness Coaching (IHWC) offers a promising, person-centered model for supporting health behavior change, yet few studies address its adaptation for neurodiverse populations. This study explored how trained coaches delivering IHWC to adults with IDD perceived, adapted, and reflected on the coaching process to enhance accessibility, accessibility, and inclusivity. Method(s): Four coaches participated in a six-week virtual IHWC program for adults with IDD. Following program completion coaches engaged in focus groups or interviews exploring preparedness, challenges, and recommendations for practice. Transcripts were analyzed using reflexive thematic analysis emphasizing experiential learning and adaptation processes. Themes were collaboratively developed to capture how coaches balanced intervention fidelity with individualized support and developed strategies for disability-affirming coaching implementation. Results : Four themes were identified: (1) evolving confidence and preparedness through direct experience, (2) adapting communication and pacing to diverse needs, (3) balancing fidelity with flexible, client-centered modifications, and (4) reflection and growth through experiential learning. Coaches identified the need for structured disability training, peer supervision, and reflective debriefing to strengthen competency. Adaptations included visual aids, simplified language, and explicit goal scaffolding to enhance participant engagement and autonomy in the IHWC process. Conclusions : Delivering IHWC to individuals with IDD requires adaptive communication, reflective practice, and flexible fidelity to core coaching principles while customizing approaches for accessibility. Findings underscore the need for neurodiversity-affirming training and supervision within the integrative health workforce. Incorporating experiential mentorship and co-learning models may advance health equity by preparing coaches to effectively engage neurodiverse populations and expand access to holistic, person-centered wellness interventions across integrative health settings. P 03.02 Relationship Between Exercise Counseling and Mental Health Outcomes in Asian Americans With Chronic Pain: A Retrospective Cohort Study Jason Guo BS , Nathan Vu Pham BS, Dr Weijun Zhang DrPH David Geffen School of Medicine at University of California Los Angeles, Los Angeles, California, United States Purpose : Chronic pain is often comorbid with mental health conditions such as depression, anxiety, suicidal ideation, and sleep disturbance, contributing to poor quality of life. Among Asian Americans, mental health challenges are compounded by cultural stigma, language barriers, and low engagement with psychiatric services. Exercise counseling may offer a culturally acceptable, non-pharmacologic adjunct for improving mental health. This study examines whether exercise counseling is associated with improved mental health outcomes in Asian American adults with chronic pain. Method(s): We conducted a retrospective cohort study using TriNetx electronic health records from 2010-2024. Asian American patients aged 18 and over with a diagnosis of chronic pain were included. Two cohorts were identified: (1) patients who received documented exercise counseling at least a month after diagnosis, and (2) propensity score-matched controls without exercise counseling, matched 1:1 on age and sex. Mental health outcomes including anxiety, suicidal ideation, mild/moderate depression, severe depression, and insomnia were compared using risk ratios. Results : A total of 4,210 patients were included (2,105 in each cohort). Exercise counseling was associated with 50% reduced risk of suicidal ideation (RR=0.50, 95% CI: 0.27–0.95, p = 0.031), 32% reduced risk of mild/moderate depression (RR=0.68, 95% CI: 0.50–0.93, p = 0.014), 50% reduced risk of severe depression (RR=0.50, 95% CI: 0.28–0.90, p = 0.017), and 25% reduced risk of insomnia (RR=0.75, 95% CI: 0.58–0.98, p = 0.033). No significant association was observed for anxiety. Conclusions : Exercise counseling is associated with significantly improved mental health outcomes, particularly lower rates of suicidal ideation, depression, and insomnia in Asian American adults with chronic pain. These findings highlight the potential role of culturally sensitive, non-pharmacologic interventions (exercise counseling) as an adjuvant to improve mental health outcomes in patients with chronic pain. P 03.03 Impact of Health and Wellness Coaching on COPD-related hospitalizations: Findings of a Large Database Study Dr Ekaterina Anderson PhD 1,2 , Dr Renda Wiener MD, MPH 3,4,5 , Alexandre Vilela Braga MS 1 , Makayla Dones BA 1 , Dr Seppo Rinne MD, PhD 1,6 , Dr Marla Clayman PhD, MPH 1,2 , Dr Justeen Hyde PhD 1,4 , Dr Christian Helfrich PhD, MPH 7 , Dr Barbara Bokhour PhD 1,2 , Dr Steven Zeliadt PhD, MPH 8,7 1 VA Center for Health Optimization & Implementation Research (CHOIR), Bedford, MA, United States 2 University of Massachusetts Chan Medical School, Worcester, MA, United States 3 VA Center for Health Optimization & Implementation Research (CHOIR), Boston, MA, United States 4 Boston University Chobanian & Avedisian School of Medicine, Boston, MA, United States 5 Boston University Pulmonary Center, Boston, MA, United States 6 Dartmouth Hitchcock Medical Center, Lebanon, NH, United States 7 University of Washington, Seattle, WA, United States 8 VA Center for Veteran-Centered and Value-Driven Care, Seattle, WA, United States Purpose : Health & Wellness Coaching (‘coaching’) is a promising person-centered intervention wherein a trained professional supports the setting and achieving of health and well-being goals in line with the client’s values and priorities. This practice is well-positioned to empower and engage patients to manage their health, especially chronic conditions. We sought to assess the relationship between coaching and hospitalizations for acute exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) among patients receiving care at Veterans Health Administration (VA) facilities. Method(s): Using administrative data, we built a national cohort of patients with COPD receiving VA care during 2021-2023. Retained in this analysis are those with >=2 coaching sessions during the study period (n=8,632) and those with none (340,452); we excluded single-session users. A Cox proportional hazard regression model, which included demographic and clinical covariates, was constructed to compare time to hospitalization, starting from last coaching session for coaching users and adjusted index date for non-users (tracked through 2024). Results : 11.1% of coaching users and 8.6% of non-users experienced a hospitalization during the follow-up period. At 1 year, 95.3% of non-users remained hospitalization-free (95% CI: 95.29%-95.44%), compared to 93.4% of users (95% CI: 92.92%-93.99%). In adjusted analyses, coaching users still had a higher hazard of hospitalization than non-users (hazard ratio: 1.28; p<0.001). Conclusions : Unlike prior studies, coaching did not lead to a reduced hazard of being hospitalized for COPD-related exacerbations among VA patients. Notably, however, prior interventions were delivered by clinicians and/or incorporated a prominent health education component, which differs from VA’s coaching and may explain the divergence in findings. Alternatively, our analyses may have insufficiently accounted for the more complex health needs of coaching users compared to non-users (identified in our prior work). Our findings highlight the importance of terminological clarity and the need to establish whether and how different forms of coaching affect clinical outcomes. P 03.04 Models for Integrating Health and Wellness Coaches into Healthcare Teams to Optimize Patient Care Dr. Sophia M. Bartels 1 , Dr. Ekaterina Anderson 1 , Dr. Kelly Dvorin 1 , Dr. Kimberlee Flike 1,2 , Matthew B. Martel 1 , Zenith Rai 1 , Dr. Justeen K. Hyde 1,3 1 Center for Health Optimization and Implementation Research, Bedford VA, Bedford, MA, United States 2 Solomont School of Nursing, UMass Lowell, Lowell, MA, United States 3 General Internal Medicine, Boston University, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : Health and Wellness Coaching is designed to help patients identify and pursue health and well-being goals aligned with what matters most to them. With team-based approaches being optimal for patient and healthcare system impact, there is growing need to identify determinants of and models for effective collaboration between coaches and other healthcare professionals. We highlight findings from a qualitative study examining models and determinants of coach integration into healthcare teams. Method(s): As part of a quality improvement project evaluating Health and Wellness coaching implementation within the Veterans Health Administration (VHA), we identified five VHA sites whose coaches reported high healthcare team integration and work satisfaction. Within these sites, we conducted interviews with 21 coaches and 7 coach supervisors. Transcripts were coded in Atlas.ti and analyzed using thematic analysis. Results : We identified six models of coach-healthcare team collaboration, listed from least to most integrated: 1) minimal coach-provider interaction (i.e., providers learning about coaching from Veterans, if at all); 2) coaches co-signing notes and receiving/providing warm handoffs; 3) coaches joining team huddles; 4) integration of coaching into diagnosis-based intervention teams; 5) leading initiatives together (e.g., Whole Health classes); and 6) fully coordinated care. The most common collaboration determinant was coaches’ co-location within clinics. Barriers included lack of provider buy-in for coaching, providers’ lack of time for collaboration with coaches or learning about coaching, and discordance between the patient-driven nature of coaching and directive care. Facilitators included demonstrating the value of coaching, leadership buy-in for coaching, coach supervisor support for integration, relationship-building with providers, and coaching certification, which increased perceptions of coach credibility. Conclusions : These findings illustrate different models for collaboration between coaches and healthcare teams and highlight factors that support and hinder collaboration. Greater integration of coaches in care teams at scale has the potential to improve care coordination and patients’ overall health and well-being. P 03.05 Navigating the Health and Wellness Coaching Process: Strong Practices from the VA Healthcare System Dr Ekaterina Anderson PhD 1,2 , Dr Sophia Bartels PhD, MSPH 1 , Dr Kimberlee Flike PhD, MSN, RN 3,1 , Dr Kelly Dvorin PsyD 1 , Zenith Rai MPH 1 , Matthew Martel BS 1 , Dr Justeen Hyde PhD 1,4 1 VA Center for Health Optimization & Implementation Research (CHOIR), Bedford, MA, United States 2 University of Massachusetts Chan Medical School, Worcester, MA, United States 3 University of Massachusetts Lowell, Lowell, MA, United States 4 Boston University Chobanian and Avedisian School of Medicine, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : Health & wellness coaching is a person-centered, holistic intervention that empowers clients to set and achieve personally meaningful health and well-being goals. How Coaches assist clients in successfully navigating the coaching process in practice is still insufficiently understood. The Veterans Health Administration (VA) has built a robust coaching workforce as part of its Whole Health system of care. We sought to understand techniques that VA’s Coaches use to steer veterans through the phases of coaching. Method(s): As part of a quality improvement mixed-methods evaluation, we conducted interviews with Coaches (n=21) and Coach supervisors (n=7) across 5 VA sites. Interview transcripts were coded in a qualitative analysis software and analyzed using content analysis. Results : VA’s Coaches have a versatile arsenal of techniques for traversing the coaching phases. Viewing the early self-assessment stage as the cornerstone of the coaching process, Coaches prioritize the crafting of trusting relationships and tailor their approach to each client’s readiness for change. When facilitating goal setting, Coaches create opportunities for confidence-building via small, achievable steps; they also ensure the fit of goals to each veteran’s life context. To help veterans stay focused, motivated, and empowered to address barriers, Coaches utilize positive reinforcement, non-judgmental curiosity, and reflective listening. Use of these techniques is influenced by Coaches’ experience, personality, and organizational context (e.g., availability of high-quality supervision, coaching duration restrictions). Conclusions : VA’s Coaches draw on a vast set of tools to help veterans move through the coaching process. There are opportunities to scale up these strong practices in VA and other health systems alike. Our findings highlight the great deal of contextual awareness, adaptability, and relational acumen that coaching requires, which speaks to the importance of continued professional development and robust supervision. Further, organizational structures and policies ought to allow Coaches the flexibility needed to effectively support veterans’ pursuit of goals. P 03.06 A Whole Health Approach to Physical Activity Promotion for People with Arthritis Dr Karen Westervelt PT, ATC, PhD, NBC-HWC 1,2 , Mr Jake Brown BS 1,2 , Dr Gail L Rose PhD 1,2 1 University of Vermont, Burlington, VT, United States 2 Osher Center for Integrative Health at UVM, Burlington, VT, United States Purpose : Arthritis affects 1 in 5 US adults - about 54 million people - and is the leading cause of work disability. Despite guidelines recommending 150 minutes of moderate aerobic exercise and two resistance sessions weekly, fewer than half of adults with arthritis meet these recommendations. To promote physical activity, the Arthritis Foundation offers the Walk With Ease (AFWWE) 6-week, self-directed walking program. At University of Vermont (UVM), we piloted a version combining AFWWE with Integrative Health and Wellness Coaching (IHWC), an evidence-based approach that supports sustained behavior change through collaborative, goal-oriented relationships. Students from our IHWC accredited training program, supervised by board-certified faculty, provide coaching. A previous evaluation of this IHWC model showed improved behavior change that was maintained for 12 weeks. Now, we are scaling this model to a community-level program for people with a common chronic condition. Our purpose is to share feasibility and preliminary walking outcomes. Method(s): Participants received the AFWWE guidebook and motivational emails. At registration, they could opt to add weekly remote student coaching. Post-program and 6-week follow-up surveys obtained demographics, arthritis status, disability, exercise behaviors, and satisfaction. Results : Fifty-eight individuals registered for UVM WWE (mean age 63; 88% female); 70% reported arthritis, 23% a disability. Thirty-nine began the program; 31 participants (79%) chose coaching. Seventeen completed the post-program survey. Participants reported a median of 4 exercise days/ week for 30–39 minutes/session, and 82% were confident they would continue being active. All expressed satisfaction with coaching, citing improved motivation and mood. At 6-week follow-up (n = 18), exercise levels were largely maintained and participants reported secondary benefits like improved diet and well-being. Conclusions : This pilot demonstrates the feasibility of a student-coaching model embedded in an evidence-based walking program. It supports physical activity adherence and experiential learning and can be scaled to broader populations with chronic illness. P 03.07 Sustained Gains from Health and Wellness Coaching: A Rapid Systematic Review Katherine Smith MPH, ACC, NBC-HWC1 1 , Barbara Iachini MA, AFMC, NBC-HWC 1 , Elizabeth Ahmann ScD, RN, PCC, NBC-HWC 1 , Micah Saviet LCSW-C, NBC-HWC 2 , Dr Lisa A Conboy MA MS ScD 3 , Ron DeMartin 1 1 Notre Dame of Maryland University School of Integrative Health, Baltimore, MD, United States 2 Springer Institute, Cheverly, MD, United States 3 Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : Health and Wellness Coaching (HWC) is an emerging profession supporting health behavior change. Research suggests HWC effectively facilitates behavior change across a variety of conditions and populations. Of interest is whether improvements last 6 months or longer once the coaching intervention ends, a time period defined as 'maintenance.' Method(s): Guided by the Cochrane Interim Guidance for rapid systematic reviews, our rapid review was conducted across a single database, the Appendix A from the Sforzo et al Compendium of the health and wellness coaching literature (2017), and the Sforzo et al Addendum to the Compendium (2019), which included peer-reviewed, database articles meeting a modified version of health and wellness coaching as defined by Wolever et al (2013). Studies eligible for our rapid review included any study that explored client-related outcomes including a follow-up data collection (quantitative) subsequent to the immediate or first post-intervention data collection. Results : From an initial pool of 231 studies, 44 met the inclusion criteria. Of those, 16 did not meet criteria for analysis, leaving 28 studies retained for analysis. In 25 of the 28 studies examined, some gains made as a result of the coaching intervention were sustained (whether partially or fully), or improved over time. 16 of the 28 studies explored sustained gains at 6 months or more. Of those 14 studies (87.5%) demonstrated some gains that were partially sustained, fully sustained, or even improved over the follow-up period. Conclusions : HWC may offer an approach that can contribute to long-term sustainable behavior change. This review demonstrates that in many instances studies using health and wellness coaching interventions show sustained effects, and in some cases improvements, 6 months or longer after the end of the intervention. This held true across physiological, behavioral, psychological and health risk assessment variables. P 03.08 Coaching for Clinicians Droma Balgobin BSN 1 , Marci Resnicoff MSN 2 1 NYU langone NYU Langone Orthopedic Hospital, New York, NY, United States 2 NYU Langone hospital, new york, ny, United States Purpose : The purpose of implimeting Coaching for Clinicans was to evaluate the impact of a pilot coaching program on reducing burnout, improving job satisfaction, and promoting work-life balance among PACU nurses. Grounded in Imogene King’s Theory of Goal Attainment, the program aimed to support clinician well-being through structured coaching sessions, with outcomes measured using the Whole Person Health Index and Goal Attainment Scale to guide future program development and sustainability. Method(s ): An educational session on coaching was delivered to 12 PACU nurses, followed by a survey assessing comprehension and interest. Seventy-five percent expressed interest, and 6 nurses were randomly selected for a pilot program. Each participated in four 30-minute individual coaching sessions over eight weeks. Pre- and post-intervention assessments used the Whole Person Health Index (WPHI) and Goal Attainment Scale (GAS) to measure well-being and goal progress. Feedback was collected to evaluate satisfaction and inform future program development. Results : Post-intervention results showed a 57% increase in average Whole Person Health Index (WPHI) scores, improving from 2.6 to 4.1, indicating enhanced physical, emotional, social, and spiritual well-being. Goal Attainment Scale (GAS) scores reflected progress in achieving personal goals. Participant feedback indicated high satisfaction, a desire for continued coaching, and suggestions for longer sessions. The pilot demonstrated that coaching effectively reduces burnout, enhances job satisfaction, and supports work-life balance among PACU nurses, with recommendations for program expansion. Conclusions : The pilot program Coaching for Clinicans, demonstrated significant improvements in clinician well-being, goal attainment, and job satisfaction, supporting its effectiveness in reducing burnout among PACU nurses. Grounded in Imogene King’s Theory of Goal Attainment, coaching provided a structured approach to achieving personal and professional goals. Positive outcomes and participant feedback highlight the value of coaching in healthcare settings. Future recommendations include expanding the program to group formats, increasing session duration, and offering flexible scheduling to enhance sustainability. Topic Area 04: Integrative Medicine Education P 04.01 Identifying Gaps in Integrative Medicine Education: A Review of a Midwestern Public Medical School’s Preclinical Curriculum Savannah N Rios B.S., Dr. Vincent Minichiello M.D. University of Wisconsin-Madison School of Medicine and Public Health, Madison, WI, United States Purpose : Integrative medicine is essential for delivering comprehensive and patient-centered care. University of Wisconsin School of Medicine and Public Health (UWSMPH) preclinical curriculum currently lacks formal integration of integrative medicine objectives. This limited exposure may hinder medical students’ knowledge, attitudes, and perceptions of integrative care. This study aims to systematically evaluate the UWSMPH preclinical curriculum, identify content gaps related to integrative medicine, and propose evidence-based recommendations to enhance integrative health education within medical student training. Method(s): A systematic review of the UWSMPH curriculum repository was conducted to identify preclinical lecture materials addressing integrative health topics during the 2024-2025 academic year. Materials were categorized according to corresponding learning objectives, UWSMPH core competencies, and depth of integrative content. Using a framework adapted from the Integrative Medicine Fellowship Core Competencies, while also aligning with UWSMPH core competencies, the analysis identified curricular gaps and opportunities to strengthen integration of foundational principles of integrative medicine. Results : Among 335 preclinical lectures containing integrative health-related terms, 8 (2.4%) focused primarily on integrative medicine, while 327 (97.6%) referenced secondary or peripheral topics such as chronic disease. Major content gaps were identified in whole-person health, mindfulness, meditation, spirituality, traditional and Indigenous healing practices, botanical medicine, and group medical visits. Overall, integrative health content was limited in depth and distribution across the preclinical curriculum, indicating a need for greater alignment with core integrative medicine competencies. Conclusions : This review reveals significant underrepresentation of integrative medicine within the UWSMPH preclinical curriculum. Addressing these deficiencies through intentional integration of core competencies could enhance students’ ability to deliver holistic, patient-centered care and foster greater engagement with integrative medicine principles. Future curriculum development should prioritize sustained, longitudinal incorporation of integrative health topics—particularly those identified as major content gaps. Further studies may assess how enhanced integrative health education influences medical students’ knowledge, attitudes, and clinical application of whole-person care principles. P 04.02 Evidence-informed practice as a bioethical imperative: a novel training program for complementary and integrative healthcare providers Margaret D Whitley 1 , Ian D Coulter 1 , Joshua Z Goldenberg 2 , Daryl Nault 3 , Kieran Cooley 4,5,6 , Katherine A Pohlman 7 1 RAND, Santa Monica, CA, United States 2 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 3 Notre Dame of Maryland University, School of Integrative Health, Baltimore, MD, United States 4 Canadian College of Naturopathic Medicine, Toronto, ON, Canada 5 University of Toronto, Toronto, ON, Canada 6 Southern Cross University, Lismore, New South Wales, Australia 7 Parker University, Dallas, TX, United States Purpose : Complementary and integrative healthcare (CIH) education includes both bioethics and evidence-informed practice (EIP), yet these domains are rarely taught together. Practicing EIP aligns with bioethical principles of beneficence, non-maleficence, veracity, autonomy, fidelity, and justice. Competence in EIP better enables clinicians to deliver safe, effective, and bioethically-sound care. This project will develop a free, online training program integrating bioethics and EIP to strengthen CIH practitioners’ research literacy. Method(s): We will design ten virtual modules linking bioethics and EIP, each with defined learning objectives. A Bioethics and EIP working group, comprising CIH institution faculty who are members of RAND REACH, will refine content and ensure relevance to CIH practice. The curriculum will emphasize evaluating evidence across scientific methods and clinically applying evidence. The program will be distributed through CIH institutions’ to promote widespread adoption. Results : This 12-month project, funded 9/2025, has established a working group of scholars from CIH institutions across North America. Topics will include balancing patient autonomy with evidence-based recommendations and evaluating traditional therapies with limited published evidence. To create the modules, we are collaborating with methods and bioethics instructors and with leaders of Process of Integrating Evidence-a multi-institution collaboration led by Parker University. Our program will be freely available to CIH institutions, with CE accreditation through professional bodies to facilitate integration into curricula and re-licensure pathways. Conclusions : CIH professions prioritize EIP, but framing EIP as a bioethical imperative is a novel approach. This virtual program will better equip practitioners to navigate ethical complexities inherent in evidence application and patient-centered care. As CIH research expands, the need for ethically grounded, evidence-informed training grows. Applying EIP in clinical practice presents unique challenges, more so now with the introduction of artificial intelligence tools. By integrating bioethics and EIP, this initiative will strengthen the ethical foundation of CIH practice. P 04.03 Building an International Exchange Platform for Medical Students Interested in Integrative Medicine Weijun Zhang DrPH 1 , Edward Hui MD 1 , Haoyue Li PhD 2 , Anna Yang MS 1 , Traci Wells PhD 1 , Jing Shu MD, PhD 3 , Dong Shang MD, PhD 4 , Min Li MD, PhD 5 , Xin Chen PhD 6 , Hung-Rong Yen MD, PhD 7 , Xiaqiu Wu PhD 8 , Andrew Jang MPH 9 , Dhave Setabutr MD 10 , Pravit Akarasereenont MD, PhD 11 , Ka-Kit Hui MD 1 1 UCLA, Los Angeles, CA, United States 2 China Academy of Chinese Medical Sciences, Beijing, Beijing, China 3 Shanghai University of Traditional Chinese Medicine, Shanghai, Shanghai, China 4 Dalian Medical University, Dalian, Liaoning, China 5 Hong Kong Baptist University, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, Hong Kong 6 University of Macau, Macau, Macau, China 7 China Medical University, Taichung, Taiwan, Taiwan 8 Zhejiang Chinese Medical University, Hangzhou, Zhejaing, China 9 Jaseng Medical Academy, Seoul, Seoul, Korea, Republic of 10 Thammasat University, Bangkok, Bangkok, Thailand 11 Mahidol University, Bangkok, Bangkok, Thailand Purpose : As integrative medicine (IM) gains global recognition, medical education remains largely siloed, limiting opportunities for cross-cultural learning and collaboration. The initiative aims to cultivate future physician-leaders capable of bridging biomedical and traditional health paradigms through shared education, research, and community engagement. Preliminary feedback from student focus groups indicates strong interest in experiential and culturally grounded IM learning opportunities. Conclusions : This international exchange platform represents a scalable model for global IM education. By linking universities with shared missions and diverse medical systems, it fosters cross-cultural competence, collaborative scholarship, and a transnational community of early-career physicians committed to advancing integrative, person-centered care. P 04.04 Why Physicians Avoid Taking Complementary Alternative Medicine Course Content When Earning Continuing Medical Education Credits Dr. MARK A PETERSON Ed.D. St. Mary's University of Minnesota, Minneapolis, MN, United States Purpose : Purpose of this qualitative grounded theory study was to reveal key reasons why medical doctors chose specific CME courses to earn their required 75 credits of continuing education units (CEU’s). The study aimed to reveal themes on why Internal Medicine physicians had diverse attitudes toward CAM topics, and how that provided valuable insights into why physicians chose the CME they earned credits for, and why they did not choose CAM/IM topics for CME credit. Method(s): Methods of this Qualitative Grounded Theory study used Braun & Clarke's 6-phase Thematic Analysis. 12 Zoom meeting interviews with Internal Medicine physicians licensed in Minnesota. Data analyzed utilizing NVivo-12 software. Respondents were asked ten open-ended questions in a semi-structured interview that took approximately 30 - 45 minutes. Each interview was audio and video recorded by Zoom meeting technology. Interviews were transcribed in both digital and hard-copy paper format using Otter.ai for coding later in the research process. Results : Results found 8 distinct themes: 1) Physicians had diverse attitudes towards CAM. 2) Need for more scientific evidence supporting CAM effectiveness. 3) CAM needs to be integrated into existing patient-care models. 4) MDs experience multiple pragmatic constraints when choosing CME. 5) Individualized needs and specific practice requirements. 6) CAM needs to be integrated into medical schools. 7) Reputations of CME providers are important selection factors. 8) Technology/electronic patient records systems need to integrate CAM. Conclusions : Study conclusions revealed that physicians avoid CAM course topics because CAM is not currently integrated into existing CME delivery structures, electronic medical records systems or patient care models. All respondents expressed pragmatic motivations for their current CME choices, individualized needs specific to their medical practice requirements, lack of familiarization with CAM in medical school, and the need for CAM subject matter experts to be included into the CME offerings and venues they do attend. P 04.05 Design and Implementation of an Integrative Internal Medicine Teaching Clinic at a FQHC: Advancing the Learning and Practice of Whole Person Care Arlene Betancourt MD, Dip ABOIM Texas Health Presbyterian Hospital, Dallas, TX, United States. UT Southwestern, Dallas, TX, United States Purpose : To describe the design and implementation of an integrative internal medicine teaching clinic within a Federally Qualified Health Center. Method(s): The competency-based goals and objectives for the clinic were designed to promote interprofessional collaboration and whole-person care. Educational resources include the Johns Hopkins Internal Medicine Ambulatory Care Curriculum and experiential learning on integrative medicine. Select residents and faculty participate in the Integrative Medicine in Residency program. Each resident sees patients at the continuity clinic an average of one half-day per week, supervised by faculty with expertise in integrative, internal, and family medicine, medicine-psychiatry, and nephrology. Additionally, residents complete a dedicated ambulatory month, incorporating experiences with psychiatry, 340B pharmacy, nutrition, optometry, community health workers, mammography and bone densitometry. The FQHC provides dental, women’s health, and pediatric services, and offers a robust language program, including interpreters and a language line to support patients with limited English proficiency. Results : The clinic opened in November 2025 through collaboration between hospital, GME, and FQHC leadership. Key milestones included recruitment of physician leaders, faculty identification and development, and workflow design. The clinic launched with 18 residents and 6 faculty members. Extended visits, EMR templates, and quality metrics support whole-person care. PHQ-9, GAD-7 and SDOH screenings are performed at every visit. In the first month, residents saw 136 patients across 13 sessions, primarily Hispanic, Black, and refugee populations speaking 68 languages. Operational outcomes included an 83% show rate (vs. 72% for other departments) and 87 internal referrals. Patient satisfaction was 89%, with residents and faculty reporting improved ability to deliver quality care. Conclusions : This initiative demonstrates the feasibility of embedding integrative internal medicine teaching clinics into FQHCs - offering a replicable and scalable model for advancing integrative health equity, enhancing resident competency in culturally responsive care, and preparing physicians to deliver whole person care to underserved populations. P 04.06 Comparing Interactive vs Traditional Teaching Formats for Integrative Medicine Topics in a Family Medicine Residency at a Federally Qualified Health Center Dr. Ariadna Mercouffer MD Institute for Family Health, New York, FL, United States Purpose : Integrative Medicine (IM) training is increasingly recognized as essential in Family Medicine practice due to its emphasis on patient-centered care and the use of holistic options that expand therapeutic choices. This study aimed to compare knowledge gain between an interactive Kahoot-based session and a traditional slide-based lecture on IM topics. Secondary objectives were to assess resident confidence, perceived usefulness, and engagement across teaching methods to inform optimal IM education strategies within a time-limited residency curriculum. Method(s): Twenty residents and faculty completed one pre-intervention survey and two post-session surveys following two 15-minute IM sessions: an interactive Kahoot session on probiotics and fiber and a traditional lecture on common dietary supplements. Surveys assessed confidence, understanding, perceived usefulness, and engagement using 5-point Likert scales. Paired t-tests compared pre/post lecture changes and between-session differences. Quantitative data were analyzed for mean change, statistical significance, and effect size. Results : The traditional lecture significantly improved in comfort (t = -3.33, p = .002, d = 1.07) and understanding (t = -2.89, p = .007, d = 0.93), while perceived usefulness remained unchanged. The interactive Kahoot session showed no significant change in pre/post scores (p > .30, |d| < 0.35). Engagement and enjoyment scores were high for both (4.35 ± 0.93 vs. 4.75 ± 0.44). The Kahoot group gave uniformly perfect engagement scores (5/5), suggesting very high engagement, though statistical testing was not possible due to zero variance. Conclusions : These findings suggest that IM education is both feasible and well-perceived in residency curriculum, supporting its inclusion to enhance knowledge, comfort, and appreciation for holistic care in family medicine practice. Although knowledge gains differed, both methods promoted positive learning experiences within a brief timeframe. These findings support incorporating varied teaching modalities in Integrative education and demonstrate that gamified learning can sustain attention and enjoyment in resident didactic environments. P 04.07 Building the Neurotrauma Evidence Synthesis Training (NEST) Program: A Process Evaluation of a Virtual Systematic Review Training Center for Complementary and Integrative Healthcare Trainees Dr Joshua Z Goldenberg ND 1 , Dr Monique Aucoin ND 2 , Dr Richard D Batson ND, MS 1 , Dr Alisha Bruton ND, MS, MS 1,3 , Dr Ryan Wexler ND, MS 1 , Dr Lita Buttolph PhD, LAc 1 , Michelle Berg 1 , Tammy Mazure 1 , Dr Heather Zwickey PhD 1 1 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 2 Canadian College of Naturopathic Medicine, Toronto, Ontario, Canada 3 Oregon Health Sciences University, Portland, OR, United States Purpose : Traumatic brain injury (TBI) affects millions globally, and Complementary and Integrative Health (CIH) interventions are increasingly being used to address TBI sequelae. The Neurotrauma Evidence Synthesis Training (NEST) program was developed as a virtual, mentorship-driven training center that explicitly includes CIH institutions. Method(s): Data sources included program records, administrative documents, financial reports, mentee and mentor interviews, participant surveys, and outputs from systematic review projects. Review quality was assessed using the AMSTAR-2 tool. Funding and sustainability strategies were examined via grant submissions, peer review feedback, and institutional commitments, with special attention to capacity-building for CIH institutions. Results : Thirty-five trainees enrolled. Most were students from CIH institution or early-stage clinician-researchers with limited prior access to high-level systematic review training. Retention was high, with only two attritions (6%). Educational activities included biweekly Evidence Synthesis Labs, neurotrauma-focused seminars, asynchronous training modules, and multi-level mentorship. Within two years, NEST registered 16 systematic reviews, published two protocols, and submitted two full reviews, both rated “high quality” on AMSTAR-2. Active projects span CIH-relevant areas including dietary interventions for metabolic outcomes, botanical medicine, and mind-body practices for neurological conditions. Funding has included a pilot grant from the RAND REACH Center, a NIDDK R25 grant, and in-kind faculty contributions. Conclusions : NEST demonstrates that a virtual, mentorship-driven, program can train CIH healthcare trainees, producing high-quality systematic reviews in neurotrauma, as well as other topics. Early results suggest that NEST is both feasible and replicable. Outputs already exceed expectations, with reviews addressing CIH-relevant interventions poised to inform whole-person care. However, adequate funding remains a significant concern two years into the program with most of the faculty effort being provided as in-kind contributions. P 04.08 Seeding the next generation of CIH research: the RAND REACH pilot grant program Michele J Maiers DC, MPH, PhD 1 , Patricia M Herman ND, PhD 2 , Heather Zwickey PhD 3 , Ian Coulter PhD 2 1 Northwestern Health Sciences University, Bloomington, MN, United States 2 RAND, Santa Monica, CA, United States 3 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States Purpose : Preliminary research and pilot studies are essential precursors to full-scale projects and provide valuable real-world experience for early-career researchers. However, securing pilot funding can be challenging—particularly for clinician scientists at complementary and integrative health (CIH) institutions, which often lack internal funding support. Method(s): The NIH-funded RAND Research Across Complementary and Integrative Health Institutions (REACH) Center addresses this gap through its Pilot Research Grants program. Funded by the NCMIC Foundation, this program offers up to $15,000 for 1–2 year pilot studies aimed at generating data for larger research proposals, fostering cross-institutional collaboration, and developing early-career investigators. Grant applications are submitted via the RAND REACH members-only portal in response to semiannual funding announcements. Proposals are structured like NIH grants and must include research aims, background, methods, human subjects and data safety plans, budget, biosketches, and letters of support. Applicants must also explain how the pilot will support future research. Each submission is reviewed by a three-person panel—one Center leader, one institutional research director, and one junior Center member—using the NIH 9-point scoring rubric. Proposals demonstrating collaboration or led by early-career investigators receive priority. Funded researchers engage in monthly meetings for mentorship, troubleshooting, and peer learning. Results : To date, 21 proposals have been submitted and nine pilot grants awarded. Nearly all involve multi-institution collaboration; seven are led by early-career investigators. All funded projects have either published findings, submitted papers, or are ongoing, with initial pilots informing subsequent large-scale proposals. Conclusions : This pilot grant model has successfully seeded impactful research and supported development of emerging CIH investigators through hands-on experience, mentorship, and structured project leadership. While the funding amount for individual grants is small, distributing funding in this manner has created a broad range of projects and allowed RAND REACH to provide real-world support for early career CIH researchers. P 04.09 Measuring What Matters in Surgical Education: Mentorship, Institutional Culture, and Equitable Development in Orthopedic Residency Training Research Fellow Cordero L McCall MBA, MPH Rothman Orthopaedic, Philadelphia, Pennsylvania, United States Purpose : Orthopedic surgery continues to be one of the most competitive specialties in medicine, and research productivity has become a common way to evaluate applicants. This project explores patterns of research activity among orthopedic residents to better understand what program and trainee factors contribute to sustained productivity and professional growth. Additional information about residency program characteristics was obtained from national databases and ranking systems. Multivariate models are being developed to examine how prematch first-author publications and program rank relate to ongoing research productivity during residency, while accounting for gender, degree type, and advanced degrees. Results : Early analysis shows that overall publication output tends to increase as residents advance through training, with those in top 25 programs demonstrating greater growth compared with peers in other programs. Prior first-author experience appears to relate to higher ongoing productivity, especially in programs with strong mentorship structures. Further analysis is underway to confirm these trends. Conclusions : Preliminary results point to the importance of mentorship, institutional environment, and access to research infrastructure in supporting long-term scholarly development. P 04.10 Impact of Integrative Health Education on Pediatric Resident Awareness and Clinical Behavior: A Two-Survey Analysis Lisa Squires BSN , Dr Maria Mascarenhas MBBS, Mya King MD, Andrea Szabo MD University of Pennsylvania, Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, Philadelphia, PA, United States Purpose : Integrative health (IH) approaches are increasingly being incorporated into pediatric care settings. Limited IH education during residency often translates to underutilization and knowledge deficits. These findings support the inclusion of integrative health topics within residency didactics and suggest that even limited exposures can create meaningful change in behavior and care delivery P 04.11 Training, knowledge, and attitudes of medical and dental students toward integrative medicine Kayla Kendall 1 , Dr. Adam Perrin MD 1,2 , Dr. Mary Guerrera MD 1 1 University of Connecticut School of Medicine, Farmington, CT, United States 2 St. Francis Hospital and Medical Center, Hartford, CT, United States Purpose : This study evaluated knowledge, attitudes, and exposure to integrative medicine (IM) among 92 medical and dental students at a single institution to assess how current curricular offerings influence readiness to discuss IM with patients. Results : Among respondents, the mean CHBQ score was 32.8 ± 6.2 (n = 92), indicating overall neutral attitudes toward IM on a 10-item, 5-point scale (maximum = 50). Willingness to discuss IM with future patients increased with greater exposure; nearly 70% of students reporting multiple IM experiences expressed moderate to high willingness to do so. CHBQ scores were highest among first-year students (34.8) compared to second-year (29.6) and third- to fourth-year (32.0–32.5) students. Conclusions : Findings reveal declining IM attitudes among upper-level students, suggesting potential influence of the hidden curriculum during clinical training. Greater exposure was associated with increased confidence and willingness to discuss IM with patients, underscoring the importance of early experiential learning. Inclusion of both medical and dental students highlights the cross-disciplinary relevance of IM in preparing future clinicians for collaborative, patient-centered care. As public interest in integrative health continues to grow, strengthening IM education and normalizing these discussions in training are essential for meeting patient needs through evidence-based, whole-person approaches. Topic Area 05: Integrative Medicine Implementation P 05.01 Be I.N.F.O.R.M.E.D. (Individuals Necessary for Research Making Educated Decisions) Community Wellness Project Regime Willis B.S. 1 , Katherine Dignan M.S. 2 1 University Hospitals, Cleveland, Ohio, United States 2 U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs, Cleveland, Ohio, United States Purpose : The purpose of this project was to provide a series of interactive educational sessions paired with wellness activities to improve the knowledge and awareness of clinical trial participation among the target populations of Veterans and African Americans. Method(s): Participants engaged in 4, 75 minute education and skill development sessions. Participants were educated on health disparities, research, ethics, how to get involved and participate in research studies. In addition participants experienced wellness sessions at every class which included a 15-30 minute experience on mindfulness, cooking, yoga and creative expression. This program was a community-based initiative, providing services directly in the neighborhoods that participants lived. Data collection of this program ran from January 2025-March 2025. During this time period 3 cohorts were completed (1 virtual and 2 in person at 2 separate community wellness centers). A post-implementation data review included 23 unique participants. Results : Results indicate participants improved knowledge at each of the 4 sessions. Knowledge of health disparities in session one, pre verse post programming, changed from fair knowledge 2.5 (SD 1.2) to good knowledge 3.1 (SD 0.8). In session 2 knowledge improved on how to be an advocate for yourself when considering research, moving from fair knowledge 3 (SD 1.3) to good knowledge 3 (SD 1.1). In session 3 knowledge of how to find a research study improved from poor knowledge 3.6 (SD 1) to good knowledge 2.1 (SD 0.9). In general no improvements were noted in physical or mental health when measured at session 1 and 4. In addition, participants had a very high interest in engaging in wellness activities at the start and end of the program with a rating of 4 (S.D 1) out of a possible 5. Conclusions : In conclusion these results indicate improvements in research knowledge after attending these sessions. This community-based initiative helped to bring healthcare professionals directly into neighborhoods, making it easier for individuals to learn about and use available services. P 05.02 Implementation of a Pediatric Integrative Medicine Program at a Children's Hospital Dr. Natalia Jaramillo PhD, Saloni Dangoria BA, Elena Gareau BS , Carly Wold, Jay Taimish, Dr. Jeffrey Gold PhD Children's Hospital of Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, United States Purpose : Integrative health practices such as acupuncture, massage, mindfulness, and yoga are increasingly being implemented in pediatric medical settings to support comprehensive care and improve outcomes. The PIM program reflects a promising model for delivering holistic support in medical settings. Future directions include expanding the program through multi-center research collaborations and identifying strategies for long-term sustainability and integration into standard pediatric care pathways. P 05.03 Sustainable Academic–Community Partnership to Expand Integrative Medicine in Underserved Communities Edward Hui MD 1 , Weijun Zhang DrPH 1 , Gene Hui MPH, MBA 2 , Annie Law MA 1 , Jack Cheng JD 2 , Nina Yuen-Loc MA 2 , Ka-Kit Hui MD 1 1 UCLA, Los Angeles, CA, United States 2 Chinatown Service Center, Los Angeles, CA, United States Purpose : Integrative medicine development in community health typically follows one of three pathways: evolving an existing integrative medicine program into a community health center; embedding integrative medicine components into existing community clinics; or building academic-community partnerships to deliver comprehensive services. Results : Since 2021, the UCLA Center for East-West Medicine (CEWM) has established a partnership with CSC - a Federally Qualified Health Center serving over 20,000 primarily Chinese and Latinx patients annually - to integrate person-centered, East-West care into community practice. • Medical Education: Established community-based training pathways including the Early Authentic Clinical Experience for first-year medical students, Discovery Area of Concentration for third-year students, and fellowship rotations in integrative community care, cultivating a pipeline of culturally competent clinicians. • Clinical Integration: The integrative medicine teaching clinic delivers approximately 300 visits per month, expanding to behavioral health through non-drug therapies such as Tai chi, qigong, meditation, and music therapy, and includes an oral health integration initiative. • Research Application: Focuses on workforce development through community-based teaching health centers, and studies addressing health disparities among Asian and Latinx populations. Conclusions : The CEWM–CSC partnership exemplifies a sustainable academic–community model advancing equitable, culturally grounded integrative health. By aligning education, clinical care, and research, this model bridges academic innovation and community needs - offering a replicable framework for implementing person-centered, non-drug, and culturally responsive care in immigrant and underserved populations. P 05.04 Moving from Grant Support to Non-Profit Status: A Case Study in Financial Independence for a Patient-Focused Program Clinical Assistant Professor Sarah Ingersoll MS, MBA 1 , Louis Castro DPT 2 , Kenneth Hayashida MD 3 , Kris Mendenhall JD 4 1 University of Southern California, Los Angeles, CA, United States 2 T.R.O. Physio + Wellness, Los Angeles, CA, United States 3 retired, Los Angeles, CA, United States 4 Nossaman LLP, Los Angeles, CA, United States Purpose : To describe a practical approach by which a small, patient-centered exercise program operating within a university setting achieved financial independence and organizational stability by transitioning from grant reliance to non-profit status. For many years, a free community exercise program serving individuals with neurological conditions depended on institutional grant support to cover personnel and operational costs. Increasing competition for grant funding and growing administrative constraints within the university environment prompted exploration of alternative funding models to ensure program continuity. Method(s): Options considered included partnering with an existing non-profit organization. However, logistical, administrative, and cultural-fit challenges made this path impractical. As payroll deadlines approached, the program launched a GoFundMe campaign, rebranded its relationship with the university as “collaboration,” and—using generative AI resources—navigated the process of establishing an independent 501(c)(3). Liability coverage appropriate for a community exercise program was secured through research into local nonprofit insurers. Results : AI-enabled research streamlined the incorporation process, allowing state registration as a public benefit corporation, liability coverage, and IRS recognition of non-profit status within weeks. Donor enthusiasm was strong, with patient contributions readily directed toward sustaining the program’s mission. Transitioning to nonprofit status provided financial stability, operational flexibility, and a more direct relationship with the program’s community of supporters. Conclusions : Establishing a dedicated nonprofit proved to be an efficient and replicable strategy for sustaining a small, mission-driven health program. This approach reduces dependency on uncertain grant funding, strengthens community engagement, and supports continuity of care. It offers a viable model for similar initiatives seeking long-term stability and independence. P 05.05 Scaling Integrative Healthcare: Practical Approaches for Health Systems Katrina Vigo MSN, RNC-MNN, HNB-BC , Parvita Bhoodai MSN, RN, AMB-BC, Julia Smith MSN, RN, MEDSURG-BC, Amelia DeWitt BSN, RN, Dr. Kathleen DeMarco PhD, NE-BC, CPHQ,RN NYU Langone Health, New York, NY, United States Purpose : Integrative health interventions have shown benefits for patient outcomes and staff wellbeing. Method(s): Two quality improvement pilot projects from a Holistic Nursing and Integrative Health Fellowship, one focused on staff grief and loss support and the other on perioperative wellness education, demonstrate processes used to successfully implement integrative health interventions across a health system.
