WHAT ARE SOME IMPLICATIONS FOR INTEGRATION, A CASE FOR PLURALITY? Dr. Claire M Cassidy PhD, LAc 1,2 , Dr. Lisa A Conboy Ma, MS,ScD 3 , Founder Tanuja Prasad BA engineering 4 1 Georgetown University, Washington, District of Columbia, United States 2 Windpath Healing Works Consulting, Bethesda, MD, United States 3 Harvard University, Cambridge, MA, United States 4 ApplyComplexity, New York, NY, United States Purpose : When comparing medical systems people often seek differences. We wish to shift the gaze toward starting with what is the same among Medicines. When the resulting knowledge is applied, it becomes easier to accept plurality and apply it in education, clinical care, research, and in policymaking. Method(s): Evidence is drawn from sources including social sciences, history, clinical trials, colleague researchers, journalism, and from interviewing practitioners of different Medicines. This work is being developed as a book called Six Medicines, Six Gifts, Steps to a Plural Medicine. This combined information offers insight into the complexity of the concept of a medical system. It is developed into discussions that can shift Readers’ perceptions toward open-mindedness and plurality. Results : Three Universals undergird all medical systems. All societies recognize suffering, and some in each society choose to help by offering medical care. All answer the same set of five Fundamental Questions. But they answer them differently, hence multiple medical systems (and their technologies). Trained persons develop a ‘medical gaze’ that reflects the medical model of their system, and these are sufficiently different that they can explain many of the miscommunications that occur as the dominant biomedicine tries to ‘make sense’ of Other Medicines. Conclusions : Knowing that all Medicines start from the same concerns and diversify as they answer the Fundamental Questions makes it easier to do well-grounded comparative study. Were this knowledge put to work, it would change how students are educated, how the patient-practitioner relationship is played out, how research is done, and could guide public policy. The mechanisms are available. What is needed is both comparative knowledge and a structure to implement the ideas and gifts of multiple medical systems. P 05.07 Short-Term Changes in Patient-Reported Emotional Well-Being and Function After Integrative Medicine Consultations Marcus Grewal BS 1,2 , Aaditya Pallerla BS 1,2 , Ria Jindal BS 1,2 , Sanjana Katiyar 2 , Harini Pallerla MS 2 , Dr. Sian Cotton PhD 1,2 , Dr. Mladen Golubic MD, PhD 1,2 1 University of Cincinnati College of Medicine, Cincinnati, OH, United States 2 University of Cincinnati Osher Center for Integrative Health, Cincinnati, OH, United States Purpose : Integrative medicine (IM) offers a patient-centered approach that combines conventional medical care with evidence-informed therapies to promote optimal healing. IM providers play a pivotal role in facilitating lifestyle modification and connecting patients to therapies such as yoga, acupuncture, tai chi, and more. We aim to evaluate longitudinal changes in emotional well-being, mood, perceived stress, flourishing, and health-related functioning in patients receiving IM care at an urban academic health system. Method(s): Patients scheduled with IM providers completed an online survey prior to their consultation, and follow-up surveys were administered after 3 months, with future 6- and 12-month surveys to be collected. Validated survey instruments include the Perceived Stress Scale (PSS), Flourishing Measure, and PROMIS-29 Profile, which measures domains including Anxiety, Depression, Fatigue, Pain Interference, Physical Function, Sleep Disturbance, and Participation in Social Roles. Data was analyzed via paired t-tests with SPSS-29 software. Results : 242 patients completed a pre-survey of which 75% were female with a mean age of 53. Currently, 25 patients have completed a 3-month post-survey. Preliminary analysis shows no significant changes in PSS, Flourishing Measure, or PROMIS-29 Profile, except for the Physical Function domain, in which a slight decrease was observed (Mpre=44.23, Mpost=41.57, p=0.012). These findings can inform the design of IM therapies and support continued implementation of whole-person approaches in clinical practice. P 05.08 Analysis of Electronic Health Records Data from U.S. Naturopathic Clinics Nini Callan ND, MS 1 , Carlo Calabrese ND, MPH 1 , Ryan Bradley ND, MPH 2 , Michelle Simon PhD, ND 3 , Steve Chamberlin ND, LAc, MS 4 1 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 2 University of California, San Diego, La Jolla, California, United States 3 Institute for Natural Medicine, Seattle, Washington, United States 4 Oregon Health & Science University, Portland, Oregon, United States Purpose : This pilot study aims to construct a HIPAA-compliant, de-identified database using CharmHealth EHR data from primary care naturopathic clinics. To evaluate database utility through two test questions: (a) trends in HbA1c among patients with Type 2 diabetes (structured data), and (b) documentation of lifestyle counseling (unstructured data); 3. To establish protocols for secure, long-term data storage, governance, and reuse for future research. Method(s): EHR data from 5-10 naturopathic clinics will be securely exported, de-identified, and transformed into standardized flat-file tables. Workflow metrics include extraction success, PHI removal accuracy, and regulatory compliance. HIPAA Safe Harbor and NIST SP 800-88 standards guide de-identification, storage, and destruction to ensure data security and reproducibility. Results : To date, 7 naturopathic clinics have agreed to participate, and data use agreements, institutional approvals, and system access procedures are underway. Data extraction, de-identification, and EMERSE-based query testing will commence following completion of onboarding and compliance milestones. Conclusions : Establishing a secure, de-identified multi-site naturopathic EHR database is feasible yet methodologically complex. This pilot provides critical groundwork for future large-scale analyses of naturopathic care and outcomes. The resulting infrastructure will support both quantitative and qualitative investigations, integrating structured and unstructured data for practice-based research. Sustainable database storage and governance will enable long-term data reuse and comparative effectiveness studies, advancing the visibility, rigor, and collaborative potential of naturopathic and integrative health research. (Parkinson’s Access to Technology & Health) was developed to advance equitable, whole-person PD care through a digital-community hybrid model that connects patients and caregivers to integrative resources such as tai chi, mindfulness, nutrition, and support groups. A community ambassador program is being designed to enhance digital literacy and care navigation. Planned evaluation metrics include patient uptake of integrative health services. As Project P.A.T.H. moves toward pilot implementation, future evaluation will assess its impact on access, engagement, and quality of life among underserved populations. Ultimately, this work illustrates how integrative and digital health approaches can advance equitable, person-centered care for individuals living with chronic neurological disease. P 05.10 Midwives of Color as Catalysts for Change in Postpartum Care: “Celebrating birthing experiences is how we do it” Paige L Prater MPH 1 , Dr. Chanda Williams 2,3,4,5 1 University of California San Francisco, San Francisco, CA, United States 2 PhD, Pacifica Graduate Institute California, Santa Barbara, California, United States 3 MA, Pacifica Graduate Institute California, Santa Barbara, California, United States 4 MA, Integrative Health Studies, California Institute of Integral Studies, San Francisco, California, United States 5 BA, Mechanical Engineering, Kettering University, Flint, Michigan, United States Purpose : Amid mounting threats to reproductive health and equity, community midwives of color provide essential perinatal care while remaining marginalized within US health care. P 05.11LB Economic evaluations of nonpharmacological treatments for chronic pain in the United States: A scoping review Hannah Cheng MS , Molly Beestrum MLIS, AHIP, Lucy Bilaver PhD, Christine Rini PhD, David Victorson PhD, Rinad S Beidas PhD Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, United States Purpose : Approximately 1 in 5 people in the United States (US) live with chronic pain. Although pharmacological treatments are widely adopted, they often carry risks. Nonpharmacological pain treatments (NPTs) are noninvasive interventions that target behaviors and brain processes underlying the experience of pain. Despite effectiveness, uptake of NPTs remains low, with affordability as a major barrier. Understanding the economic value of NPTs is essential for guiding uptake across health systems. Method(s): Guided by the PRISMA-ScR, this scoping review examined studies of NPTs for individuals with chronic pain that reported both costs and outcomes. Eligible studies were original research published between 2000 and 2025 that conducted economic evaluations of NPTs in US health care settings. Search strategies were developed with a medical librarian using keywords on chronic pain, nonpharmacological treatments, and economic evaluations. Searches were conducted in Medline (PubMed), Embase, Cochrane Library, and Scopus. Results : A total of 1,785 nonduplicated studies were identified for title and abstract screening. Of these, 199 (11.1%) underwent full-text screening, and 30 (1.7%) were included for data extraction. Two-thirds of included studies were published in the past decade. Many studies employed cost-effectiveness analysis; few examined long-term economic outcomes. Across NPTs, interdisciplinary and integrative care models were associated with reductions in downstream healthcare utilization, yielding net cost savings. Psychosocial interventions such as cognitive behavioral therapy and mindfulness-based stress reduction consistently demonstrated cost-effectiveness. Spinal manipulative therapy demonstrated mixed cost-effectiveness across pain conditions. Physical therapy incurred higher costs but shared similar benefits to chiropractic care and exercise-based interventions. Conclusions : Despite being cost-saving and cost-effective, integrative care models and psychosocial interventions remain underutilized for chronic pain management compared to physical therapy. Policies that facilitate the scaling of these NPTs may improve their accessibility and transform chronic pain care. P 05.12LB Activating Hidden Clinical Capacity: A Workforce-Based Hybrid Integrative Medicine Model in an Academic Hospital Professor Kengo Nakata MS, MA, LAcMx 1,2,3 , Professor Keiko OGAWA-OCHIAI MD, PhD, 1,2,3 1 Hiroshima University Hospital, Hiroshima, Hiroshima, Japan 2 Hiroshima University, Hiroshima, Hiroshima, Japan 3 Kampo Medicine Promotion Organization Inc., Minato, Tokyo, Japan Purpose : Healthcare systems worldwide face critical workforce shortages and escalating costs, yet substantial therapeutic resources remain underutilized. In Japan, Kampo medicine is insurance-covered while acupuncture is predominantly self-pay, limiting institutional integration despite increasing demand for multidisciplinary care. Following completion and validation of the full 2024 operational dataset, a previously unrecognized systems-level pattern emerged. This study presents a late-breaking workforce-capacity analysis evaluating how a hybrid integrative medicine model can activate hidden clinical capacity within an academic hospital. Method(s): We implemented a physician-led referral pathway in which Kampo physicians conducted initial evaluations and collaborated with licensed acupuncturists for ongoing management. Electronic medical records supported cross-disciplinary information sharing, reinforced by reciprocal clinical education to ensure safe hospital-based integration. During final data integration in December 2025, we performed the first comprehensive system-level analysis incorporating longitudinal referral growth, workforce utilization, productivity impact, and financial sustainability. Results : New acupuncture referrals increased from 12 in 2021 to 67 in 2024 (ANOVA p<0.001), totaling 150 patients. Across four years, 1,552 acupuncture sessions were delivered. Introduction of acupuncturists doubled the number of patients managed per Kampo physician while reducing outpatient burden. Despite representing a minimal proportion of national healthcare expenditure, the licensed acupuncture workforce functioned as a scalable clinical capacity extender. Hybrid financing generated approximately ¥9.72 million (∼USD 65,000) in self-pay revenue without increasing physician staffing. Referrals spanned multiple departments including palliative care, internal medicine, and surgery. Conclusions : This late-breaking reinterpretation demonstrates that redistributing underutilized therapeutic professionals can expand care capacity without increasing physician workload. By reframing integrative medicine as a workforce-extending strategy rather than a supplementary service, the Hiroshima model offers a scalable framework for academic health systems confronting global staffing constraints. Timely dissemination is warranted because delayed presentation would diminish the relevance of these findings for institutions actively redesigning care delivery. P 05.13LB Expanding Access to Whole Health through a Community-Engaged Research Framework: Outcomes from TakeCare’s 9-Week Peer Group Pilot Well-Being Program Melissa H Abadi Ph.D. 1,2 , Kirsten T. Thompson M.A. 1 , Stephen R Shamblen Ph.D. 3 , Bonnie O. Richard Ph.D. 1 , Ruth Q. We adapted a nine-week healthcare-based group program to a community-led program to assess feasibility and obtain preliminary outcomes. Future studies will examine the program’s impact on long-term outcomes, as well as against a comparison group. Our goal is to scale programming to enhance access. Topic Area 06: Physical Activity/Lifestyle Medicine P 06.01 BE-WEHL 22q: Developing and Implementing a Wellness Education Program for Children with 22q11.2 Deletion Syndrome Mr. Luc A Santilli BA, BS 1 , Dr. Alain J Benitez MD, MSTR 1,2 , Ms. Donna M McDonald-McGinn MS, CGC 1,2 , Dr. Lauren K White PhD 1 , Ms. Robin Miccio MS, LMT 1 , Dr. Maria R Mascarenhas MBBS 1,2 1 the Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, Philadelphia, PA, United States 2 the Perelman School of Medicine at the University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia, PA, United States Purpose : 22q11.2 deletion syndrome (22q11.2DS) is the most common microdeletion, occurring in ∼1 in 1,000 pregnancies and 1 in 2,000 live births. Anxiety is highly prevalent in 22q11.2DS and frequently precedes the onset of psychosis. There is a need for accessible, systems-based interventions tailored to this population. Integrative wellness practices have shown promise in reducing anxiety and promoting well-being. We developed and piloted “BE-WEHL-22q,” an 8-week virtual wellness education program specifically adapted for children with 22q11.2DS and their families, incorporating mindfulness, yoga, sleep, nutrition education, and novel biomarkers examining the brain-gut axis. We hypothesized that BE-WEHL-22q would positively impact anxiety, resilience, and potentially alter gut microbiome composition. Method(s): Children 7–14 years with a confirmed 22q11.2 deletion and at least one caregiver participated in this IRB-approved study. The virtual 8-week BE-WEHL-22q curriculum covered Coping and Resilience, Pause and Reflect, Caring Touch, Activity and Rest, Mindful Movement Yoga, Healthy Eating, Fresh Mindset, and Navigating the Journey, a session specific to parenting children with 22q11.2DS. Psychosocial measures, dietary intake, stool, and urine samples were collected at baseline, post-intervention, and at 1-, 3-, and 6-month follow-ups. Observational data, family feedback, and practice logs were also collected. Results : Thirty-three participants were enrolled; 10 completed the full program to date (median age 10.8 years; 57% female; 76% white). Mothers represented 97% of caregivers. All reported the program was helpful and would recommend it. Families frequently engaged additional members, despite home-based challenges such as noise and limited space. Biological samples (23 total) from 17 participants were collected for future analysis. Conclusions : BE-WEHL-22q is a novel, feasible, well-received virtual wellness program for families affected by 22q11.2DS. Next steps include evaluating its impact on anxiety and behavior and analyzing microbiome-related biomarkers. If effective, BE-WEHL-22q may offer a scalable, home-based intervention for children with genetic and behavioral vulnerabilities. P 06.02 Analysis of the Reach of the American Medical Women’s Association Healthy Lifestyle Revolution Campaign: A Pilot Study Alayna Ritter BS 1 , Paul Kang MS 1 , Dr. Dolapo Babalola MD 2 , Dr. Manasi Kekan MD, MMM 3 , Dr. Beatriz Olson MD 4 , Dr. Eliza Chin MD, MPH 5 , Dr. Bisi Alli DO 6,1 1 Creighton University, Phoenix, AZ, United States 2 American Medical Women's Association, Atlanta, GA, United States 3 American Medical Women's Association, Houston, TX, United States 4 American Medical Women's Association, Middlebury, CT, United States 5 American Medical Women's Association, San Francisco, CA, United States 6 American Medical Women's Association, Phoenix, AZ, United States Purpose : The American Medical Women’s Association Healthy Lifestyle Revolution is a campaign that encourages physicians to share evidence-based lifestyle tips on social media, with a goal of making credible wellness information more accessible. This pilot study analyzed videos posted by physicians for the campaign to gain a better understanding of the engagement and reach of these videos on YouTube and LinkedIn. Method(s): Videos using the hashtag #AMWASTEPSTOWELLNESS were reviewed for length, theme, narration style, visual quality, visual content, content strategy, credentialing, and how long they had been posted. Engagement outcomes included total likes and views on YouTube, as well as total comments, reactions, and shares on LinkedIn. In total, 33 videos were analyzed (YouTube: 16; LinkedIn: 17). Results : YouTube consistently had more views and responses than LinkedIn. Videos with more than one theme had the highest engagement (β = 52.8; 95% CI: 25.8–79.9; p < 0.001), while stress management content trended toward lower engagement. Exercise-themed LinkedIn posts generated more comments and reposts (p = 0.06). Including credentials increased responses (+22.4; p = 0.042), and videos posted longer gained more engagement over time (0.28 additional responses per day; p = 0.015). Videos that used both selfie and visual elements with a conversational tone tended to get more views (p = 0.017). Video length itself wasn’t a major factor, but longer videos were generally tied to fewer responses. Conclusions : Across both platforms, narrative style, visual format and theme impacted trends regarding viewer engagement. This pilot study highlights how strategic use of social media by physicians can enhance access to evidence-based health information and contribute to more effective public health communication. P 06.03 The Perceived Importance of Health Behaviors in US Adults Yuktha Shanavas , Melany Taranto, Dr. Kaushal Nanavati Medical Degree, Dr. Jamie Romeiser Romeiser Ph.d, MPH Upstate Medical University, Syracuse, New York, United States Purpose : Chronic diseases are incredibly prevalent in US adults. Complex management needs and rising healthcare costs contribute to individual and community burdens. While adopting healthy lifestyle behaviors can help mitigate chronic conditions, many adults do not meet the recommended lifestyle guidelines. As beliefs often drive and shape actions, understanding how individuals value healthy behaviors is critical. This study examined perceptions of lifestyle behaviors and identified sociodemographic factors associated with differing levels of importance for overall well-being. Method(s): We used nationally representative survey data from the Pew Research Center's American Trends Panel (2024, n = 10,133). Participants were asked, “How important are each of the following for your overall well-being?” Items included “Eating healthy”, “Exercise”, “Managing stress and anxiety”, “Sleeping well”, “Keeping up with friends and family” and “Being involved in your community”. Responses were dichotomized into high/very vs. neutral/low importance. Prevalence of high importance was examined across sociodemographic characteristics. Separate multivariable logistic regression models identified factors associated with high perceived importance for each item. All analyses were conducted at the 0.05 significance level. Results : Most responders reported sleep (82%), managing stress and anxiety (79.9%), eating healthy (69.6%), and socialization with friends and family (66%) as highly important to well-being. Fewer valued exercise (59.2%) or community involvement (21.8%). After adjustment, higher odds of rating these behaviors as important were most commonly observed among older adults, women, those with higher education and higher income. Conclusions : Uneven value was placed on different lifestyle pillars, with younger individuals and men most consistently viewing them as less essential to well-being. Understanding these perceptions can provide insight into behavior and barriers to adherence. This in turn informs the creation of comprehensive treatment plans and calls for a broader collaboration across clinicians and public health platforms to promote the importance of all health behaviors. P 06.04 Targeting Anxiety Sensitivity in College Students: Dance Course Curricula and Protective/Risk Factors Ms. Malena M Price MSc 1,2,3 , Dr. Amishi Jha PhD 1 1 University of Miami, Coral Gables, FL, United States 2 Harvard Medical School, Cambridge, MA, United States 3 Massachusetts General Hospital (MGH), Boston, MA, United States Purpose : College students face heightened risk for psychological health challenges. One factor that can increase vulnerability to these issues is anxiety sensitivity (AS), or the fear of anxiety-related sensations. Despite the importance of AS in college students' impairment, little is known about psychological processes that may heighten or protect against this vulnerability, or how existing college courses may reduce it. This study investigated dispositional mindfulness and decentering as potential protective factors and mind wandering as a potential risk factor. In addition, we examined whether participation in dance courses, relative to Psychology courses, benefits AS. Method(s): We recruited college students (N = 271) across three course conditions: mindfulness-infused dance movement therapy (DT-M), traditional dance (TD), and Psychology. Students completed two remote testing sessions at the beginning and end of the semester. AS was measured using the Anxiety Sensitivity Index-3 (ASI-3). Dispositional mindfulness and decentering were measured using the Five Facet Mindfulness Questionnaire (FFMQ) and the Experiences Questionnaire (EQ-D), respectively. Mind wandering was measured using the Spontaneous and Deliberate Mind Wandering Scale (SDMWS). Results : Taking an individual differences approach, higher dispositional mindfulness and decentering were associated with lower AS, while higher spontaneous mind wandering was associated with higher AS. Multilevel model results showed that students in dance classes (DT-M + TD) experienced significantly greater reductions in AS-physical concerns over the semester than students in non-dance, Psychology courses, even after accounting for exercise variables. Conclusions : These findings reveal the potential protective nature of dispositional mindfulness and decentering for AS, and the risk-imposing nature of mind wandering. Moreover, results suggest that existing curricular dance offerings, including those embedding mindfulness, may be beneficial for reducing AS and anxiety-related vulnerabilities in college students. Such dance offerings may serve as a pragmatic and scalable solution for protecting students’ psychological health. P 06.05 Maternal Early Life Stress and Physical Activity during Pregnancy Dr. Laura E Laumann PhD 1,2 , Dr. Destiny Printz Pereira PhD 1,2 , Dr. Gina Erato Garza PhD 1,2 , Janet O Joseph BS 3,2 , Dr. Laura R Stroud PhD 1,2 1 The Warren Alpert Medical School of Brown University, Providence, RI, United States 2 The Miriam Hospital, Providence, RI, United States 3 Brown University, Providence, RI, United States Purpose : Research suggests that individuals with a history of early life stress (ELS) are more likely to engage in high-risk behaviors during pregnancy. Physical activity (PA), specifically moderate-vigorous physical activity (MVPA), may buffer against negative health outcomes. Little research has examined how PA during pregnancy may differ by ELS exposure. Thus, the aim of this exploratory analysis was to explore differences in PA during pregnancy by ELS exposure in a sample of pregnant women oversampled for smoking behaviors (22% smoking). Method(s): 94 pregnant women (Mage=26.9; 54.3% White; 31.9% low SES) completed a modified, 20-item version of the Adverse Childhood Experiences Scale and the Pregnancy Physical Activity Questionnaire (PPAQ) in their second trimester as part of their participation in a larger study. Listwise deletion was applied to missing data. Participants were categorized into groups by ELS exposure: Low (0-1; n=32), moderate (2-3; n=29), and high (4+; n= 33). PA was measured in MET (metabolic equivalent of task) hours/week. We hypothesized that greater ELS exposure would be associated with less MVPA. Results : Participants engaged in Mdn=63.1 MET hours/week of MVPA. Results of a Kruskal-Wallis test demonstrated no significant differences in MVPA across levels of ELS exposure, p >.05. Further analyses were conducted to probe the reason for high levels of MVPA in the present sample. Approximately 38% of the sample's MVPA MET hours/week consisted of occupational activity and 32% consisted of household activity. MVPA did not significantly differ by smoking status, SES, or occupation. Conclusions : A during pregnancy did not differ by ELS exposure in the present sample. Compared to validation samples for the PPAQ, women in the present sample engaged in relatively high levels of MVPA, not accounted for by SES or smoking status. Further analyses will assess factors related to high MVPA during pregnancy. P 06.06 Facilitate Physical Activity Engagement and Psychosocial Support in Prostate Cancer Survivors with a Golf-based Multimodal Activity Program Associate Professor Jacek Pinski MD, PhD 1,2 , Guanrong Cai 3 , Professor George Salem PhD 3 1 USC/Norris Comprehensive Cancer Center, Los Angeles, CA, United States 2 USC/Division of Biokinesiology & Physical Therapy, Los Angeles, CA, United States 3 University of Southern California, Los Angeles, CA, United States Purpose : Prostate cancer and its treatments can impair physical function and quality of life, highlighting the importance of physical activity and psychosocial support in survivorship care. Group-based multimodal recreational activity (MRA) programs foster activity engagement and social connection. Golf offers physical and psychosocial benefits and can be integrated into structured MRA programs. The Golf Recreational Exercise for Enhanced Survivorship (GREENS) program is a 10-week supervised group-based MRA intervention combining exercise, golf skill instruction, on-course play, and social support. This stage I behavioral study examined feasibility (safety, adherence, acceptance) and preliminary efficacy on physical function and quality of life in prostate cancer survivors receiving hormone therapy. Method(s): Fifteen survivors (mean age 73.1±5.7) were recruited from a comprehensive cancer center and electronic medical record registry. The 18-week design included three assessment timepoints (baseline [T0], pre-intervention [T1], post-intervention [T2]), a 5-week delayed-entry control period, and a 10-week intervention (20 sessions). Feasibility outcomes included adverse events (CTCAE), attendance, session adherence, and thematic analysis of exit interviews. Physical function (Short Physical Performance Battery [SPPB]) and quality of life (FACT-P and Physical Component Summary [PCS]) were analyzed using linear mixed-effects models. Results : Ten participants completed all procedures; dropouts were unrelated to the intervention. No CTCAE grade ≥2 adverse events occurred. FACT-P total scores did not change significantly; however, PCS improved (p=0.04), with a 1.8-point increase at T2 (p=0.04). Conclusions : The GREENS program was safe, well accepted, and showed promising improvements in physical function and prostate cancer–specific quality of life, supporting further evaluation in larger and more diverse populations. P 06.07 The Role of Table Tennis in Integrative Management of Parkinson’s Disease: Early Results from the NeuroPong™ Program Antonio Barbera MD 1,2,3 , Joel Edman DSc 4 1 Movement Disorders Center, University of Colorado School of Medicine, Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, Colorado, United States 2 Rocky Mountain Multiple Sclerosis Center, University of Colorado School of Medicine, Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, Colorado, United States 3 International Parkinson and Movement Disorder Society, Milwaukee, Wisconsin, United States 4 Metropolitan State University of Denver, Denver, Colorado, United States Purpose : Parkinson’s disease (PD) is a progressive neurodegenerative disorder with motor and non-motor symptoms that reduce quality of life. Medications alleviate some symptoms but do not fully address functional, cognitive, or psychosocial needs. Integrative approaches that combine movement, cognitive engagement, and social interaction may offer broader benefits. Table tennis incorporates aerobic activity, motor learning, balance, coordination, attention, and adaptability across ability levels. The NeuroPong™ Program formalizes table tennis into a therapeutic framework aimed at improving motor control, cognition, and emotional well-being in people with PD. Method(s): Twenty-six individuals with PD completed a 12-week table tennis-based intervention held twice weekly for 120 minutes. Sessions included warm-ups, skill training, structured game play, and cool-downs. This report presents four representative cases, supported by video documentation, illustrating functional changes observed during participation. Results : Case 1: A 59-year-old man with deep brain stimulation and significant dyskinesia improved upper-limb control, depth perception, hand–eye coordination, and daily functioning, reporting enhanced quality of life. Case 2: A 65-year-old man with dyskinesia and impaired movement regulation successfully switched playing dominance, achieving smoother motor control and reduced anxiety, with marked improvement in daily function. Case 3: A 66-year-old woman with upper-limb dyskinesia and multitasking difficulty demonstrated smoother arm movements, improved directional control, and enhanced ability to follow instructions, with substantial anxiety reduction. Case 4: A 64-year-old woman with tremor, trunk stiffness, and poor rhythm perception showed improvements in timing, trunk mobility, hand control, and emotional comfort during play. Conclusions : These early findings demonstrate the feasibility, safety, and therapeutic promise of the NeuroPong™ Program. Participants experienced motor improvements, such as better upper-limb control, coordination, and reaction time, as well as psychosocial gains including reduced anxiety and increased confidence. Table tennis uniquely blends repetitive motor learning, cognitive engagement, and social interaction, offering multidimensional benefits not typically achieved through conventional rehabilitation. P 06.08 The Role of Table Tennis in Integrative Management of Multiple Sclerosis: Early Results from the NeuroPong™ Program Antonio Barbera MD 1,2,3 , Joel Edman DCs 4 1 Movement Disorders Center, University of Colorado School of Medicine, Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, CO, Aurora, CO, United States 2 Rocky Mountain Multiple Sclerosis Center, University of Colorado School of Medicine, Anschutz Medical Campus, Aurora, CO, United States 3 International Parkinson and Movement Disorder Society, Milwaukee, WI, United States 4 Metropolitan State University of Denver, Denver, CO, United States Purpose : Multiple sclerosis (MS) is a chronic immune-mediated disorder of the central nervous system characterized by fluctuating motor, cognitive, and emotional symptoms. While traditional therapies manage disease progression, many individuals continue to experience unmet physical and psychosocial needs. Integrative approaches such as the NeuroPong™ program offer a complementary strategy. Table tennis, a low-impact, adaptable activity, supports balance, coordination, cognition, stamina, and well-being, with flexible modifications for fatigue, heat sensitivity, and mobility limitations. Method(s): The NeuroPong™ Program is a table tennis–based prehabilitation and neurorehabilitation intervention for neurodegenerative conditions. Fourteen participants completed a 12-week course consisting of twice-weekly 120-minute sessions incorporating warm-ups, skill training, game play, and cool-downs. This report highlights four MS cases, supplemented by video documentation, to illustrate functional changes observed during the program. Results : Case 1: A 60-year-old woman with relapsing-remitting MS entered with cane-assisted gait, left-sided weakness, foot drop, and dysarthria. By week 12, she demonstrated improved balance, left-leg flexion, coordinated strokes, and reduced fatigue. Case 2: A 58-year-old man with relapsing-remitting MS and stick-assisted gait gained the ability to bend his knees, play independently, and maintain balance, with notable fatigue improvement. Case 3: A 72-year-old woman with primary progressive MS using a wheelchair showed enhanced upper-limb strength, coordination, dexterity, and endurance after switching to left-hand play. Case 4: A 57-year-old woman with secondary progressive MS improved left-leg flexion, gait quality, and balance, progressing to play without walking sticks. Conclusions : Across MS subtypes and disability levels, all four individuals demonstrated functional gains in mobility, coordination, strength, or balance, with no falls recorded. Tailored adaptations supported success, and participants reported meaningful psychosocial benefits. Although preliminary and based on a small sample, these findings support further investigation of table tennis as a safe, flexible, and engaging integrative intervention for people with MS. P 06.09 Gerofit Program Outcomes at the Columbus Chalmers P. Wylie VA Adam Judd RT, Heather Bierbaugh PTA, Deborah Wilson LISW-S, Heather Seymour MSW, LISW, Lora Cunningham RN, Reversa Joseph MD Chalmers P Wylie Veterans Administration, Columbus, Ohio, United States Purpose : Physical inactivity in older adults contributes to functional decline, increased fall risk, and reduced quality of life. The Gerofit program, a supervised exercise and health promotion intervention offered through the Whole Health program at the Chalmers P. Wylie VA, aims to improve physical function, mobility, and overall well-being among Veterans aged 65 and older through personalized, progressive group exercise. The program launched at the Columbus site in April 2023 and has continued to expand participation. Method(s): Veterans aged ≥65 enrolled in Gerofit completed standardized functional assessments at baseline, 3, 6, and 12 months, including Sit-to-Stand, Arm Curl, 6-Minute Walk, 2-Minute Step Test, 8-Foot Get-Up-and-Go, and Balance testing. Participants also completed a satisfaction survey (Likert scale 1–6) assessing mood, meaning, and perceived life benefits. A total of 168 assessments were completed since program initiation, reflecting strong engagement and retention. Results : Significant improvements were observed across all domains. Sit-to-Stand repetitions increased by ∼63% and completion time decreased by ∼36%. Arm Curl repetitions increased by ∼46%. Six-Minute Walk distance improved by ∼24%, and 2-Minute Step Test counts increased by ∼56%. Get-Up-and-Go time decreased by ∼36%, and balance duration improved by 100%. Satisfaction scores were consistently high (mean 5.3–5.5/6), with over 90% of Veterans reporting improved mood, 88% reporting greater daily function, and 95% expressing pride and meaning in program participation. Conclusions : Gerofit participation was associated with meaningful improvements in strength, endurance, mobility, balance, and psychosocial well-being among older Veterans. Given these outcomes and sustained engagement, the Columbus VA plans to expand this model to younger Veterans (<60 years) through VetFit, extending the benefits of structured, Whole Health–aligned exercise to broader Veteran populations. Topic Area 07: Manual Therapies P 07.01 A Pilot Study on the Use of Massage Therapy and Healing Touch to Enhance the Overall Well-Being of Patients with Cancer Ria Jindal BS 1,2 , Marcus Grewal BS 1,2 , Aaditya Pallerla BS 1,2 , Shannon McKnight MS, LMT, BCTMB 2 , Angela Johnson LMT 2 , Harini Pallerla MS 2 , Dr. Mladen Golubic MD, PhD 1,2 , Dr. Sian Cotton PhD 1,2 1 University of Cincinnati College of Medicine, Cincinnati, OH, United States 2 University of Cincinnati Osher Center for Integrative Health, Cincinnati, OH, United States Purpose : Prior research has demonstrated that massage therapy and healing touch can improve pain and anxiety in patients undergoing cancer treatment. The objective of this pilot study was to evaluate how these interventions affect the overall well-being of patients with cancer in both an inpatient and outpatient setting. Method(s): Patients receiving inpatient or outpatient cancer treatment were offered massage therapy and/or healing touch by two licensed therapists. Participants completed a pre- and post-intervention mixed-methods iPad survey with three validated measures: the Perceived Stress Scale (PSS-4), the Pain, Enjoyment of Life, and General Activity (PEG-3) scale, and the Edmonton Symptom Assessment Scale (ESAS). Paired statistical analysis of pre- and post-intervention scores was conducted using SPSS-29, and thematic analysis was applied to open-ended questions. Results : Fifty-one unique participants were enrolled (55% female, 88% White; mean age, 60 years), and 64 paired pre- and post-evaluations were completed. The most common diagnoses were blood cancer (58%), pancreatic cancer (14%), and colorectal cancer (12%). Patients requested massage therapy (81%), healing touch (28%), or both (9%). The primary reasons for requesting care were stress (30%), pain (28%), and anxiety (17%). Before the intervention, 44% of patients rated their health as “fair” or “poor.” Post intervention, participants reported significant reductions in pain, tiredness, nausea, depression, anxiety, drowsiness, and shortness of breath (p<0.05). Well-being increased significantly (p<0.05), and appetite trended upwards (p=0.06). On open-ended responses, 98% of participants indicated the therapy was beneficial, and 100% would recommend the service to others. Conclusions : This study demonstrates the short-term benefits of massage therapy and healing touch on the well-being of patients with cancer. Limitations include potential self-selection bias and the absence of a control group. Future directions include reproducing the study as a randomized controlled trial (RCT) and expanding to broader oncology populations. P 07.02 Establishing Priorities to Advance Patient Safety Culture in the Chiropractic Profession: A Mixed Methods Study with Global Chiropractic Leaders Dr Stacie A Salsbury PhD, RN 1 , Dr Kent Stuber DC, MSc, PhD 2,3 , Dr Maranda Kleppe DC 1 , Dr Sidney Rubinstein PhD 4 , Prof Richard Brown DC, LLM, FRCC 5 , Dr Katherine A Pohlman DC, MS, PhD 2 1 Palmer College of Chiropractic, Davenport, IA, United States 2 Parker University, Dallas, TX, United States 3 Canadian Memorial Chiropractic College, Toronto, Ontario, Canada 4 Vrije Universiteit, Amsterdam, North Holland Province, Netherlands 5 Richard Brown Chiropractic Consultancy, Stroud, Cotswolds, United Kingdom Purpose : Establishing and sustaining a robust patient safety culture in chiropractic necessitates growth from our current focus on minimizing adverse events to implementing strategic objectives aligned with global initiatives. Our objective was to examine chiropractic leaders' perspectives on global priorities to enhance patient safety culture within the profession. Method(s): We recruited chiropractic leaders associated with the World Federation of Chiropractic (WFC) for a multi-phased study designed to establish organizational priorities for patient safety initiatives. Respondents completed online surveys via REDCap, video-conferencing interviews for a remote adaptive nominal group process and ranking exercises with open-ended items. Descriptive statistics and qualitative thematic analysis collated findings to identify priorities, strengths, and barriers to improving patient safety culture. Results : Fifty respondents completed the initial survey, including 47 chiropractors, of which 36 have active practices. Respondents represented all WFC regions and the majority participated in subsequent phases. Top patient safety priorities included: 1) reporting and monitoring adverse events and safety incidents, 2) adopting professional standards and healthcare worker safety protocols, 3) training chiropractors to engage in safety conversations with patients, 4) incorporating patient safety into clinical education, licensing, regulatory, and accreditation requirements, 5) encouraging chiropractors to participant in patient safety initiatives within chiropractic and collaborate across healthcare professions, 6) disseminating patient educational materials and evidence-based resources for chiropractors on clinical processes to reduce patient risk, 7) implementing safety-related clinical pathways and health record documentation in chiropractic settings, 8) committing to using Safety I (reactive safety management) and Safety II (proactive safety management) frameworks to build resilience, 9) developing and collecting meaningful outcome measures of patient safety experiences, and 10) establishing professional competences in patient safety. Conclusions : Global leaders ranked their priorities for patient safety culture initiatives, establishing a needs assessment and action agenda for an international chiropractic organization. P 07.03 A Survey of Chiropractic Trainees and Program Directors: Insights into Postdoctoral Training in Clinical Research and Health Policy Dr. Maranda Kleppe DC 1 , Dr. Wren Burton DC, MPH 2 , Dr. Victoria Bensel DC, MS, MPH 3,4 , Dr. Macy Randolph DC 2 , Dr. Robert Vining DC, DHSc 1 1 Palmer College of Chiropractic, Center for Chiropractic Research, Davenport, IA, United States 2 Osher Center for Integrative Health, Harvard Medical School & Brigham and Women’s Hospital, Boston, MA, United States 3 Department of Biomedical Informatics and Data Science, Yale School of Medicine, New Haven, CT, United States 4 VA Connecticut Healthcare System, New Haven, CT, United States Purpose : Few postgraduate programs exist for chiropractors in clinical research and health policy. Method(s): We conducted an electronic survey of 18 postdoctoral trainees and program directors from 13 known U.S.-based clinical research and health policy programs affiliated with academic institutions or healthcare systems. Trainees surveyed completed an eligible program within the past 5 years (after June 2020) or were enrolled in a program as of June 2025. Surveys included questions on program resources and career development. Directors provided program characteristics and funding details. Quantitative data were analyzed descriptively while qualitative data were analyzed thematically. Results : Sixteen trainees and 7 directors participated, with response rates of 89% and 54%, respectively. Two directors did not participate due to discontinuation of program funding. Trainees identified formal mentorship (94%), academic coursework (88%), and research skill training (69%) as the most valuable resources. Directors highlighted challenges in securing or sustaining funding for their postdoctoral training program. Conclusions : Current programs facilitate key skill development, provide a foundation for careers in research and health policy, and demonstrate productivity through scholarly output. However, stable funding to support the few available programs that develop a pipeline of research and health policy professionals remains extremely limited. P 07.04 A Survey of U.S. Chiropractors Assessing the Importance and Feasibility of Quality Measures for Chiropractic Care: Exploring Differences Across Healthcare Settings Dr. Maranda Kleppe DC , Jacob McCarey MS, Sujatha Kedilaya, Robert Vining DC, DHSc Palmer College of Chiropractic, Center for Chiropractic Research, Davenport, Iowa, United States Purpose : A recently developed set of quality measures (QMs) for chiropractic care was provisionally validated by chiropractors and administrators in U.S. integrated care settings and chiropractic educational institutions. This study examined U.S. licensed chiropractors’ perceptions of the importance and feasibility of implementing QMs in varied practice settings to support further refinement and prioritization for pilot implementation. Method(s): We conducted an electronic survey of U.S. licensed chiropractors from March-May 2025 on the importance and feasibility of implementing QMs in respondent settings. We targeted recruitment efforts to ensure respondent perspectives from 1) Multidisciplinary and single discipline practices; 2) Referral and portal-of-entry access settings; 3) Low and high population density areas; and 4) primary care Health Professional Shortage Areas. Respondents rated each QM on a 5-point Likert scale (low to high) for both importance and feasibility. Measures were ranked by mean ratings and rank orders were compared across subgroups defined by the settings described above. Kendall’s Tau correlation coefficient (range: -1 to 1) was used to assess rank-order correlation between subgroups. Results : Respondents (n=869) were 69% male, with a mean of 23.8 years of experience. Sixteen percent (n=141) reported working in multidisciplinary settings, of whom 66% (n=93) reported familiarity with QMs. QM rankings differed little across respondent settings demonstrated by high (≥0.75) rank-order correlations for importance and moderate or higher (≥0.55) for feasibility. ‘Documenting adverse events’ was the highest rated measure for importance and ‘Performing an exam for a presenting problem’ was the highest rated measure for feasibility with means of 4.79 and 4.65, respectively. Of the 10 highest-rated clinical process measures, eight relate to supporting patient safety. Conclusions : Provider setting had minimal influence on measure ratings. QMs supporting safe care were consistently rated highest, suggesting pilot implementation should prioritize safety-related measures independent of practice setting characteristics. MassageNet is the largest PBRN focused exclusively on therapeutic massage and was initially established in 2009. After an enthusiastic initial start, MassageNet became essentially dormant after 2016 and establishing N=513 massage clinician members. Research collaborators were able to inquire and launch approved research studies within MassageNet beginning in late 2022/early 2023, respectively, through the MTF Research Committee. Results : By IMTRC 2025 (April 29, 2025), MassageNet had collected N=876 enrollment surveys and N=320 practice descriptor surveys from 49 US States and over 19 countries across 6 continents. MassageNet addresses longstanding challenges in massage therapy research by fostering practitioner engagement, generating real-world data, and supporting evidence-informed practice. As MassageNet enters its growth phase (2026-2029), it is poised to further integrate therapeutic massage into broader healthcare research and delivery systems. P 07.06 “Neck pain …a part of me”: A Qualitative Study of Combined Chiropractic Care and Tai Chi for Chronic Neck Pain Emma Dubinsky MS 1,2 , Jacqueline Guzman 1 , Wren M Burton 1,2 , Matthew H Kowalski 1 , Cynthia R Long 3 , Jacob McCarey 3 , Daniel Litrownik 1,2 , Kristina Twaalfhoven 1,2 , Viviane Nguyen 1,2 , Thomas J Hagen 4 , Pamela M Rist 1,2 , Robert Vining 3 , Karen Kilgore 1 , Peter M Wayne 1,2 1 Osher Center for Integrative Health, Harvard Medical School and Brigham and Women’s Hospital, Boston, MA, United States 2 Division of Preventive Medicine, Department of Medicine, Brigham and Women’s Hospital and Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 3 Palmer Center for Chiropractic Research, Palmer College of Chiropractic, Davenport, IA, United States 4 Khalsa Chiropractic, Cambridge, MA, United States Purpose : Chronic neck pain (CNP) is a significant public health concern. This gap highlights the need for an introductory elective for MD students. Students achieved greater confidence in basic palpatory diagnosis and introductory OMT applications. Four students pursued optional clinical shadowing after the course. Conclusions : The OMM elective successfully met its objectives, demonstrating enhancement in students’ understanding of osteopathic principles and whole-health patient care. The positive outcomes provide a compelling case for integrating OMM exposure into allopathic medical education to enrich students' understanding of diverse healthcare approaches, foster interprofessional understanding, and improve comprehensive patient assessment and management. P 07.08 A Sustainable Model for Implementing Massage Therapy in a Comprehensive Cancer Center Benjamin P Smith B.S Psychology 1 , Shelley White PhD 1,2,3 1 Huntsman Cancer Institute, Salt Lake City, Utah, United States 2 Huntsman Mental Health Institute, Salt Lake City, Utah, United States 3 University of Utah, Salt Lake City, Utah, United States Purpose : Integrating massage therapy into comprehensive cancer centers faces significant funding and sustainability hurdles. This presentation describes the successful, replicable model of the Huntsman Cancer Institute (HCI) Massage Therapy Program. Established in 2012 within an NCI-designated center, it serves as a framework for institutions building integrative services. By detailing solutions to operational challenges, we provide a roadmap addressing the theme “Scaling What Works” in a complex academic environment. Method(s): Presented as an organizational case study, the HCI program is a core service staffed by 11 therapists (∼6.0 FTEs). Key components include a hybrid funding model (nursing budget, philanthropy, patient revenue) and a clinical delivery model spanning the care continuum: no-cost inpatient sessions, fee-based outpatient appointments, and staff support. The program operates from a purpose-built space using standardized safety protocols, including the Tracy Walton Pressure Scale, for risk management. Results : After over a decade, the program is deeply embedded, delivering thousands of encounters annually. Its long-term sustainability is achieved through the hybrid funding model, ensuring stability and growth. Clinical integration is evidenced by provider referrals, EHR documentation, and participation in NIH-funded trials. Success includes a stable, specialized team and infrastructure optimized for integrative care, demonstrating a viable financial and operational structure. Conclusions : The HCI program provides a proven, sustainable model for integrative oncology. Success hinges on diversified funding, strong institutional support, and a multi-faceted delivery model. Looking ahead, we aim to evolve beyond a multidisciplinary approach toward enhanced interdisciplinary care, exploring shared medical appointments and groups empowering patients with self-massage and mindful body awareness. This framework offers a practical roadmap, “scaling what works” to improve accessibility and quality of care nationwide. P 07.09 Massage Therapy and Pain Outcomes within a Patient-Centered Medical Home Tracy L Rupp PhD., MAc Defense and Veterans Center for Integrative Pain Management (DVCIPM), Department of Anesthesiology, Uniformed Services University (USU), Bethesda, MD, United States. Henry M. Jackson Foundation for the Advancement of Military Medicine, Inc., Bethesda, MD, United States Purpose : Massage therapy is effective for reducing short- and long-term pain, yet its implementation and impact in real-world clinical settings remain understudied. P 07.10 System Redesign for Integrating Chiropractic Care into Safety-Net Primary Care for Low Back Pain: Identifying and Prioritizing Referral Barriers Anna Natrakul 1 , Julia Bozkurtian 1 , Asifa Rahim MPH 1 , Katherine Gergen-Barnett MD 2 , Richard Printon DC 3 , Susan Haddow MD 3 , Michael LaValley PhD 4 , Lance Laird ThD 2 , Michael A Fischer MD, MS 1 , Robert B Saper MD, MPH 5 , André Bussières DC, PhD 6,7 , Roni Evans DC, PhD 8 , Natalia E Morone MD, MS 1 , Eric J Roseen DC, PhD 1 1 Section of General Internal Medicine, Department of Medicine, Boston University Chobanian & Avedisian School of Medicine and Boston Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States 2 Department of Family Medicine, Boston University Chobanian & Avedisian School of Medicine and Boston Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States 3 Division of Integrative Medicine, Department of Medicine, Hennepin County Medical Center, Minneapolis, MN, United States 4 Department of Biostatistics, Boston University School of Public Health, Boston, MA, United States 5 Department of Wellness and Preventive Medicine, Cleveland Clinic, Cleveland, OH, United States 6 Département Chiropratique, Université du Québec à Trois-Rivières, Trois-Rivières, Québec, Canada 7 School of Physical and Occupational Therapy, Faculty of Medicine, McGill University, Montreal, Québec, Canada 8 Integrative Health & Wellbeing Research Program, Earl E. Bakken Center for Spirituality and Healing, University of Minnesota, Minneapolis, MN, United States Purpose : Few patients with low back pain (LBP) access guideline-recommended nonpharmacologic treatments after visiting a primary care provider (PCP), especially in underserved communities. We examined barriers to accessing chiropractic care following a PCP consult for LBP at safety net hospitals. Method(s): We conducted a two-phase sequential mixed methods study involving process mapping with failure modes and effects analyses (FMEA) at four primary care clinics in Minnesota (MN) and Massachusetts (MA). PCPs, clinic staff, and onsite or community-based chiropractors were invited to interviews. Each participant created a process map outlining steps for a patient with LBP to see their PCP and then a chiropractor, as well as barriers to each step. Deductive thematic analysis informed by the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research was used to combine maps. An online survey was administered to participants to prioritize barriers through FMEA by assigning each barrier a risk priority number (RPN, 1–1000), with higher scores denoting greater importance. Results : Thirty-three interviewees (8-9 per clinic) created process maps, and 71 distinct barriers were identified. One interviewee per clinic site helped select 32 important potentially modifiable barriers for FMEA evaluation. Ninety-three participants (mean age=45; 74% female; 45 PCPs, 32 staff, 11 chiropractors) completed the FMEA survey. Barriers with high RPN scores included “Insurance Uncertainty” (mean RPN: 193.6) and “Patient Knowledge Gap” (186.6). Barriers scoring high in MN but not MA clinics: “Lost Hope” (165.2) and “Weak Recommendation” (162.8). Barriers scoring high in MA but not MN clinics were: “Patient Scheduling Burden” (201.4) and “Insurance Search Burden” (193.9). Conclusions : Uncertainty about insurance coverage and patient knowledge gaps were identified as key barriers to receiving chiropractic care across health systems. Other barriers varied across sites, reflecting local organizational and contextual differences. Addressing these barriers will require tailored multi-level implementation strategies to improve access to chiropractic care in underserved primary care settings. P 07.11LB The Impact of Massage therapy on Pain and Anxiety in patients with gynecologic cancer receiving Cancer Treatment (IMPACT): A Feasibility Study Protocol Jill S. Topic Area 08: Measurement P 08.01 Measurement Applicable to All: Validating an Invariant Multidimensional Assessment of Interoceptive Awareness Short Form (MAIA–SF) Across Health and Healing Contexts Maria F Carriel MPH, Dr Janell L Mensinger PhD Nova Southeastern University, Davie, 33314, United States Purpose : Ensuring psychological measures function equivalently across populations is foundational to rigorous research and clinical practice. Measurement invariance (MI) provides a method for testing whether item meaning is comparable across groups, yet MI is often overlooked. Interoception—the adaptive integration of bodily signals—supports self-regulation and homeostatic balance. The Multidimensional Assessment of Interoceptive Awareness (MAIA) is widely used in integrative health research, but its length and evidence of bias across background characteristics have raised concerns about its utility. Prior efforts to develop a brief MAIA were made in an Eastern European sample; preliminary data suggests presence of noninvariance in US samples. To address these challenges, we validated an invariant MAIA Short Form (MAIA-SF). Method(s): Data were collected in July 2025 using CloudResearch Connect, employing US Census-matched quotas on sex, age, and racial/ethnic identity. Two independent samples of adults (N=2000) completed the original MAIA-2 plus nine pilot items. Exploratory factor analysis supported retention of seven pilot items, yielding 44 items for invariance testing. Multi-group confirmatory factor analyses with robust maximum likelihood estimation assessed configural, metric, and scalar invariance across characteristics relevant to interoceptive functioning: gender, age, eating disorder status, BMI, childhood trauma exposure, and weight stigma. Twenty-four items demonstrating strong factorial validity, local fit, and parameter stability were retained. Results : The final eight-factor model showed excellent global fit (χ 2 [224]=689.4; RMSEA=.032; CFI=.977; SRMR=.027) and strong MI across all variables (ΔCFI ≤ .003). A subsample (N=298) completed the MAIA-SF for validation and test–retest reliability. Internal consistency was adequate across subscales (ω=.71–.92), and test–retest reliability was moderate to strong (ICC=.60–.81) for seven factors. Lower ICC for Not Distracting (ICC=.44) may reflect fluctuations in behavioral responses to pain. Conclusions : The MAIA-SF provides a concise, unbiased, and psychometrically robust tool suitable for evaluating interoceptive sensibility in clinical practice and mind–body intervention research. P 08.02 Assessing Well-being among Older Adults: Acceptability, Feasibility, and Utility of a Modified Well-Being Signs Measure Dr. Sophia M. Bartels 1 , Dr. Claire E. Grant 1 , Aileen Mcgrory 1 , Dr. Catherine Dawson 2 , Dr. Eileen Dryden 1 1 Center for Health Optimization and Implementation Research, Bedford VA, Bedford, MA, United States 2 VA Bedford Healthcare, Bedford, MA, United States Purpose : Evaluating patient well-being can assist clinicians in anchoring healthcare recommendations to what matters most to their patients; however, measuring well-being is challenging. In 2021, Veterans Health Administration (VHA) researchers developed a 3-item measure called the Well-Being Signs (WBS). To optimize use of the WBS among Geriatric populations, our team identified a need to administer the WBS to caregivers and to add open-ended questions to the measure, rather than relying on the numeric scores alone. We then sought to understand the feasibility, acceptability, and utility of implementing the modified WBS within Geriatrics clinics. Method(s): In 2025, we piloted the modified WBS within two VHA Geriatrics clinics and conducted interviews with Veterans, caregivers, and clinic staff about their experiences being administered and implementing the WBS, respectively. We used rapid qualitative analysis to analyze interview transcripts. Results : The modified WBS was administered to 151 older Veterans and caregivers, with 17 agreeing to interviews about their experience. Overall, Veterans and their caregivers were able to complete the WBS and liked talking to providers about what matters most to them. However, some had challenges providing numeric responses on a 0-10 scale. Most of the 8 clinicians we interviewed thought it was feasible to incorporate the WBS into clinic flow. Additionally, they perceived that the modified WBS added value by starting a conversation with Veterans about their well-being and tracking well-being over time. However, some struggled to see its advantage over other efforts to understand what matters most to the Veteran and with knowing what to do for patients with low WBS scores; consequently, only one clinic planned to continue using the WBS beyond the pilot. Conclusions : The modified WBS holds promise in improving understanding and intervention around Geriatrics patients’ well-being; however, implementation tools are likely needed to support its clinical use and broader spread. These themes informed categorical domains for the checklist, expanded to include definitions and examples for clinical use. Topic Area 09: Mental Health P 09.01 Interoceptive Awareness as a Mechanism of Change in Touch-Based Therapies for Generalized Anxiety Disorder Benjamin P Smith B.S Psychology 1 , Becky Kinkead PhD 1,2,3 1 Huntsman Cancer Institute, Salt Lake City, Utah, United States 2 University of Utah, Salt Lake City, Utah, United States 3 Huntsman Mental Health Institute, Salt Lake City, Utah, United States Purpose : While touch-based interventions like Swedish Massage Therapy (SMT) reduce symptoms of Generalized Anxiety Disorder (GAD), the neurobiological mechanisms remain unclear. Interoception—perceiving internal bodily signals—is critical for emotional regulation and often disrupted in GAD. Addressing the theme “measure what matters,” this study investigates interoceptive awareness as a mechanism explaining therapeutic efficacy. The primary aim is to determine if baseline interoceptive awareness moderates clinical outcomes in GAD patients receiving SMT versus Light Touch (LT). Secondarily, we identify neural and autonomic correlates of changes in interoceptive awareness to provide a multi-level understanding of therapeutic effects. Method(s): Leveraging two ongoing randomized clinical trials, we recruited 80 participants (40 GAD, 40 non-psychiatric controls). Participants were randomized to six weeks of twice-weekly, 45-minute sessions of either SMT (engaging affective C-tactile pathways) or LT (engaging discriminative Aß-fibers). Interoceptive awareness was measured pre- and post-intervention via the Multidimensional Assessment of Interoceptive Awareness (MAIA-2). Anxiety severity was assessed weekly (HAM-A). Neurobiological mechanisms were explored via pre- and post-intervention resting-state fMRI and autonomic measures, including heart rate variability (HRV) and galvanic skin response. Results : With final results anticipated by the conference, we hypothesize baseline MAIA-2 profiles will moderate treatment response. Specifically, higher Body Trusting and Self-Regulation scores should predict greater symptom reduction in the SMT group, whereas LT responsiveness may hinge on different interoceptive facets. Furthermore, we expect longitudinal improvements in interoceptive awareness will correlate with functional connectivity changes in emotional regulation regions (e.g., insula, ACC) and improved autonomic balance. Conclusions : This research elucidates how touch-based therapies ameliorate GAD symptoms. Identifying distinct neurophysiological pathways moves the field beyond one-size-fits-all approaches toward personalized, evidence-based treatments. Findings will help clinicians “scale what works” by targeting interventions based on individual interoceptive profiles. This approach enhances the efficacy of integrative care, contributing to a nuanced model of whole-person health. P 09.04 Growing through Adversity: Prosocial Behaviors' Correlation with Psychological Outcomes in Marginalized Youth Saloni Dangoria BA , Elena Gareau BS, Dr. Natalia Jaramillo PhD, Dr. Jeffrey I. Gold PhD Children's Hospital of Los Angeles, Los Angeles, CA, United States Purpose : Adverse Childhood Events (ACEs) have a statistically significant correlation with internalizing and externalizing behaviors; however, possible moderators for this relationship are yet to be identified in marginalized youth. Previous literature has revealed that engaging in prosocial behaviors can diminish the clinical risk of developing internalizing and externalizing problems. This study examines the moderating role of prosocial behaviors on the relationship of ACEs on internalizing and externalizing factors. Method(s): 89 patients (ages 9-19) represent a diverse adolescent population (62.9% female, 36.0% male, 1.1% nonbinary), ethnicities (38.2% Hispanic/Latinx, 32.6% White, 7.9% Black, 7.9% Mixed, 5.6% Asian, 7.8% Unknown), and insurance types (61.8% Medi-Cal, 37.1% PPO/HMO, 1.1.% Self-Pay). Patients' caregivers completed the Pediatric ACEs Related Life Events Screener (PEARLS) and Behavior Assessment System for Children 3rd Edition (BASC-3). To investigate prosocial behaviors, the study examined BASC-3 parent-report outcomes for Adaptive Skills and each of its sub-scales (Adaptability, Social Skills, Leadership, Functional Communication, and Activities of Daily Living). Results : Pearson correlations revealed statistically significant associations between patient’s total number of ACEs and adaptive skills (p < .01), externalizing factors (p < .001) and internalizing factors (p < .001). Testing a model of moderation, adaptive functioning did not weaken the relations between ACEs and a) externalizing or b) internalizing behaviors. Conclusions : Despite ACES being highly correlated with behavioral problems and prosocial behaviors, adaptive skills did not moderate the impact of ACEs on externalizing and internalizing factors. Although results were insignificant, it is important to continue investigating methods to reduce the impact of ACEs on the psychological and behavioral profile of marginalized children. P 09.05 Better Dead Than Diagnosed? The Deadly Impact of Culturally Intertwined Stigmas on Mental Health Help-Seeking Behaviors and the Suicide Rate in India Rifa Sipai High School Las Positas College, Livermore, CA, United States Purpose : India faces a worsening mental health crisis, with suicide rates rising despite global advances in psychological care. This study investigates how culturally embedded stigma in India shapes help-seeking behaviors and contributes to suicidality. Addressing gaps in cross-cultural psychological research, it examines public, self, gendered, and structural stigma, as well as cultural beliefs and mental health literacy. Method(s): Using a qualitative design, the study combined a review of peer-reviewed literature with semi-structured interviews conducted with ten participants of Indian background (five male, five female; ages 16–40) selected through purposive sampling. Interviews explored cultural identity, perceptions of mental health, stigma experiences, help-seeking barriers, and views on suicide. Audio-recorded interviews were transcribed verbatim and analyzed using inductive thematic analysis with constant comparison and peer debriefing to enhance credibility. Results : Findings revealed four interconnected forms of stigma. Public stigma was rooted in collectivist values, framing mental illness as a source of family shame and a threat to social standing, particularly regarding marriage. Self-stigma emerged as internalized guilt and perceived weakness, leading to emotional suppression and denial. Gendered stigma disproportionately affected women, who described pressure to conceal distress to protect marital prospects. Structural stigma appeared in dismissive healthcare encounters, limited culturally sensitive care, and provider bias that discouraged psychiatric referral. Suicide was commonly interpreted as a moral or familial failure rather than a mental health outcome, reinforcing secrecy and avoidance. Together, these stigmas create multilayered barriers to care, sustaining untreated psychological distress and suicide risk. Conclusions : Effective prevention must extend beyond expanding services to include culturally informed education, anti-stigma initiatives, and systemic reform. Transforming cultural narratives and institutional practices is essential to improving equitable access to mental health care and reducing suicide vulnerability. P 10.03 Interoceptive Mechanisms of a Mobile Heart Rate Variability Biofeedback App for Disordered Eating in Healthcare Workers: Proof-of-Concept Findings Dr. Janell L Mensinger PhD , Maria Fernanda Carriel MPH Nova Southeastern University, Fort Lauderdale, FL, United States Purpose : Recent US-based datasets (Ns>1000) show over 1-in-4 adults suffer from clinically significant disordered eating. Methods for preventing subthreshold problems from becoming full-blown eating disorders are needed. Healthcare professionals suffer from high levels of burnout and stress. Chronic stress often results in maladaptive eating patterns, and research shows this was particularly pronounced during the COVID-19 pandemic. An 8-week, single-arm feasibility trial of a mobile heart rate variability biofeedback (HRVB) app supported strong acceptability and preliminary efficacy for reducing disordered eating in this population. The present study investigated potential mechanisms of change from pre- to post-intervention. Method(s): Participants were 24 healthcare workers recruited during the COVID-19 pandemic who scored in the clinically significant range on the Loss of Control Eating Scale-Brief (LOCES-Brief) (92% female, 83% non-Hispanic white, 79% nurses, Mean age=42 years; SD=11.8). We conducted mediation analyses using the MEMORE macro for repeated measures; indirect effects were evaluated via 95% Monte Carlo confidence intervals. Results : For EDEQ-7 scores, significant indirect effects were found for MAIA-2 ‘noticing’, ‘attention-regulation’, ‘emotional-awareness’, ‘self-regulation’, and ‘body-listening’ subscales. For LOCES-Brief, significant indirect effects were found only for the MAIA-2 ‘trusting’ factor, with 29% of the HRVB effect mediated. Conclusions : Findings suggest HRV biofeedback may reduce disordered eating by enhancing interoceptive skills. These results support further investigation into biofeedback as a low-cost, scalable intervention for populations at risk for dysregulated eating. Future studies should evaluate fully powered controlled efficacy trials of mobile HRVB as a preventive strategy for eating and body-related mental health concerns. P 10.06 Abbreviated Mindfulness-Based Cognitive Therapy: Enhancing Accessibility Without Compromising Efficacy Dr. Pallavi D. Visvanathan PhD 1 , Dr. Tanya M. Spruill PhD 2 , Dr. Amanda J. Shallcross ND, MPH 3 1 Manhattan Center for Mindfulness-Based Therapy, New York, NY, United States 2 NYU Grossman School of Medicine, New York, NY, United States 3 Cleveland Clinic, Cleveland, OH, United States Purpose : Mindfulness-Based Cognitive Therapy (MBCT) is an evidence-based intervention with robust support from rigorous randomized controlled trials demonstrating efficacy for preventing depression relapse and reducing depressive symptoms. However, the standard 8-week protocol requiring 2-2.5 hours per week (16-20 total contact hours) presents a significant barrier to participation and limits scalability. Time burden, scheduling conflicts, and logistical challenges consistently emerge as primary reasons for non-enrollment and attrition, particularly among working adults, caregivers, and individuals with limited resources. Method(s): In response to these accessibility challenges, our team developed MBCT-Brief, an abbreviated protocol requiring 8 contact hours—approximately 50% of the time required for the full-length protocol—while intentionally preserving core therapeutic components including mindfulness meditation practices, cognitive and behavioral exercises, participant inquiry, and home practice. Results : We leveraged principles from several evidence-based adaptation frameworks for reducing intervention length while maintaining therapeutic integrity and fidelity to MBCT's theoretical foundations. Four independent studies have evaluated MBCT-Brief across diverse populations and settings. Findings consistently demonstrate high satisfaction ratings and improved engagement (average session = 7/8 sessions) and retention (86%) compared to standard-length MBCT. Preliminary clinical outcomes show promising effects for reducing depressive symptoms and stress, with medium to large effect sizes (Cohen’s d = 0.62 - 1.2), comparable to full-length MBCT. Conclusions : These converging findings suggest that MBCT-Brief offers a viable solution to scalability challenges limiting widespread implementation of mindfulness-based interventions. By reducing time burden by half without sacrificing therapeutic integrity, this approach can reach populations previously unable to access MBCT. Importantly, this systematic abbreviation framework may be applied to other mindfulness-based interventions—such as Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR)—that face similar participation barriers. This approach represents a promising pathway toward maximizing the public health impact of evidence-based mindfulness interventions. Method(s): We conducted five rounds of online semi-structured focus group interviews on participants with migraine who had completed either online Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR) or online Headache Education as part of the randomized phase 3 clinical trial of BE WELL (Brain Education and Wellness) with Migraine. Using an interview guide, discussions explored participant views on benefits, challenges, and implementation recommendations of group-based online mindfulness and headache education. Themes emerged to be discussed include benefits and challenges: of an online intervention (accessibility, feasibility, technology); and of group-based interventions in patients with migraine (accountability, decreased isolation, disease validation, increased sense of community, unexpected migraine attacks). Participants in both groups reported impact on stigma and empowerment. Additionally, specific benefits of MBSR intervention include acute symptom management, body awareness, reduced stress reactivity, and greater self-compassion. Other factors reported that may mediate effects include impact on sleep, family, and relationships. Most participants reported overall improved disease burden and would recommend online mindfulness to others. Conclusions : The mechanisms of mindfulness may be multi-faceted, and the additional benefit of an online intervention taught in group format may provide synergistic benefits. Both mindfulness and headache education help target overall disease burden and strengthen whole-person health. Patient voices can help illuminate the rich learning of mindfulness from the lived experiences of those with significant disease burden. P 10.08 Decreased MMP-9 Gene Expression following Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction Jeffrey Greeson Ph.D. Rowan University, Glassboro, NJ, United States Purpose : A growing number of studies are now investigating changes in gene expression associated with mindfulness meditation as a plausible biological mechanism that could mediate effects on health. Whereas previous studies have mainly focused on changes in gene expression associated with the Conserved Transcriptional Response to Adversity (CTRA) or similar stress- and inflammation-related signaling pathways, no studies to our knowledge have examined specific changes in matrix-metalloproteinase 9 (MMP-9) gene expression following Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR). Method(s): Given the role of chronic stress in activating MMP-9 and the key functional role of MMP-9 in contributing to coronary heart disease risk and depression, this pilot study explored whether MBSR may impact MMP-9 gene expression in healthy, stressed adults (n=30, F=63%, White=70%, mean age=40). Whole blood samples were collected at rest and in response to acute stress in the lab, both before and after a standard, 8-week MBSR program. Conclusions : Building on prior work, these findings reveal another plausible molecular pathway through which 8-weeks of mindfulness meditation training can potentially lower MMP-9 activity, a known biomarker implicated in regulating stress physiology and associated stress-related chronic disease. Based on these significant preliminary findings from a single-arm trial, larger controlled trials are needed to replicate this effect and extend observed changes in gene expression following mindfulness training to clinically meaningful health metrics. P 10.10 Breast Cancer Survivors’ Experiences with Daily Surveys and Perspectives on a Digital, Daily Mindfulness Intervention: Insights from a Qualitative Analysis Dr Chelsea J Siwik PhD 1 , Eleni Stogianni BA 2 , Shilpa Jujjavarapu MSc 3 , Dr Patricia J Moran PhD 4 , Dr Ashley Mason PhD 4 , Dr Frederick M Hecht MD 4 1 Cleveland Clinic, Cleveland, OH, United States 2 VA Palo Alto Health Care System, Palo Alto, CA, United States 3 Palo Alto University, Palo Alto, CA, United States 4 University of California, San Francisco, San Francisco, CA, United States Purpose : Mindfulness-based interventions (MBIs) can reduce cancer-related distress. During the interview, we explored participants’ experiences and gathered feedback on a digital MBI. We used thematic content analysis to identify patterns across transcribed interviews. Results : The following themes emerged: (1) completing daily surveys promoted accountability to healthy behaviors (diet and exercise) and increased awareness of thoughts, feelings, and actions; (2) the brief, digital, daily format of the surveys was well-received, though participants valued an initial face-to-face interaction with study staff for a sense of connection; (3) a daily, digital MBI was appealing to most participants, with high interest in a 10-minute per day commitment, but participants noted concern for potential overuse of mobile devices and limited interpersonal connection. Conclusions : Delivering MBIs in a digital, daily format via text offers a promising way to reduce access barriers and appears to be acceptable to cancer survivors. However, balancing digital convenience with the connection that is typically fostered in groups warrants further study. Future research should identify strategies to preserve interpersonal connection within digital delivery models.
