Participants reported high acceptability of both interventions, with no serious adverse events. Conclusions : A combined digital CBT-I and PBH intervention was feasible, acceptable, and associated with preliminary improvements in insomnia. These findings support further investigation in a larger randomized trial. P 10.14LB Effects of MBSR on Generalized Anxiety Disorder Symptoms: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis Vinicius Vieira Neves MD, MSc 1 , Blair T Johnson PhD 2 , Nana Amma Larbi MD 1 , Rebecca Acabchuck PhD 1 , Laura Haygood 1 , Shufang Sun PhD, LP 1 , Simon Goldberg PhD 3 , Elizabeth A Hoge MD 4 , Judson Brewer MD, PhD 1 , Chen Li PhD 2 , Eric B Loucks PhD 1 1 Brown University, Providence, RI, United States 2 University of Connecticut, Storrs, CT, United States 3 University of Wisconsin-Madison, Madison, WI, United States 4 Georgetown University., Washington, DC, United States Purpose : The efficacy of mindfulness-based interventions for treating anxiety disorders has shown mixed effects in systematic reviews, but there has been little evidence synthesis of randomized controlled trials (RCTs) in the past decade. The purpose of this study was to evaluate the effects of Mindfulness-Based Stress Reduction (MBSR) on the most common form of anxiety, Generalized Anxiety Disorder (GAD). Method(s): Systematic searches of eight databases identified RCTs of MBSR for adults with GAD, diagnosed via clinical interview or validated scales. We calculated standardized mean differences (Hedges’ g) for between-group comparisons, synthesizing effect sizes using random-effects models. The study protocol was preregistered (PROSPERO CRD420251140213). Results : Across 8 RCTs (n=1,012), there was a statistically significant, moderate effect of MBSR on GAD symptoms, pooling across all control conditions (Hedges’ g=-0.46; 95%CI [-0.81,-0.11]; p=.01). Heterogeneity was substantial (I2=86.8%) but largely explained by comparison type. In comparisons against waitlist and treatment-as-usual (k=5, n=547), MBSR effects were large and statistically significant (Hedges’ g=-0.71; 95%CI [-1.12,-0.30]; p<.001). Conversely, trials employing active comparison conditions (pharmacotherapy or attention control; k=3; n=464) showed no significant difference (Hedges’ g=-0.03; 95%CI [-0.28,0.22]; p=.80). This finding was driven by two large non-inferiority trials (k=2; n=375) comparing MBSR to first-line pharmacotherapy (escitalopram) (Hedges’ g=0.07; 95%CI [-0.13,0.27]; p=.52), confirming equivalent efficacy. Heterogeneity was moderate within active comparisons (I2=44.8%) but remained high within waitlist/TAU (I2=81.6%), reflecting variability in minimal-treatment benchmarks. Conclusions : These findings indicate that MBSR is clearly superior to usual care or waitlist conditions and performs similarly to first-line pharmacotherapy. Consequently, these 'null' findings support the hypothesis of clinical non-inferiority rather than ineffectiveness, establishing MBSR as a viable, non-pharmacologic treatment option. This is particularly critical for patients who prefer non-drug interventions or discontinue medication due to adverse effects, justifying MBSR’s consideration within reimbursement and referral frameworks. P 11.02 Exploring Herb–Drug Interactions between Chinese Herbal Medicines and Anti-Inflammatory Drugs Miss Faiza Azhar NICM Health Research Institute, Western Sydney University, Sydney, NSW, Australia Purpose : The concurrent use of Chinese herbal medicines (CHMs) and anti-inflammatory drugs is common for managing chronic inflammation, yet potential herb-drug interactions (HDIs) remain underexplored and may alter therapeutic outcomes. This study focuses on investigating interactions between widely used CHMs and conventional drugs through in vitro assay, evaluating nitric oxide inhibition and cytotoxicity. The findings aim to identify synergistic or antagonistic effects, supporting safer and more effective co-administration in chronic inflammatory conditions. Method(s): Four commonly used anti-inflammatory Chinese herbs i.e., Curcuma longa L., Gingko biloba L., Panax notoginseng (Burk.), and Allium sativum L. were selected based on their pharmacological relevance and combined with anti-inflammatory drugs (aspirin, diclofenac sodium, ibuprofen and prednisolone). A total of 16 combinations were screened for nitric oxide inhibition and cytotoxicity in RAW 264.7 macrophages. Combination index (CI) analysis was employed to determine interactions. Moreover, the AI-based predictive algorithms were applied for improved interaction prediction. Results : Preliminary findings revealed synergistic interactions between Allium sativum L. and anti-inflammatory drugs, with the strongest synergy observed with prednisolone (IC 50 = 185 µg/mL), followed by ibuprofen (190 µg/mL) and aspirin (100 µg/mL), showing potent nitric oxide inhibition and cytoprotective effects (CI < 1). In contrast, diclofenac combinations were antagonistic, with A. sativum (>1000 µg/mL), Curcuma longa (342 µg/mL), Panax notoginseng (259 µg/mL), and Ginkgo biloba (150 µg/mL) all exceeding the synergy threshold (CI > 1). Conclusions : These findings demonstrated that Allium sativum L. exhibited the most promising synergistic anti-inflammatory effects when combined with prednisolone, ibuprofen, and aspirin, showing strong nitric oxide inhibition and cytoprotective potential without cytotoxicity. In contrast, diclofenac-based combinations showed clear antagonism across all tested herbal extracts. These preliminary results highlight A. sativum as a key candidate for further investigation into its underlying mechanisms using in vivo zebrafish models. P 11.03 The Effects of Supplemental Curcumin on Anxiety Symptoms in Adults: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis Dr. Monique Aucoin ND MSc 1,2 , Katherine Kartusch MSc 2 , Zoel Beal Msc 3 , Alisha Bruton 4 , Laura LaChance 1 , Joshua Goldenberg 5 1 Canadian College of Naturopathic Medicine, Toronto, ON, Canada 2 University of Guelph, Guelph, ON, Canada 3 University of Guelph, Guelph, On, Canada 4 Oregon Health & Science University, Portland, Oregon, United States 5 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, Oregon, United States Purpose : Anxiety disorders are growing in prevalence. Evidence suggests that curcumin, the primary polyphenol in turmeric, may aid in the alleviation of depression symptoms, due to its anti-inflammatory and neuroprotective properties; however, less is known about its impact on anxiety symptoms. The objective of this study was to conduct a systematic review and meta-analysis of human experimental studies assessing curcumin's impact on anxiety symptoms. Method(s): PubMed/MEDLINE and Web of Science were searched using a comprehensive search string. Eligible studies were human experimental studies that provided curcumin supplementation to adult participants and reported an anxiety symptom outcome. Studies using combination products were excluded. Article screening, data extraction, and risk of bias assessment were completed independently in duplicate. Pooled standardized mean differences (SMD) with corresponding 95% confidence intervals (CI) were calculated using random effects meta-analysis. Additionally, we converted study results to the instrument most familiar to clinicians and conducted meta-analysis. Heterogeneity was measured using the I2 statistic. Results : Of the 382 articles identified, 23 met the inclusion criteria in the systematic review; 19 randomized controlled trials were included in the meta-analysis. At the end of the intervention, compared with the control arm, participants in the curcumin treatment arm reported significantly lower anxiety symptom severity scores (SMD=-0.41; 95% CI: -0.62, -0.20; I2 =71%). When the outcome data was converted to the Beck Anxiety Inventory scale, the pooled mean difference was -2.92 (95% CI: -5.11, -0.73). Seventeen of the 22 randomized trials had a low risk of bias. Conclusions : Curcumin supplementation probably improves anxiety symptoms slightly. Method(s): The protocol for this review is registered on Open Science Framework ( https://osf.io/ufm7k/ ). The search strategy was developed to retrieve records of clinical studies, reviews, and trial protocols using combined English and transliterated terms for PC and whole herb CHM products in humans. Studies must have at least one clinical outcome. Records were reviewed independently and in duplicate and inter-rater reliability was assessed using Cohen’s K. Methodological quality will be assessed using risk of bias tools. Results : The literature search returned 2,554 deduplicated records from 10 databases. Moderate agreement was achieved at initial review (K = 0.52, overall agreement = 96%), and 76 records were moved forward for full text review. To date, 23 records have been included for final analysis and 10 articles are under final full-text review. Conclusions : This ongoing systematic review represents the most up-to-date synthesis of evidence on the use of CHM in PC since 2022, and indicates a steady increase in the volume and interest of TCM research in the field. Final extraction and synthesis of data will include study quality, intervention design, clinical outcomes including survival, symptom control, and quality of life. At the time of the 2026 Congress, we will complete evidence synthesis to convey the trends and therapeutic scope of CHM research in PC. P 11.05 American Ginseng Slices (AG Slices) in Clinical Practice: A Scoping Review of Randomized Controlled Trials Dr. Yike Song Bachelor of Arts in English , Dr. Lihong Han master degree, Dr. Tingxiu Liu master degree, Dr. Kai Liu master degree, Dr. Xiaohan Zhang master degree, Dr. Zeyang Shi bachelor degree, Dr. Yaqian Wen bachelor degree, Dr. Rihe Liu bachelor degree, Prof. Zhaolan Liu PhD Beijng University of Chinese medicine, Beijing, Beijing, China Purpose : American ginseng slices(AG slices) hold a significant position in Complementary and Alternative Medicine, traditionally used to address fatigue, cognitive decline, and metabolic disorders. Despite their widespread application, clinical evidence supporting their efficacy remains fragmented across different health conditions. This scoping review systematically examines randomized controlled trial (RCT) evidence to identify researched health conditions, summarize outcome measures, and highlight knowledge gaps, thereby providing guidance for future research and clinical practice. Method(s): This review followed established methodological frameworks for scoping studies.We systematically searched seven databases including CNKI, Wanfang, VIP, CBM, PubMed, Cochrane Library, and Embase. Literature management software was used for deduplication. Two researchers independently conducted preliminary screening and data extraction, with disagreements resolved by a third researcher. The analysis focused on three dimensions: health conditions and population characteristics, dosage forms and administration methods of AG slices, and primary clinical outcome measures. Results : This review included 27 randomized controlled trials with a total sample size of 1,429 participants.The studies originated from multiple countries, including China (15 studies) and Canada (7 studies). Analysis revealed that the top five disease areas by number of studies were hypertension (3 studies), type 2 diabetes (3 studies), cancer-related fatigue (2 studies), chronic fatigue syndrome (2 studies), and acute myocardial infarction complicated by arrhythmia (2 studies). The primary outcome measures encompassed multiple dimensions including glycemic parameters, symptom scores, physiological indicators, and laboratory tests. In terms of administration forms, capsules (12 studies) were the most common, and daily doses primarily ranged from 3-6 g (16 studies). The safety profile was generally favorable across the included studies. Conclusions : This scoping review demonstrates that American ginseng slices show the most consistent efficacy in the management of type 2 diabetes,while evidence quality in other disease areas requires further validation. Future high-quality studies are needed to further elucidate the therapeutic value of American ginseng slices. P 11.06 A Community Pharmacy Role Model for Herbal Medicine in Thailand Ms. Siwaphorn Peecharoensap M.Sc. 1 , Dr. Mayuree Tangkiatkumjai PhD 2 1 Siwaphorn Pharmacy, Angthong, -, Thailand 2 Department of Clinical Pharmacy, Faculty of Pharmacy, Srinakharinwirot University, Nakhon Nayok, -, Thailand Purpose : This study aimed to develop and propose practical roles for community pharmacists in Thailand. Method(s): This action research examined the pharmacist’s role in herbal medicines in a pharmacy to develop pharmacy roles in this field. A pilot project was conducted at Siwaporn Pharmacy from 2024 to 2025, in which the pharmacist developed a standard operating procedure (SOP) to ensure the efficacy and safety of using herbal medicines, either alone or in combination with conventional medication. Results : Siwaporn Pharmacy was the first pharmacy in Thailand to develop an SOP for prescribing herbal medicines. The owner was concerned about the rational use of herbal medicines. The SOP consisted of: (1) a checklist and recommendations for the rational use of Andrographis in mild COVID-19, (2) a checklist to prevent acute kidney injury from herbal medicines in patients with renal insufficiency, (3) a checklist sticker was placed at the product counter to guide customers before purchasing over-the counter herbal medicine, with a focus on high-risk groups susceptible to adverse effects, such as the elderly and patients with liver or kidney insufficiency, and (4) a screening process for potential herbal-drug interactions before prescribing herbal medicines or conventional medicines, particularly for medications at high risk of interactions, such as warfarin. The pharmacists used the mobile application Herbal Expert by Mayuree to access reliable information on herbal medicines before prescribing or recommending them. Conclusions : This practical model focuses on the safe and rational use of herbal medicines, including risk management strategies, providing patient guidance, and aiming to prevent serious herbal adverse effects and herb-drug interactions. Further studies should implement this model in pharmacies nationwide and evaluate its impact on clinical outcomes and patient satisfaction. P 11.08 Brain Health & the Microbiome (bMicrobiome Study): A Proof-of-Concept Study Integrating Shotgun Metagenomics, Metrology, and Multidisciplinary Analysis in Cognitive Health Dr. Leigh A. Frame PhD, MHS George Washington University, Washington, DC, United States Purpose : The gut-brain axis plays a key role in neurodegenerative disease, connecting microbial dysbiosis with inflammation, metabolism, and cognitive decline. Findings support growing evidence linking gut microbial composition and function to early cognitive decline. P 11.10 Association Between Chinese Herbal Medicine Use and Reduced Prevalence of Metabolic Syndrome: A Secondary Analysis of a Large Taiwanese Cohort Professor Chien-Yeh Hsu PhD 1 , Professor Jia-Wen Guo PhD 2 1 National Taipei University of Nursing and Health Sciences, Taipei, Taiwan, Taiwan 2 University of Utah, Salt lake city, Utah, United States Purpose : This study examined whether self-reported Chinese Herbal Medicine (CHM) use is associated with metabolic syndrome (MetS) prevalence using data from a large Taiwanese health check-up database. While CHM is widely used as complementary therapy and may improve metabolic parameters, population-based studies on CHM use and MetS prevalence are limited. Method(s): Cross-sectional data from the Mei Jau Health Management Institution, a large network of private health screening centers in Taiwan, included 38,697 adults aged ≥18 years with complete demographic, lifestyle, and clinical information. CHM use was assessed through self-administered questionnaires asking whether participants were currently using CHM. Results : The mean participant age was 40 years (SD = 13.4), and 7% (n = 2,677) reported current CHM use. MetS prevalence was 12% (n = 4,785). Conclusions : This study provides real-world evidence that CHM users were significantly less likely to have MetS after adjusting for demographic and lifestyle factors, suggesting a protective association with metabolic health. Future research should identify which specific CHM types are most beneficial for metabolic outcomes. P 11.12 A multicenter, randomized, double-blind, positive drug parallel- controlled clinical trial to evaluate the efficacy and safety of Xiao'er Fengreqing Oral Liquid in the treatment of Acute Pharyngitis/tonsillitis in Children Kai Liu 1 , Yu Du 2 , Lei Shi 1 , TingXiu Liu 1 , Hui-lan Liu 3 , Zhao-Lan Liu 1 1 Centre for Evidence-Based Chinese Medicine, Beijing University of Chinese Medicine, Chaoyang District, Beijing, China 2 University of Colorado Denver, Business School, Denver, Colorado, United States 3 The Third Affiliated Hospital of Beijing University of Chinese Medicine, Chaoyang District, Beijing, China Purpose : Pediatric acute pharyngitis/tonsillitis (PAPT) is common and largely viral, but frequently leads to unnecessary antibiotic use. Xiao’er Fengreqing Oral Liquid (XFOL), a 20-herb traditional Chinese medicine (TCM) formulation with anti-inflammatory and antiviral activity, is widely used, yet controlled evidence remains limited. Method(s): In this three-center, randomized, double-blind, parallel-group, non-inferiority trial, children aged 3–12 years with PAPT (onset ≤48 h) were allocated 1:1 to XFOL plus placebo Xiao’er Yanbian Granules (XYG) or XYG plus placebo XFOL for 5±1 days. Co-primary endpoints at Day 5 were: (1) sore-throat resolution (Wong–Baker Faces Pain Rating Scale [WBS] = 0) and (2) response rate (≥50% WBS reduction). The non-inferiority margin was 10%. Secondary outcomes included time to onset and disappearance of sore throat (WBS and TCM scores), fever resolution time, and TCM syndrome response. Safety was assessed in all treated participants. Results : All 120 randomized participants completed follow-up. Day-5 WBS response rates were 100% in both groups. Sore-throat disappearance at Day 5 was 98.3% with XFOL vs 90.0% with XYG; non-inferiority was met and the risk-difference 97.5% CI was entirely above zero, indicating superiority for this endpoint. Median time to sore-throat disappearance was shorter with XFOL (3.8 vs 4.2 days; P=0.014), and several secondary TCM throat-pain measures similarly favored XFOL. Fever resolution did not differ significantly. No XFOL-related adverse events occurred; transient mild proteinuria (1 vs 3 cases) resolved spontaneously. Conclusions : XFOL was effective and well tolerated for PAPT within an integrative-medicine framework, providing clinically meaningful symptom relief with a favorable safety profile and potential to support antibiotic-sparing care. Further larger and longer positive-controlled trials are warranted. P 11.13 Efficacy and safety of American Ginseng for Diseases Interventions: A Large-Scale Systematic Review and Meta-analyses of Randomized Controlled Trials Ms Tingxiu Liu Master of Medicine 1 , Ms Yike Song Bachelor of Arts in English 1 , Mr Kai Liu Master of Public Health 1 , Mr Zeyang Shi Bachelor of Computer Science 1 , Prof Zhaolan Liu Ph.D. in Epidemiology and Biostatistics 1 , Prof Yu Du 2 1 Center of Evidence-based Medicine, Beijing University of Chinese Medicine, Beijing, Beijing, China 2 University of Colorado Denver, Denver, Colorado, United States Purpose : American ginseng (AG), also known as Panax quinquefolius, is a prized herb originating from North America that has long been used in food and medicine. Its indications may cover diseases of the cardiovascular and endocrine systems. However, the quantity and quality of existing clinical evidence for its applications, as well as the outcomes used to evaluate its efficacy, have yet to be systematically established. Method(s): The full protocol was registered on PROSPERO (CRD420251147209) and adheres to the PRISMA statement. This study will systematically search 7 bibliographic databases, including PubMed, the Cochrane Library, Embase, CNKI, Wanfang, CQVIP, and SinoMed, for articles published before December 3rd, 2025. Only randomized controlled trials will be included in the analysis. Multiple researchers will independently perform study screening and data extraction. The search results will be imported into NoteExpress for deduplication and data extraction, and the meta-analysis will be performed in RStudio. All RCTs will be categorized according to the disease of the study population, followed by subgroup analyses based on the specific type of AG interventions. The quality of included studies will be rigorously assessed using the RoB-2 tool, and the certainty of evidence will be rated using the GRADE framework. Results : The study will be created to illustrate the current state of clinical research on AG across different diseases. Specific outcomes for different diseases will be analyzed. If substantial heterogeneity exists, its potential sources will be explored through sensitivity analyses such as leave-one-out analysis or meta-regression. Conclusions : This study will systematically review the evidence for the clinical use of American ginseng. It will summarize the efficacy and safety of American ginseng in treating various diseases, providing guidance for clinicians, researchers, and policymakers on its rational use and future research directions. P 11.14LB Herbal Decoction and Lumbar Spine Surgery in Patients With Lumbar Disc Herniation: A Real-World Study Using Linked Electronic Health Record and Claims Data Dr Yoon Jae Lee KMD, PhD 1 , Ye-Rim Yun KMD 2 , Jaeyoung Youn KMD 2 1 Jaseng Medical Foundation, Seoul, Seoul, Korea, Republic of 2 Jaseng Korean Medicine Hospital, Seoul, Seoul, Korea, Republic of Purpose : Lumbar disc herniation (LDH) is a major cause of low back pain and may require surgery when conservative treatment fails. In Korea, herbal medicine is widely used as part of non-surgical integrative care; however, its long-term association with lumbar surgery risk has not been evaluated because herbal decoctions were historically not captured in insurance claims data. Method(s): We conducted a retrospective cohort study linking electronic health records (EHRs) from four Korean medicine hospitals with National Health Insurance claims data. Patients newly diagnosed with LDH (ICD-10 M51) between 2016 and 2017 were included. The index date was defined as one year after the first hospital visit, and patients were followed until July 31, 2021. Exposure was defined as receiving herbal decoction for ≥30 days versus <30 days before the index date. Patients with red-flag conditions or prior lumbar surgery were excluded. Propensity score matching (1:1) was performed using age, sex, insurance type, Charlson Comorbidity Index, and baseline leg pain numeric rating scale. Lumbar surgery was identified using procedure codes. Kaplan–Meier analysis and Cox proportional hazards models were applied. Results : Among 6,669 eligible patients, 2,504 received herbal decoction for ≥30 days. After matching, 2,473 patients remained in each group with good covariate balance. The ≥30-day group showed a significantly lower cumulative incidence of lumbar surgery than the <30-day group (log-rank P=0.033). Across sequential Cox models, longer herbal decoction use was consistently associated with a reduced risk of surgery (hazard ratio range, 0.60–0.74) after adjustment for demographics, comorbidity, healthcare utilization, and baseline pain severity. Conclusions : In this real-world linked EHR–claims study, herbal decoction use for ≥30 days was associated with a lower risk of subsequent lumbar spine surgery in patients with LDH. These findings support herbal medicine as a potential non-surgical treatment option and warrant confirmation in prospective randomized studies. P 11.15LB Withania somnifera (Ashwagandha) Extracts Promote Resilience to Age-Related Changes in Neurological Function in Drosophila Rudo Duri M.S. 1,2,3 , Ellala Limoico 2,3,4 , Alexander Law B.S. 2,5 , Jesus Martínez B.S. 2,3 , Dr. Amala Soumyanath Ph.D. 2,3 , Dr. Doris Kretzschmar Ph.D. 2,5 1 Helfgott Research Institute, National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 2 BENFRA Botanical Dietary Supplements Research Center, Oregon Health & Science University, Portland, OR, United States 3 Department of Neurology, Oregon Health & Science University, Portland, OR, United States 4 Department of Chemistry, Portland State University, Portland, OR, United States 5 Institute of Occupational Health Sciences, Oregon Health & Science University, Portland, OR, United States Purpose : Aging-related neurological decline often manifests as impaired locomotion and cognitive function. Withania somnifera (ashwagandha) is traditionally used to support healthy aging, yet relative contributions of root- and leaf-derived constituents to functional outcomes remain incompletely understood. This study evaluated effects of W. somnifera root extracts, derived fractions, and leaf extracts on age-associated locomotor decline in a Drosophila melanogaster model. Method(s): Wild-type Canton-S Drosophila melanogaster flies aged 28–42 days were used to assess locomotor and sensorimotor function via fast phototaxis assays, with males and females analyzed separately. Aqueous (WSAq) and hydroalcoholic (WSE) leaf extracts were administered for 14 days. The aqueous root extract (WSAq-20) was fractionated into ethanol-insoluble, aqueous alkaline–soluble, aqueous acidic–soluble, and chloroform-soluble fractions. Fractions were tested at concentrations equivalent to their proportion in the parent extract. Withanolide content in extracts and fractions was measured using liquid chromatography–mass spectrometry (LC–MS). Results : Leaf extracts produced sex- and dose-dependent effects: aqueous leaf extract modestly improved phototaxis in males at 0.5 mg/g (p = 0.030) but not in females. WSAq-20 significantly improved phototaxis in aging flies of both sexes (p<0.05). Withanolides were enriched in the chloroform-soluble fraction; however, neither this fraction nor the ethanol-insoluble fraction improved phototaxis. However, aqueous alkaline and acidic fractions significantly enhanced performance, suggesting activity driven by water-soluble constituents. Conclusions : These findings demonstrate that W. somnifera root and leaf extracts modulate locomotor function in aging Drosophila in a sex- and extract-specific manner. Non-withanolide, water-soluble compounds of the root contribute to functional benefits, warranting further investigation of active compounds underlying these effects. Topic Area 13: Nutrition P 13.01 Community Gardens Improve Social Isolation...Community Perspective Terrah Keck-Kester MD 1,2 , Laurie Crawford MPM 2 , Susan Veldheer DEd, RD, LDN 1 , Kayla Rutt DPH 1 1 Penn State College of Medicine, Hershey, PA, United States 2 Penn State Health, Hershey, PA, United States Purpose : Over a billion people worldwide suffer from mental health conditions. The world health organization emphasizes that investing in mental health benefits people, communities, and economies. Social isolation and loneliness are strongly linked to mental health conditions. Community gardens (CG) have been associated with improvements in mental health, but less is known about community perceptions of the benefits of CG on social isolation and loneliness. This study explores community perceptions of CG impact on social isolation. Method(s): Two hundred and nine surveys were distributed at 18 CG in Central Pennsylvania. Survey items included questions about personal feelings of isolation reported as none (never/occasionally) vs any (some of the time/often). Dichotomous survey items included questions about sense of community, the garden as a welcoming and inclusive place, and the garden’s effect on isolation. These surveys were uploaded into Redcap and descriptive statistics were utilized to analyze the data. Results : We received 152/209 (73%) completed surveys. Of the CM that were surveyed at the CG, 40% (61/152) say that they some of the time or often feel isolated from others; 93% (141/152) of CM think that coming to the garden helps them feel less isolated from others, 99% (151/152) of CM find that the gardens are an inclusive environment, and 100% (152/152) of CM feel that the gardens give them a sense of community. Conclusions : Isolation and loneliness negatively impact health. CG have long been recognized as spaces that promote connection, purpose, and well-being. These surveys contribute to the growing body of evidence by capturing the perspectives of CM themselves. Their responses affirm that gardening is not only therapeutic, but also a powerful tool for reducing isolation and fostering social cohesion. By integrating community perceptions into our understanding of health interventions, we strengthen our ability to deliver whole-person care. P 13.02 SHOP WITH THE DOC: FEASIBILITY AND IMPACT OF CULTURAL GROCERY TOURS ON NUTRITION EDUCATION Dr. Michelle Loy MD 1 , Ellie Hsu 2 , Lianna Levine Reisner MSOD 3 1 Weill Cornell Medicine/ New York Presbyterian, New York, NY, United States 2 New York University, New York, NY, United States 3 Plant Powered Metro New York, New York, NY, United States Purpose : Grocery store tours are increasingly used for nutrition education, yet evidence on their effectiveness and outcomes remains limited. This pilot evaluated the feasibility and impact of culturally tailored grocery tours on participants’ knowledge and behaviors. Method(s): “Shop with the Doc” was piloted between February 2023 and August 2024 by a board-certified lifestyle medicine physician in collaboration with Plant Powered Metro New York, a nonprofit promoting plant-based nutrition. Four no-cost tours were conducted at Chinese and Indian markets (Ranch 99, Jmart, Patel Brothers), highlighting health benefits of culturally relevant foods. Participants were invited to complete a survey assessing experience and changes in shopping and eating habits. Metrics included attendance, demographics, and survey responses; descriptive statistics were applied. Results : Attendance ranged from 5–15 per session. Of 68 registrants, 11 completed surveys (9 attended a tour). Respondents were predominantly female (89%) and aged ≥65 (56%). Ethnic diversity included White (33%), Black/African American (22%), Asian/Pacific Islander (22%), and Declined (23%). Participants reported discovering new foods (e.g., mushrooms, bok choy, noodles) and learning benefits of vegetables, beans, ginger, and turmeric. Seventy-eight percent reported changes in purchasing behavior, increasing intake of unprocessed plant foods. Challenges included information recall and limited organic options. Most felt confident making healthier choices (67%) and were very likely to recommend the tour (89%). Suggestions included providing handouts, adding cooking demonstrations, scheduling at less busy times, offering thematic tours, and managing group size. Limitations included low response rate and potential responder bias. Conclusions : This pilot was feasible and well-received, suggesting potential for positive changes in purchasing behaviors and dietary variety. Further research is needed to confirm findings, assess long-term impact, and explore scalability of culturally tailored grocery tours as a strategy to enhance nutrition education in diverse communities. P 13.03 Educational Integration of Continuous Glucose Monitoring for Nutritional Self-Regulation Training Yulia Yusipova European University of Longevity, Hollywood, florida, United States Purpose : To evaluate the effectiveness of using continuous glucose monitoring (CGM) as an educational tool for improving self-awareness, nutritional regulation, and metabolic literacy among participants in an integrative medicine curriculum. The study aimed to explore how real-time glucose feedback can enhance learning outcomes and foster behavioral change in dietary decision-making. Method(s): The educational intervention was implemented as part of an eight-week training module within a certified program on integrative and longevity medicine. Qualitative analysis indicated enhanced emotional stability and reduced reactive eating episodes. The integration of CGM feedback into the learning process also improved engagement and comprehension of metabolic principles, with 94% of participants rating the module as “transformative” for personal or professional use. Conclusions : Integrating CGM-based biofeedback into nutrition education represents a promising method to bridge theory and behavior in integrative medicine. Real-time metabolic data enhance self-regulation, deepen understanding of individual variability, and promote lasting lifestyle changes. P 13.04 Designing Nutrition Studies for Long COVID: Qualitative Insights from a Patient-Reported Evaluations of a Ketogenic Metabolic Therapy Dr. Dana Dharmakaya Colgan MA, MS, PhD 1,2 , Dr. Lita . Buttolph PhD, DSOM, MS 1 , Dr. Diane Stadler PhD 2 , Tabitha Grow BS 1 , Dr. Heather Zwickey PhD 1 , Mary Ruddick MS,MS 3 , Dr. Todd E. Davenport DPT, PhD, MPH 4 1 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 2 Oregon Health & Science University, Portland, OR, United States 3 Enable Your Healing, Houston, Texas, United States 4 University of the Pacific, Stockton, California, United States Purpose : Long COVID affects over 400 million people worldwide and has no FDA-approved treatments. Patients often rely on self-directed strategies, making their lived experiences an essential, yet underutilized, source of evidence. Ketogenic metabolic therapy (KMT) has demonstrated mechanisms relevant to Long COVID, including improved mitochondrial energy production, reduced oxidative stress, and modulation of inflammation, but remains underexplored in this population. Our goal was to engage stakeholders who had completed a community-based, virtually delivered KMT plus lifestyle (KMT-LS) program to increase our understanding of key elements necessary to conduct a future clinical trial. Method(s): We conducted a cross-sectional, mixed-methods study of adults who completed, Enable Your Healing, a 12-week virtual program integrating KMT with lifestyle components. Recruitment emails were sent to 194 participants; 41 completed the online REDCap survey (21% response). Quantitative data were summarized descriptively. Qualitative data were analyzed using conventional content analysis. Results : Participants (mean age 41; 90% female) reported diagnoses including dysautonomia (71%) and ME/CFS (60%). Five themes emerged: Program benefits, program challenges, recommendations for optimization, and scientific considerations for future clinical trials. Conclusions : This study explored patient-reported experiences with a virtually delivered, multicomponent KMT-LS for individuals with Long COVID, ME/CFS, and dysautonomia. Findings suggest nutrition trials for Long COVID should incorporate longer intervention periods, multicomponent design, patient-centered outcomes, lived-experience integration, and tailored supports to optimize feasibility and impact. By integrating these lessons, nutritional research in Long COVID can move toward interventions that are not only mechanistically grounded, but also acceptable, feasible, and impactful in the lives of patients. P 13.05 Patients’ Experiences with a Virtually Delivered Ketogenic Metabolic Therapy for Post-infectious Dysautonomia, Myalgia encephalomyelitis/chronic fatigue syndrome, and Long COVID Indicate Feasibility, Acceptability, and Symptom Improvements: A Cross-Sectional Study Dr. Dana Dharmakaya Colgan MA, MS, PhD 1,2 , Dr Diane Stadler PhD, RD 2 , Tabitha Grow MS 1 , Dr. Heather Zwickey PhD 1 , Mary Ruddick MS,MS 3 , Dr. Todd E Davenport DPT, PhD, MPH 4 1 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 2 Oregon Health & Science University, Portland, OR, United States 3 Enable Your Healing, Houston, Texas, United States 4 University of the Pacific, Stockton, California, United States Purpose : Over 400 million people worldwide are living with Long COVID, yet there are currently no FDA-approved treatments. Method(s): A cross-sectional study was conducted to analyze quantitative data obtained from online surveys completed by individuals with post-infectious dysautonomia, myalgic encephalomyelitis/chronic fatigue syndrome (ME/CFS), and Long COVID who participated in a 12-week, remotely delivered ketogenic metabolic therapy and lifestyle (KMT-LS) intervention. Conclusions : Participants found a ketogenic metabolic therapy to be a feasible and acceptable, with reported benefits across multiple symptom domains. P 13.06 Ketogenic Metabolic Therapy for Long COVID: A Single-Arm, Open-Label Pilot Study Assessing Feasibility, Acceptability, and Preliminary Efficacy Dr Dana Dharmakaya Colgan MA, MS, PhD 1,2 , Dr. Diane E. Stadler PhD 3 , Tabitha Grow BS 1 , Dr Heather Zwickey PhD 1 , Mary Ruddick MS, MS 4 , Dr. Todd E. Davenport DPT, PhD, MPH 5 1 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 2 Oregon Health & Science University, Portland, Or, United States 3 Oregon Health & Science University, Portland, OR, United States 4 Enable Your Healing, Houston, Texas, United States 5 University of the Pacific, Stockton, California, United States Purpose : Approximately 400 million individuals are estimated to suffer from Long COVID. Ketogenic metabolic therapy (KMT) is hypothesized to modulate key mechanisms underlying Long COVID, including metabolic dysfunction, inflammation, oxidative stress, and autonomic dysregulation. This small proof of concept study aimed to explore the feasibility and acceptability of a KMT paired with lifestyle interventions (KMT-LS) for individuals with Long COVID. Method(s): We conducted a mixed-methods, single-arm pilot study to evaluate a community-based, 12-week, remotely delivered KMT-LS intervention. Eligible adults met criteria for Long COVID with post-infectious dysautonomia and myalgic encephalomyelitis/chronic fatigue syndrome (ME/CFS). Feasibility and acceptability were assessed using predefined metrics of enrollment, retention, adherence, satisfaction, and safety. Exploratory outcomes at post-intervention and 6-month follow-up examined changes in symptoms and functioning using minimal clinically important difference (MCID) thresholds and non-parametric paired-sample t-tests (Wilcoxon). All data were collected and managed using the Research Electronic Data Capture (REDCap) platform. Results : A total of 11 participants completed the study, meeting benchmarks for feasibility and acceptability. All achieved and maintained nutritional ketosis (β-hydroxybutyrate > 0.5 mmol/L). MCID were observed in fatigue, mood, social and physical function, sleep, and pain (PROMIS-29; Duke Activity Scale), autonomic regulation (Malmo POTS Symptom Scale), breathlessness (Modified MRC Dyspnea Scale), and life satisfaction (Satisfaction with Life). Statistically significant improvements at postintervention were reported in fatigue (p=.03), sleep (p=.04), life satisfaction (p=.01), and autonomic regulation (p=.04), and at 6-month follow-up in fatigue (p=.05), social function (p=.03), sleep (p=.03), autonomic regulation (p=.02), pain interference (p=.04), and physical functioning (p=.008). Conclusions : This pilot study demonstrates that a virtually delivered 12-week KMT-LS is feasible, acceptable, and safe for individuals with Long COVID presenting with ME/CFS and post-viral dysautonomia. High adherence and participant satisfaction, coupled with preliminary signals of clinical improvement, support the rationale for larger controlled trials to investigate efficacy and mechanistic pathways of KMT-LS in post-infectious chronic illness. P 13.07 Broad-Spectrum Micronutrient Supplementation in Childhood ADHD: Two Independent RCTs Mr. Dallin Hardy M.Sc Hardy Nutritionals, Raymond, Alberta, Canada Purpose : Academic publishing often fragments data from a single study across multiple papers, obscuring the full research narrative. Readers may also miss connections between an original RCT and replication studies. Furthermore, meta-analyses rarely narrow their scope to two or three studies of an identical intervention. This poster consolidates results from two similar RCTs using the same intervention in comparable populations into a single cohesive narrative. Method(s): The Methods section of this poster consolidates the methodological approaches employed across the two double-blind, placebo-controlled trials and their associated ancillary publications, providing a unified overview of study designs, procedures, and analytical frameworks. Results : The Results section of this poster presents the key findings from the two double-blind, placebo-controlled trials and their associated ancillary publications, presenting outcome measures, statistical and safety data including one-year follow-up after each RCT. Conclusions : These two independent RCTs demonstrate that broad-spectrum micronutrient supplementation effectively improves global functioning, emotional dysregulation, aggression, and anxiety in children with ADHD. The micronutrients were safe and well-tolerated, with no significant side effects compared to placebo, even at high doses (9-12 capsules daily). About 50% of children showed significant improvement within 2 months, increasing to 66% by 4 months, and ongoing benefits were sustained for at least a year in responders with no emergence of adverse effects long-term. discontinuation often led to regression, indicating the need for continued use. Global measures were more sensitive to micronutrient effects than core ADHD symptom scales, suggesting that the micronutrients are not a targeted treatment but rather improve ADHD impairment indirectly by conveying pervasive holistic benefit. In addition to improving symptomatology to a clinically significant degree for a majority of participants, including full remission in the long term, micronutrients enhanced physical growth. Meanwhile, medications showed no better ADHD management but were associated with significantly worsened anxiety in addition to the risk of stunting. These high quality, replicated studies confirm the safety and efficacy of broad-spectrum micronutrients for ADHD populations highly representative of clinical practice. P 13.08 RCT of Broad-Spectrum Micronutrient Supplementation: Outcomes for Maternal Depression and Infant Neurodevelopment Mr. Dallin Hardy M.Sc Hardy Nutritionals, Raymond, Alberta, Canada Purpose : Academic publishing often fragments data from a single study across multiple papers, obscuring the full research narrative. This poster compiles the significant findings from the NUTRIMUM trial and its subsequent observational follow-up studies, investigating the efficacy and safety of broad-spectrum micronutrients (BSM) for antenatal depression and their impact on maternal and infant outcomes. The RCT phase focused on maternal depression, global functioning, and the predictors of a positive response to BSM during pregnancy. Exploration of potential mechanisms included both blood and stool analysis. Subsequent analyses explored both maternal and infant birth outcomes as well as infant development, and postnatal maternal mood. Method(s): The Methods section of this poster consolidates the methodological approaches employed in the double-blind, placebo-controlled trial and seven associated ancillary publications, providing a unified overview of study designs, procedures, and analytical frameworks. Results : The Results section of this poster presents the key findings from double-blind, placebo-controlled trial and the associated ancillary publications, presenting outcome measures, statistical and safety data including one-year follow-up for mother and infant. Conclusions : The NUTRIMUM RCT and follow-up studies highlight the significant benefits of broad-spectrum micronutrient supplementation for mothers with antenatal depression and their infants. The NUTRIMUM trial showed that micronutrients outperformed standard recommendations in improving maternal functioning and self-perceived health, with no observed harm. Follow-up studies confirmed that micronutrient exposure reduced risks linked to maternal depression, and yielded better infant outcomes such as improved gestational age, birth length, and neurobehavioral performance compared to antidepressant exposure. Infants exposed to micronutrients exhibited temperament similar to those from typical pregnancies, with no adverse effects in the first year. Notably, mothers receiving micronutrients had significantly lower post-partum depression rates, indicating a lasting restorative effect. These findings emphasize the critical role of nutrition in pregnancy for optimal maternal and infant brain health. Broad-spectrum micronutrients may offer a safe and effective alternative, potentially surpassing antidepressants. This research sets a strong foundation for larger, more diverse studies to establish nutritional supplementation as a primary option, enhancing choice and outcomes for families. P 13.10 Pilot testing of a diet cost calculation process for dietary counselling studies Monique Aucoin ND MSc 1,2 , Hainan Yu 3,4 , Melissa Murphy 5,6 , Nicole Yoannou 1 , Laura LaChance 7 , Kieran Cooley 7,8,9 , Patricia Herman 10 1 Canadian College of Naturopathic Medicine, Toronto, ON, Canada 2 University fo Guelph, Guleph, ON, Canada 3 Ontario Tech University, Oshawa, On, Canada 4 Canadian Memorial Chiropractic College, Toronto, ON, Canada 5 California State University San Bernardino, San Bernardino, CA, United States 6 Bastyr University, San Diego, CA, United States 7 Canadian College of Naturopathic Medicine, Toronto, On, Canada 8 Southern Cross University, East Lismore, NSW, Australia 9 University of Toronto, Toronto, ON, Canada 10 RAND Corporation, Santa Monica, CA, United States Purpose : Nutrition is a significant modifiable risk factor for the development and progression of many illnesses; however, it is recognized that adherence to dietary recommendations can be poor. One often-cited barrier to adherence is the perceived cost of healthy food. While many diet diary templates exist, none have been developed to capture the data needed for calculating the cost of a diet consumed during a diet intervention study. Method(s): The objective of the present project was to develop and pilot test 1) a novel diet diary template with instructions, and 2) alternative approaches for calculating diet cost, which can be used in clinical trials to measure the difference in cost between baseline and intervention diets. We created a draft diet diary and instructions which were used by participants for one week. We used the data to test the feasibility of calculating weekly diet cost. Two methods were used: calculation of the cost based on the actual price of the items purchased (Method 1), calculation using standardized prices (Method 2). Acceptability of the diet diary and instructions was assessed by conducting participant interviews. The methods to calculate costs were compared in terms of feasibility of cost calculation and researcher time needed. Results : Of 15 participants enrolled, 14 completed the interview and 12 submitted a complete diet diary. All participants reported that the process was acceptable or highly acceptable. Participant feedback was used to guide changes to the diet diary and instructions. Costs were calculated for 85.3% and 96.3% of food items using Method 1 and 2, respectively. The time to complete each method per participant, per week was 6.5 and 5 hours, respectively. Participants who gave feedback confirmed that the edits addressed their previous concerns. Conclusions : The diet diary and instructions were acceptable to participants and feasible for the collection and costing of diet data. P 13.12 Cystic Fibrosis Healthy Eating Series: A Program series developed to provide practical skills to patients and their families Mr. Luc A Santilli BA, BS 1 , Ms. Robin Miccio MS, LMT 1 , Dr. Maria R Mascarenhas MBBS 1,2 1 The Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, Philadelphia, PA, United States 2 the Perelman School of Medicine at the University of Pennsylvania, Philadelphia, PA, United States Purpose : Nutritional management is central to Cystic Fibrosis (CF) care. To promote sustainable behavior change, it is critical to engage the entire family in learning how to prepare simple, nutritious, and enjoyable meals that align with CF dietary needs. Method(s): In partnership with the CHOP CF Family Partner Group, CF specialists, nutritionists, and a medical chef educator, we developed a pilot virtual interactive culinary medicine series for CF families. Survey findings showed strong results (measured by % satisfaction): recipe relevance 83.5%, class duration 90%, virtual format 91%, likelihood of recipe adoption 92%, and prior recipe reuse 38%. Conclusions : A virtual culinary medicine program for CF families was feasible and well-received. Future sessions will focus on increasing participation and incorporating more budget-friendly, family-oriented recipes that balance nutrition, simplicity, and flavor. P 13.14 Reevaluating the Role of Dietary patterns in Depression Management: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis of Randomized Controlled Trials Ms. Rahele Tavakoly PhD (Doctorate) 1,2 , Ms. Alina Moosburner Master 1,2 , Mr. Dennis Anheyer PhD (Doctorate) 1,2,3 , Mr. Holger Cramer PhD (Doctorate) 1,2 1 Institute of General Practice and Interprofessional Care, Tübingen, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 2 Robert Bosch Center for Integrative Medicine and Health, Stuttgart, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 3 Department of Psychology and Psychotherapy, Witten, North Rhine-Westphalia, Germany Purpose : Background: Emerging interest in nutritional psychiatry has spurred numerous clinical investigations into the potential of dietary interventions for managing depressive disorders. However, the efficacy of specific dietary patterns remains unclear. Objective: To critically assess and synthesize current evidence from randomized controlled trials (RCTs) evaluating the effects of structured dietary patterns interventions on clinical outcomes in adults diagnosed with depressive disorders. Method(s): A comprehensive literature search was conducted in PubMed/MEDLINE, Embase, Cochrane CENTRAL, PsycINFO, Scopus, and ProQuest from inception to April 30, 2024. Eligible studies were RCTs comparing the effects of defined dietary patterns with active or passive control conditions on depressive symptoms in clinically diagnosed adults. Secondary outcomes included remission rates, health-related quality of life (HRQoL), and adverse events. Data were pooled using a random-effects model, and results were reported as standardized mean differences (SMDs) with 95% confidence intervals (CIs). Risk of bias was assessed using the Cochrane RoB-2 tool, and the certainty of evidence was evaluated via the GRADE framework. Results : Five RCTs (n = 952) focusing exclusively on the Mediterranean diet (MD) were identified. Participants consisted solely of individuals with major depressive disorder or subclinical depression. Meta-analysis revealed no statistically significant short-term benefit of MD over active controls (SMD = −1.25; 95% CI: −5.11 to 2.61) or passive controls (SMD = −0.22; 95% CI: −0.74 to 0.29) in reducing depression severity. Similarly, short-term MD showed no significant impact on HRQoL when compared with active controls (SMD = 0.71; 95% CI: −3.38 to 4.79). Intermediate and long-term data were consistent with these findings. The overall risk of bias for the included studies was rated “some concerns”, and most of the outcome’s results showed “very low” certainty of evidence. Conclusions : Current evidence from RCTs does not support a clinically meaningful benefit of Mediterranean diet interventions for treating depressive disorders. These results highlight the need for rigorously designed trials exploring diverse dietary patterns frameworks, and longer follow-up durations. Registration: This review was prospectively registered in PROSPERO (CRD42024541885). Pathway maps illustrate vitamin and mineral cofactor influence on metabolic processes and enzyme efficiency. Results : The objective of this presentation is to achieve the following results: 1. Attendees will understand the definition, relationship, rationale and methodology for setting nutrient reference values; EAR, RDA, UL, NOAEL, and LOAEL. 2. Attendees will learn additional sources of credible nutritional information and the guidelines afforded to them for clinical applications. 3. Attendees will learn how essential vitamin and mineral cofactors influence metabolic processes and enzyme efficiency. Conclusions : This material reaffirms the centrality of nutrient reference values in public health and clinical nutrition while emphasizing the necessity of nuanced interpretation helping to improve nutritional evaluation and intervention strategies. In summary, this presentation provides a forward-looking framework that bridges established dietary reference values with evolving concepts of metabolic regulation, thereby guiding more precise, evidence-based approaches to nutrition science and practice. Method(s): A structured 19-item survey was generated to gain a holistic perspective of the culinary medicine programming offered within health professional education at each Osher Center. The authors compiled the responses to explore commonalities and distinctions across the Osher Centers. The quantitative data were analyzed using descriptive statistics, and a subset of authors thematically grouped the open-ended qualitative responses. The authors reviewed these findings as a committee to critique findings and collectively determine results. Results : Preliminary data show culinary medicine programs across the five Osher Centers target numerous academic audiences within health professions, indicating a need for curricula that expose health professionals along their academic journey. While program duration and modalities vary, all emphasize experiential learning, interprofessional teaching teams, and translation of nutrition knowledge into practical, patient-centered skills focused on whole health. Conclusions : The Osher Center culinary medicine programs align with key elements identified in recent scoping reviews of culinary medicine education within medical schools. Their integration within the Osher Collaborative’s mission for whole health transformation offers a distinctive, scalable model that prioritizes measurable outcomes in nutrition and lifestyle education. This approach equips future health professionals with evidence-based skills to advance prevention and whole-person care while expanding effective, sustainable practices across clinical and community settings. P 13.17 Bridging the Nutrition Education Gap: A Trainee-Led Food as Medicine Elective in Medical Training Seema Sajjan Bachelor of Science , Zephelline Huerta Bachelor of Arts, Dr. Alia Badawi Doctor of Medicine, Dr. Osha Osha Chesnutt-Perry Doctor of Medicine, Dr. Tanya Datta Doctor of Medicine Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai, New York, NY, United States Purpose : Chronic noncommunicable diseases (NCDs) account for 74% of global deaths, many of which are diet-sensitive and preventable. This trainee-led initiative demonstrates how embedding nutrition science and cultural awareness can prepare future clinicians to deliver patient-centered, evidence-based counseling. Broader implementation may improve health equity, foster trust among diverse communities, and strengthen the role of nutrition in clinical care. This framework serves as a model for advancing integrative, preventive medicine education across medical institutions. P 14.03 Comparing Reiki and Mindfulness for Chronic Knee Pain: Results from a Placebo-Controlled Randomized Clinical Trial Kennedi Childs , Dr. Adam Hanley PhD Florida State University, Tallahassee, FL, United States Purpose : Knee osteoarthritis (KOA) is a leading cause of chronic pain and disability. Pharmacologic options provide temporary relief and carry risks, highlighting the need for scalable nonpharmacological interventions. Reiki, a biofield therapy, and mindfulness meditation show promise for pain management but lack rigorous evaluation. This trial evaluated the efficacy of Reiki compared to sham Reiki (placebo), mindfulness meditation, and waitlist control for reducing KOA symptom severity. Method(s): In a four-arm, randomized controlled trial ( NCT05541718 ), 164 adults with physician-confirmed KOA were randomized (1:1:1:1) to Reiki, sham Reiki (Feiki), mindfulness meditation, or waitlist control. Active interventions consisted of four weekly 30-minute sessions. Primary outcome was Western Ontario and McMaster Universities Osteoarthritis Index (WOMAC) total score, assessed at baseline, 1-month, and 2-month follow-up. Analyses included mixed-effects models and responder analyses (≥30% reduction in symptoms). Results : A significant Condition × Time interaction emerged (p < .05). At 2-month follow-up, Reiki (d = 0.29, p = .023) and mindfulness (d = 0.46, p < .001) participants reported significantly greater symptom reduction than waitlist, with effects persisting through follow-up. Reiki reduced KOA symptoms by 32% (clinically meaningful), comparable to mindfulness (38%) and greater than Feiki (20%) or waitlist (13%). Responder rates (≥30% symptom reduction) were 55% for Reiki and mindfulness, compared to 33% for Feiki and 36% for waitlist. Retention was excellent (96%). No adverse events were reported. Conclusions : Reiki and mindfulness significantly reduced KOA symptoms over two months, with effects exceeding placebo and waitlist. Reiki demonstrated strong feasibility and acceptability, supporting its potential as a complementary pain management approach for chronic knee pain. Method(s): The BE WELL with Migraine Champion Providers Network™ supports clinician referrals across the United States into a remotely conducted mindfulness clinical trial.. Participant trial recruitment includes community outreach and clinician referrals. The primary study team obtains informed consent and collects all study outcomes. Clinicians interested in referring patients register for the Network via a REDCap survey. The ongoing clinical trial allows for recruitment of trial participants and Network members through 2026. Conclusions : The BE WELL with Migraine Champion Providers Network™ models the development and implementation of a national clinician referring network.. While growth and implementation of a national network takes time, our results demonstrate the yield of appropriate referrals eligible for randomization was more than double community recruitment. P 14.06 A Patient Headache Educational Program to serve as a Control Intervention for Non-pharmacological Studies in Migraine Ms. Kristina Twaalfhoven BA 1,2 , Ruth Wolever PhD 3 , Pamela Rist ScD 1,2 , Peter Wayne Ph.D. 1,2 , Carolyn A Bernstein MD 1 , Karen Kilgore PhD 1 , Kena McDermott DC 4,5 , Meredith Beaton-Starr MS, OTR/L 1 , Dennis Munoz Vergara DVM, MSc, PhD 1,2 , Robert Vining DC, DHSc 6 , Matthew H Kowalski DC 1 , Jason Sico MD, MHS 4,5 , Cynthia Long PhD 6 , Viviane Nguyen BS 1,2 , Macy Randolph DC 1,2 1 Osher Center for Integrative Health, Harvard Medical School and Brigham and Women’s Hospital, Boston, MA, United States 2 Division of Preventive Medicine, Department of Medicine, Brigham and Women’s Hospital and Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 3 Vanderbilt University Medical Center, Nashville, TN, United States 4 Veterans Affairs Medical Center, West Haven, CT, United States 5 Yale University, New Haven, CT, United States 6 Palmer Center for Chiropractic Research, Palmer College of Chiropractic, Davenport, IA, United States Purpose : As interest in non-pharmacologic treatments for migraine prevention increases, there is an urgent need to evaluate if these treatments are well-designed randomized trials. However, designing a “control” for non-pharmacologic treatments is complex. One solution is to design credible educational programs to use as comparators that partially control for natural history and patient engagement, attention and positive expectancy. Purpose: to develop and validate through a modified Delphi process a “Headache Health Education” (HHE) program to be used as the control arm in a pilot study of multi-modal chiropractic care for episodic migraine. Method(s): Leveraging existing patient educational materials, we drafted an initial HHE program with 14 modules covering migraine-related topics. Designed to be delivered by research assistants in a one-to-one video conference, modules provide education using a slide deck and guided conversation, grounded in adult learning theory. The script-based sessions are informed by tenets of polyvagal theory and motivational interviewing. The initial program was validated using a Delphi process. A panel of subject matter experts provided ratings and qualitative feedback regarding key elements of the control intervention. A second round of the Delphi process was used following refinement of the HHE program. Results : Initial endorsement of the HHE program was strong but included considerable variability. While agreement with most statements on the Delphi survey averaged greater than 6 (out of 7), there was a wide range of scores. After refinement of the program, endorsement improved with lower variability during the second round of the Delphi process. Improvements included two new topics, new videos, and training for research assistants in skills to engage participants. Conclusions : Development of an HHE program to use as a comparator in a randomized control trial of a non-pharmacological treatment for migraine is feasible. Studies underway will examine the fidelity and acceptability of program delivery and elicit feedback from study participants. P 14.07 Engagement in mindfulness-based psychotherapy among migraine patients with comorbid trauma and depression: Implications for integrative care Dr. Hallie Tankha PhD 1,2 , Ms. Narissa P. McCarty MS 2 , Dr. Jacob Hill ND, MS 1,2 , Dr. Amanda J Shallcross ND, MPH 1,2 1 Cleveland Clinic Lerner College of Medicine of Case Western Reserve University, School of Medicine, Cleveland, Ohio, United States 2 Cleveland Clinic, Cleveland, Ohio, United States Purpose : Posttraumatic stress disorder (PTSD) and depression are highly prevalent among individuals with migraine and significantly worsen migraine-related outcomes. However, first-line migraine treatments do not address comorbid mental health symptoms, representing a critical gap in clinical care. Mindfulness-based psychotherapy offers a promising integrative approach to address migraine and mental health symptoms simultaneously. Understanding treatment-engagement patterns in patients with complex symptom presentations can inform patient-centered care. Method(s): Adults with migraine (n=854) completed screening measures for a “skills-based psychological treatment study:” PTSD Checklist for DSM-5 (PCL-5), Patient Health Questionnaire (PHQ-8), and a single item assessing current engagement in mindfulness-based psychotherapy. We examined the prevalence of comorbid PTSD-depression symptoms and their relationship to mindfulness-based psychotherapy engagement. Results : Overall, 32.1% (n=274) met criteria for probable PTSD (PCL-5≥31) and 50.4% (n=430) endorsed moderate to severe depression (PHQ-8≥10), with 28.2% (n=241) endorsing both. Across the entire sample, 16.3% (n=139) reported mindfulness-based psychotherapy engagement. Findings showed increased engagement as mental health symptoms increased: 10.2% (n=40/391) among those with neither symptom elevation, 17.5% (n=33/189) with elevated depression only, 21.2% (n=7/33) with elevated PTSD only, and 24.5% (n=59/241) with both elevated symptoms. Participants with both PTSD and depression elevations were 2.84 times more likely to engage in mindfulness-based psychotherapy than those with neither. Conclusions : Individuals with migraine showed graded engagement in mindfulness-based psychotherapy based on symptom severity, with highest engagement among those with comorbid PTSD and depression. This suggests that mindfulness-based approaches may be particularly attractive to individuals with complex mind-body presentations. Mindfulness-based approaches offer integrated treatment that aligns with patients’ lived experience of migraine as impacting both physical and mental wellbeing. These findings also highlight a critical gap in current migraine care: trauma and depression must be addressed within migraine treatment rather than as separate concerns. Mindfulness-based psychotherapy offers an accessible patient-centered and trauma-informed approach for this integrated care model. P 14.09LB The relative contributions of pain distribution, intensity, and psychological factors to physical function in multisite pain: an exploratory cross-sectional analysis Wren Burton DC, MPH 1 , Cynthia Long 2 , Jacob McCarey 2 , Robert Vining 2 , Brad Manor 3 , Jeffrey Hausdorff 4,5 , Matthew Kowalski 1 , Yan Ma 1 , Peter Wayne PhD 1 1 Osher Center for Integrative Health, Brigham and Women’s Hospital, Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 2 Palmer Center for Chiropractic Research, Palmer College of Chiropractic, Davenport, IA, United States 3 Hinda and Arthur Marcus Institute for Aging Research, Boston, MA, United States 4 Sagol School of Neuroscience and Department of Physical Therapy, Faculty of Medical and Health Sciences, Tel Aviv University, Tel-Aviv, NA, Israel 5 Department of Orthopedic Surgery, Rush Alzheimer’s Disease Center, Rush University Medical Center, Chicago, IL, United States Purpose : Multisite musculoskeletal pain is highly prevalent, and several pathways may explain its impact on physical function. Conclusions : Greater global pain intensity was associated with worse composite function, with number of pain regions contributing somewhat to this relationship. Psychological factors and pain interference contributed minimally. These findings call for further longitudinal research exploring potential mediating relationships and exploring multimodal and multidisciplinary approaches which address pain, functional outcomes, and other related elements of biopsychosocial health. P 14.10LB Changes to Pain, Functioning, Pain Catastrophizing, and Posttraumatic Stress Disorder of Life Following a 5-Session Pain-Sensitive Teleyoga Class for Veterans with Chronic Pain Dr. Belle Zaccari PsyD VA Portland Health Care System, Portland, OR, United States. P 14.11LB Central Sensitization and Embodied Connection as Mechanistic Targets Linking Fibromyalgia and Complex PTSD Symptoms: Somatic and Affective Pathways Ms Shalynn M Murphy MS , Dr. Janell L Mensinger PhD Nova Southeastern University, Fort Lauderdale, FL, United States Purpose : Fibromyalgia and complex posttraumatic stress disorder (CPTSD) frequently co-occur, yet the mechanistic pathways linking chronic pain conditions to trauma-related symptom severity remain poorly understood. Integrative health models suggest that central sensitization, positive embodied experiences (e.g., connection, safety, agency), and emotion regulation may represent modifiable intervention targets across pain and trauma contexts. Method(s): Using a large U.S. census-matched sample (N=2000), we examined whether fibromyalgia diagnosis was associated with greater CPTSD symptom severity (International Trauma Questionnaire) and tested a theoretically driven serial mediation model. Central sensitization (Central Sensitization Inventory), embodied connection (Brief Experiences of Embodiment Scale), and difficulties with accepting emotions (DERS-Non-acceptance) were specified as sequential mediators using PROCESS (v4.2), controlling for age, gender, race, and BMI. Indirect effects were estimated with percentile bootstrapped (5000 samples) 95% confidence intervals. Results : Individuals reporting a fibromyalgia diagnosis (N=89, 4.5%) reported higher CPTSD symptom severity (B=4.09, SE=1.10, p<.001), independent of age, gender, race, and BMI. Central sensitization emerged as the primary and most robust upstream mediator (explaining ∼80% of the total indirect effect), with additional significant indirect effects through reduced embodied connection and emotion acceptance as downstream mediators of CPTSD symptoms. The full serial pathway—fibromyalgia → central sensitization → (dis)embodiment → emotion (non)acceptance → CPTSD—was significant. Sensitivity analyses demonstrated identical mediation structures when migraine, TMJ, or pain intensity ratings were substituted for fibromyalgia diagnosis. Given that bidirectional effects between trauma history and pain are well established, we also examined alternative pathway specifications to evaluate the robustness of the hypothesized ordering. Conclusions : Although cross-sectional, these findings identify statistically robust, theoretically coherent pathways linking pain-related conditions to CPTSD symptom severity. Results support central sensitization, embodied connection, and emotion acceptance as promising, intervention-relevant targets for integrative, trauma-informed approaches to chronic pain and CPTSD. Longitudinal interventional studies using mind-body strategies (e.g., biofeedback, yoga) to target somatic experiences and embodied processes are needed. Topic Area 15: Provider Well-Being P 15.01 The Compassion Champion Model: A Peer- Led Framework for Cultivating and Sustaining Compassion in Organizations Sivaranjani Penna MD 1,2 , Melanie Bateman RN, BSN 1,2 1 Welugu, Narberth, PA, United States 2 Bryn Mawr Hospital, Bryn Mawr, PA, United States Purpose : Despite strong evidence linking compassionate healthcare to improved clinical outcomes, enhanced patient experience, clinician well-being, reduced burnout, lower costs, and decreased medicolegal risk, compassion remains underprioritized across healthcare organizations. There is a pressing need for models that embed compassion into daily practice, transforming it from an aspirational ideal into a normative component of healthcare culture. We propose the Compassion Champion Intervention, an integrative model that harnesses healthcare professionals’ innate capacity for humanitarian care. Through structured training, peer reflection, and brief compassion practices embedded in daily workflow, this model seeks to cultivate and sustain compassion as a foundation of clinician well-being, patient connection, and organizational culture. Method(s): To evaluate feasibility and impact, a nine-month mixed-methods framework is proposed. Primary outcomes include compassion, burnout, feasibility, and acceptability; secondary outcomes include patient experience and organizational engagement. The intervention comprises three phases: (1) identifying professionals who exemplify compassionate care (top quartile on a compassion scale (2) training them as Compassion Champions through six weekly 60-minute sessions on compassion, reflection, and communication (3) empowering them to lead daily huddles and monthly Compassion Circles for four months, fostering reflection, barrier-solving, and peer support, before transitioning to unit-led compassion circles and weekly huddles. Quantitative data will be analyzed using mixed-effects modeling, and qualitative reflections through thematic analysis. Results : The intervention is expected to enhance compassion, reduce burnout, and improve patient experience. Qualitative findings are anticipated to reveal increased self-awareness, emotional regulation, and confidence in integrating compassion into clinical practice. Integration analysis will map behavioral and cultural changes, and highlight mechanisms of sustained compassion. within organizations. Conclusions : The Compassion Champion Intervention offers a pragmatic, scalable framework for embedding compassion within healthcare systems. By transforming compassion from an individual virtue into a shared practice, it aligns clinician well-being with patient-centered care and organizational culture, supporting sustainable, system-wide compassion. P 15.02 The Micro Self-Reflection Initiative: A Feasible, Low-Burden Approach to Cultivate Compassion and Well-Being in Healthcare Professionals Sivaranjani Penna 1,2 , Melanie Bateman 1,2 1 Welugu, Narberth, PA, United States 2 Bryn Mawr Hospital, Bryn Mawr, PA, United States Purpose : Compassionate healthcare enhances patient outcomes, adherence, and clinician well-being while reducing burnout. Yet, structured compassion training is rarely implemented and often unsustainable due to workload and time constraints. There is a need to move from didactic teaching to brief, reflective practices that integrate compassion into daily routines. The Micro Self-Reflection Initiative addresses this gap by leveraging clinicians’ inherent compassion and natural pauses in workflow to cultivate real-time reflection. Method(s): Grounded in self-compassion and mindfulness, the initiative introduces one-minute micro-reflections within daily routines to strengthen emotional awareness, compassion for self and others, and overall well-being. Healthcare professionals aged 18 and older will complete baseline, post-intervention, and three-month follow-up surveys assessing reflection habits, self-compassion (Self-Compassion Scale), compassion toward others (Healthcare Provider Compassion Scale) and burnout (Maslach Burnout Inventory-Human Services Survey).. Following a 30-minute virtual orientation, participants engage in four one-minute reflections daily for eight weeks, supported by personalized reminders and guided prompts. Change in self compassion, compassion to others, well-being, burnout, feasibility, acceptability, perceived benefits, and sustained impact will be evaluated using mixed methods. A preliminary survey was conducted to assess early feasibility and acceptability. Results : Among 32 respondents, feasibility was strongly supported (56.3% agree; 15.6% strongly agree) with moderate acceptability (46.9% agree; 9.4% strongly agree). Neutral responses (25% feasibility; 37.5% acceptability) indicate opportunities to enhance engagement and understanding. Positive effects are anticipated across self- and other-directed compassion. Broader studies are needed to assess long-term impact. Conclusions : This conceptual framework suggests that brief, structured micro-reflections are feasible and may enhance compassion and emotional regulation in demanding clinical environments. While acceptance requires further strengthening, integrating one-minute reflections into daily routines offers a low-burden, sustainable model to foster compassion, reduce burnout, and support well-being across healthcare settings. P 15.03 Integrative Medicine in Orientation Week: Preparing Students for the Demands of Medical Training David Pogosyan University of Arizona College of Medicine - Tucson, Tucson, AZ, United States Purpose : The transition to medical school introduces considerable academic and personal demands, yet early curricula often lack structured opportunities for cultivating well-being skills. Students also reported stronger intentions to revisit wellness goals within six months (7.97 to 8.79, p<.01) and increased interest in exploring purpose and meaning during training (7.42 to 8.41, p<.01). Post-event feedback indicated that the wellness inventory and goal-setting session was the most impactful (37%), while mindfulness emerged as the most valued domain (43%). Qualitative responses emphasized renewed motivation and confidence in sustaining positive lifestyle changes. Conclusions : Introducing structured, experiential wellness programming at matriculation was associated with significant improvements in self-reported wellness skills and intentions. This model provides a feasible, replicable approach to fostering early self-care habits, establishing a shared language of well-being, and addressing modifiable risk factors for burnout. Future research should include comparison groups and longitudinal assessment of stress, burnout, and academic outcomes. P 15.04 Medical Intern and Resident experiences in Employee Whole Health Katherine Dignan MS, RD,LD, CDCES VA Northeast Ohio Healthcare System, Cleveland, Ohio, United States Purpose : The objective of this review is to determine the impact of the VA Paid Employee Whole Health program implemented to promote well-being of the students and trainees at VA Northeast Ohio Healthcare System. Method(s): Each of the 146 unique participants could attend unlimited employee activities on a voluntary basis provided by Employee Whole Health and all completed 4 hours of mandatory employee experiences. The mandatory Whole Health experiences included education on stress and Whole Health and experiences of Mindfulness, Tai Chi/Qigong, Yoga and creative expression. Results : At the start of their internship/residency 86% of participants reported exercising on a regular basis, 77% reported sitting for most of their duties at work and 26% reported practices of Mindfulness, Yoga or Tai Chi regularly. 38% of trainees were male and 62% were female. The average rating before the activity was 5.5 (SD 1.9) with 1 being no stress and 10 being high stress. After participation results indicate a drop in stress level to 3.7 (SD 2.1). Using the brief resilient coping scale, participants had noted improvement over the 1 year period. Perceived daily stress and good quality sleep were negatively impacted during the one year trainee program. Participants reported their general health was at a rating of 2.4 (SD 2) at the beginning of the program moving to post program at 2.6 (SD 0.9) with 0 indicating excellent health. Conclusions : In conclusion this review helps to support the growing body of evidence that Employee Whole Health interventions can support the health and well-being of our interns and residents. P 15.05 Expanding Therapeutic Impact: Inclusion of Caregivers in a Community-Based Parkinson’s Disease Program Clinical Assistant Professor, University of Southern California Sarah Ingersoll MS, MBA 1 , Kris Mendenhall JD 2 , Kenneth Hayashida MD 3 , Louis Castro DPT 4 1 University of Southern California, Los Angeles, CA, United States 2 Nossaman, Los Angeles, CA, United States 3 retired, Los Angeles, CA, United States 4 T.R.O. Physio+Wellness, Los Angeles, CA, United States Purpose : To describe a successful model for actively engaging caregivers in an exercise program originally designed for individuals with Parkinson’s disease. Method(s): Caregiver involvement began informally but quickly proved beneficial, enhancing the group’s cohesion and overall experience. While the participant pool is self-selected and attrition is not systematically monitored, longitudinal surveys offer insights into evolving participant priorities. Results : The program has demonstrated consistent growth and strong engagement among patients, caregivers, and volunteers, with participation increasing prior to the pandemic, and then, more steadily, from 2021 to 2025. Conclusions : An exercise program designed for patients can be effectively adapted to engage caregivers as co-participants.
