P 15.08 Care for the Caregiver Program: Scaling a peer-based, integrative, evidence-informed approach to healthcare workforce well-being Dr. Viktoriya Karakcheyeva MD. MS, Dr. Leigh Frame , Dr. Patrick Corr George Wshington University, Washington, DC, United States Purpose : Healthcare workers around the world face chronic exposure to distress, burnout, and compassion fatigue, with adverse consequences for their mental health, patient safety, and healthcare system sustainability. Method(s): A convergent mixed methods design was used. Quantitative data (n = 35 pre-test; n = 13 post-test; n = 7 matched) were collected using the ProQOL-5 instrument. Qualitative data from two focus groups and eight individual interviews were analyzed thematically. The study was guided by the Knowledge–Attitudes–Behaviors (KAB) framework. Results : Quantitative analyses revealed meaningful improvements across indicators of secondary traumatic stress and burnout, such as reductions of more than half a scale point in feelings of being preoccupied with those they help (Δ = –0.80), being easily startled by unexpected sounds (Δ = –0.77), difficulty separating personal and professional life (Δ = –0.73), feeling personally affected by others’ traumatic stress (Δ = –0.62), and feeling worn out from their role as a helper (Δ = –0.70). Thematic analysis identified enhanced understanding of PFA, increased confidence in using helping skills in both professional and personal contexts, and fostered a sense of community through monthly check-ins. Conclusions : The CFC Program represents a scalable, evidence-informed integrative model that merges peer support, PFA, and hope building practices. It addresses critical gaps in healthcare workforce well-being by promoting proactive, accessible, and relational well-being strategies. Findings support expansion of this model to other healthcare settings as a cost-effective, culturally adaptive solution, aligned with whole-person care. P 15.09 Coast to Coast Nursing Wellbeing Challenge Candace McNulty MSN, Ashley Rush LCSW-QS Orlando VA, Orlando, FL, United States Purpose : Nurses are frequently exposed to occupational stressors that can negatively influence their well-being and, in turn, patient care quality and safety. Nurse well-being in hospital settings is closely linked to job satisfaction, organizational commitment, and key patient outcomes. This project reports on a four-week interfacility Nursing Wellbeing Challenge conducted at the Orlando VA and San Francisco VA (October 6–November 1, 2025) designed to promote nurses’ engagement in structured well-being activities and to evaluate change in self-reported well-being. Method(s): The intervention was grounded in the VHA Whole Health model, which includes eight dimensions of well-being. Two dimensions were highlighted each week, with participants selecting and logging evidence-based well-being practices via a standardized electronic form. Pre- and post-intervention surveys assessed three domains—physical well-being, mental/emotional well-being, and day-to-day life—on a 1–5 Likert scale. Results : More than 250 nurses across the two sites enrolled in the challenge. In the Orlando cohort, mean pre- and post-scores demonstrated improvement across all domains: physical well-being increased from 3.29 to 3.84, mental/emotional well-being from 3.51 to 4.00, and day-to-day life from 3.60 to 3.96. Participants additionally submitted photographs and brief narratives illustrating “what matters most” in their well-being journeys, and weekly videos from Orlando VA nursing staff modeled practical self-care strategies and reinforced social norms around well-being engagement. Conclusions : These preliminary findings suggest that a brief, structured, Whole Health–aligned wellbeing challenge is feasible at scale and may positively influence nurses’ perceived well-being. Given the established associations between nurse well-being, retention, and patient outcomes, such interventions may represent a pragmatic approach to supporting a healthier and more sustainable nursing workforce. Planned next steps include expansion of the Nursing Wellbeing Challenge to additional VHA sites in 2026 and the incorporation of longitudinal outcome measures, including well-being scores, participation patterns, and workforce indicators. P 15.12 A Dose of Wellness: Weekly Self-Care at the University of Colorado Skaggs School of Pharmacy Dr Monika Nuffer PharmD, NCCA University of Colorado Skaggs School of Pharmacy and Pharmaceutical Science, Aurora, CO, United States Purpose : Chronic health conditions continue to represent a major burden within the United States (US) healthcare system, representing 90% of total healthcare expenditures annually. Unhealthy lifestyles and behaviors can significantly contribute to chronic disease. Pharmacy schools’ primary focus is on medications and their interactions with the body and diseases, with limited instruction in areas of wellness. Pharmacy programs should adopt a holistic curricular approach, promoting healthy lifestyles and disease prevention, including integrative medicine and wellness. They pretend to be a consumer with an ailment at a vitamin store to evaluate potential treatments offered. They apply breathing techniques, sample new culinary herb recipes, and prepare aromatherapy products, reflecting on each experience, with the goal of building healthy stress-reducing behaviors. Results : Approximately 750 students completed this elective course with 20 course offerings across the past 14 years. The course fills to capacity every year, and student feedback is strongly positive, averaging 4.7 to 4.9 on a 5-point scale. The emphasize for IHM was fantastic!” “Loved the weekly wellness.” Conclusions : This course has successfully demonstrated the role of various IHM modalities within pharmacy and the importance of healthy behaviors to build resilience and prevent burn-out. P 15.13 A Brief Mindfulness Skills Course for University Employees is Feasible and Effective: Changes in Perceived Stress, Mindfulness, Relational Well-being, and Workplace Performance Jeffrey Greeson Ph.D. Rowan University, Glassboro, NJ, United States Purpose : There is surging interest in workplace mindfulness programs to address employee stress, resilience, and performance, however, significant gaps in knowledge remain around feasibility, acceptability, effectiveness, and predictors of therapeutic change. This study evaluated whether a brief, 4-session Mindfulness Skills Course (MSC) for university employees, covered by the institution’s wellness benefits, was effective in reducing stress, increasing mindfulness, improving feelings toward oneself and others, and increasing work performance. Method(s): Prospective observational cohort study design. Participants completed self-report surveys before and after the MSC. Class attendance and home mindfulness practice were recorded to assess feasibility and adherence. Paired t-tests and Pearson correlations were used to evaluate program effectiveness and predictors of change. Results : 190 employees enrolled during the first year of roll-out. Of those, 164 (86%) completed pre-course surveys, 134 (82%) attended at least three class sessions, and 123 (75%) completed the post-course surveys and program evaluation. Paired samples t-tests showed improvements for perceived stress, mindfulness, and relational wellbeing with moderate to large effect sizes (d=.44 - .78; p’s< .001). Self-perceived work performance increased with a small effect (d=.33; p< .001). More frequent home meditation practice was positively correlated with a larger increase in mindfulness (r=.20; p< .05). Increased mindfulness correlated with reduced stress (r=-.55), greater positive emotions towards self (r=.38), and reduced negative emotions towards self (r=-.40) and others (r=-.42) (all p’s< .001). Reduced stress was correlated with increased perceived work performance (r=-.22; p< .05). Higher baseline stress levels predicted greater increases in mindfulness (β= .37, R2= .14, p<.001), while lower baseline mindfulness predicted greater reduction in stress (β= .27, R2= .07, p<.01). Conclusions : Taken together, these results support the feasibility, effectiveness, and scalability of a brief, 4-week, employer-sponsored mindfulness skills course, which may be most helpful for individuals with higher stress and lower trait mindfulness. The total duration of the program for each participant is approximately 1.5 hours. A pre-program and post-program survey were administered to all participants. P 15.15 Burnout and Mindfulness Among Medical Researchers: Findings from a Large-Scale, Global Cross-Sectional Survey Dr. Jeremy Y Ng PhD 1,2,3,4 , Ms. Niveen Syed BSc 1,2 , Ms. Gabriela Melendez BSc 1,2 , Dr. Anna K Koch PhD 5 , Prof. Dr. Holger Cramer PhD 1,2 1 Institute of General Practice and Interprofessional Care, University Hospital Tübingen, Tübingen, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 2 Robert Bosch Center for Integrative Medicine and Health, Bosch Health Campus, Stuttgart, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 3 Department of Health Research Methods, Evidence, and Impact, Faculty of Health Sciences, McMaster University, Hamilton, Ontario, Canada 4 School of Public Health, Faculty of Health, University of Technology Sydney, Syndey, New South Wales, Australia 5 Charité - Universitätsmedizin Berlin, corporate member of Freie Universität Berlin and Humbold-Universität zu Berlin, Charité Competence Center for Traditional and Integrative Medicine (CCCTIM), Berlin, Berlin, Germany Purpose : Burnout, characterized by emotional exhaustion, cognitive and emotional dysregulation, and psychological distancing, is a growing concern across professions and contributes to reduced well-being. Medical researchers often operate in high-pressure environments and play a critical role in advancing scientific and clinical knowledge but remain an understudied group. Consequently, this study assessed burnout risk and trait mindfulness, a protective factor, among medical researchers worldwide. Method(s): Published authors in MEDLINE-indexed journals between December 13, 2023, and January 25, 2024, were identified using a search strategy. Eligible participants (medical researchers with a terminal degree or ≥5 years in a research role) completed an anonymous online survey that included sociodemographic questions, the Burnout Assessment Tool-12 (BAT-12), and the Freiburg Mindfulness Inventory-14 (FMI-14). Sociodemographic characteristics were summarized as frequencies, while BAT-12 scores were categorized as no risk, at risk, or very high risk, and FMI-14 scores (ranging from 14 to 56, with higher scores indicating greater trait mindfulness) were organized using descriptive statistics. Results : A total of 1,732 participants completed the survey. Most were senior researchers (60.10%) and employed full-time (88.11%). Nearly half were faculty members (48.43%), followed by clinician-researchers (16.80%) and academic research staff (14.79%). Participants worked primarily in Europe (35.85%) and the Americas (34.70%) and were aged 36-55 (61.72%). Common research areas included clinical (31.49%), preclinical (23.73%), and health services and systems (18.25%), with Public Health (12.98%), Biochemistry/Genetics (8.22%), and Nervous System (5.92%) as popular disciplines. Overall, 38.8% of respondents were at risk or very high risk of burnout. Mental distancing was most affected (52.2%), followed by exhaustion (36.9%), emotional impairment (29.7%), and cognitive impairment (15.6%). Participants had a mean mindfulness score of 37.51 (SD = 7.64). Conclusions : These findings show substantial burnout risk among medical researchers and highlight the prevalence of trait mindfulness, supporting future work on targeted interventions to improve researcher well-being globally. P 15.16 Occupational Well-being of Advanced Practice Providers Comparative to Physicians in US Academic Medical Centers Bridget S Garmisa MSN, MS Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, Philadelphia, PA, United States Purpose : To describe prevalence of burnout, professional fulfillment (PF) and intention to leave (ITL) among advanced practice providers at academic-affiliated health care systems and compare these well-being markers to physicians. Method(s): Cross-sectional analysis of survey administered to physicians and APPs at academic medical centers affiliated with the Healthcare Professional Well-being Academic Consortium. Results : Survey respondents included 19,532 physicians and 6,873 APPs (response rate 52.1% physicians, 49.5% APPs). Among APPs, 73.6% identified as women while 42% of physicians identified as women. Among APPs, 33.6% met criteria for PF, 38.1% met criteria for burnout, and 35.3% reported moderate or higher ITL. APPs have an equal likelihood of PF and are less likely to experience burnout (OR 0.8, 95% CI 0.7-0.9, p<0.001) compared to physicians when adjusting for demographics. APPs are more likely to experience ITL compared to physicians (OR 1.3, 95% CI 1.2-1.5, p<0.01) when adjusting for demographics, PF, and burnout. Conclusions : Demographic findings reveal higher proportions of APPs identify as women and are younger comparative to physician colleagues. Age and gender influence occupational well-being parameters for both physicians and APPs. Despite demographic differences, APPs were less likely to experience burnout than physicians, while having an equal likelihood of PF. Understanding key drivers of APP PF and burnout may inform beneficial learnings for healthcare teams given the favorable occupational well-being profile. More research is needed on drivers of APP ITL to inform interventions to reduce APP turnover. P 15.17LB Bridging the Gap: Analyzing Clinician Engagement with an Interactive Decision-Support Tool for Integrative Oncology Ms. Melissa Zappa Dac , Ben Smith BS, Shelley White PhD Huntsman Cancer Institute, Salt Lake City, UT, United States Purpose : Integrative health services in oncology face persistent challenges in educating providers regarding the clinical benefits and safety of integrative therapies. The Wellness and Integrative Health Center (WIHC) at Huntsman Cancer Institute is a multidisciplinary center offering a wide range of integrative health services, including acupuncture, osteopathic manual medicine, massage, nutrition, personalized exercise prescriptions, music therapy, expressive arts therapies, and more. This wide range of service offerings can be daunting to allopathic medical providers and can present as a barrier to referrals when physicians and nursing staff don’t fully understand the benefits of these services or when to appropriately recommend or refer to integrative offerings. To address this, the WIHC launched the ‘Thrive with Wellness’ educational campaign, a multifaceted approach featuring an innovative interactive referral tool designed for real-time clinical use. Method(s): The interactive tool, built via a customizable Qualtrics interface, allows clinicians to select a patient’s top three symptoms and receive tailored, evidence-based recommendations. The interface captures granular data on user roles, frequent symptom clusters, and the specific services recommended most often. Results : Analysis reveals that integrative medicine is increasingly utilized to fill specific care gaps. Pain was a primary concern in 61% of physician-reported issues, followed by fatigue (42%) and musculoskeletal complaints (34%). High-prevalence symptom clusters identified include: Pain/Fatigue/Musculoskeletal complaints. Pain/Fatigue/Neuropathy. Mental health facets (e.g., mood disturbances, depression, or anxiety) were consistently present in all of the third most common cluster combinations. Conclusions : Quantifying these trends provides essential insights into the educational needs of oncology providers. Identifying where care teams repeatedly seek guidance allows researchers to target interventions that move the needle toward truly interdisciplinary care and improved patient outcomes. P 15.18LB Caring for Our Future Healers: Adverse Childhood Experiences and Well-Being Among Healthcare Professional Students Dr Michelle E Clausen PHD, MSN, CNM, Dr Patrick G Corr Ed.D., MPH, AFAMEE The George Washington University, Washington, DC, United States Purpose : Adverse Childhood Experiences (ACEs) are strongly associated with long-term mental and physical health outcomes through their effects on stress physiology, emotional regulation, and mind-body functioning. Integrated findings underscore the urgency of embedding trauma-informed, mind-body–oriented, and relationally supportive approaches within healthcare education to promote resilience, protect learner well-being, and strengthen the future healthcare workforce. Topic Area 16: Traditional Medicine P 16.01 Knowledge, Experience and Interest in Traditional, Complementary and Alternative Medicine among Veterans attending a Neurology Clinic Rahul Chauhan BS 1 , Varsha Vasu BBA 2 , Dr. Archana Purushotham MD, PhD 3,2 1 University of Texas, Galveston, TX, United States 2 Baylor College of Medicine, Houston, TX, United States 3 Michael E. DeBakey VA Medical Center, Houston, Texas, United States Purpose : There is broad interest in Traditional, Complementary and Alternative Medicine (TCAM) among US veterans, but they experience several barriers to receiving TCAM care. Results : A total of 885 veterans were surveyed. Barring Ayurveda, biofeedback and naturopathy, most reported good knowledge of other modalities. P 16.02 Determinants of Traditional Korean Medicine Utilization among Frail Older Adults: Using the 2022 Korean Longitudinal Study of Aging Boyoung Shin KMD, MPH , Prof Bo-Hyoung Jang KMD, PhD Kyung Hee University, Seoul, Seoul, Korea, Republic of Purpose : South Korea is rapidly becoming a super-aged society, and frailty is a key determinant of health care utilization among older adults. Traditional Korean Medicine (TKM), covered by public insurance, is widely used to manage chronic conditions, yet determinants of its use among frail older adults remain unclear. Education showed an inverse association, with lower use among middle school (OR=0.49, p=0.051) and high school or higher groups (OR=0.47, p=0.015). Although previous studies showed mixed income effects in the general population, this study identified a clear income gradient. Reducing financial barriers and integrating TKM into geriatric care may promote equitable access. Further research should examine causal mechanisms and health outcomes. P 16.04 Tongue Tooth Marks and Stress: Links to Norepinephrine and Kampo Patterns Dr. Hongyang Li Hiroshima University Hospital, Hiroshima, Hiroshima, Japan Purpose : In Kampo medicine, stress-related changes are evaluated through the four diagnostic methods. Tongue appearance, pulse, and abdominal findings are key indicators of both physical and psychological states. Psychological stress may induce teeth clenching and tension in the tongue and jaw muscles, affecting autonomic and endocrine functions. This study investigated the physiological basis of Kampo diagnostic findings of stress, focusing on tongue tooth marks as indicators of sympathetic activation and comparing stress-related markers across Kampo patterns. Method(s): Healthy women aged 40–59 years (BMI 18–27) with subjective physical discomfort were recruited. Assessments included tongue imaging for tooth marks, ultrasonography of masseter muscle thickness and stiffness, and Kampo diagnostic examinations. Stress-related markers—plasma catecholamines, salivary cortisol, and autonomic balance (LF/HF ratio)—and psychological stress (STAI) were evaluated. Statistical analyses were performed using SPSS software Version 28. Results : A total of 66 participants were recruited. Among them, 72% scored above the STAI cutoff, indicating susceptibility to psychological stress. Participants with tooth marks(n=30) had significantly higher plasma norepinephrine levels compared to those without tooth marks. These findings align with our previous presentation at the Japanese Society of Fatigue Science, showing positive correlation between tooth marks and norepinephrine, and weaker associations with cortisol variation and LF/HF ratio. Analysis based on Kampo pattern classification showed that 13 participants were categorized as liver deficiency, 32 as spleen deficiency, 20 as kidney deficiency, and 1 as lung deficiency. Norepinephrine was higher in liver deficiency than kidney deficiency, though not significant. Conclusions : Participants with tongue tooth marks showed higher norepinephrine levels, suggesting tongue diagnosis may indicate stress-related sympathetic activation. Norepinephrine was also higher in liver-deficiency patterns, supporting the Kampo view that the liver governs emotion and is sensitive to stress, reflecting underlying autonomic differences. P 16.09 Quantitative Analysis of Plantar Pressure in Taijiquan Standing Meditation Dr. Scott Yam 1 , Dr. T Julian Chu 2 1 Queen's University, Kingston, ON, Canada 2 CMC Taichi Academy DC & NC, Cary, NC, United States Purpose : Low-impact, balanced-based, meditative-aerobic trainings (LBMAT) are gaining popularity in an aging demographic. LBMAT can benefit from a more quantitative translation approach to reach a broader audience. We propose foot pressure measurement for ground reaction force (GRF) characterization during practice. Method(s): Measured the foot pressure variations of 23 Taijiquan/ Taichi practitioners (2 advanced, 18 intermediate, 2 novice) in a dynamic, upright stance. Taijiquan/ Taichi practitioners are asked to: (1) maintain an upright posture, (2) keep their legs straight, (3) have feet flat and stationary on the pressure mat, (4) keep their socks on, (5) and maximize GRF on mat. This could imply significant differences in the activation and synchronization of the underlying muscle groups that maximize the GRF measurement by the advanced practitioners. Similar distinctive signatures in left and right foot GRF are also observed between other advanced and beginning practitioners. Conclusions : There is a significant qualitative difference in the GRF foot pressure between beginning and seasoned Taijiquan/ Taichi practitioners. While this is well known in the LBMAT community of seasoned practitioners, it is not widely known in the beginner circle. It is likely the first time this has been recorded by a pressure mat in this setting. Given the small sample size (23), a larger data set from a more diverse group will provide a higher confidence over the preliminary results to the general public. The preliminary results provide a potentially new approach to foot GRF monitoring during LBMAT practice. Together with other appropriate non-invasive wearable sensors, this could lead to a novel platform that enables the efficient translation of LBMAT training among an aging demography. This could lead to a more quantitative and repeatable approach to LBMAT related healthcare studies. In doing so, tailored implementation strategies can be developed and tested to optimize intervention uptake into clinical practice. Two reviewers independently screened titles, abstracts, full-texts, and extracted study characteristics. Currently, we are extracting and coding determinants using the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research (CFIR) and its five domains (intervention characteristics, outer setting, inner setting, characteristics of individuals, implementation process) to synthesize factors influencing implementation. Results : We screened 8,478 titles/abstracts, reviewed 302 full-texts and included 114 articles. Across 114 included articles, data from 5,919 informants (e.g., patients, healthcare providers, intervention instructors, caregivers, trial staff) was collected. Themes related to implementation barriers and facilitators will be presented. Conclusions : Qi gong, tai chi, and yoga have been implemented in diverse ways across healthcare systems and clinical populations, leading to considerable variability in their implementation. Future mind-body research should proactively address implementation determinants throughout study design and intervention delivery to improve effectiveness and sustainability. P 16.13 Balancing Fidelity and Flexibility in a Pragmatic Trial: Monitoring Remote Tai Chi for Knee Osteoarthritis (TAICHIKNEE) Weijun Zhang DrPH 1 , Eric Roseen DC, PhD 2 , Brian S. Mittman PhD 3 , Robert Saper MD, MPH 4 , Sherwood Alexis 5 , Pilar M Sanjuan PhD 6 , Emily Hurstak MD, MPH 2 , Andrew Sussman PhD, MCRP 6 , Lance Laird ThD 7 , Narissa McCarty MS 4 , Chenchen Wang MS, MSc 5 1 UCLA, Los Angeles, CA, United States 2 Boston Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States 3 Kaiser Permanente, Pasadena, CA, United States 4 Cleveland Clinic, Cleveland, Ohio, United States 5 Tufts Medical Center, Boston, MA, United States 6 University of New Mexico, Albuquerque, NM, United States 7 Boston University, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : Ensuring intervention fidelity is critical for maintaining rigor and internal validity in complex health interventions delivered across diverse settings. Pragmatic trials must uphold fidelity to core components while allowing flexibility for real-world adaptations. Despite increasing adoption of telehealth, few studies have systematically evaluated fidelity monitoring strategies for remotely delivered mind-body interventions like Tai Chi. Method(s): The TAICHIKNEE pragmatic trial evaluates feasibility and effectiveness of remotely delivered Tai Chi for patients with knee osteoarthritis across five U.S health systems. A comprehensive fidelity monitoring plan was implemented with three key components: (1) standardized intervention manuals for both instructors and patients; (2) structured instructor training, including manual review, didactic sessions, supervised practice, and pilot teaching; and (3) iterative refinement through ongoing supervision and retraining for instructors who did not meet 80% fidelity threshold. Fidelity data from the first two of six planned cohorts (200 classes in total 20 sessions) were collected through fidelity checklist forms (randomly sampling 10% of sessions), instructor process notes, and supervision meeting summaries. Quantitative checklist data were summarized descriptively, while qualitative data were analyzed thematically. Results : Preliminary analyses with 20 fidelity checklists indicate high adherence to core Tai Chi structure, sequence, and pacing across instructors. This has been supported by approximately 30-hour of combined didactic training, supervision, and retraining. Common implementation challenges included technology barriers, variability in participants’ physical abilities, and instructor pacing differences. Ongoing supervision and feedback facilitated rapid correction, reinforced adherence to core elements, and supported context-sensitive adaptations. Iterative revisions to manuals and training protocols improved clarity and usability, and alignment with pragmatic trial principles. Conclusions : Systematic fidelity monitoring for remote Tai Chi is both feasible and essential for ensuring scientific rigor and reproducibility in pragmatic trials. The combination of structured observation, instructor self-reported process documentation, and iterative supervision offers a scalable framework for balancing fidelity with flexibility. P 16.14 Neural Effects of Core Shamanic Drumming Music on Chronic Pain Brock Pluimer 1,2 , Alessandra Anzolin 3 , Apeksha Sridhar 1,2 , Richard E Harris 1,2 1 Susan Samueli Integrative Health Institute, Irvine, CA, United States 2 Department of Anesthesiology and Perioperative Care, Irvine, CA, United States 3 Spaulding Rehabilitation Hospital, Harvard University, Massachusetts General Hospital, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : This study evaluated the feasibility of using EEG hyperscanning to monitor neurophysiological responses during shamanic drumming in fibromyalgia patients. Building on evidence that shamanic healing induces altered consciousness and reduces pain in temporomandibular disorder, we investigated whether shamanic journeying produces measurable pain relief in fibromyalgia and identified neural correlates. This addresses gaps in understanding neurophysiological mechanisms underlying shamanic healing for chronic pain management. Method(s): Eight female fibromyalgia patients were randomly matched with one of three shamanic practitioners for five treatment sessions over eight weeks. During V1 and V5, simultaneous EEG recordings were collected from both patient and practitioner using Brain Products 32-channel water-based nets. Each session included classical music (CL) as an active comparator followed by shamanic drumming (SH). EEG data were sampled at 500Hz, bandpass filtered (1-45Hz), and re-referenced to the average. Pain outcomes were assessed using validated measures (Fibromyalgia Impact Questionnaire-Revised, Brief Pain Inventory, Pain Catastrophizing Scale) at baseline and one-month follow-up. Results : Shamanic drumming significantly reduced fibromyalgia pain across multiple validated measures, p < 0.05, Fibromyalgia Impact Questionnaire Revised and Brief Pain Inventory, with effects lasting one month post-treatment. At V5, compared to V1, patients showed a 10.91% increase in alpha relative power during shamanic sessions (p=0.21), indicating relaxed, internalized focus, while classical music decreased alpha by 4.73%. Notably, alpha coherence between patient and practitioner increased globally from V1 to V5, suggesting enhanced inter-brain synchronization between the patient and practitioner. Conclusions : This pilot study demonstrates the feasibility of using EEG hyperscanning to investigate shamanic healing practices and reveals task-specific neurophysiological signatures associated with therapeutic outcomes in FM. The pronounced divergence in alpha activity between SH and CL conditions, coupled with sustained pain reduction, provides preliminary objective neurophysiological evidence supporting shamanic drumming as a chronic pain intervention. Larger sample studies are warranted to confirm these findings with adequate statistical power. P 16.15 Traditional Asian Mind-body Exercises for Vasomotor Symptoms and Other Menopausal Symptoms in Women Experiencing Peri- and Post-Menopause: A Scoping Review Wen Tu Ph.D. Candidate 1 , Caroline Sariah Howell BS 1 , Lydia A Howes MSI 2 , Lisa Taylor-Swanson Ph.D 1 1 College of Nursing, University of Utah, Salt Lake City, Utah, United States 2 Eccles Health Sciences Library, University of Utah, Salt Lake City, Utah, United States Purpose : This scoping review aims to systematically map the current literature on the effects of Asian mind-body exercises on vasomotor symptoms (VMS) and other menopausal symptoms in peri- and postmenopausal women. The goal is to address existing knowledge gaps and provide insights to inform healthcare interventions and future research. Method(s): Guided by the Joanna Briggs Institute (JBI) Manual for Evidence Synthesis and the Arksey and O'Malley framework, this review adhered to PRISMA-ScR guidelines to ensure methodological transparency and reproducibility. Literature screening and data extraction were conducted using Covidence (Veritas Health Innovation), with citation management and duplicate removal performed via EndNote (Clarivate Analytics). Comprehensive searches including peer-reviewed studies and grey literature were conducted in both English and Chinese databases during two time points (November 2024 and August 2025). Results: The review screened 2,555 papers, excluding 26 duplicates and 2,080 irrelevant studies. After full-text assessment of 449 papers, 403 were excluded for inappropriate population, outcomes, or interventions, or lack of full text, leaving 46 articles for extraction. The included studies were comprised of randomized controlled trials, quasi-experimental designs, systematic reviews, and non-research sources. Overall, evidence indicated that mind-body exercises improve VMS, mood, sleep quality, anxiety, cognitive function, and overall quality of life. Ba Duan Jin and other Qigong forms were particularly effective in reducing depressive symptoms and stress, while Tai Chi showed potential benefits for sleep and cognitive performance. Common methodological limitations included small sample sizes, heterogeneous protocols, and limited longitudinal data. Conclusions : This first scoping review of English and Chinese literature highlights traditional Asian mind- body exercises as safe, feasible, and potentially beneficial for managing menopausal symptoms. Findings support their use in holistic care and offer women accessible, low-risk options. The review underscores the need for high-quality, standardized, and longitudinal studies to explore underlying mechanisms, optimize intervention formats, and expand applicability across diverse populations. P 16.16 A Retrospective Study Evaluating the Benefits of Group Qigong for Patients with Cancer Elias N Gennaoui 1,2 , Krisstina Gowin DO, ABOIM 3 1 California University of Science and Medicine, Colton, CA, United States 2 The Cherng Family Center for Integrative Oncology, Irvine, CA, United States 3 City of Hope, Orange County, CA, United States Purpose : Qigong is an evidence-based mind-body intervention for patients with cancer and is represented in the American Society of Clinical Oncology-Society for Integrative Oncology guidelines for management of symptoms such as anxiety, depression, and fatigue. This retrospective study compares pre- to post-session changes in symptom burden among patients with cancer participating in Qigong classes at City of Hope (COH) and investigates the real-world impact of Qigong outside a clinical trial setting. Method(s): Patients participated in a complementary Qigong class at COH from September 2023 to June 2025, which were offered in-person or virtual. Participants were asked to complete a pre- and post-session modified Edmonton Symptom Assessment Scale (ESAS) scale. Descriptive analysis was completed for patient demographic information. Paired t-tests compared pre- and post- session scores. Results : Data were analyzed for 39 participants, of whom 64% were female and 36% male. The majority (62%) were aged 65 years or older. Most participants (64%) attended sessions in person, with a median of one session completed across both formats. The most common cancer types represented were breast (18%), multiple myeloma (8%), and prostate (8%) cancers. Significant improvements were observed in pain (mean change = 0.60; p < 0.001) and tiredness (mean change = 1.03; p = 0.008) following Qigong sessions, while changes in depression, well-being, and helpfulness were not statistically significant. Conclusions : Qigong participation was associated with reductions in pain and tiredness among patients with cancer. These findings highlight the value of integrating Qigong into supportive oncology care to help patients manage treatment-related symptoms and improve quality of life in both in-person and virtual settings . Future studies are needed to evaluate the dose response relationship of Qigong intervention. P 16.17 Preprints Posted on the Topic of Traditional, Complementary, and Integrative Medicine Research: A Bibliometric Analysis Dr. Jeremy Y. Ng MSc, PhD 1,2,3,4 , Ms. Brenda X. Lin HBSc 1,2 , Ms. Sabrina Abdella 1,2 , Ms. Magdalene Abebe HBSc 1,2 , Ms. Isabella Y. Tao HBSc 1,2 , Dr. Holger Cramer PhD 1,2 1 Institute of General Practice and Interprofessional Care, University Hospital Tübingen, Tübingen, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 2 Robert Bosch Center for Integrative Medicine and Health, Bosch Health Campus, Stuttgart, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 3 Department of Health Research Methods, Evidence, and Impact, Faculty of Health Sciences, McMaster University, Hamilton, Ontario, Canada 4 School of Public Health, Faculty of Health, University of Technology Sydney, Sydney, New South Wales, Australia Purpose : Traditional, complementary, and integrative medicine (TCIM) includes diverse healthcare practices used globally. TCIM research faces persistent barriers, limited funding, and methodological challenges, that restrict the production and dissemination of strong evidence. Preprints, which make manuscripts publicly available before peer review, offer a pathway to accelerate dissemination, broaden access, and strengthen transparency, particularly in fields with constrained resources. This bibliometric analysis characterizes TCIM preprints across preprint servers. Method(s): We identified TCIM-related preprints using the ASAPbio server directory, focusing on servers with subject filters such as “traditional medicine,” “complementary medicine,” and “integrative medicine.” All TCIM preprints available between June 4, 2024 and January 14, 2025, were included. Extracted metadata included server, posting date, article type, author affiliations, country of corresponding author, funding, keywords, versions, and publication outcomes. Server characteristics (scope, indexing, permanence, commenting) were also recorded. Descriptive statistics summarized preprint characteristics. Results : From 2012 to 2024, 1980 TCIM-related preprints were identified across 11 eligible servers, representing 11 935 unique authors. Research Square hosted the largest share (n=1185), followed by SSRN (n=480) and OSF Preprints (n=191). The number of TCIM preprints peaked in 2021 (n=664, 33.6% of all postings), with a subsequent decline in 2022 and gradual rebound in 2023–2024. China was the most prolific contributor (n=785, 39.4%), followed by the United States (n=143, 7.2%) and India (n=98, 4.9%). Institutions such as the Shanghai University of Traditional Chinese Medicine and Beijing University of Chinese Medicine were leading sources of output. Of the preprints, 612 (30.9%) were subsequently published in peer-reviewed journals, most commonly in BMC Trials (n=441, 67.4%). The median time from preprint posting to journal publication was 4.89 months. Conclusions : This bibliometric analysis provides the first comprehensive overview of TCIM preprints, highlighting both opportunities and challenges in the adoption of preprinting within this field. P 16.18 Quality Assessment of Blinding in Traditional Chinese Medicine Clinical Trials: Based on two randomised controlled trials Mr. Zeyang Shi , Dr. Yuan Sun, Prof. Jian-ping Liu, Prof. Zhao-lan Liu Beijing University of Chinese Medicine, Beijing, Chaoyang, China Purpose : This study aims to conduct a blinded implementation quality assessment of two randomised controlled trials using two quantitative blinded quality evaluation methods, with a view to promoting their application in clinical practice within traditional Chinese medicine. Method(s): This study employed blinded evaluation methods for traditional Chinese medicine to collect relevant blinding indicators. Two quantitative blinded quality assessment methods—the James Blinded Index (JBI) and the Bang Blinded Index (BBI)—were used to conduct blinded quality assessments of two randomised controlled trials. Results : First Randomised Controlled Trial: The overall JBI for all subjects was 0.26, with a 95%CI of [0.18, 0.34], suggesting a reasonably effective blinding design. Grouping results revealed: the trial group's BBI was 0.72 with a 95%CI of [0.55, 0.89], indicating suboptimal blinding efficacy as a significant proportion of trial participants correctly identified their own group. The control group's BBI was 0.24 with a 95%CI of [0.00, 0.49], suggesting acceptable blinding efficacy. Second Randomised Controlled Trial: The JBI for all subjects was 0.59, with a 95%CI of [0.45, 0.72], indicating successful blinding in this trial. Grouping results demonstrated: the trial group's BBI was 0.14, with a 95%CI of [-0.15, 0.44], indicating satisfactory blinding efficacy; The control group BBI was 0.20, with a 95%CI of [-0.11, 0.51], also demonstrating satisfactory blinding efficacy. This indicates that a significant proportion of subjects in both the trial and control groups failed to correctly identify their own group assignment. Conclusions : Blinded evaluation not only reflects the accuracy of blinded outcomes and data but also enhances the credibility of research findings. It is recommended that future blinded clinical trials of traditional Chinese medicine employ a combined approach of JBI and BBI for blinded assessment throughout the research process. P 16.19 Pranic Healing: A Fast-Growing Bioenergy Healing Modality for Pain Management in the Emergency Department Dr. Glenn Mendoza MD, MPH , Marianne Chung MPH Pranic Healing Research Institute, Lyndhurst, NJ, United States Purpose : Pranic Healing is a no-touch, energy healing technique practiced in hospitals and clinics in over 120 countries. Roughly 42% of Emergency Department (ED) visits were related to pain in patients. Pain management has become a highly specialized field requiring a multidisciplinary approach including complementary alternative medicine. This study evaluated the effect of Pranic Healing on pain reduction in adult ED patients at St. Joseph Hospital Medical Center, New Jersey. Method(s): A randomized control study was done in a quaternary regional ED with patients assigned to control group (received pain medication) and experimental group (received pain medication and PH). Adult patients (age >59 years) with acute and chronic pain were randomized after obtaining consent. Unstable patients were excluded. A Likert Scale was used to assess pain pre- and post-intervention. The study was stopped secondary to change in hospital policy to use pain medications in ED. Results : The study was conducted on 47 patients (control n=18, experimental n=29) with an average age of 70.5±8.8 (control) and 70.4±9.3 (experimental) years. No difference in age, ethnicity or education status. The difference in pain scale pre/post-intervention was 0.8 in the control group and experimental group was 5.9 (p=0.017). Changes in heart rate, respiratory rate, systolic and diastolic pressures were appreciated but were not different. Conclusions : There was a significant change in pain scores in the experimental group without changes in vitals. The study shows Pranic Healing is a safe energy healing technique. Pranic Healing appears to play a role in pain management in conjunction with pain medications. To validate these results we need larger studies and further evidence such as changes in biomarkers of pain. P 16.20 Reframing Integrated Care: Traditional Healing as a Core Component of Well-Being Simone Sena BS University of New Mexico School of Medicine, Albuquerque, NM, United States Purpose : American Indian & Alaska Native (AI/AN) populations often have limited access to health care and even greater limitations to accessing culturally-safe health care that represents a holistic approach. Results : Through review of the literature, we found evidence that integrating THP is both viable and beneficial and acts as a core component of patient well-being and cultural safety. Our “Centering THP in Integrated Care Framework” honors and preserves cultural heritage while enhancing patient care by including a diverse array of healing supports. This ensures holistic wellness that improves health outcomes and confidence for AI/AN communities when engaging with their local healthcare systems. Conclusions : When Integrated care is implemented in Tribal communities, centering such efforts in Traditional Healing Practices (THP) will enhance their effectiveness and serve the larger spiritual and cultural aspects of health and healing that Western medicine does not presently address. The “Centering THP in Integrated Care Framework” can serve as a guide for clinics, clinicians, and communities to foster a deeper and comprehensive sense of connectedness between healthcare providers and their patients. P 16.21 A Bibliometric Analysis of Case Reports in Traditional, Complementary, and Integrative Medicine Dr. Mirela I. Bilc PhD 1,2 , Dr. Jeremy Y. Ng 1,2,3,4 1 Institute for General Practice and Interprofessional Care, University Hospital Tübingen, Tübingen, BW, Germany 2 Robert Bosch Center for Integrative Medicine and Health, Bosch Health Campus, Stuttgart, BW, Germany 3 Department of Health Research Methods, Evidence, and Impact, Faculty of Health Sciences, McMaster University, Hamilton, Ontario, Canada 4 School of Public Health, Faculty of Health, University of Technology Sydney, Sydney, New South Wales, Australia Purpose : In Traditional, Complementary, and Integrative Medicine (TCIM), case reports play a key role in capturing diverse therapeutic approaches and patient outcomes. This bibliometric analysis aims to quantify the volume and development of TCIM case reports and explore their thematic focus and scientific relevance. Method(s): A bibliometric search was conducted on September 12, 2025, in the MEDLINE database via Ovid. The search strategy combined keywords and MeSH terms related to TCIM and covered the entire database history. Results were limited to case reports as the publication type. Keyword co-occurrence analysis was performed using VOSviewer, focusing on keywords with at least 50 occurrences. Results : An initial search yielded 442,821 records, with 16,409 (3.7%) identified as case reports. For trend analysis, publications from the last 20 years (2005–2025; n=8,510) were examined. From 2005 to 2025, publication numbers were generally stable, with around 370–400 annual case reports early on, a peak of 467 between 2013–2015, a decline until 2020, and a resurgence reaching 569 in 2024—possibly related to the COVID-19 pandemic. Ten major thematic clusters emerged, including clinical/demographic features, acupuncture, herbal therapies, side effects, quality of life, holistic health, and diagnostics. Conclusions : Case reports remain a stable and scientifically relevant publication type in TCIM, documenting clinical insights beyond controlled trials and reflecting core integrative medicine themes such as acupuncture, herbal therapies and adverse events. P 16.22 Non-pharmacological interventions for cough in patients with lung cancer: A systematic review and meta-analysis PhD Candidate Mengyao Cao Master of Nursing University of Hong Kong, Hong Kong, Hong Kong, China Purpose : To synthesize evidence on non-pharmacological interventions for managing cough in lung cancer patients. Method(s): Nine databases (PubMed, Embase, AMED, CINAHL Plus, Cochrane Library, Web of Science, ProQuest, China National Knowledge Infrastructure, and Wanfang Data) were systematically searched from inception to December 2024. Randomized controlled trials evaluating the effects of non-pharmacological interventions on cough in lung cancer patients published in English or Chinese were included. Study quality was appraised using the Revised Cochrane Risk-of-Bias Tool for Randomized Trials. Meta-analyses were performed for quantitative synthesis, with sources of heterogeneity examined using meta-regression and subgroup analyses. An overall effect size was estimated with standardized mean differences (SMDs) using Hedge’s g and confidence interval (CI). A narrative synthesis was provided when a quantitative synthesis was inapplicable. Results : Thirty-eight studies representing 2,995 lung cancer patients were identified. These studies investigated acupuncture therapy, moxibustion, pulmonary rehabilitation, self-management intervention, physical exercises, psychoeducation support, mindfulness, and multicomponent interventions. Non-pharmacological interventions showed positive effects on cough severity (standardized mean difference: -1.45; 95% confidence interval: -2.07 to -0.82) and cough-related quality of life (SMD: 0.90; 95% CI: 0.65 to 1.14). Additional benefits were observed for expectoration (SMD: -0.47; 95% CI: -0.86 to -0.09), dyspnea (SMD: -0.67; 95% CI: -0.95 to -0.39), and general quality of life (SMD: 1.31; 95% CI: 0.13 to 2.50). Pulmonary rehabilitation showed a greater effect on cough severity (SMD: -3.75; 95% CI: -5.04 to -2.45) than other non-pharmacological interventions. Conclusions : Non-pharmacological interventions are promising in improving cough, expectoration, dyspnea, and general quality of life among lung cancer patients. Pulmonary rehabilitation showed the most promising effect. Future research should adopt objective cough measures in addition to self-reported measures. P 16.23 Assessment and Control Strategies for Immortal Time Bias in Traditional Chinese Medicine Clinical Research: An Empirical Study Dr Yuhan Chen 1 , Prof Yu Du 2 1 Center for Evidence-Based Medicine, Beijing University of Chinese Medicine, Beijing, Beijing, China 2 University of Colorado Denver, Denver, Colorado, United States Purpose : To explore the potential risk of immortal time bias and control methods in traditional Chinese medicine (TCM) cohort studies. Method(s): Firstly, this study summarized the definition, classification of immortal time bias and its current prevalence in cohort studies through literature analysis. Then, a data simulation based on a clinical trial of an oral TCM liquid was conducted. The study defined “N-day cohort studies” where the intervention group included patients with TCM use > N days (N=0-14; N=0 represented the original RCT). The impact of misclassification and exclusion types of immortal time bias was assessed. Poisson regression was used for 90-day mortality, and logistic regression for the composite outcome of death or disability. Time-dependent Cox regression and landmark analysis were employed to evaluate and control for immortal time bias. Analyses were conducted using STATA 17.0 and Python 3.8. Results : The literature review found that immortal time bias is a common yet frequently overlooked bias in TCM cohort studies. Data simulation revealed that immortal time bias can significantly affect the accuracy of cohort study results. In this simulation, when the misclassified immortal time exceeded 4 days, the hazard ratio for 90-day mortality in the intervention group shifted from greater than 1 to less than 1, resulting in a false-positive outcome. Landmark analysis, time-dependent Cox regression, etc., are relatively effective bias control methods. Conclusions : Immortal time bias can substantially distort findings in TCM cohort studies, potentially leading to false-positive conclusions. Statistical methods like landmark analysis and time-dependent Cox regression are effective for bias mitigation. In the design and implementation of cohort studies, the impact of immortal time bias on results should be vigilantly monitored and controlled at the study design and statistical method levels. P 16.24LB A Scoping Review of Indigenous Frameworks and Models of Wellbeing: Implications for Decolonialism, Multi-eyed Seeing, and Equity in Integrative Healthcare Dr. Joanne Qina'au PhD UCSF Osher Center for Integrative Health, San Francisco, CA, United States Purpose : Equitable and decolonial approaches to integrative healthcare require grounding in diverse ontologies of wellbeing. Indigenous wellbeing models and frameworks reflect values that support holistic individual thriving as a part of ecological thriving. In contrast, Western approaches often compartmentalize wellbeing, narrowing health practice and contributing to persistent disparities. Despite the proliferation of Indigenous-developed frameworks, no scoping review has synthesized contributions in this area. This review aimed to identify models and frameworks of wellbeing developed by Indigenous communities and scholars from Turtle Island (North America) and Moananuiākea (the Pacific) to inform more culturally safe and sustaining approaches to public and global health. Method(s): Guided by the PRISMA-ScR framework, both peer-reviewed publications and grey literature were sourced from five databases (PubMed, Web of Science, EBSCO Academic Search Complete, CINAHL, and ProQuest). Additional articles were hand sourced. Included articles posed an original model, or framework of wellbeing, developed for Indigenous people, families, or communities, by Indigenous authors from those respective lands. Results : A total of 1,473 database references and 89 additional references were identified for screening. After 62 full-text reviews, 33 manuscripts were included for analysis. Twenty-one publications originated from Moananuiākea (64%) and twelve originated from Turtle Island (36%). Synthesis of the data yielded seven themes: (1) holistic integration of all life domains; (2) spirituality and the ongoing presence of ancestors; (3) culture and preservation of tradition; (4) collective wellbeing and relationality; (5) land as a source of identity, health, and responsibility; (6) use of place-specific Indigenous metaphors and language; and (7) empowerment and self-determination. Conclusions : Indigenous communities globally are calling for a shift from conceptual discussion of wellbeing toward tangible decolonial policy and application within health systems. Integrative healthcare systems are in a prime position to engage Indigenous wellbeing practices, epistemologies, and ontologies for the wellbeing of all, including the planet. Topic Area 17: Whole Person Health P 17.01 From Measurement to Meaning: Evaluating the Impact of the Whole Health Approach on Chronic Disease Management in Veteran Healthcare Dr. Craig M. Anderson D.C. 1 , Ms. Gina E Foltz MS, RD, LDN 2 , Dr. John E Hackett PT, DPT, COMT, TPS 1 , Ms. Heather R Jensen MHA 1 , Dr. Jason B Cook DC 1 1 VA Tennessee Valley Healthcare System, Nashville, TN, United States 2 Office of Patient Centered Care and Cultural Transformation, Washington DC, DC, United States Purpose : To evaluate the influence of the Whole Health approach on chronic disease management and Veteran well-being. By tracking changes in biometric indicators and clinical outcomes, the Whole Health Impact Dashboard (WHID) aims to assess if integrating values-based care with biomedical, behavioral, and complementary and integrative health (CIH) practices, results in statistically significant health improvements. Grounded in what matters most to each Veteran, the dashboard was designed to support data-driven shared-decision making and personalized treatment planning. Method(s): The study cohort included Veterans first engaged in Whole Health within a three-month period, with 18-month lookback and follow-up periods. Biomarkers were extracted from VHA Corporate Data Warehouse including Systolic, Diastolic, LDL, HDL, Weight, HbA1C, Glucose. The mean change and paired t-test used to calculate statistical significance (p <0.05). Because each Veteran served as their own control, confounding variables were minimized. Whole Health services were indicated by use of clinic (CHAR4) or encounter (Health Factor) tracking mechanisms. Results : Preliminary analyses suggest statistically significant reductions in biomarkers for chronic disease management following engagement with Whole Health services. Of the 7 included biomarkers, the average p-value was 0.004 indicating high-statistical significance improvement. Conclusions : VA Secretary Doug Collins' strategic driver is to put Veterans first by centering on their well-being. Findings support that the Whole Health approach is key to advancing this goal by significantly improving chronic disease management in Veterans. The WHID demonstrates that Health and Wellness Coaching along with personalized treatment planning have the most positive impacts, leading to measurable health improvements. Outcomes are magnified when Whole Health services are combined with other CIH modalities. Personalized, proactive, whole-person care may have positive downstream effects on system efficiency and reduce avoidable hospitalizations. Future research with larger samples, longer follow-up, and mixed-method designs will further clarify its impact and guide improvements in Veteran care quality. P 17.03 Arts and Humanities for Health and Well-Being: Understanding Factors Underlying Robust VA-Community Partnerships Vincent Song AB 1 , Maria Venegas PhD 1,2 , Makayla Dones MS 1 , Anna Barker MS 1 , Justeen Hyde PhD 1,3 , Donna Faraone MPA 4 , Barbara Bokhour PhD 1,5 , Ekaterina Anderson PhD 1,5 1 VA Center for Health Optimization & Implementation Research (CHOIR), Bedford, MA, United States 2 Boston University School of Medicine, Boston, MA, United States 3 Harvard Medical School, Boston, MA, United States 4 VA Office of Patient Centered Care and Cultural Transformation, Lyons, NJ, United States 5 UMass Chan Medical School, Worcestor, MA, United States Purpose : With its transformation toward a Whole Health system of care, the U.S. Veterans Health Administration (VA) is promoting Veterans’ health and well-being via arts and humanities offerings. We sought to understand the state of VA-community partnerships and factors underlying robust partnerships. We applied rapid qualitative analysis to the transcripts. Results : VA-community partnerships in the sphere of arts and humanities programming varied in terms of formal status (from purely informal to fully formalized partnerships) and funding (from non-monetary to fee-for-service arrangements). Stronger partnerships generally shared the following characteristics. VA staff were passionate about the mission and collaborated effectively across multiple departments. In some cases, VA staff were physically present and collaborated with the CP instructors/facilitators to increase Veterans’ comfort level. CPs were eager to ensure accessibility and accommodate Veterans’ unique characteristics and needs, as well as motivated to work with Veterans due to personal interest and/or organization’s mission. Both parties expressed mutual respect, communicated clearly and consistently, and displayed flexibility and patience about logistical obstacles. Conclusions : VA Medical Centers and community organizations and artists partnered in diverse ways to create arts and humanities offerings that promote Veterans’ health and well-being. Although there does not appear to be one right way to form a robust partnership, several patterns emerged, with the strongest partnerships supported by flexible interpersonal relationships and shared investment in Veteran success. These findings contain important lessons for VA and other health systems pursuing community partnerships to promote patients’ health and well-being. My goal is to build healthy teens that can flourish into adulthood. P 17.07 Healing Beyond Hospitalization: Bringing Whole Health to Acute Care Candace McNulty MSN, Ashley Rush LCSW-QS Orlando VA, Orlando, FL, United States Purpose : The VA’s Whole Health program is a transformative care model that shifts from disease-centered treatment to focusing on what matters most to each Veteran. Integrating physical, mental, and emotional health, it emphasizes proactive, personalized, and patient-driven care. Despite its availability, many vulnerable Veterans remain unaware of or disconnected from Whole Health resources. Method(s): This project aims to bridge that gap for hospitalized Veterans by: 1) providing education about the Whole Health program, 2) offering holistic bedside interventions, and 3) facilitating Whole Health consults at discharge. A collaboration between the Whole Health program and Acute Care Nursing at the Orlando VA, the pilot included 309 patients, 84.1% of whom had not previously heard of Whole Health. As a result of the pilot, 64% received consults placed upon discharge. Results : Bedside interventions included deep breathing, virtual reality for wellbeing, meditation, guided imagery, chair Tai Chi, and the Personal Health Inventory, with Battlefield Acupuncture and NADA interventions planned. Using feedback from two pilot data sets, the team improved consult workflows, developed a process map, and created a Power BI tracking tool for data management. These refinements led to a 16% increase in completed consults. Conclusions : Whole Health empowers Veterans and has demonstrated benefits in managing chronic conditions such as diabetes, obesity, pain, and tobacco dependence. The Whole Health Pathway through Holistic Nursing serves as an entry point to connect Veterans to preventive care and Health & Wellness Coaching after discharge, supporting self-efficacy and long-term well-being. Building on the Orlando VA’s success, the project is applying for replication across other VHA sites to evaluate scalability, track Well-Being scores, and assess secondary impacts such as digital divide consult improvement and increased Whole Health utilization. P 17.08 Understanding Whole Health Use in VA's Primary Care-Mental Health Integration Clinics among Rural Veterans with Co-Occurring Chronic Pain and PTSD: A National Retrospective Cohort Study Dr Martha Michel PhD, MS 1 , Dr Steve Zeliadt PhD 1,2 , Dr. Charles Engle MD, MPH 1,2 , Dr. David E Reed, II PhD 1,2 1 VA Puget Sound, Seattle, WA, United States 2 University of Washington, Seattle, WA, United States Purpose : Recent paradigm shifts in healthcare delivery include virtual and whole person care. The Veterans Health Administration (VA) has led these efforts through VA Whole Health, which addresses the mind, body, and spirit. However, it remains unclear which Veterans are using these services and how they impact symptom management in VA’s Primary Care-Mental Health Integration (PC-MHI) clinics. The objective of the study is to examine whether rurality was associated with greater Whole Health use in PC-MHI in a national sample of Veterans with co-occurring chronic pain and PTSD. Method(s): We conducted a retrospective cohort study of n = 3,328 Veterans with co-occurring chronic pain and PTSD who received PC-MHI services and screened positive for significant symptoms of PTSD, depression, and/or anxiety. We defined our exposure period as 12-months from their initial PC-MHI visit or final PTSD, depression, or anxiety assessment. We examined demographic and health characteristics at baseline, Whole Health use, and Whole Health use modality (virtual vs. in-person vs. both) during the exposure period. Negative binomial regression was used to identify whether rurality predicted PC-MHI service use during the exposure period. Results : Veterans residing in rural areas were significantly more likely to receive PC-MHI Whole Health services (IRR=1.11, 95% CI: 1.01–1.21). Older Veterans (e.g., age 75+: IRR=1.43, 95% CI: 1.23–1.67), women (IRR=1.17, 95% CI: 1.09–1.27), and Hispanic Veterans (IRR=1.20, 95% CI: 1.04–1.39) were also more likely to receive services. Higher comorbidity burden was modestly associated with increased service use (IRR=1.02, 95% CI: 1.00–1.03). Conclusions : Rural Veterans are more likely to use VA Whole Health services in PC-MHI, suggesting this is an appropriate modality of Whole Health delivery. Findings support continued investment in virtual Whole Health services to ensure equitable access and symptom improvement for Veterans with complex mental and physical health needs. P 17.09 Quality Improvement Project: Integration of Whole Health in Primary Care visit Dr. Davinder Bhullar MD , Ms. Cailtlyn Christine Rodriguez NP Louis Stokes Cleveland VA, Cleveland, Ohio, United States Purpose : To understand and articulate why integrating Whole Health into primary care is important for building patient trust and improving health outcomes . When patients see their personal values and life goals reflected in their care, they are more likely to participate actively, adhere to recommendations, and feel respected by the health care team. Collaborative goal setting and shared decision-making will improve patient satisfaction. Method(s): Educated healthcare providers on the principles and practices of Whole Health in one on one sessions and in group WH 202 training by a Whole Health educator Provided training on how to identify and integrate the veteran’s Mission Aspiration Purpose (MAP) into care plans by using VHA developed tools and protocols for assessing the MAP of each veteran. Involved veterans in defining and agreeing on shared health goals. Referrals to the Whole Health coaching program to support veterans in understanding and engaging with the Whole Health approach. Use coaches to monitor progress and provide ongoing support and motivation. Evaluated the effectiveness of Whole Health integration with patient feedback through patient satisfaction survey, self-attestation of health outcomes such as mental and physical health and the VHA developed wellbeing signs. Results : The patients completed the satisfaction survey and VHA well being signs before integration of Whole Health in their visit and treatment plan and 3 months after showing 35% increase in trust in health care team, 38% self reported improvement in mental health, and physical health and 40% increase in scientific knowledge and actionable tools for self care 52% increase in perception of collaboration between patient and provider in creating of treatment plan. Conclusions : Integrating Whole Health into primary care is essential for building patient trust in the healthcare team. By focusing on what is important to the veteran and creating shared goals, healthcare providers can foster a trusting relationship that leads to better adherence, engagement, and overall health outcomes P 17.10 “See Me, Hear Me, Comfort Me”: Integrating Whole Health Principles to Honor Veteran Preferences for End-of-Life Care Dr. Denise Kresevic RN PhD APN-BC FGSA, FAAN , Dr. Rosalie Diaz Psy.D. VA Northeast Ohio Healthcare System, Cleveland, OH, United States Purpose : Honoring Veterans’ individualized preferences for end-of-life interventions is central to both hospice philosophy and Whole Health. Traditional pharmacological approaches may contribute to sedation and reduced engagement. Balancing comfort and alertness requires integrating non-pharmacological modalities to enhance physical and psychological well-being. Method(s): To address this, the “See Me, Hear Me, Comfort Me” conceptual model was developed, rooted in critical components of hospice care—communication, symptom management. This model aligns with Whole Health principles emphasizing care begins with what matters most to Veterans, not what’s the matter with them. Results : A pilot program was implemented with 27 terminally ill Veterans admitted to an inpatient hospice unit during the COVID-19 pandemic. Veterans identified preferred pharmacological and non-pharmacological interventions for managing symptoms such as pain, anxiety, and delirium. Providers received education on both the model and the VA Whole Health approach through various resources. Complementary and integrative health (CIH) virtual offerings included aromatherapy, guided imagery, mindfulness, meditation, and stress management. Staff successfully implemented multiple non-pharmacological strategies, including lighting adjustments (63%), emotional support (63%), noise reduction (59%), repositioning (48%), and snack provision (48%), while family involvement was limited (15%) due to pandemic restrictions. Findings indicate that even in resource-constrained environments, individualized care preferences can be honored and implemented effectively. With the addition of non-pharmacological interventions, overall, pain scores decreased significantly from admission (mean=2.74, sd=3.59) to discharge (mean=.59, sd=1.62) (paired t=2.99, p=.006). Behavioral symptoms improved 89% of the time, resolved 7% of the time, and 4% stayed the same post intervention. Similarly, confusion also decrease slightly (McNemar test z=.28, p=.78), from admission (n=8, 29.6%) to discharge (n=7, 25.9%). Conclusions : The model demonstrates use of both pharmacologic and non-pharmacologic approaches enhances end-of-life comfort, dignity, and connection. Integrating Whole Health principles within hospice care supports personalized, multi-modal interventions addressing physical, emotional, and spiritual dimensions of comfort. P 17.11 Ayurveda and Whole Health: Bridging Traditional Wisdom and Modern Well-Being Dr. Anupama Kizhakkeveettil BAMS (Ayu), MAOM, L.Ac., C-IAYT, FAIHM, PhD Southern California University of Health Sciences, Whittier, CA, United States Purpose : The purpose of this presentation is to explore Ayurveda as a traditional healing system that aligns closely with the Whole Health model. Rooted in balance and self-awareness, Ayurveda emphasizes prevention, lifestyle harmony, and individualized care. This work aims to highlight how Ayurvedic principles through physical, emotional, and spiritual integration can serve as a framework for cultivating resilience, purpose, and sustainable well-being in modern health care. Method(s): This presentation draws from classical Ayurvedic texts, current integrative health research, and clinical observations within Whole Health practice. Guided by key Ayurvedic principles including Dinacharya (daily routine), Ritucharya (seasonal regimen), Ratricharya (night routine), Tridosha balance, Sadvritta (code of conduct), and Rasayana (rejuvenation therapies), the approach examines how diet, lifestyle, herbal support, and mind-body practices such as yoga, meditation, and breathwork contribute to holistic well-being. These elements are analyzed in alignment with Whole Health domains emphasizing personal development, connection, and self-care. Results : Evidence from Ayurvedic and contemporary research shows that personalized lifestyle routines, yoga, meditation, consciousness-based practices, herbal formulations, balanced nutrition, and rejuvenation therapies enhance vitality, emotional balance, and stress resilience. Ayurveda emphasizes living in tune with natural cycles and circadian rhythms through mindful routines and seasonal adaptation. This alignment between personal behavior and nature’s rhythms reflects the core of Whole Health, fostering self-awareness, prevention, and empowerment in maintaining long-term physical, emotional, and spiritual well-being. Conclusions : Ayurveda embodies the true essence of Whole Health by recognizing the inseparable connection between body, mind, spirit, and environment. It also emphasizes compassion, community engagement, and interpersonal relationships as vital to healing. At its core, Ayurveda is person-centered, looking beyond symptoms to understand the individual and what truly matters to them. By guiding people to live in balance with themselves, their communities, and nature, Ayurveda stands as a timeless and complete model of Whole Health. P 17.12 Benefits of Using HeartMath at the Atlanta VA Dr Chanique Cousins MBBS, BBMedSci 1,2 , Dr Meena Visvanathan MD 1 , Anthony Bell LPN 1 , Alice Fann MD, Phd 1 1 VA, Atlanta, Georgia, United States 2 Georgia State University, Atlanta, Georgia, United States Purpose : The Veterans Health Administration (VHA) Whole Health Program promotes an integrative model empowering veterans to set personal health goals and engage in evidence-based wellness modalities. HeartMath, an intervention focusing on emotional and physiological regulation through Heart-Focused Breathing, was implemented virtually at the Atlanta VA Medical Center. Method(s): Between February 2024 and September 2025, 35 veterans participated in HeartMath sessions. Follow-up telephone surveys assessed recall, frequency of technique practice, and perceived benefits across domains including mood, emotional regulation, pain, sleep, focus, relationships, and decision-making.
