Among those recalling, 87% reported integrating HeartMath techniques into daily life, particularly during stressful or painful situations. More than 60% observed improvements in mood, emotional control, and physical comfort. Modest correlations suggested that higher practice frequency was associated with enhanced emotional regulation, mood stability, pain management, and focus. Although limited by sample size and categorical response options, findings indicate consistent HeartMath practice may increase program effectiveness. Conclusions : Early evaluation demonstrates high acceptability and perceived benefit among veterans who remain engaged. However, one-third inability to recall the intervention underscores retention challenges, highlighting the critical need for sustained engagement and follow-up strategies. To advance the revolution in healthcare, standardizing meaningful outcomes and reinforcing adherence to practice are essential steps to measure what truly matters. Scaling proven components of HeartMath across the VA Whole Health system supports adopting evidence-based, personalized, and integrative care models that deliver measurable impact at scale. P 17.13 Taking a Whole Health Perspective to Understand Surgical Transition Emma M Millon PhD , Andrea Shang PhD, Christen Boardman BS, RN, RNC-OB, C-EFM, Patrick Treacy MS, BSN, RN, Giselle Pena, Mason Ballinghoff, Kevin Moore DNP, RN, Devynn Emory RN, Benjamin Bass MS, Rachel Bluebond-Langner MD, Lee Zhao MD, Moriah E. Thomason PhD, Kathleen A. DeMarco PhD, RN NYU Langone Health, New York, NY, United States Purpose : This study addresses the significant health disparities and unmet healthcare needs among sexual and gender minoritized (SGM) individuals by focusing on the experiences of transfeminine individuals undergoing vaginoplasty. Gender confirmation surgery (GCS), such as vaginoplasty, is transformative, with up to 35% of SGM individuals pursuing surgery to improve mental health, life satisfaction, and quality of life. Despite its benefits, little is known about the biopsychosocial factors influencing surgical outcomes. This longitudinal mixed-methods study adopts a whole health approach to examine the socioemotional and physical well-being of people undergoing vaginoplasty and inform gender-affirming interventions for enhanced surgical care. Method(s): Over five months, data were collected pre- and post-surgery. Four data methods were employed: (1) semi-structured interviews, (2) psychometric assessments of body congruence, gender dysphoria, and socioemotional well-being, (3) ecological momentary assessment (EMA) via daily SMS capturing mood, stress, discrimination, social support, and sleep quality, and (4) physiological monitoring using the Ōura Ring, tracking metrics such as heart rate, temperature, and activity. Results : As of submission, 18 participants (89% employed, 60% aged 26-35, 82% White) have completed pre-surgery assessments; 9 have completed post-surgery assessments. Among those with pre- and post-surgical data (n=9), EMA adherence was 91%, with 99% of participants adhering to daytime wearable use. Findings from participant interviews indicated that emotional readiness, mental health preparedness, therapy, social connections, and clear communication with medical teams were critical in reducing pre-surgical anxiety and fostering successful recovery. Many participants described the surgery as empowering and pivotal to enhancing their sense of self. Results demonstrate the feasibility of using wearables and daily surveys in SGM populations during the critical time of surgical transition and the importance of social and mental health support. Conclusions : This study highlights the need for personalized, evidence-based, gender-affirming perioperative care to promote equity and optimize outcomes for transfeminine individuals undergoing GCS. P 17.14 Program Evaluation of the Deepening Practice Lab: Transforming Clinical Practice through Whole Health Integration Dr. Amy Collings PhD 1 , Dr. Natalie J Cross PhD 1 , Dr. Heather Nevins-Jones PsyD 2 1 VHA Office of Patient Centered Care and Cultural Transformation, Washington, DC, United States 2 Robley Rex Department of Veteran Affairs Medical Center, Louisville, KY, United States Purpose : The Deepening Practice Lab is an implementation science tool designed to empower healthcare professionals in the Veterans Health Administration (VHA) to center their clinical practice on “what matters most” to Veterans. By starting with clinician wellness and professional values alignment, the Lab helps translate Whole Health Clinical Care Education into practice. The Lab, based on Transformational Learning, Appreciative Inquiry, and Positive Psychology, provides space for clinicians to discuss and integrate Whole Health Clinical Care. This evaluation examines the feasibility, usability, acceptability, and adoption of the model. Method(s): The Lab includes an orienting session to build community and set shared goals, followed by weekly 30-minute huddles over 10-12 weeks. Topics cover clinician self-care, Mission, Aspiration, and Purpose (MAP), co-creating Shared Goals with Veterans, and incorporating Well-Being Signs into clinical practice, among others. Participants were identified via referrals from Whole Health leadership, self-referrals, and Field Implementation Consultants. All participants had prior Whole Health Clinical Care education. Qualitative and quantitative feedback was gathered from 17 participants across four medical centers. Results : Preliminary feedback indicates high satisfaction with the Deepening Practice Lab, with participants highly engaged and willing to recommend it to colleagues. Voluntary participants had higher satisfaction scores than those recommended by supervisors. Interest in facilitating labs was mixed, with perceived lack of time and resources noted as barriers. Anecdotal feedback was favorable, highlighting helpful aspects and minimal suggestions for improvement. Participants effectively incorporated Whole Health elements into their practice. Conclusions : Preliminary feedback suggests the Deepening Practice Lab is feasible, usable, and acceptable, with ongoing adoption among clinicians identified as Whole Health Integration Champions. The Lab fosters clinician wellness and growth, facilitating Whole Health principles' integration into clinical practice. These promising trends indicate potential for widespread adoption and sustained impact within the VHA and other healthcare organizations. P 17.15 A qualitative exploration of faith-based support for healing moral injury in military veterans: “You’re human, and we’re human, and you’re not alone” Margaret D Whitley 1 , Kathryn P Derose 1,2 , Julia Vidal Verástegui 3 , Eunice Wong 1 1 RAND, Santa Monica, CA, United States 2 University of Massachusetts, Amherst, Amherst, MA, United States 3 RAND, Washington, DC, United States Purpose : The Whole Health model includes spiritual wellbeing as integral to overall health. Approximately 40% of recent U.S. veterans experience moral injury (MI) —psychological distress from actions violating one’s personal moral codes. MI contributes to PTSD and suicide risk, yet traditional treatments often overlook its spiritual dimensions. Faith-based organizations increasingly address MI through spiritually-grounded programs, but little is known about their structure, implementation, or impact. This study qualitatively examines faith-based MI programs for veterans nationwide. Method(s): We are conducting semi-structured qualitative interviews with representatives from faith-based programs supporting veterans with MI. We identified organizations through an environmental scan of online and grey literature sources. Guided by the Consolidated Framework for Implementation Research, interviews explore program structure, mechanisms of impact, partnerships with Veterans Affairs (VA), and evaluation. Transcripts are analyzed using RAND’s Muse QDA software, applying a blended inductive-deductive coding approach to identify key themes and illustrative examples. Results : We have completed 12 interviews representing 10 U.S. organizations and have 6 additional interviews pending. Most programs are Christian-affiliated and emphasize small-group workshops or retreats fostering confidentiality, reflection, and forgiveness. Activities include storytelling, writing, and symbolic rituals to process MI and reconnect. Programs vary in their collaboration with VA and evaluation rigor but consistently report improved emotional, social, and spiritual wellbeing among participants, including reduced isolation and renewed faith engagement. One program leader summarized the impact saying: “we know you’re human… and you’re not alone.” Conclusions : Faith-based programs play a distinctive role in addressing MI among veterans by integrating spiritual healing with psychosocial support. This qualitative study provides foundational insight into their structure, mechanisms, and partnerships, contributing to a limited but growing evidence base. Findings highlight opportunities for collaboration between faith-based and clinical systems to enhance veteran care. Future research should evaluate program effectiveness and explore applicability to other populations exposed to morally injurious events. P 17.16LB Orienting a Whole Health University: Preliminary Findings from SCU's Whole Health Employee Training Dr. Steffany Moonaz PhD , Dr. Leah Grout PhD, MPH, Ms. Barbara Gibson MS, Dr. Melissa Nagare DC, LAc Southern California University of Health Sciences, Whittier, CA, United States Purpose : U.S. healthcare systems face escalating costs, worsening outcomes, and a provider burnout crisis. Education models have struggled to keep pace with the need for integrative, prevention-focused care. Southern California University of Health Sciences (SCU) addresses this gap by adapting the U.S. Department of Veterans Affairs’ (VA) evidence-based Whole Health model—shown to improve satisfaction, reduce opioid use, and enhance workforce well-being—to a university-wide setting. Method(s): SCU offers a voluntary employee orientation to WH, either a half-day on campus or a 90-minute online session. The voluntary program evaluation includes Qualtrics surveys at baseline, post-training, and 6 months later (pending). Baseline surveys include demographics, PROMIS-10, Whole-Person Health Index (WPHI), Wellbeing Signs (WBS), and Thriving at Work (TAW). The post-training survey includes repeat outcome measures and mixed-methods satisfaction and suggestion questions. Paired T-tests and Wilcoxon signed-rank tests compare pre- and post-training outcomes, depending on the data's normality. Results : So far, 23 of 50 enrolled participants completed the post-training surveys. Completers were mostly full-time and represented most departments. Over half identified as White, middle-aged, and women. They reported strong training satisfaction, with 90% rating it as Excellent or Good, 91% reporting that it deepened WH understanding, and 96% Very Likely or Likely to apply it. In open-ended questions, they mostly valued Connection with others, Goal setting, Understanding WH, and Considering meaning/values. Outcomes were mostly non-significant, except for a slight decrease in WBS (p=0.02) and a trend toward improved WPHI (p=0.06). Conclusions : As a Whole Health University, SCU is committed to changing health education and healthcare. Feedback from this survey will be used to refine existing and future WH programming for SCU employees. Ongoing assessment will continue to evaluate the impact of this approach for all stakeholder groups with future plans to engage students, patients and the broader SCU community. Topic Area 18: Yoga P 18.01 Socio-Cultural Predictors of Engagement Preferences in a Culturally Adapted Yoga Intervention for Latina Cancer Survivors Gabrielle R Osher BS 1,2 , Alexandra Haney 1,2 , Sofia Koziura 1,2 , Bruriah Horowitz MEd 1,2 , Christina Sauer MS, CAGS 1,2 , Judy Guitelman 3 , David Victorson PhD 1,2 1 Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, United States 2 Osher Center for Integrative Health at Northwestern University, Chicago, IL, United States 3 Alas Wings Latina Association for Breast Cancer, Chicago, IL, United States Purpose : Latina cancer survivors face significant disparities in access to culturally tailored supportive oncology care. Our group’s previous Spanish-language yoga study among 35 Latina cancer survivors with overweight and obesity demonstrated significant benefits for physical symptom reduction and improved psychosocial well-being. This secondary analysis examined associations between cultural preferences, as well as changes in biomarkers over time, in Spanish-speaking Latina cancer survivors who participated in a 12-week yoga intervention with follow-up at 24 weeks. Method(s): Data were drawn from a single-arm, 12-week yoga intervention for Spanish-speaking Latina cancer survivors, with assessments at baseline, 12 weeks (post-intervention), and 24 weeks (follow-up). Pearson’s correlation coefficients were computed to examine associations between cultural preferences related to the yoga program and socio-demographics, anthropomorphic and physiological indicators, and PROMIS measures (physical function, fatigue, sleep disturbance, pain interference, depression, anxiety, positive affect & wellbeing, and satisfaction with social roles). Results : Higher baseline systolic blood pressure correlated with less preference for a Latino instructor (r=.35). Higher baseline and 24-week physical function, positive affect scores, and social role satisfaction related to less preference for cancer-survivor instructors (r=−.34, −.40, −.36, −.44). Higher income and longer U.S. residency correlated with less preference for Spanish-speaking instructors (r=−.39, −.41) and peers (r=−.40). Greater baseline sleep disturbance predicted stronger preference for survivor peers (r=.40). All associations were significant (p<.05). Conclusions : Individuals with lower acculturation, greater distress, or limited physical function tended to seek connection through shared language, ethnicity, or survivorship, while those with more socioeconomic resources or well-being emphasized the activity itself over cultural concordance. These trends align with acculturation processes, suggesting greater U.S. exposure reduces reliance on shared identity for comfort and comprehension. Flexible intervention models offering diverse identification modes—linguistic, cultural, or experiential— may best enhance engagement among diverse cancer survivor populations. P 18.02 Yoga for Corporate Wellness: Assessing the Impact of a Daily Surya Namaskar Routine on Mental Well-Being Among a Global Workforce Ms Shubhangi S Joshi Masters (Yoga) 1 , Dr. Neha Gothe PhD 2 1 Multinational Organization, Pune, Maharashtra, India 2 Northeastern University, Boston, MA, United States Purpose : The objective of this study was to evaluate the impact of a daily yoga-based workplace intervention, titled “The Surya Namaskar Challenge,”on employees’ mental well-being across a global workforce, measured using the World Health Organization-Five Well-Being Index (WHO-5). The study explored whether consistent practice of Surya Namaskars (sun salutations) could enhance psychological health, positivity, and overall well-being among employees from diverse regions and cultural contexts. Method(s): The Surya Namaskar Challenge included Asanas, Pranayama, Yoganidra practice led by certified yoga teacher. A virtual session 60 mins was offered daily for participants to engage in a group format. Each session started with centering, Sukshma Yoga (Warm Up) followed by a guided Surya Namaskar coaching and participants were asked to repeat the sequence multiple times in 30 mins at their own pace and concluded with yoganidra relaxation. The WHO-5 Well-Being Index was administered before and after the intervention. Results : The program initially received 3923 global registrations, with an average of 400 participants consistently engaging in daily sessions across various regions.A total of 161 employees (average age: 38 years; 58% male) from a multinational corporation participated in a daily morning practice of Surya Namaskars for five consecutive weeks. Data were analyzed to assess changes in overall WHO-5 scores and the proportion of participants demonstrating improvement in mental well-being. Post-intervention analysis revealed a significant improvement in mental well-being, with mean WHO-5 scores increasing from 69% at baseline to 84% after five weeks of daily practice, t(160) = 8.81, p<.001. A majority of participants (104 reported positive change, 32 - no change, 25 - negative change) exhibited individual enhancement in their well-being scores. At the end of the program, participants reported qualitative feedback with many reporting greater physical function (flexibility, stability etc), better emotional regulation, better sleep, improved productivity & peer relationship at work. Conclusions : The Surya Namaskar Challenge demonstrates that a brief, consistent, and accessible yoga-based intervention can effectively promote mental health and foster a culture of holistic wellness among employees across global organizations. P 18.03 Feasibility of an Online Yoga Program for Managing Symptoms of Post-Treatment Lyme Disease Syndrome (PTLDS) Zulkayda Mamat PhD 1,2 , Adriel Brown PhD 1,2 , Louise Mahoney 1 , Santiago Allende 1 , Jasmin Francisco 1 , Annie Lau 1 , Peter Bayley PhD 1,2 1 Palo Alto VA Medical Center, Palo Alto, CA, United States 2 Stanford University, Stanford, CA, United States Purpose : This study evaluated the feasibility and efficacy of a 12-week synchronous online yoga program for managing post-treatment Lyme disease syndrome (PTLDS) symptoms, focusing on pain, physical functioning, and cognitive performance. Method(s): Thirteen participants with PTLDS (aged 21-60 years; 92% female) were enrolled in a 12-week online yoga program using HIPAA-compliant telehealth software. The program included 75-minute weekly sessions led by certified yoga instructors, with 15-20 minutes of daily homework on five non-treatment days. The protocol incorporated seated, standing, supine postures, breathing exercises, and meditation, with modifications for limited mobility. Outcome measures were assessed via the Health-Related Quality of Life - Short Form (SF-36), Brief Pain Inventory (BPI), and the Cambridge Neuropsychological Test Automated Battery (CANTAB). Results : The primary feasibility goals were met with a retention rate of 92.50%, treatment adherence of 82.70%, and a treatment satisfaction score of 3.4/5. The missing data rate was low at 3.90%. Significant improvements were observed in pain levels, as indicated by the SF-36 pain scale (t[11] = -3.19, p = 0.01, d = -0.92), and pain interference significantly decreased during daily activities, as measured by the BPI (t[11] = 2.61, p = 0.02, d = 0.75). Participants also reported fewer physical limitations during personal activities (t[11] = -2.46, p = 0.03, d = -0.71; SF-36). Cognitive improvements were shown by a significant reduction in errors on the CANTAB Spatial Working Memory task (t[8] = 3.11, p = 0.02, d = 1.04). Although other CANTAB tasks assessing memory and sustained attention showed trends toward improvement, these results did not reach statistical significance. Conclusions : The online yoga program demonstrated feasibility and was associated with significant reductions in pain, improved physical functioning, and enhanced cognitive performance among participants with PTLDS. These findings suggest that online yoga may be a scalable, accessible, and effective intervention for managing PTLDS symptoms. P 18.04 “Examining Cognitive, Sleep, and Psychosocial Changes from a Culturally Adapted Yoga Program in Midlife Women: A Pilot and Single-Arm Phase II Study” Dr Fauzia Nausheen MD 1 , Dr. Ashley Faytol 2 , Dr. Alexandra L Lopez Vera 1 , Dr Jyoti Mishra, 2 , Jafer V Vazquez Alcaraz 2 , Dr Hazel Soriano 2 , Dr Ariel J. Lang 2 1 CUSM, Colton, CA, United States 2 University of SanDiego, San Diego, CA, United States Purpose : To evaluate the feasibility, acceptability, and preliminary effects of a culturally adapted yoga and meditation program on sleep, stress, quality of life, and cognition in midlife women. Method(s): Pilot enrolled 6 midlife women and informed the design of a Phase II, single-arm, within-subjects study (n = 48). Assessments occur at 0, 8, and 16 weeks to assess change over an 8-week waiting period followed by an 8-week intervention. Program includes weekly guided yoga sessions and daily at-home meditation (Spanish/English). Outcomes include sleep quality (PSQI), perceived stress (PSS), menopause-related quality of life (MENQOL), and cognitive function. Results : Pilot Phase: Quantitative: Across all six participants, the mean MENQOL score decreased from 42 (range 13–61) pre-yoga to 16 (range 2–44) post-yoga, with an average reduction of about 26 points. Out of the six participants, five showed meaningful improvement in their scores, ranging from modest (–11) to very substantial (–55). One participant’s score slightly worsened (+2), indicating variability in individual response. Overall, the pattern suggests that yoga was associated with a general trend of improvement in menopause-related quality of life. PSS improved for most participants (e.g., 26→17; 24→18; 6→1). PSQI improved in 4 of 6 participants (e.g., 10→4; 9→7). Among the cognitive domains, there was significant change in distractor processing (p = .01) and promising movement in working memory (p = .04) and emotion bias (p = .08). Qualitative Participants described yoga and meditation as enjoyable, relaxing, socially supportive, and beneficial for improving sleep, reducing stress, enhancing relaxation, and increasing self-awareness. Phase II (n = 48) is ongoing. Qualitative interviews will further explore openness to future adjunctive tools such as vestibular stimulation. Conclusions : Pilot results indicate improvements in quality of life, stress, sleep, and cognition; Phase II will further clarify individual changes and assess the feasibility of wider community implementation. P 18.05 Feasibility of Remotely-delivered Yoga Nidra and Self-help Education in Older Adults Taking Benzodiazepine Medications Ryan Bradley ND, MPH 1 , Erica Sharpe PhD 2,3 , William Sieber PhD 1 1 University of California, San Diego, La Jolla, CA, United States 2 National University of Natural Medicine, Portland, OR, United States 3 SUNY Empire, Saratoga, NY, United States Purpose : Older adults are especially vulnerable to anxiety and insomnia, and drug treatments can lead to dependency, falls and other accidents. The purpose of this research was to determine the feasibility of remotely delivered yoga nidra and self-help education for older adults with anxiety and/or insomnia. Method(s): We conducted a randomized, education-controlled clinical trial to determine the feasibility of remotely-delivering yoga nidra (YN), a traditional, recorded meditation, for anxiety and/or insomnia in older adults taking benzodiazepine medications. Participants were randomised to either having access to YN recordings or access to sleep hygiene, anxiety and benzodiazepine education (SHABE) materials for 2 weeks. After a Week 2 assessment, they were provided access to the alternative materials, and given ad libitum access to YN + SHABE for an additional 6 weeks. Feasibility was determined based on recommended metrics from the Bowen framework (e.g., demand, acceptability, implementation, etc.) Anxiety symptoms (GAD-7) and categorical sleep changes were recorded at Baseline, and again at Weeks 2, 4 and 8. Results : Twenty-two older adults were randomized (35% yield from screening) and 19 completed the trial (86% retention). At Week 2, the YN group had greater reductions in GAD-7 scores compared to SHABE: -3.25 (4.2) vs. -0.18 (2.3), p=0.06. Compared to Baseline, GAD-7 scores for the entire sample (YN + SHABE) were significantly reduced at Week 8: -2.7 (3.3), p=0.002, with meaningful within group changes in both inital groups: Initial YN: -4.0 (3.5), p=0.03 and Initial SHABE: -1.7 (2.9), p=0.07. Regarding sleep, at Week 2 55% of the YN group reported “improved” sleep vs. 18% of the SHABE group. By Week 8, 74% of participants reported “improved” sleep. Conclusions : Remotely-delivered yoga nidra and education appears feasible and acceptable to deliver to older adults taking benzodiazepine medications. Yoga nidra combined with education may reduce anxiety and improve sleep in older adults. Future research is needed in larger sample sizes to refine estimates of effect. P 18.07 A Retrospective Study Evaluating the Benefits of Group Yoga for Patients with Cancer Elias N Gennaoui 1,2 , Krisstina Gowin DO, ABOIM 3 1 California University of Science and Medicine, Colton, CA, United States 2 The Cherng Family Center for Integrative Oncology, Irvine, CA, United States 3 City of Hope, Orange County, CA, United States Purpose : Yoga is an evidence-based mind-body therapy recommended for managing anxiety, depression, and fatigue in patients with cancer. This retrospective study examined pre- to post-session changes in symptom burden among patients participating in yoga classes at City of Hope (COH), evaluating the impact of yoga outside a clinical trial setting. Method(s): Patients participated in a complementary yoga class at COH from October 2023 to May 2025, which were offered in-person or virtual. Patients attending 2–8 sessions were compared with those attending more than 8 sessions to assess changes in scores. Results : We analyzed results among 28 participants. Participants attended a median of 2.5 yoga sessions, with 78% participating in person. Among single-session participants, tiredness decreased significantly (mean change = 1.17, p = 0.001), with trends towards reduced depression (0.83, p = 0.075) and pain (0.83, p = 0.054). Among participants who attended 2-8 sessions (n=8), the mean changes showed significant post-yoga decreases in depression (-1.10, p < 0.001), tension (-1.80, p < 0.001), and tiredness (1.54, p < 0.001). Compared with these participants, those who completed more than 8 sessions (n=8) demonstrated smaller mean improvements in tension (-0.68, p =0.022) and pain (-0.68, p = 0.004), suggesting that symptom relief may plateau after multiple sessions. Conclusions : Yoga participation at COH was associated with significant reductions in tiredness, tension, and depression, and improved well-being. Benefits were greatest with multiple sessions, suggesting cumulative effects. Overall, yoga participation was associated with meaningfully improved physical and emotional symptoms among cancer patients. But MY only showed increases in serotonin (T1: 18.05±4.92; T2:20.09±2.95; p=0.285) and endorphin (T1:59.28±12.87; T2:63.03±24.37; p=0.639). Increases in these hormones indicate positive psychophysiological changes. Conclusions : Contrary to early studies, PE outperformed MY in addressing psychological distress, suggesting age and teaching style may influence outcomes. However, further research is needed. P 18.10 Culturally Tailored Yoga and Inflammation: Psychosocial, Physiological, and Cultural Correlates Among Spanish-Speaking Latina Cancer Survivors Gabrielle R Osher BS 1,2 , Alexandra Haney 1,2 , Sofia Koziura 1,2 , Bruriah Horowitz MEd 1,2 , Christina Sauer MS, CAGS 1,2 , Judy Guitelman 3 , David Victorson PhD 1,2 1 Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine, Chicago, IL, United States 2 Osher Center for Integrative Health at Northwestern University, Chicago, IL, United States 3 ALAS Wings Latina Association for Breast Cancer, Chicago, IL, United States Purpose : Latina cancer survivors face disparities in access to culturally tailored supportive oncology care. This secondary analysis examined associations between inflammatory biomarkers (C-reactive protein [CRP] and interleukin-6 [IL-6]) and patient-reported outcomes (PROs), physiological measures, and cultural preferences, and longitudinal changes in biomarkers after the 12-week intervention and at 24-week follow-up. Method(s): Data came from a single-arm, 12-week yoga intervention for Spanish-speaking Latina cancer survivors, with assessments at baseline, 12 weeks, and 24 weeks. ANCOVA models assessed changes in biomarkers over time, adjusting for baseline expectancy and yoga session attendance. Associations with systolic blood pressure highlight importance of cardiometabolic risk screening and primary care coordination. Results support integrating culturally responsive yoga into survivorship care and further investigation into intervention modalities. P 18.11 Yoga versus health education for persistent fatigue in patients with post-COVID syndrome: A multicenter randomized controlled trial Lisa Mörchen 1,2 , Prof. Dr. Holger Cramer 1,2 1 Institute for General Practice and Interprofessional Care, University Hospital Tübingen, Tübingen, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 2 Robert Bosch Center for Integrative Medicine and Health, Bosch Health Campus, Stuttgart, Baden-Württemberg, Germany Purpose : Post-COVID syndrome, defined by persistent symptoms lasting beyond 12 weeks of a SARS-CoV-2 infection, affects both severe and mild COVID-19 cases. Chronic inflammation and immune dysregulation seem to be associated with post-COVID fatigue. Despite its impact on healthcare and the economy, effective treatments are limited. Yoga and health education have been shown to be effective for fatigue in other related conditions. The aim of this study therefore is to investigate the efficacy, safety, and cost-effectiveness of yoga and health education on post-COVID persistent fatigue. Method(s): A randomized controlled trial with 100 patients with persistent fatigue due to post-COVID syndrome is being conducted at three study centers. Patients are randomized to two interventions, yoga and health education. Both interventions include 12 weeks of 90-minute supervised group sessions and 60 minutes of home practice per week. The primary outcome measure is fatigue on the Chalder Fatigue Scale 12 weeks after randomization. Secondary outcome measures include post-exertional malaise (DSQ-PEM), health-related quality of life (SF-12, EQ-5D-5L), anxiety, depression (HADS), stress (PSS10), sleep quality (PSQI), hand grip strength, laboratory parameters and adverse events. Physical activity analysis and heart rate variability are assessed exploratively. In addition, health economic analyses as well as mediator and moderator analyses including self-reported body awareness, self-efficacy, personality traits, and treatment credibility/expectations are conducted. Results : Presentation of the primary outcome measure is planned following data analysis in February. Conclusions : The study can provide insights on the efficacy, safety, and cost-effectiveness of yoga and health education for post-COVID fatigue to inform health policy and improve healthcare. Its strengths include an active control group to account for therapist and patient attention, comprehensive assessments of patient-reported outcomes, laboratory and performance data, and a societal perspective. Whitehead MPH, C-IAYT, ERYT 200 3 , Marlysa Sullivan DPT, PT, C-IAYT, ERYT 500 3 , Stephanie L. Taylor PhD 4 , Spencer Hildie BS 5 , Collin Calvert PhD 6,7 , Timothy P. Hogan PhD 8,2 , Steven B. Zeliadt PhD 5,9 , Diana J. Burgess PhD 6,7 1 Research and Development Service, Dallas VA Medical Center, Dallas, TX, United States 2 Department of Health Economics, Systems, and Policy, Peter O’Donnell Jr. School of Public Health, UT Southwestern Medical Center, Dallas, TX, United States 3 Office of Patient-Centered Care and Cultural Transformation, United States Veterans Health Administration, Washington, DC, United States 4 Center for the Study of Healthcare Innovation, Implementation and Policy, Greater Los Angeles VA Healthcare System, Los Angeles, CA, United States 5 Center of Innovation for Veteran-Centered and Value-Driven Care, VA Puget Sound Healthcare System, Seattle, WA, United States 6 Center for Care Delivery and Outcomes Research, Minneapolis VA Health Care System, Minneapolis, MN, United States 7 University of Minnesota Medical School, Minneapolis, MN, United States 8 Center for Health Optimization and Implementation Research, VA Bedford Healthcare System, Bedford, MA, United States 9 Department of Health Services, School of Public Health, University of Washington, Seattle, WA, United States Purpose : The Veterans Health Administration (VHA) provides eight complementary and integrative health (CIH) therapies, including yoga, to Veterans. This evaluation assessed progress toward, and barriers to, implementation of yoga nationally across VHA. Method(s): We conducted a national survey of VHA facilities. We invited individuals who were identified as being most knowledgeable about yoga offerings at each facility to complete the online survey. Data were analyzed using descriptive statistics. Results : We invited 230 individuals to participate and received 172 responses (75% response rate) representing 113 of 132 VHA facilities from which we sampled. Most (92%) indicated yoga was being offered at their facility, and received subsequent questions about their yoga programs. Three-fourths (76%) reported that yoga was offered in-person (76%), however, 57% indicated virtual options were also available. At facilities where condition-specific yoga was offered (35%), pain was the most common condition (80%). At facilities where practice-specific yoga was offered (26%), adaptive yoga was most commonly offered (90%). Within most facilities (77%), any interested Veterans may participate and were most commonly connected through care team member referral (80%). Full-time VHA employees provided yoga at most facilities (75%), and most had ≥200-hour certifications. The most common Veteran-related barriers included concerns about difficulty (52%), lack of knowledge of potential benefits (47%), and negative perceptions (43%) of yoga. Most common care team member-related barriers included not knowing when it is appropriate to (47%) or how to (45%) refer Veterans to yoga. Most common logistical barriers included limited human resources support (37%), lack of recognition of yoga as a local or national priority (36%), and difficulty hiring yoga instructors (34%). Conclusions : VHA has made considerable progress toward national implementation of yoga. Strategies for further bolstering implementation include marketing and education to patients/care team members, increased administrative support, and improved visibility of yoga as a priority. P 18.13 Cost-Effectiveness of Yoga: A Systematic Review Alina Schleinzer 1,2 , Julia Fernandes 2 , Thomas Ostermann 3 , Holger Cramer 1,2 1 Institute of General Practice and Interprofessional Care, University Hospital Tübingen, Tübingen, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 2 Robert Bosch Center for Integrative Medicine and Health, Bosch Health Campus, Stuttgart, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 3 Department of Psychology and Psychotherapy, University Witten/Herdecke, Witten, Nordrhein-Westfalen, Germany Purpose : Due to the limited availability of healthcare resources, cost-effectiveness is a critical factor for policymakers when deciding which interventions to incorporate into routine care. Although the clinical effectiveness of yoga has been studied, its economic impact is less well-known. While some studies provide economic data on yoga, no systematic review has comprehensively summarized the evidence on its cost-effectiveness. This review aims to address this gap by examining the current literature on the cost-effectiveness of yoga as a therapeutic intervention and providing evidence for health policy and resource allocation decisions. Method(s): A systematic review was conducted by searching electronic databases for relevant studies. The review included randomized controlled trials that reported full health economic assessments of costs and health outcomes. Yoga was the primary intervention compared with any control group. Two authors independently performed the screening process, data extraction, and quality assessment. Quality was evaluated using the Consensus on Health Economic Criteria (CHEC) assessment tool. Results : Six studies met the inclusion criteria. From a societal perspective, yoga was cost-effective, achieving more incremental quality-adjusted life years (QALYs) at a lower cost than standard treatment. From the perspective of healthcare systems or employers, yoga led to additional QALYs but at a higher cost. However, the incremental costs remained below the €20,000 willingness-to-pay threshold. The quality assessment found that 63% to 89% of the studies met the quality criteria. Conclusions : This review of six studies is the first systematic review to examine the cost-effectiveness of yoga. The results indicate that yoga costs are below the willingness-to-pay threshold, suggesting that yoga is cost-effective, especially from a societal perspective when productivity losses are considered. P 18.14 Defining Yoga Therapy in U.S. Clinical Research: Implications for Standards, Certification, and Healthcare Integration Dr. Kelli Bethel PT, DPT 1 , Leigh Leibel PhD Candidate, MSc 2 , Dr. Marilyn Peppers-Citizen PhD 3 , Dr. L. Susan Wieland MPH, PhD 4 , Maxwell Bethel MSc, OMS-II 5 1 University of Maryland Graduate School, Baltimore, MD, United States 2 Columbia University Irving Medical Center, New York, NY, United States 3 Notre Dame of Maryland University School of Integrative Health, Baltimore, MD, United States 4 Georgetown University School of Medicine, Washington, DC, United States 5 Touro College of Osteopathic Medicine, Great Falls, MT, United States Purpose : This study evaluated how yoga therapy is defined, differentiated from general yoga, and implemented in U.S.-based clinical research, and examined the extent to which this research aligns with professional standards established by the International Association of Yoga Therapists (IAYT). Although IAYT provides a consensus definition and scope-of-practice guidelines, the degree of adherence to these standards within U.S. clinical research remains unclear. Method(s): We performed a scoping review of peer-reviewed clinical research describing 'yoga therapy'. Of 178 clinical studies published between 2016 to 2024, 22 studies published in the U.S. met inclusion criteria for this U.S.-focused analysis. Data extraction captured study design, targeted health conditions, intervention protocol components, interventionist professional qualifications, assessment practices, and the use of intervention modifications. Findings were compared with U.S.-specific IAYT definitions and scope-of-practice standards. Results : Among the 22 studies, 27% explicitly defined yoga therapy and 23% differentiated it from general yoga. Baseline assessments were reported in 96% of studies, while history or current condition assessments appeared in 55%. Therapeutic goal setting was reported in 23% of studies, and intervention adaptability in 64%, with physical or procedural modifications described in 55% of those cases. Intervention protocols most commonly included yoga postures (82%), breathing practices (73%), and meditation or mindfulness (73%). Relaxation techniques were included in 36% of studies, lifestyle guidance in 9%, and hand gestures or yogic philosophy in 5%. Interventions were delivered by yoga therapists in six studies, with four reporting professional certification. Conclusions : Yoga therapy terminology and implementation in U.S. clinical research demonstrate inconsistent alignment with IAYT definitions and standards, particularly regarding comprehensive assessment practices, inclusion of non-physical components, and credential reporting. Greater standardization will be essential to strengthen professional credibility, support policy recognition, and facilitate the integration of yoga therapy into the U.S. healthcare system. P 18.15 Remote-Delivered Yoga Interventions for Pain Management: a Scoping Review Dr. Mirela I. Bilc PhD 1,2 , Alina Schleinzer 1,2 , Holger Cramer 1,2 1 Institute for General Practice and Interprofessional Care, University Hospital Tübingen, Tübingen, BW, Germany 2 Robert Bosch Center for Integrative Medicine and Health, Bosch Health Campus, Stuttgart, BW, Germany Purpose : Yoga is increasingly recognized as an effective non-pharmacological approach for managing various types of pain. Remote delivery of yoga has gained interest as a way to improve accessibility and overcome barriers to in-person participation. This scoping review aimed to map the extent and type of evidence related to remote-delivered yoga interventions for pain management. Method(s): The review was conducted in accordance with JBI methodology for scoping reviews and reported following PRISMA-ScR guidelines. A comprehensive search was performed in August 2025 across Medline, Cochrane Library, Embase, PsycINFO, CINAHL, and the grey literature database BASE. Eligibility screening and data extraction were performed independently by two assessors. Results : A total of 48 peer-reviewed studies were included. Eighteen studies (38%) addressed pain as a primary symptom, while 30 studies (63%) considered pain as a secondary symptom. In 43 studies (90%), yoga was the sole intervention, while 5 studies (10%) combined yoga with educational content. Yoga was remote-delivered synchronously via video conferencing in 23 studies (48%), asynchronously through platforms such as audio, video, VR, or web-based content in 15 studies (31%), and using both delivery methods in 6 studies (13%). Four studies (8%) did not report delivery details. The study designs were diverse, including 25 randomized controlled trials (52%), 9 before-and-after studies (19%), 5 non-randomized studies (10%), 5 mixed-methods studies (10%), 3 qualitative studies (6%), and 1 survey (2%). Seventeen of the included studies (35%) were classified as feasibility studies. In terms of timing, 11 (23%) studies were conducted pre-COVID-19-pandemic, 23 (48%) during the pandemic, and 14 (29%) were likely conducted during the pandemic based on publication date only. Conclusions : This scoping review highlights a growing and diverse body of evidence on remote-delivered yoga interventions for pain management across various populations and settings. However, variability in delivery methods and intervention reporting suggests the need for further high-quality research to assess effectiveness and guide implementation. P 18.16 Cost-effectiveness of a yoga intervention in addition to usual care for patients with post-covid fatigue compared to health education in addition to usual care Alina Schleinzer 1,2 , Lisa Mörchen 1,2 , Mirela-Ioana Bilc 1,2 , Michael Jeitler 1,2,3,4 , Christian Keßler 3,5,4 , Holger Cramer 1,2 1 Institute of General Practice and Interprofessional Care, Tübingen, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 2 Rober Bosch Centrum for Integrative Medicine and Health, Stuttgart, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 3 Institute of Social Medicine, Epidemiology and Health Economics, Charité - Universitätsmedizin Berlin, Berlin, Berlin, Germany 4 Department of Internal and Nature-Based Therapies, Immanuel Hospital Berlin, Berlin, Berlin, Germany 5 Charité – Universitätsmedizin Berlin, Corporate Member of Freie Universität Berlin, Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin, Charité Competence Center for Traditional and Integrative Medicine (CCCTIM), Berlin, Berlin, Germany Purpose : Post-COVID fatigue, the most common symptom experienced by patients after a SARS-CoV-2 infection, represents a new clinical condition that affects individuals, their environments, the healthcare system, and the broader economy. Effective treatments are needed to address these negative consequences for affected individuals and systems. Thus, when evaluating therapeutic interventions, it is crucial to consider the necessary resources to achieve desired effectiveness, particularly given limited healthcare resources. This study aims to evaluate the cost-effectiveness of yoga in addition to usual care versus health education in addition to usual care. Method(s): An economic evaluation is being conducted alongside a multicenter, randomized, controlled trial (RCT) of a 12-week yoga program for patients with persistent fatigue due to post-COVID syndrome. As Economic evaluation, cost-effectiveness and cost-utility analyses are being carried out and presented from the societal perspective. The Chalder Fatigue Scale score is used as the health outcome for the cost-effectiveness analysis. For the cost-utility analysis, quality-adjusted life years (QALYs) are derived from the EQ-5D-5L. The incremental cost-effectiveness ratio (ICER) and the incremental cost-utility ratio (ICUR) are calculated, and cost-effectiveness and cost-utility acceptability curves are generated. Additionally, sensitivity analyses are performed to determine the robustness of the estimated costs. Results : One hundred patients were randomized to either the intervention group (yoga) or the control group (health education). The RCT is expected to conclude in January of 2025. Conclusions : To our best knowledge, this is the first study to evaluate the cost-effectiveness of yoga for post-Covid fatigue. The strengths of this study include its societal perspective, its use of QALYs for comparison with other treatments, and its use of patient-reported outcomes. The study's findings will provide evidence of the potential of yoga and health education to improve patient outcomes, optimize healthcare delivery, and reduce the economic burden. P 18.17 Yoga for asthma: A systematic review and meta-analysis Alina Schleinzer 1,2 , Thomas Zsakany 1 , Michael Jeitler 1,2,3,4 , Mirela-Ioana Bilc 1,2 , Dennis Anheyer 1,2,5 , Holger Cramer 1,2 1 Institute of General Practice and Interprofessional Care, Tübingen, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 2 Robert Bosch Centrum for Integrative Medicine and Health, Stuttgart, Baden-Württemberg, Germany 3 Institute of Social Medicine, Epidemiology and Health Economics, Charité - Universitätsmedizin Berlin, Corporate Member of Freie Universität Berlin and Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin, Berlin, Berlin, Germany 4 Department of Internal and Nature-Based Therapies, Immanuel Hospital Berlin, Berlin, Berlin, Germany 5 Department of Psychology and Psychotherapy, University Witten/Herdecke, Witten, Nordrhein-Westfalen, Germany Purpose : Asthma, a chronic respiratory disease, affects three hundred million people worldwide, and its prevalence has increased significantly since 1980. While pharmacological treatments are central to asthma management, many patients use yoga to improve their condition. A 2014 systematic review and meta-analysis of 15 studies concluded that yoga, including breathing exercises, is more effective than standard care in alleviating asthma symptoms and is relatively safe for patients with asthma. However, the authors concluded that there is insufficient evidence to recommend yoga as a routine intervention for asthma patients. Given the growing body of research on this topic in the last 10 years, this systematic review aims to provide an updated overview of the effects of yoga on asthma patients. Method(s): Electronic databases were searched up to April 2024 to identify relevant articles. Randomized controlled trials (RCTs) of yoga for patients with asthma of all ages, compared to any control intervention, were included. Eligible RCTs had to assess asthma control, symptoms, quality of life, or pulmonary function. Standardized mean differences (SMDs) with 95% confidence intervals (CIs) were calculated. Two authors independently conducted the screening process, data extraction, and quality assessment. The quality of the included studies was assessed using the Cochrane Risk of Bias Tool 2.0. Results : A total of 37 RCTs with 3,185 participants met the inclusion criteria and were included in the qualitative analysis. Twenty-three of these studies (62%) were published less than 10 years ago. More than half of the included studies, 19, were conducted in India. Meta-analysis and quality assessment are currently being performed, and the results will be presented at the congress. Conclusions : This study provides an updated overview of the current evidence on the effectiveness of yoga for people suffering from asthma.
